# Chapter Summaries

## Volume 01 index

**Volume 01 — The Bellweather-Living Road (Chapters 1–50)**, outlined in `outline/volume-01.md`.

| Blocks | Chapters | Content |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1–10 | The nineteenth of Mudmonth: the bell, the crossing, the return he did not prepare for, the seizure, the fever. **Written.** |
| 2 | 11–20 | What the fever cost everyone else: the hearing, the refused claim, the renewal line, the path going on without him. |

Stage arc: Stage 0 into Stage 1, qualified at Chapters 41–47 and not before. Time: the sixth of Mudmonth to the last week of Fallowmonth, YR 312.

## Batch 0001 — Chapters 1–10, "The Nineteenth of Mudmonth"

**Ch 1 · the sixth of Mudmonth, late afternoon · Aren.** Flood stage at Sedge Reach. Aren counts eight load points on a four-bay deck, chalks the piers, and argues four parties with money at stake into one more day: light loads through Thursday, no carts, half-fare boat passes for the millers. Journeyman Bind on a batten under the third bay. The cracked Bellweather bell is still on the Old Toll cradle and the bridge guild wants it off the roll at six marks. He tells everyone he bought it as scrap bronze for the tools a foundry will not recast for him, which is true and not the reason. Friendly people repeat the fever account to his face; he does not correct them. He gives the barge master the ninth and the truth instead of a date, then signs a hold on the bell in his own name, good to the guild's sitting on the twenty-fourth, with the tool-chest as surety. Six marks he cannot raise, against a chest worth three and a half. **Change:** a private decision four other people have to live with. **Ends:** he sleeps with his back to the wall.

**Ch 2 · the eleventh, early morning · Aren.** The bell is grown into its cradle; the split lip knocks the yoke every time it moves. Wenna Koll wants the crossing kept, because eleven of her people are stranded by the road everybody is arguing about. Tovan offers the honest six marks and wants the metal gone before noon. The guild's removal notice is dated this morning, eight days before the anniversary, and the clerk is already on the road. **Aren refuses the money and signs the guild's bond for six marks, with Tovan as the witness he did not choose.** He also **names the tool-chest himself, before the clerk has finished asking** — he had the decision in his coat when he walked onto the bridge at eight in the morning and worked out half a second afterwards that he had made it already. That is the choice the chapter is for: not the tool, which the clerk would have suggested, but a man volunteering to be the answerable one before anybody offers him the form. Tovan puts the true thing on the river: *you were the one they asked to hold it.* He puts the bell back and works his own sill, which is the only paid work in his life. After the bell is back on the cradle and both men have their hands full and are standing clear, it gives one dull note by itself, out of the split, and the whole bridge hears it and every one of them explains it. Wenna: a woman from the salt loft has been asking the ferrymen which repairer signed the sill. **Change:** he has chosen the paper that makes him answerable over the money that would make him free, and told nobody why. **Ends:** he cuts lime out of the sill and finds himself listening for it.

**Ch 3 · the twelfth, afternoon · Mara.** The Bellweather Claims Archive, two flood-safe rooms over a salt merchant, in a room with its rule written on the wall in fair copy: an anchor is recorded on a sworn tradesman's word and not on a clerk's. She needs Aren, and the registrar at the Crown registry has already quoted her four shillings for a roll that is with the marshal's emergency office. She shows him what she cannot argue with: **Ferris Oat's thirty-one days of brine weights with the arithmetic intact**, and Bess Alder's parish list that splits four thousand back into two hundred dead at Alder Cross. **Exchange:** custody entry, claim number, brine-weight extract, and her signature on a county filing, in return for an **undated** promise that he will not ring the bell. He signs the whole of it having already chosen the nineteenth, and does not tell her the bell rings by itself. She notes the shape of the silence in her day-book. Once, coldly, in the margin: *Vey, Hanna, 9,* of Hushwater — no relation, matter closed, and it must never be opened again. **Change:** hostile cooperation is now a contract, and she is not fooled. **Ends:** eight days, one undated promise, and a renewal in the province on the second of Greenmonth.

**Ch 4 · the night of the eighteenth into the nineteenth, ninth hour · Aren.** The storm. A carter's man drowned on the seventeenth and the ferrymen go out to ring for the drowned on the cracked bell, because it is the only bell on the reach anyone will leave alone. He breaks the promise. Three days of waiting, three things in his body rather than his head — a place, an anchor, a witness he does not have — and the road-crews' saying he has never credited: three days out and three days back. The bell is lifted by its headstock, never its body, and the crack must not go face up. Oathlight dull red inside the split. **Gatehouse:** *A road needs a living hand to open it.* The seam comes into the rain as weather, not light. He steps through off his own repacked sill, thinking for four seconds that he is going to fix it. **Change:** the roadmark, and the verse of his mother's river song that will not land on its note again. **Ends:** a whole, sound bell going across a hundred roofs in a city that is working.

**Ch 5 · path time, cold morning · Aren.** North-bank steps of a great arched bridge, in the wrong place, with no papers and a bar. The toll-man wants a farthing for coming up steps he did not cross. Children ask what he is here for. The bridge carries a washing line over the middle span every morning, and a four-bay timber gate bridge eight hundred feet off gives him the geometry of the arch, the cutwaters, the repointing, and a hole under the second pier. Then he says the name, and the street goes quiet, and the question behind every face is *is the fever back.* **The fever was real here**: two hundred and eleven dead of the low quarter, a local ledger, a woman with her shutters shut. He says he is not here to fetch anyone and nobody is owed to him — and then catches himself already arranging his face to hear their grief. **The elapse turns.** He learns the fish-scale argument from a woman hanging washing: the table tells you what the river has done, the fingers tell you what your boat will find. He writes the roadmark in his notebook. **Change:** he came for survivors and wants their story, and the shame arrives before the understanding. **Ends:** the bench sends a ferrywright.

**Ch 6 · path time, midday · Aren.** Third slip, a hull on the blocks, a garboard split nine feet aft of the knuckle. **Sera Quen** says out loud that she will not be grateful, because a debt owed to a grateful stranger is a leash, and that a steward maintains and a keeper is a word for a man somebody else has appointed to own it. She refuses his method twice: his anchor is the hull's own frame, the doorway beside the wound. **He pushes anyway, because he is better than the room, and the working fails the way workings fail** — the force goes into the anchor, a seam runs three feet, and a hull-hand of eighteen opens his hand on the iron. Quen teaches it properly: a narrow bound, a four-inch block in compression, the old bollard stone as the anchor, **Aren as the counterweight, not the anchor**, and a witness who would have refused, said out loud. It is then established that the first thing happened on this slip cannot be denied and is in the yard book under her hand. She warns him that a motion does not travel. **Change:** relief at not being the best man in the room, immediately followed by the knowledge that relief is not welcome. **Ends:** a note from the bench — *The bell remembers the night* — and a warning that the bench does not want money and wants its answer withheld until it has its terms.

**Ch 7 · path time, evening, and the six days that follow · Aren.** The guild hall over the arch, the bench on the crown stone, an oath-stone in a lead-lined trough under cold water, renewed every Longlight, cut on the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301: *no order, writ, or office shall close it while hands remain on it.* **The third bell** is the crossing-closed bell, rung from the crown once and long, and it cracked on the third stroke of the refusal — so the hinge is the night the guild refused the writ, and the bell is still in the founder's yard on two trundles, unrecast. A writ came to the north gate on the fourteenth of YR 301, was read in this room by a man who was told to read nothing else, and was refused on the deck at the second hour of the nineteenth; the tender found two hundred people on the deck who nobody had ordered, and its captain sat on the toll stone in the rain for a day and a half with a written duty and no instruction to use it, and a second boat brought a written recall on the twenty-second with a name he refuses to read aloud. The guild kept the **counterfoil**; the signature is on the other half, in a register, in **toll-house hands**, and the toll-house steward is already trying to buy a foreign stranger with a trade road. A second man puts the **Hushwater crossing book** on the table before the terms: eleven crossings, nine names, a cooperative that counts ferry hands and hearths and not souls, a best estimate of near sixty, and the whole argument that *dependent places* is where a person is made to disappear. The bench's terms: their refusal, in his hand, to whoever sends the next order, and a promise to return. **And the volume's hinge, stated by Aren in a plain sentence so a reader can check it:** it is **one bell**. Nine stone, split lip to shoulder, and the founder's mark on the shoulder is the Bellweather founder's mark, which he read in a Merefen scrap yard on a wet morning before he ever came up the north steps. It is under a tarred cloth on two trundles in that yard **and** it stood on an iron cradle on a bridge abutment in his own country, and he rang it there in the storm, and both facts are true, and he has tried for six days to make them agree and cannot. The Crown's own clearance entry in Merefen reads *unclassified salvage*, with no city and no river on it, entered in **YR 302, a year after the writ**. Nobody in the batch explains it and no one may. The newest fact: an eight-inch crack in the **outer ring of the second pier**, not growing this month, whose honest repair is a new cutwater — nine months of the bench's capacity against a debt pledged to the ninth of a grandson. He volunteers for the thaw work, and on the fourth day walks four miles of perfect raised causeway north into reed, and turns round at the old tide mark. **Change:** investigator to participant, given work he would have taken anyway. **Ends:** the only iron in that city with his work in it is a pin, and he has no way home.

**Ch 8 · the twenty-seventh, midday, market morning · Aren.** He arrives on his own sill into a fever, a clean rectangle in the moss, and Rell's watch. **Nine days, not three.** The market is on because the fourth-day market of the twenty-fourth was lost with the road, and the Tuesday barge is lying off with no berth because the wharf road is four hundred yards of bridge road. The sealed order is dated the **sixteenth** and delivered on the evening of the eighteenth, before the storm and before anything had happened, and it names *the road-keeper of Merefen, and the holder of the Bellweather hinge.* The bell went off the abutment at first light on the nineteenth and the cradle has been empty for eight days. Rell believes the bell brought the fever, says so, and will answer for the seizure. In front of forty witnesses Aren says the whole thing twice: the city is standing, the bench refused a writ, the bell cracked ringing the refusal, and he was twenty-one, sent with a partial order, and he never reached the inner city, and for three years he gave the account that the fever took Bellweather while his own notes sat in a coat. He cannot sing the verse. He puts his palm on the pale cross in the sill. **Gatehouse:** *What you carry back will not remain only yours.* **Change:** a private certainty traded for a public record he does not control. **Ends:** he cannot get up the steps.

**Ch 9 · the twenty-seventh to the thirtieth · Tovan.** A fortnight's scour repair done in three and a half days by the wrong brother; six hours of it his own error, learned about at the bottom of a crib at the fourth hour. A chalked tally on the back of his hand and eleven days' pay he cannot give. Three clients want a date; he gives them the ninth and refuses to be asked a fourth time in front of his crew. The **unnamed factor** for the western-channel stone merchant has eleven tons of paving setts stranded on a bank, a quarter of his year's margin already gone, and a want of his own that has nothing to do with a bell: to be a man who has not yet eaten it. He decides to cut new stock at the western end so the stone becomes somebody else's problem for a season, and says he will come back on the first and ask again, in the yard, not in front of the crew — "a concession and not a favour." **He stays addressed by office; the six-name cap is not spent on him.** The guild clerk will take three and a half against six and the chest is worth three and a half. He goes up to the room. **The refusal, in his own voice and with no appeal in it:** he will mend the bridge, he will not hold the road, he will not keep this, and he will not be the man holding the span when it goes. And the estrangement gets a shape, said once, for the first time: he was ten when Aren left, he wrote at eleven about a roof and an apple tree, and there was no word back for two years, and he did the roof himself over three summers and has never said so until now. **Aren's fever-talk corrects itself twice in one speech:** the number two hundred and eleven came from **the ferryman on the north bank**, and the woman on the gallery is the one who asked *How would you know?* — they are not the same person and neither is the woman on Ferry Lane. Aren cannot answer the refusal, feverish, and the day ends. He is glad, at four in the morning, that the answer to *will you say it if I say it* came to him in a room where nobody could hear. **Change:** the loyalty survives and the availability does not. **Ends:** he pays what he has, tells the crew he will be there as long as there is a bridge, and tells almost no one.

**Ch 10 · the thirtieth, the day the fever breaks · Aren.** The salt-loft rooms, and **Lysa Fenn**, magistrate of Merefen, who has come out from the provincial town at Mara's written request, and who tells him — unasked, and to Mara's pointed silence — that she took a filing from him eleven years ago, that it is a different document from the bell, and that she will not discuss it in this room. **Mara's terms are the scene:** a filed exhibit, a chain of custody, a date, and a second witness — and she will not file a crossing claim on his word. The chain has a hole in it the size of a bell and the hole is the document: it is Crown salvage, and an exhibit in Crown custody is Crown property, so the Archive's rule of one set of records outside Crown custody is invoked for the first time in four years and earns itself. Lysa takes the exhibit **beneath** his account, so that the first thing a reader sees is a seal and a date and not a man explaining himself, sets the seizure hearing for the **fourth of Greenmonth**, and refuses to hear the crossing first. A second paper calls him road-keeper again and summons him to the provincial town on the ninth of Greenmonth; there is no such office in the charter, the county list, or the crown's schedule. He asks Lysa what a keeper is and she asks him. **He hands over the counterfoil with no condition and keeps no copy**, and signs the sworn account: the partial order, the failure to reach the inner city, three years of the fever account, eleven years of field notes in a coat, and both of his omissions, to Mara and to his brother. Lysa Fenn states the honest answer to the seizure now, in advance, as **three days from the day written on the paper to the morning the bell came off the abutment** — *not* eight (the empty cradle when he got back) and *not* nine (his absence); the three days are what make the order indefensible, because it predates the storm. The town's own ledger comes to **eleven marks and four pence**, itemised, and Mara refuses to let it share a page with the exhibit. She straightens an iron clamp with the motion of the narrow Mend, Lysa names it a mend and records that she does not know what she saw, and both of them notice. **Change:** the road he has held alone for eleven years now belongs to somebody else's filing system. **Ends:** the anchor-glass slip — two Steppe discs, four marks, money before cutting, **and the four marks are Tovan's** — and a man who can do nothing about any of it except be in Merefen when it is needed.

## Batch 0002 — Chapters 11–20, "What the Fever Cost Everyone Else"

Block 2. **Zero Gatehouse lines in the batch, by budget** — both of Volume 01's two were spent on the ringing and the return. The System's absence is the pressure: the road in him is open and is not usable, and nothing in Merefen answers when he puts his hand on the stone. POVs Aren ×5 (11, 13, 15, 18, 20), Mara ×3 (12, 14, 19), Tovan ×1 (17), Lysa Fenn ×1 (16, the only time in the volume anybody holds her view). Six names, all pre-established: **Jos Rusk, Bram Ottery, Halven, Pei Tarrow, Sarn Oyelaran, Ilvane Quarren**.

**Ch 11 · the first of Greenmonth, mid-morning · Aren.** Two days out of the fever room and the town has decided it does not need him. He wants a job he can be paid for and gets a shear pin, eight pence and a shilling, from a brother who owes him eleven days and will not be asked. The scour hole on Sedge Reach is the work that is actually waiting and it is unaffordable: eleven tons of stone, a fortnight with the road shut, a crew of four paid by somebody, and the trust has nothing until Longlight — **it is arithmetic, not cruelty.** He finds the guild's **bench mark re-cut by hand in Mudmonth and a quarter of an inch low**, takes a level off it twice, writes the figure, and is told a road warden does not put papers in against the bench's own mark. Free, unwanted, correct, and worth twenty years of levels. **The factor** for eleven tons of stranded setts gets the true price of his own mistake and a date he is not promising. The Old Toll sill's cross is a **working scar, dry inside a streaming bridge** — a scar, his, and the sill will take one more and not a third. He puts his hand flat on it and **nothing answers**: no warmth, no voice, no seam. Then the glasswright: **Master Ilvane Quarren**, sixty-eight, will not sell to a man who cannot say who will be standing on the stone when it goes, and says his real want out loud — the Steppe road to be a road and not a queue, to be able to stop, and a bridge somewhere with his name on the seat and the man who set it alive. **Ends:** four words on a market board with no rule over it — *Bell taken by the crown on the 19th. Nobody asked. Ask Kest, he was there* — and a note from the salt loft: *A man has been in the outer room three days running… he says it is about the crossing.*

**Ch 12 · the first of Greenmonth, afternoon · Mara.** A notice to file before the second, and a man on the bench in the outer room who has been there three days. **Jos Rusk**, 51, Hushwater ferryman, survivor, has come to file and is not here about money. **Anwen Rusk's crossing book**: nine years, every crossing, in a hand other men checked, and Rusk says out loud what it cannot do — *a hand that is regular proves a boat went across, on a day, in a direction, with a cargo; it does not prove why a village was not there in the morning.* **Mara's personal refusal**, made in front of a claimant: she will not head a claim *Bryn Rusk, 11, lost on the last ferry out*, because a child at the top of a page is the easiest possible shape for a piece of paper to take and it is the shape that loses. **She puts the cause in dispute in writing — *the record does not show the cause* — and tells him it will cost him**, because a claim with a wrong cause can be disproved in a year and a claim with the right cause cannot be proved at all. He is not argued with; he is heard, and the hearing is written. The **Culling Order** is named once, in his mouth, with no member attached, and Mara holds the word in a room a magistrate can see and a clerk cannot. The last page: eleven lines, and the eleventh in a **different hand** — *Persons, 11. Not landed, drowned channel.* **Ends:** very quietly, *Has anybody told the ferrymen?* — and she has to say no.

**Ch 13 · the second of Greenmonth, mid-morning · Aren.** The finding is read aloud in the trust room, by a county clerk who refuses to improve it. Clause one: no claim to Bellweather survives. Clause two: the crossing and its dependent places **was not built**, and is not maintained. Clause three renews the county's emergency power for a year, to unnamed officers. Aren does the thing he is good at — he reads the sentence as a structure and finds the load path, and tells a room that *not built* is a statement about masonry and not about a city, and that if the Crown asks the sentence to hold one more weight it will be asked about a bridge and not a fever, in a room where there is a mason. **He gets one thing wrong**: he names the marshal's office in front of eleven people on the morning a finding was read, and a clerk leaves having decided to write a memorandum, which is the cheapest weapon in the Crown's armoury. Lysa Fenn will have to put him nearer a bench. **Mara's correction**, in front of eleven people, is the chapter's spine: her thirtieth-of-Mudmonth exhibit is now a document about a thing that never existed, and one clerk's word on a wrapper can call it *unverified* in eleven seconds; and *its dependent places* fits a city and fits the seven villages and has been used for both, and does the work of both — **said in two sentences, never in one, and the four thousand is not printed beside the villages anywhere in the series.** **The clerk's parting shot** — the office that holds the file is the one that will be asked what the file is for — names the real enemy without appearing. **Ends:** a printed fee schedule from the Crown registry, signed by a registrar, and his name.

**Ch 14 · the second of Greenmonth, afternoon · Mara.** **Halven**, registrar at the provincial registry, travels with a locked chest between his boots. He is not a villain; he is a man with a post, a family, a legal exposure and a price, and the price is **a charge for a search**, which is how a Crown registry keeps itself from being blamed. He brings the Crown's Bellweather salvage and clearance volume and, unasked, discloses a **gap on a shelf in the fourth press** where the *Anchors, schedules and dispositions, Alder Vale* book — the one book in the registry that names objects — was entered in YR 309 and is not there, off the roll, by no hand of the office. Mara writes his sentence word for word and makes **Sarn Oyelaran** attest it in her own hand, because a copyist's note is a fact about a book and a keeper's note is an argument about a Crown. **The money fight, in front of Halven:** she pays the four shillings to hold the date, cannot pay the search, writes Lysa Fenn for an order to inspect a register without fee — **eleven days** — and is honest about both facts, including that a keeper of a public register has no jurisdiction, cannot refuse a fee without a magistrate, and is therefore unassailable. Halven's want is money; Sarn's want is **a post of her own**, and Mara writes a four-line warrant for a third post in her own hand in the day-book, not in a file. **Ends:** Sarn stops in the light, reads a whole page instead of ruling it, and says the Bellweather clearance of **YR 302** is out of place in a bound order, in a hand the same age as its neighbours.

**Ch 15 · the fourth of Greenmonth, the ninth hour, market morning · Aren.** The hearing, in a salt-loft room with a wedge in the door and thirty-one people on the stair. Three documents on the table in order of age: the guild's notice of the eleventh, the sealed order of the **sixteenth**, the claim exhibit of the thirtieth. **Captain Rell** under oath, correct, frightened, and right that a Crown paper delivered to an officer competent to execute it is not refusable. **Lysa Fenn says the three days out loud in a public room** — the sixteenth to the morning of the nineteenth, off a paper written before the storm — and rules the seizure **lawful**, refuses the bell, and does not restore the guild's trust, because a municipal trust cannot be affirmed over a lawful Crown holding on the same stone. She records the authority as unproducible and warns the room that being told the law was on its side is the most expensive thing that will happen in Merefen this month. **Aren testifies about a bell and nothing else**: nine stone, a cradle grown into the stone, a bar through a headstock loop, a split outward from the sound bow, a founder's mark he read in a Merefen scrap yard. **The landing is Bram Ottery's statement**, written unasked at his own table **on the night of the second of Greenmonth** and given to the clerk at the seventh hour on the fourth, entered with the hour: what the watch did and why and who stood where — and it names the man who has been telling the market since the seventeenth that the bell carries the fever, **who is in that stair**, saying it again, and the court cannot un-sell it in a morning. Ottery's statement costs him: Rell relieves him of the sergeant's post and of nine and three months' accrual, in a corridor, and there is no promotion in it. **Ends:** the chalk on the toll-house board says lawful procedure and abeyance, and the rest of it happened in a stair.

**Ch 16 · the fourth of Greenmonth, afternoon · Lysa Fenn.** The only time in the volume anybody holds her view. In the provincial town, out of the assizes' way, she writes the finding in four sheets and signs it, and understands what she has done: **she has made herself a party.** Clause five is the mechanism — the authority is unproducible and the officers unnamed, so any office that acts again is on notice that this court asked and was not answered, and the asking is dated. She leaves a private minute saying the finding is not a victory. Her clerk brings her the thing she has been circling: the third clause renews the county's power annually **to officers nobody anywhere can name**, and a power nobody can name is a power nobody can ask about — and the clerk has brought it from a filing room, where it does not belong. **The emergency power is the hearing; the bell is the peg.** She names the eleven-year-old salvage filing once more and refuses it again, and says out loud, for the first time, the want she has wanted for a year: **to make the law useful to people who cannot afford a title or a patron**, on a salary the reeve's office calls a discourtesy, held by her family, never once appointed to anything. She stamps the inspection order — returnable on the fifteenth, eleven days — and hands Aren the finding to carry to the keeper. **Ends:** a magistrate who has gained a question and a name, and a town told in chalk that the law was on its side.

**Ch 17 · the fifth to the seventh of Greenmonth · Tovan.** The **fifth is a stated cut**: the market cleaned up, the pitch off the cobbles, nobody new, no new paper, the bell still where it is. And the town has started joining two half-sentences: *he's the one that talks, and he's the one that was away.* The work finishes — the thing a bridge carpenter dreads more than a flood. **The factor** puts four marks into the yard against a month of face-stone cutting, in the yard's chest, which is the crew's wages, and Tovan understands in about four seconds what he has done. **The guild clerk**, who is polite and therefore the worst of it, is sent to ask the brother what the road warden means to do, and gets a two-part answer: the bench wants to know whether the bond is a risk to the roll, and that is all it wants. **Pei Tarrow**, 52, freight on the Old Toll channel, mother a Hushwater Tarrow alive at sixty-eight four miles out, comes to the yard unasked to say **he is not a symbol** and to ask for freight. He has no book, no list, no dead. Tovan gives him the work, the wages, the same day off as the other three, and two rules: nobody asks him about Hushwater, and nobody gets an answer about Aren either way — because the yard is the only place in this town where a man can be paid for a day's work without being asked what he thinks about a bell. **Tovan's own want at last, in his own voice:** a paid yard and a bridge season and a brother who is not a road. **Ends:** a boy reports a bell on the far bank on the seventh, in daylight, in a dead calm, with nobody near it — and the tower has a line of sight down the old raised road. **The chapter stays in Tovan and ends on the report: the coda that used to run into Pei Tarrow's interior and end on his Friday has been cut, and the close is now Tovan doing carpenter's arithmetic in his own yard — a bell under a seal, a note in a dead calm, and no instrument in the county that will ever be asked the question. The two deck witnesses are unnamed (a hired cartage man and a hopper boy).**

**Ch 18 · the seventh of Greenmonth, the ninth hour · Aren.** The report in a cooper's bill: an hour, a direction, a sight-line, and a boy of thirteen who then went up the tower and looked and saw nothing. He goes up and looks himself. **The silence, written as deliberate:** he names Bellweather-Living, the arch, the bench, the stone under the water, the third bell, the boy of seventeen, the full minute — and gets nothing. Not a warmth, not a fold in the air, not a word. He tries the cradle iron: cold, and the only thing in it is a Crown paper. Then he **finds a working that does work** — a promise the width of a shake into a scaffold upright, and the timber standing true under a thumb while two other uprights walk out of true — and the two facts are the same fact: oathcraft is a promise made to an anchor, the sill is a door and not the thing behind it, and **the road in him is nominally open and is not a journey.** Eleven days and not one word sent or received, and a bench of nine holding an arch on his word. He writes the length of his own waiting into the notebook so he can never afterwards say he did not try, and then does the thing he has not done since the sixth of Mudmonth: sits down on the abutment and counts nine load points on a bridge he is not going to enter. **The choice at the bottom of Weir Street:** the watch, which would make a sound into a reason, or the Archive, which would make it a document worth nothing for five years and impossible to argue with. **Ends:** he writes the address of the salt loft on the wrapper. He has a door, a glass sill, four marks, and a witness to find, and he has none of the four. **Repair note:** the chapter carries **no System display at all** — the working that works is shown by the timber standing true under a thumb and two other uprights walking out of true, with no light, no named craft-word, and no sensation. The sill returns nothing, written as deliberate silence. The three forward-look dates are fixed: the filing is **tomorrow** (the eighth), the crossing is **in two days** (the ninth), and he began filing **a month ago**.

**Ch 19 · the eighth of Greenmonth, the salt-loft rooms and the yard · Mara.** The renewal's damage, made concrete: a second column in the day-book headed *entered under the finding of the second, and therefore a year weaker*, and — under the wall rule about a sworn tradesman's hand — **a new rule she has never added before: no combined figure is entered in this office.** Sarn brings the Crown volume again: the YR 302 clearance entry is **out of place, in a hand the same age as its neighbours**, and Mara spends three days deciding she is entitled to the word *altered* and not *out of place*. The decision: **she does not publish it.** Sarn argues, publishes-be-damned, and is right — and then does the thing Mara did not ask for and writes her own attestation in her own hand, with the date, the hour, and no opinion. **A registrar's fee, understood:** the four shillings bought a *date of search*, not the ledger, and the gap on the shelf stands until the order returns on the fifteenth. **The magistrate's clerk**, who has walked four miles up a hill to serve a finding, comes to ask whether the seven villages are *dependent places* — because if he enters them as such they fall with the crossing, and if he enters them as names they are not parties to a finding about a bridge. He asks for a figure, and **Mara says why, out loud, in front of a court officer, that this office never adds**: a total is one number and can be disproved once, and nobody has to look at the reason; a name can only be looked for. He enters them as names, in the county's spelling, with the figures separately and *sum not stated* in the total line **and a space left where the eighth figure would go.** **Jos Rusk** returns as promised, with the market's version of the eel-man's stair and the lesson of it — a record got him further in a week than nine years of being right. The wrapper's **eleventh line** needs a tradesman's attestation, and the only man whose hand a court will take is the man two Crown papers already call a keeper; Mara says what it costs him and says the true thing, which is that a man nobody can read can be destroyed at leisure. **In the yard**, she wants the quarter-inch bench mark entered in her book, Tovan refuses to put a clerk's figure on a factor's paper, and she reads the cutting contract standing up and understands both ways to have four marks: a bond is faith, a debt against a contract is a document. **The bell report is filed as reported** — an hour, a direction, a sight-line, three witnesses, and no conclusion, the only page in the building nobody can improve on. **Ends:** she sends a slip down to a yard with a figure on it, and has never in four years sent a man a note that was not in a file.

**Ch 20 · the eighth of Greenmonth, evening, the yard and the Old Toll sill · Aren.** **The decision chapter.** The clerk comes into the yard, not to the bench, and offers the obvious thing — a second bond on the roll against a chest worth three and a half — and **Aren refuses it out loud, in front of his brother and four men, by handing the clerk his own sentence back**: *a man who signs six and has three and a half is a man who has signed on faith, and faith is not a currency we take.* The clerk, who is a careful man, names the alternative: **a debt, with a rate, a term, and a third party's name on it.** Tovan offers it without being asked, and says so in the room so that the story in the town afterwards will be half wrong. **Aren puts a mark and a quarter of his own on the bench and the advance is two marks and three quarters**, entered as a debt and not a surety, cleared at eight pence a day, carried on if the cutting fails, and never once in a sentence with his brother's name in it. **The clerk has to describe the surety, and the market's question arrives:** how many. Aren gives the villages by name, refuses the Crown's four thousand beside them, and says the keeper's reason out loud in a yard and credits it to her and to the magistrate's finding of the fourth — *a total is one number and can be disproved once; a name can only be looked for; the emergency power is the peg and the bell is the hearing* — and the clerk enters **no reason given** and a blank sum line, and prefers to be argued with. **The shed, at the seventh hour:** the fire is lit, there are two journeymen, and **Quarren's own shelf is the answer** — a pair of Steppe discs that came down in the winter at the count price, a year annealed since before he paid for them, standing in a cold rack since the first of Frostmonth with nobody's name on them. The slip is not a lie; a price list assumes a man comes in today. He asks the question again — who will be standing on the stone — and **Aren answers with a brother who has said he will not promise him anything, and no idea who is at the other end**, and Quarren says that is the answer he can sell glass to. He also says what the pair costs him: his shelf is empty, there is no fresh Steppe glass north of here before Longlight, and that is thirty years of a complaint answered with his own stock. **Nine shillings** is being quoted in Merefen off his own board, and it is **more than half his price** — four marks is sixteen shillings — and the man who wrote the slip is the one who calls it out. **The sill, at the last light:** the hand on the cross gets nothing, again, deliberately. Aren cuts the seat himself, in a single block of the fourth course, square to a struck line, in fifty minutes. Quarren dresses the discs and says it is the first seat he has seen square in a hundred, sets them edge to edge across the two lines of the cross with the maker's mark under the near disc, and then **hands over a page out of his own account book headed *setting of anchor-glass, seat in cut stone, and how it will fail*** — the first customer in thirty years to ask how it fails instead of how it holds. **Tovan witnesses the setting and refuses to witness the road**: he will put his hand and the hour on the fact that a thing was set, and he will not put his name under a sentence about a road, because he has not believed in one since he was seventeen. **Before he signs, he tells them both the date.** The ninth is said aloud **on the bridge, to a brother and a glasswright**, not to the street: the bench he will not be in, the clerk who will write *absent*, and then the second thing, that he does not know who is at the other end. Tovan answers it by **choosing** his line in the light of it — he will not put his name under a road and will put it under a bridge, and the distinction is to be written on the paper that same night. **Aren's undertaking**, read aloud on the bridge and signed by three hands — a maker's account, a carpenter's sight of a thing set, a date and an hour — and it says plainly that **he has no person to witness what he is about to do, and has a record instead**, and that the record is not the road. A copy goes to the Archive's press, outside Crown custody. **The bill, named aloud in the street at the eleventh hour:** four marks, two marks and three quarters of it repaid at eight pence a day; a market on the ninth with no road warden in it; **the ninth-of-Greenmonth summons unanswered, which is a gap in a provincial ledger and a fact about him in a year**; and no person on either end of the road. **This is the second of three crossings this season,** and the first one cost nine days against a saying that allows three, three days of fever, a market that did not happen, and a bell under a Crown seal that the Crown was within its rights to take. He goes home with the ninth twenty-four hours off and closing. **Ends on the decision, not a cliffhanger: he is going, and the reader can name why and what it costs.**

## Batch 0003 — Chapters 21–30, "The Second Crossing and the Refusal"

Block 3. **Zero Gatehouse lines and zero System displays, by budget.** POVs Aren ×8 (21, 22, 23, 25, 26, 27, 28, 30), Mara ×1 (24), Tovan ×1 (29). Four new names: **Alis Merrow, Hollis Dree, Captain Ruen, Reeve Aldis Fenn**; the two unfilled slots are deliberately left unfilled.

**Ch 21 · the ninth of Greenmonth, the eighth to the tenth hour · Aren.** Market morning, the yard shut, four men paid for a day off and nothing to put them on. **The Old Toll sill at the eighth hour:** the seal, the empty cradle, the seat he cut, the two discs, the oak collar with three blows in it, the maker's mark hidden under the near disc, and **he does not touch the seal and he does not try the sill** — the reason is not the glass and not the tally of two against three, it is that nobody at the far end knows the date. The **Merefen bridge guild bench at the ninth hour**: the wage advance already on the roll as a debt, and **a witness's sight of a thing set on the eighth of Greenmonth** with the date and the hour and *no reason given* — the **second** time that phrase has gone on a roll in Merefen that month, with the clerk saying there will be a third before the month is out, and that the reason will not be asked of him. (The **third** is the ninth page, entered at the yard on the **tenth** in Ch 29.) **Reeve Aldis Fenn** comes to the bench and her first sentence is about the road and not the bell, and she minuted *before* the vote that the surety was not in front of the bench. The clerk puts three options on the table; Aren takes the third — chest at three and a half, balance on his wages — **withdraws before the vote**, and the reeve says the last true thing in the room, which is that a vote taken in a man's absence is a vote about a bond and not about a man, and that in a year nobody will be able to tell the difference. He writes a sheet with an hour on it, Tovan Kest signs it and puts it on the ninth page of the week-book and not the general one, and says *no road, yes bridge*. **Change:** he leaves a town that is not waiting for him, on purpose, with a bench voting behind his back. **Ends:** nineteen days ahead of him and a city that has been up since the fourth hour.

**Ch 22 · path time, the first day, the north steps and the guild hall · Aren.** The record comes up with him and is not the thing that brings him, and there is nobody on the steps to hand it to, which tells him everything about a record and a road in four seconds. The **girl on the third step** has been holding him to a date for a week of household arguing; the **toll man** takes the last farthing, enters a farthing against a cross and a half mark against a ticket, and gives a ticket, and says the same thing he said in Mudmonth. The market under the second arch is doing the trade of a place that has lost nothing. **The guild hall, eleven people, two more than last time:** the bench's terms change — the refusal in their hand, a promise to return, and now *a witness, and the bench wants to know what a witness costs*, because a document with one hand on it is a favour and forty-one houses have shut. **Alis Merrow**, path-side toll-house steward, introduces himself, says the room is not the guild's room, and won a day and a night's argument in three houses for a foreign stranger. **Aren offers the account, not a thing:** a sworn account in a magistrate's court in a county four days off, a date, a seal, and a man who has said in public that a city of about four thousand is standing — offered *as a thing to be filed, not a thing to be believed*, and received as **Received, unverified, on the word of a stranger**, with the clerk adding that the north's account does not name this city and never will. The second pier's eight inches, the nine-month cutwater, the tolls pledged to the ninth of a grandson, the mark taken twice a year by two men and a clerk, the three northern toll-houses shut with four feet of paving under the shutter. The man with the mallet gives him a job: **the fourth camp on the north bank, making setts for the gate bridge approach.** **Change:** he came back with a case and found a bench that wants a witness, and a city that is solvent-ill on a bridge it cannot mend. **Ends:** Merrow has heard a foreign stranger offer a document, and the toll houses keep the other half of the writ.

**Ch 23 · path time, the second to the eleventh day · Aren.** **The relief of being a man on a job** is the chapter's spine, and it is discovered, not awarded. The second pier in an engineer's terms: the ring stone is sound and the mortar behind it was raked out from inside by water coming up under the arch, a new cutwater is nine months of the bench's capacity, and **a bench that cannot keep a promise is a worse thing than a bench that never made one** — which the man with the mallet moves off himself and onto a book. Eleven years of the clerk's measurements read in one sitting. **The correction:** the second of the second pier refuses his grout, shows him that a hard pointing in a moving ring is a crack somewhere he cannot get at, and they pack the joint in clay and lime to a thumb and leave it an inch and three-quarters, and Aren does the last four feet badly and then better, and writes down the man's reason exactly, and **takes the correction**. The chapter's honest hour is named as such: it is the only hour in eleven years he has stood in a room with a man better than him. **Hushwater Reach** on the ninth morning, the crossing book's second page, eleven crossings and nine names and hearths counted instead of souls, **near sixty** and the imprecision in the counting and not in the hamlet — and the woman with the book **will not let it be copied**, because a copy is a second thing somebody can lose. The counting-house rule, from the same woman: you go in because it owes you. **Change:** hands that know his hands, and a debt he has started. **Ends:** a counting house opens at the second hour and does not open for a man who is owed nothing.

**Ch 24 · the sixteenth of Greenmonth, the fourth hour onward · Mara.** Six days of holding a file for a man who is not in it, and a board in the market with a rule over every line and two lines without one. **A memorial** from the county's man, in motion since the second, defined and answered by being dull: this office answers instruments, and it makes the gap in the chain into a document with a date on it. **The inspection order returns on the fifteenth** and the *Anchors, schedules and dispositions, Alder Vale* is not on its shelf, and what she is shown instead is the fourth press's own shelf register, in which the book is entered with a **removal date — the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301 — and no name, no office and no authority.** A date can be looked for. A registrar has been helpful in a direction that is not hers and has had it attested. **A claim in the outer room that is not a Hushwater claim:** a woman from Stonebarrow with a dressed fragment of a bridge face and a name she will not give, and the honest two-part answer about what the record says and what happened. **The decision:** a second claim number entered **on her own account, against nobody**, for the production of records, resting on the out-of-place page and the missing book, on the stated ground that a file held by one keeper for one man is a favour and a file under a number is a thing a court has to answer. She prices it in four costs and pays. **And a woman who has been right four times has begun to price it and has noticed herself doing it.** The night: the cost of a crossing entered on a sheet of her own paper, in the fair hand, so that he is told it by a paper and not by his own body. **Ends inside her own point of view:** the yard on Weir Street and the Crown letter in the toll-house drawer are two things she is not going to be told about, and what she has is a hand, three numbers, and no opinion — and a page waiting for him to come back and be angry at it.

**Ch 25 · path time, the twelfth day, the wharf and the counting room · Aren.** The rule is a rule about who is owed and he is owed nothing. The correct currency for a counting house **is not a road and not a promise: it is a man who puts his own name under a figure about a road he cannot see.** Merrow corrects him, sells him a purchase on credit — a season's certificate of a load, four tons, **season to be named by the buyer and not by the office** — and puts him in the room. Merrow's want in his own voice: three northern houses shut since YR 301, four feet of paving, a hundred and twenty marks of uncollected toll, and a city that cannot mend its own bridge asking a creditor's office for a favour. The press opens. Four hundred leaves, five columns, about nine hundred lines, **and it is not a history, it is a book of what was offered.** The line for the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301, and its press-mark — **press four, die eleven, impression two of the season** — because a creditor does not keep the wax, the wax is rubbish in eleven years. And then the **seal-book**, the office's own wax impressions, and in the middle of a hundred and forty years of the Crown's traffic: **a hand laid flat on a book.** He has seen that device on a post, on a storehouse door, and in a magistrate's finding in her own name. **Then Merrow stops him** — *not that column, not in this room, and not today* — makes him come back at the second hour tomorrow, and locks the press on his own belt. **Change:** a door opened by the wrong argument, and a debt to somebody who is not the guild. **Ends:** the page is in a hand he has seen on wax.

**Ch 26 · path time, the thirteenth day, the counting room, and the guild hall that night · Aren.** Four conditions first, entered by the clerk: no copy; a fair copy that is not produced and is not signed; the fact leaves and the line does not; **and no motive.** The whole line is read: the writ, the tender on the fourteenth against the arch, the return on the twentieth, the withdrawal on the twenty-second, the three hands, the day's column — **two hundred on the deck, four days stood, one load of flour carried off the north quay and entered as ours** — and in the column headed *tendered by*, a name. **Edric Hunn, marshal.** The room does not make a sound. An old clerk, nineteen, discloses that his grandmother wrote the third hand and told it wrong in a market for eleven years; a younger one says a name in a room is a war and he can feel it in his teeth. **Aren does the arithmetic and does not name a motive** — and is four words into one when the old clerk puts his pen down and enters the arrest against him. Merrow's answer to a living marshal: *the line exonerates you and convicts you in the same four lines, and the half you will not get across is the one that costs you the case.* The name is said **twice in the city and no more** — once in the counting room, once in the guild hall because the bench-clerk will not minute a name he has not heard in a room. The bench discovers the tolls in that line were **released**, and the man with the mallet asks whether he is to be frightened or pleased. Nothing is done, and it is minuted that the toll houses were informed and did not act. **Change:** the name, the press-mark, and a room that has to decide. **Ends:** the guild bench-clerk asks the question nobody asked — **does the crossing want to be found?**

**Ch 27 · path time, the fifteenth day, the guild hall with the shutters up, and the arch at midnight · Aren.** Two hundred ordinary people at the back. The bench reads its own eleven-line account of the night — **not his account and not worse than his** — and he declines to compete with it. **Hollis Dree**, the city's toll-clerk and archivist, refuses to be sensible in front of two hundred people and says the sentence that turns the book: *a copy is a claim; a restoration is not a repair, it is a thing done to a thing by the person who pays, and the person who pays owns it afterwards, and no clause in the world says otherwise*; and *we are not waiting to be believed, we are keeping what we has been kept because being believed is not a thing that lasts and keeping is.* The **woman on Ferry Lane**, hooded, who kept her shutters shut for eleven years, refuses the room's whole argument with one sentence. He says the hardest thing available to him — that he believes this bench and not the Crown, and that it entitles him to nothing — and the bench gives him the only thing it has: **a consent with conditions, a named steward, a visible road, and nothing else.** **Sera Quen** is named as the person, and the fourth term is that any paper from the north comes to her hands and is answered by her or not at all — and the man with the mallet **separates her out loud, in the hall, from the woman who keeps the Hushwater crossing book** who refused Aren a copy in Ch 23, so the two cannot be merged later. **The two hundred and eleven is stated here for the second time in the whole book, by Dree out of the city's own ledger**; the ferryman on the north bank gave it to Aren first, in Ch 5, and remains the first source. **Ends:** Captain Ruen, who mended a hull with Aren's work in it two years ago and has said nothing to anybody, puts the question properly on the crown of the arch — is the man building the fourth camp the same man who was on that deck on the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301? — and Aren says yes.

**Ch 28 · path time, the night of the fifteenth and the eighteenth day, the arch and the hall · Aren.** He answers Ruen **in the wrong order on purpose**, giving the cheap half first: a Crown's officer, twenty-one, in a cloak, at the second hour, on that deck. Then the rest — his written order covered the Alder Vale crossing and eleven men and not this city; he sent a rider on the seventeenth for a written extension and it came back on the eighteenth in one line refusing it; he did not hold the deck, and by noon there was a finding saying a captain lost a bridge. Ruen: *do not take it off me, I would rather be angry.* Then the **bench, shutters up, sixty at the back**, and the extension of the Chapter 10 sworn account: the four things in the document, and the **fifth**, which is not in it — *I was on this deck on the night of the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301, in a Crown's cloak, at the second hour* — and then the field book out of the breast pocket of the coat he crossed in, put on the table, entered by the clerk with the hour, and the note that it is a statement of a man and not a finding of the bench, entered because the bench does not have a column for the other things and has refused to invent one. He does not ask for absolution and is not offered it. **The terms:** the consent, the named person, the visible road, a promise with a date, two witnesses and one record. **The price, named by him in two halves:** the **third and last crossing of the year**, pledged to a date in the middle of the year that nobody can promise he will meet — and **a new seat nobody has cut**, because the one he has has been used twice and the glasswright would not sell a third. **Change:** the path's terms for his return stop being a favour and become an agreement, and the man the book has been defending is a witness against himself in front of the people he came to help. **Ends:** the arithmetic, out loud, to a ceiling, and a page that cannot be asked to turn round.

**Ch 29 · the ninth to the sixteenth of Greenmonth · Tovan.** The bench, from a stool by the door. **Nobody mentions the bell for two hours and a half** — the guild is not avoiding it, it is taking one vote about one thing a man can be held to. The reeve has already given the long version in the morning; in the afternoon she gives Tovan the short one, and says the finding and the guild minute do not agree and were written three days apart and that is the honest state of this town. The vote is carried before he has decided what to say. **His one sentence, before the vote, four facts and no opinion:** six marks, a shut road, both true at the ninth hour, and the man four days off. Then he does not vote, because he is a party, and says so in a public room so nobody can call it modesty. The guild clerk comes into the yard on the tenth of his own motion and tells him what an office is. The **carrier** tells him the yard's real business: *you have had more in it since the sixth of Mudmonth than in the four years I have been coming to it, and I know exactly why, and it is not because the bridge is any good* — a queue is a man who comes down a street and looks at a thing that is wrong and says a figure out loud. **The Crown letter on the twelfth, addressed to an office that does not exist**, undeliverable, unanswerable, carried by Tovan to the toll-house and **chalked on the outside of a drawer** with the date, the hour, *No such office in this county* and *Not answered*, in the toll-clerk's own hand. The reeve refuses to receive it and he does not let her. And he takes the toll-house's kerb, which he does not like, and says so in the yard before anybody can ask. **Change:** a completed beat, and the man the yard was built to keep out of the road turns out to be the reason the yard has work. **Ends:** the sixteenth, a cart, and a man who did not ask a single question on the way up the road.

**Ch 30 · the sixteenth to the twentieth of Greenmonth · Aren.** He comes up out of the sill and cannot stand, and gets a cart. **The account, said out loud in a filing room, in his own hand, to a keeper of records who has to write it down, with Tovan Kest in the room and his week-book on the table so that the chain of custody has its second witness in front of her:** nineteen path days against six of the main world and the arithmetic that does not add up; the four marks and the two marks and three quarters; two fevers of three days; the verse he still cannot land on its note; a market on the ninth with no road warden in it; an unanswered summons; **a sergeant he has never spoken to, whose unasked statement he has used twice without once thinking about the man**; and a bench that has to decide in a year whether to restore a trust over a lawful Crown holding. **And that he is not going to get the bell back and is not going to try for it.** It is filed into the same file and under the same claim number as the first one, so that a man opening it in a year reads a seal and a date before a man explaining himself. The brine weights are not filed. **He asks for the bell report to be read back in the room**, and Mara reads it, and the limits with it. **And then he tells her the one thing he has never told her**, which is what a crossing costs in the body: the bill is not the crossing, it is that the crossing is the only thing he is good at and it costs him more the better he gets, and he has been managing her about it for two months. She enters it in the file, on his word and not her observation, and says she is not going to be relieved by it. **The fever breaks on the eighteenth.** He walks the shut wharf road, reads the market board, reads the chalk on the drawer, and turns his notebook to a page he has never seen and cannot read for a season. **Ends:** a decision with the bill attached — a bell-shaped hole in a parapet, an eight-inch crack in an outer ring four days away, a claim with a blank line under the villages, a bench that sits again in a year, eight hundred setts, and iron on stone every four seconds.

## Batch 0004 — Chapters 31–40, "The Road Shut"

Block 4. **No crossing in either direction, by budget and by plan** — the two Gatehouse lines were spent in Batch 0001 and neither the sill nor anything else answers in any of these ten chapters. POVs Aren ×5 (31, 34, 36, 38, 40) / Mara ×3 (32, 35, 39) / Tovan ×2 (33, 37). Four new names of the six allowed: **Dace Millward, Iven Sarrat, Rowan Petch, Harl Mounce**; two slots unfilled.

**Ch 31 · the twentieth and twenty-first of Greenmonth · Aren.** The arithmetic in a room over a cooper, written out because writing it is the only way of being certain the writing has not improved anything: nothing is owed to him by anybody, and the Old Toll crossing has no owner and therefore no crew. **The Sedge Reach is the only thing in Merefen that is holding and it is the one thing nobody needs**, and he is on it anyway at the fifth hour, and the discovery of the chapter is the difference between *being asked for* and *being available*, which he has never had to learn. **The guild's man with the levels, at the seventh hour, refuses the roll and explains why: a figure in our roll is a figure we are answerable for and a thing we cannot be corrected in** — the mark has been re-cut by hand three times in thirty-five years, always downward, and every level in the county is out by a quarter of an inch in the direction that makes a deck look better than it is. Two readings go in a notebook in a coat. **Then the work that is on nobody's roll and that he writes nowhere**: four hours dressing the arrises either side of a worn hollow in the fish-weir sill for Wenna Koll, and six feet of oak handrail with two draw-bored pegs on the nine-tread stair outside his own door, made out of a cooper's bin. **And in the yard, in front of four men, a factor asks whether the road is shut for good** and he answers the question asked and puts the load-bearing fact on the table unasked: it is not shut to you, it is closed to nobody because there is nobody to open it, and the only document that can open it does not exist. **Ends at the toll-house at the seventh hour, standing in a room, while a Crown's letter addressed to the holder of the tolls of the Old Toll crossing is opened by the man it is addressed to** — suspending the tolls from the first of Longlight and directing the holder to prevent *entertainment* and correspondence about the crossing — and the toll-clerk chalks the outside of the envelope with the day, the hour, three witnesses, and one clause of his own: **the tolls are suspended by the Crown and not by this town.**

**Ch 32 · the twenty-first of Greenmonth · Mara.** The order of the eighteenth comes off the post stamped on the eighteenth: a **returnable order, returnable on the ninth of Longlight**, and the question in it is not a question about records. **Petitioning, subpoenaeing and petrifying, named in order**, with the reason the first two die and the third does not. **A petitioner with a provincial clerk behind him: Dace Millward**, whose prayer asks not what the Archive holds but *who else holds copies and where*, and whose affidavit is a **schedule of attendance with four descriptions and no names** — and among the four is *a man of Hushwater, twice, in his own name*. **Rowan Petch, 26, discloses of his own motion** that the provincial court holds a copy of her file, that it holds no exhibit, and that a clerk wrote a *particular* on the ninth of Greenmonth describing that exhibit from her wrapper — and in writing the office's name into a file of the county's superior court, the office a magistrate formally asked for and was not answered on the fourth of Greenmonth. He asks only that it be said he did it on his own motion and not on a fee. **The two documents that are the same institution and the same method are her fourth cost.** She tells Jos Rusk before anybody else and does not apologise, and he says he will not send anybody to the five ferrymen until winter and will write to her first with a date. **And she makes the copies — in the room, in daylight, entered in the day-book in the fair hand with a date and an hour, one to a second keeper and one to a man in a yard** — because *a record held by one keeper is a favour and a record held in three places is a thing a court has to answer three times*, and a bond of six marks is faith and a debt against a contract is a document. **Ends:** the returnable order in the building, with a recital naming an office the county cannot produce, and a week between them in which nobody told her they were the week.

**Ch 33 · the twenty-eighth of Greenmonth · Tovan.** Four men, a factor's stone nearly dressed, a second load that may or may not come. **What a shut wharf road costs in three trades**: the miller's cart goes four miles round and then acquires an ox-cart and a man with a stick; Wenna Koll has the wharf and does not want it, and knows that a market that does not happen is a hole in a book and not a loss to a town; the factor's margin. **The board has a new line with no rule over it — *Road shut since the 19th of Mudmonth. Ask the road warden why* — and the chapter's discovery is that the town has joined two true half-sentences into a false cause**, and that a sentence in a market cannot be un-said in a market, ever. He tells the carrier who said it that the right thing to do about a sentence in a market is nothing at all. **On the Old Toll deck with a bar, forty minutes, and the chapter's best idea**: *a crew is not an owner. A crew is three men and a fortnight and a wage out of a box, twice a year, at the two low waters, and a crew never once decided anything about this bridge. The owner is the thing that answers, and answering is the expensive part* — and this town has never had to make that distinction, because for two generations the owner was a bench, and a bench has a book. **He says the three things the yard is not in a gateway at the seventh hour, and invents the third: nothing that belongs to a man goes in this yard's book, and only a thing set.** And he enters his brother's unpaid handrail in the yard's own book, in his own hand, with a date, an hour and a rule over it, and does not tell him. **Ends:** a factor at the gate asking Tovan, not Aren, who in Merefen is answerable for a crossing, and the answer is no, and a date.

**Ch 34 · the second of Longlight · Aren.** Bridge season, the river down, more work in the county than in four years. **Four jobs: a schedule, a courier, a copying post at three shillings a week, and a culvert at a farmer's** — and the whole shape of the week is **a schedule is a record and a post is an office**. He takes the schedule and the culvert and refuses the other two, and the county's schedule has a ruled column headed **Keeper** on every line, and he cannot correct the form and cannot correct the market. **He puts the quarter of an inch on the county's own paper, in the remarks column, with the date, the hour and a witness**, and the guild's man does not stop him, and the reason is the best sentence in the chapter: *a thing that is ours is a thing we are answerable for and a thing we cannot be corrected in. So keep the figures you can be got at.* **And he refuses to become a man anybody can be got at** — the return that is half an inch out in the direction that makes a deck look better than it is is now in a book two hundred miles off because he chose a document nobody in this town could see. **Ends on the notebook and a form with a printed space on it**, dated the second of Longlight, with a space that can be left blank and read as an answer, and a woman two flights above a salt merchant who has already written the coincidence down and told him not to write to her about it.

**Ch 35 · the ninth of Longlight · Mara.** Three people ask, courteously, in one morning, whether the office exists: the magistrate's clerk with Lysa Fenn's own question, the **salt merchant** who wants the second floor back and says three things in the worst order on purpose, and a member of the assembly who wants to know whether the town votes for the door. Then **Rowan Petch arrives with a form and a writ of enquiry into custody** — and explains, in his own hand and without being asked, that it exists **because her return was late**. **What petitioning, subpoenaeing and petrifying are, in the order they are used, and why the third is the one that works**: *the writ does not want to succeed. What it wants is the ninth of Rainmonth to arrive.* **The bar on moving or copying a record takes effect when the writ is stamped**, and she worked out afterwards that her whole margin was six days and nobody could have told her, because the writ did not exist yet. **She is dull** — a technique, not a personality — fills every printed space with a reference, a date and an hour, and makes no concealment and no argument. **She appoints Sarn Oyelaran a second keeper with a key, and sends a certified copy of the town's own instrument of foundation out of the building to a creditor**, because *a document left with a person is a thing a person can be arrested for, and an office can only be closed*. **Ends:** the endorsement of the return comes back with Petch's own memorandum appended, unfiled, and in it a name read off a watch establishment roll without being looked up — and she writes Rell a line and sends a boy with a shilling of the office's money, and does not file it, and thirty days have started.

**Ch 36 · the tenth of Longlight · Aren.** The Old Toll road first: the cradle gone, the moss back over the swept rectangle, a seal that has not run and will not run in a hundred days, and **his own hand on the seat and nothing in it.** Then the watch house, where a **commendation with four nails and a seal** is nailed to the wall and the man who nailed it says he is not being honoured, he is being **picked**, and it is a fact with a date on it that can be looked at. **Captain Rell is pressed from above and stays in post**: an instruction dated yesterday, stamped at the second hour, that is not a threat, names no law, requires no court, tells the watch to prevent contact, and tells it to **prevent the assembly of any body of this county in respect of the object** — and does not have to tell the town. He carried it out in a day, moved the bell into a room with a lock and a man in it, did not renew the seal, and told Aren on a step in the rain **that he had made the chain of custody unbroken on purpose and that a record that is unbroken is a thing somebody can use**, and that the one gap in it is nine minutes long and belongs to Aren. **Aren does not confront him.** He asks one question, in a doorway, in trade terms, and it is about custody — *who holds it at three in the morning when this building has four men on and one of them is asleep* — and the answer is that the watch has a form to fill in on the fifteenth of Longlight, and Rell has no court, no superior, and eleven years of doing the correct thing to be able to say he was wrong. **Ends:** the instrument is returnable on the fifteenth, and a Crown's man wants an answer, and there is nobody in the county who can help a watch captain fill one in.

**Ch 37 · the fifteenth of Longlight · Tovan.** A cart, a bell, and four people who saw it go into a locked room in Weir Street. The bench sits on a **Saturday**, called by the reeve, and the Saturday is minuted. **Aren is entered absent at his own request, and the clerk enters that the bench was told he is named in a provincial ledger and declined to consider it** — a bench entering a man as absent and meaning it as a protection. **Tovan says the true thing that is not an opinion** — *my brother did not make himself answerable to that office; he made himself answerable to us*; every document the Crown has in this county is a true document this bench wrote — **and then does not vote, because he is a party, in a public room, and the clerk enters that it is the second time.** The resolution: **the Old Toll crossing is kept in repair out of the bench's own materials and its own men's time, and the bench is not the owner and does not claim to be.** And the Crown's superior court asked the bench to name the officer holding the object, and **the county refused to execute the order and returned it, and in returning it wrote the matter into a printed space on the back — so the office of keeper of the Bellweather hinge arrived in Merefen free, in a county clerk's hand, with a date.** The guild's man does not like it and wants it on his wall. **Ends:** a minute, a road kept, and nine men in a room who decided that on their own money on a Saturday afternoon and told nobody.

**Ch 38 · the eighteenth of Longlight · Aren.** **Edric Hunn's first letter** arrives at the fourth hour in county brown paper with a grey wax seal, and it is eleven hundred words and every word of it is true, and it accounts for every room he has been in since the first of Mudmonth, including the hall on the eighteenth day of the other world, the field book on the bench's table, and a sentence of his own chalked on a market board and copied into four hundred mouths. **It contains no offer.** Its one act of generosity is the four sentences that end eleven years: *at twenty-one you were the best man in the room — that is not a compliment, it is a load-bearing fact.* One sentence in eleven hundred has a subject, a verb and a second person in it, and it requires no court. **And it has no person's name in it but his own and the sender's**, and he works out in a quarter of an hour that the only book in this county with that in it is the Archive's attendance column — and that the names are the last thing the office does not have, and the letter is asking for them. **He writes back over four days: a receipt by seal and date and hour, the date, a fact, and a condition** — what the instruction costs in trade terms, and unasked, the dated question of the fourth of Greenmonth. No names. No refusal. No defence. **And the letter is now a signed document in a room over a cooper with no chain of custody on it and no way of ever having one**, which is the price of the batch, paid on the page. **Ends:** a provincial clerk's memorandum, dated the twenty-fourth, saying that the names are the only thing in the matter not already held, and that the question is no longer about this county, and that the office will require the assistance of another office, and that the other office has a woman in it, and that the other office has a form.

**Ch 39 · the twenty-first of Longlight · Mara.** A Friday market on a bridge it was not built for, a board with ten lines and six without a rule, and a new one: *Bridge in the other world is going. Kest has been there. Kest is not saying.* **Harl Mounce, a barge mate, in a room with a witness, being told the order of things for the second time and saying so.** He lay four days on a towpath at Gallow Ford while two men and a boy with a book took a number off a mark in the coping of a second pier; a steward of thirty years told him eleven, and eight at the last marking, in a wharf conversation, and neither number is in any person's book; and he said *going* four times and the steward never said it once, and he has been paid by four rooms in a market for a bigger thing than he heard. **She enters the report with five limits**, and the fifth is the chapter: *the same description is a Crown's office at a bridge in this country and a bench marking a ring on a pier at the lowest water in a city this county's own finding says was never built, and no person has said they are the same and no person here can say they are not* — and she records the hour at which she decided **not to ask him**, against nobody, with no opinion. She does not correct the market, because a corrected market story is a market story with a correction in it. And she discovers what a keeper of records costs: a joke with a name in it, and the word **keeper** doing three jobs in one town. **Ends:** the counterfoil and the report in one building and never in the same room, entered in the day-book and not the press, with the fact that there is a man who could be shown both and that the office is not going to name him in a file, because to name him in a file would be to charge him with something.

**Ch 40 · the twenty-second of Longlight · Aren.** The arithmetic, and one figure at the bottom of it: **eleven shillings and fourpence against sixteen for two discs of glass.** Two things have changed in a room over a cooper and neither can be acted on — a letter that has gone to the post, and a barge mate's report. **And the chapter's crisis is that he does not know whether the eleven is his ring**, because the same description is a Crown's office in this country and a bench at the lowest water four days off, and he goes at it from four sides and has four dead ends, and a ring opens in the cold and shuts in the warmth, so eight and eleven are probably not the same season, and a bench with two numbers a year for four generations has never once compared them and cannot tell from its own book whether the ring is standing still. Three things he could do; two cost something he does not have; **the third is the thing he has never done, and he decides it in a room and not in a notebook.** He goes to the yard for **a line and not a paper** because nothing that belongs to a man goes in that book and only a thing set. He goes to the watch house and gives Rell the one thing he asked for in trade terms and is told to stop coming up the street, and then told why he cannot. **In the filing room he lays three pieces on the table, admits a fourth he has not written and a fifth he will never lodge, and is told that coming up a stair with five things and no paper is the most careful thing anybody has done in that building in four years** — and she works out the chain's order, refuses to hold it as a custody, and agrees to hold it as **a lending** she will have to list in a court on the ninth of Rainmonth, and names the cost before she agrees to it. He writes the fourth piece in the room above the cooper and carries it down in his coat. Millward enters the chain in a creditor's roll and says the price: **four things in it that are not tolls, and a man in a counting house who has read it once a year for eleven years and never stopped at a line.** The yard holds the fact and cannot produce the paper. **He does not go.** **Ends four days off, from the north side, and not through the book, and with nothing coming:** the bench at the lowest water on the twenty-sixth doing the marking it promised a date for in a hall — two men and a clerk, a rule, **eight inches in a book and eight inches at the last marking** — the woman who keeps that road keeping her hands open and nothing arriving, and the only question Bellweather-Living spends that morning on being the second one, put off to daylight: whether the road is a road, or a door, or a hole in a wall a man can put his hand through whenever it suits him. **About nine people did.**

## Batch 0005 — Chapters 41–50, "The Oath and the Two Bridges"

Block 5, and **the only block in Volume 01 with a crossing in it**: one crossing out, one scheduled crossing, one return. POVs Aren ×5 (41, 42, 45, 47, 49) / Mara ×4 (43, 46, 48, 50) / Tovan ×1 (44). **No new name in the batch, and four of the six-name cap unfilled** — corrected in the Batch 0005 review pass, because three files asserted otherwise. **Edric Hunn is not new: he is first named in `chapter-0026.md` and is a signature on the Crown's letter from Chapter 38, and the batch-0005 prompt itself calls him established**; what the batch gave him is his first page and his first room. Nobody is named who was not already named in Chapters 1–40; the man with the mallet, the salt merchant, the sett-cutter and the toll-clerk on the north steps are all addressed by trade. **Zero Gatehouse Order lines, by budget** — the word appears once, in Ch 47's notebook, as a standard of measurement for a held road, and a later audit must not read the zero as a claim about the word. One System display in the batch (the slate of terms, Ch 49). Weekday chain from continuity items 93 and 111: **26 Longlight Wed · 27 Thu · 28 Fri · 29 Sat · 30 Sun · 1 Fallowmonth Mon · 2 Fallowmonth Tue** (the return, a Tuesday) · 9 Fallowmonth Tue (Ch 49 opens) · 22, 24, 27, 28, 29 Fallowmonth (Ch 50). **The Gatehouse's warning was three weeks and the drift is three weeks and it is stated in the prose in Ch 47's notebook as a warning that was wrong and not a surprise.**

**Ch 41 · the twenty-sixth of Longlight, Wednesday, the lowest water of the year · Aren.** The chapter's two halves are the road and the crossing, and they are the same decision.
