He is **available, not needed** — forty-one structures in a county book with a ruled column headed *Keeper* and nobody in Merefen who has needed him since the sixth of Mudmonth. **Two men from the bench clear the moss out of the bed of the Old Toll abutment with a cold chisel, four hundred yards from a seat with two used discs in it, and they do not know he is on the bridge.** No seat, no glass, no money: four marks is sixteen shillings and he has eleven and fourpence and no maker within two hundred miles, and the third of three has been sitting in him since the sixteenth of Greenmonth with a date on it in a book in another country. **He goes out in daylight, on a working morning, with a bar, because there is a date and a bench and a woman with a book.** He tells two people the same sentence in the same words — Tovan at the ninth hour, Mara at the eleventh — and **Tovan writes the first road ever entered in the yard's book, unasked, because Aren did not ask him for anything**; Mara enters the sentence in the day-book and refuses to put a second statement beside it, because *the protection is the repetition and not the paper*. Then the reeve and Mara witness a dressed stone and forty yards of tarred hemp at the fourth hour, in a cart-skirt on a bridge deck, and the reeve's own clause is *the purpose of it is not entered and I was not asked for it and did not offer it*. The night's notebook: **the seat is not the road — a road is two ends and a date and a person at each end who has agreed to be there.** Ends in four and a half seconds at the north steps, with a toll of a half mark and a farthing and a bell on the coping with a rope on it and nobody near it.

**Ch 42 · the twenty-sixth of Longlight, the far end, the guild hall · Aren.** He goes to the hall first instead of to the pier, and notices himself not doing what a bridgewright does. **The marking book has twenty-four entries in it and nobody has ever compared them.** He reads them all out loud; the bench-clerk says in a room that he has never compared them and wants that said, *because it is the reason that page is worth reading and nobody has read it*. **A ring stone opens in the cold and shuts in the warmth: the two low waters are the twenty-fourth of Rainmonth and the twenty-sixth of Longlight, the gap between the two sets is two tenths and the gap is the winter, the whole twenty-four is between eight and eight and three tenths, the cold set has come down three tenths in eighteen years, and the ring is standing still.** And then, in the same room, the thing he came for: **three inches in a year is not a ring. It is a foundation, or a tower with a sight-line and a bad rule, or a different bridge** — so the eleven in a Merefen file cannot be his ring, and his Chapter 40 discovery has arrived as a fact on a page in another city. At the fourth hour of the next morning he is in the water up to the thigh and reads the **scour** in an hour: four feet across, two deep, eleven feet under the footing, and the honest repair is a new cutwater and **nine months of the bench's whole capacity against a pledge to the ninth of a grandson.** He says it in the fewest words he can find in a full hall and the clerk enters it and reads it back twice and then the man with the mallet asks the bench the question nobody has asked in four generations: **does this bench hold the arch, or does it not?** Aren does not answer it — nine seconds' arithmetic that a stranger has taught a city its own oath is a thing that goes into a market in six weeks — and says only *put a date on it and a man on it, and go back to the water in the spring.* **The creditor stands up with a bundle of string and does not thank him for that either.**

**Ch 43 · the twenty-seventh of Longlight to the second of Fallowmonth · Mara.** The writ of the ninth of Longlight is stamped and the office is petrified for thirty days and **a thing that cannot be copied becomes a thing that cannot be produced**, in her own building, at her own charge: eleven hours a day, six days, two of them at four shillings eightpence each. The return is nine hundred words and a schedule of nine hundred holdings, all of it dull. **The eighth space asks for items received after the date of the writ, and the answer is the hard one** — nine items received and held and not disposed of, including two lendings, one of which names a tradesman by date. She priced it in advance, at the seventh hour on the night of the twenty-second, and the price is a name in a schedule of holdings on a date in a building two hundred miles off. **The other form arrives on the first of Fallowmonth with a ruled column headed *Keeper*, no date on it, and a fifth column headed *how often* — and she answers it with a number instead of four names and writes out the reason in full: a name in a form in a provincial office is a thing a man is made out of, and a form with no date on it does not have to be answered and therefore does not have to be tried.** Then the seventh of Fallowmonth's cousin: **the brine weights are in the room, and she sits down and works out what the roll of the dead would be, and does not build it, and writes down that she did not and why** — the city keeps its own dead, twice refused this office a copy and been right both times, and a keeper who has claimed the dead cannot afterwards stand in a hall in that city and be a person with no opinion. **The second document that leaves the building goes to a carpenter, not a creditor: a true copy of the writ itself, walked down Weir Street on foot, because a piece of Crown paper in a yard is a piece of Crown paper in a yard** — and the price of it is Toban's book getting a Crown instrument in it. Ends with the tube: **two writs of the same kind three days apart on the same paper in the same matter, and a clerk in the provincial town who has worked out that a writ of enquiry into a record office is the cheapest thing his office has ever found.**

**Ch 44 · the twenty-sixth of Longlight to the second of Fallowmonth · Tovan.** Four men, a bench's own materials, a crossing nobody owns, and a brother gone. The **ordinary autumn work** on the twenty-sixth, in daylight, with two bench men clearing the bed of the stone with a cold chisel because there is one week in the year a man can get a chisel there — and the yard's book gets a road in it for the first time in nineteen years, unasked, with a rule over it, and the last clause is that the account is on the bench's roll and not in this book, because *a bench's roll is a thing a Crown's man can be asked for*. **The Crown's mould of grey wax over a doorway at the back of the yard**, off a storehouse door, in the building since Mudmonth, never used, never written down, and the reason: a yard is not an office and a seal is an office. **Captain Rell stands outside the guild-house door for eleven minutes on the second of Fallowmonth and a bench and a watch enter the same hour in two books** — and that is the first time the hole in Lysa Fenn's finding has cost a town something procedural: *a bench which cannot meet about the object is a bench that has been governed by an instruction without a court, and a man can be held to an order he was never served with.* Tovan says the true thing for the third time in five months and does not vote, and the true thing is about himself: **he has said no to a road three times in that room and has never once said no to his brother, and that is the worst thing about him and it is not in any book.** The Crown's hand comes up Weir Street with a schedule and a ruled column and he gives it the number of the lines and the hour and the fact that this yard inspects no crossing in this county — a fact instead of a name, worked out in a gateway in Greenmonth, the same argument a woman is making two hundred miles off and neither of them got it from the other. **A carrier's report reaches the Market Square board on the twelfth: a crossing shut two days on a Wednesday night in a city four days off, and a guild has sworn in a hall to hold it, and a stranger from the south is why** — late, wrong about the night, and the fourth line is *Ask Kest. He knows what the oath is.* Ends at the second hour of the morning of the second of Fallowmonth, alone on the Old Toll deck with a lamp: **the stone comes up a quarter of an inch and sets down on the other side of itself, and there is a second and a half between the two movements, and nothing falls.**

**Ch 45 · the twenty-eighth to the thirtieth of Longlight, four days in the city · Aren.** The second examination, with a sounding pole, at the fifth hour, in the water: **four feet six, and the north lip gone a hand's breadth, and four and five tons of stone that is not there any more and is in the river two hundred yards downstream making a bar that will be a town's problem in four years.** The man with the mallet says *you are the best man in this room*, and the answer is that a man who has read forty scour holes is not the best man in a room, he is the only man in it who has ever read one, and the man who has built forty knows what forty cost, which is the only currency that matters. **The city offers him a bench seat and a wage out of the guild's own box and the fourth camp, and he says no in eleven seconds, and the reason the man with the mallet gives for the refusal is the best reason anybody has given him for anything in the book: a foreign man who cuts setts on the fourth camp is a foreign man on the fourth camp, and a bench is a thing that is pointed at and not a thing that is pointed with.** Then Alis Merrow's third want: **the writ released the tolls of the arch in YR 301 and the pledge to the ninth of a grandson was made against a thing that stopped being theirs eleven years ago, and a creditor who has a grandson of nine has to go and tell him and his mother what the arch is worth in three weeks** — and what he wants is the oath renewed while there is still a city here to renew it to. The oath is four lines and **it is not a promise about a stone; it is a promise about who answers**, and the bench renews it at the tenth hour of the twenty-ninth, in front of two hundred people, with a clause that took nine minutes to get in: *the bench has not been able to name a date by which the promise will be discharged and is asked by no person to name one.* **Aren is asked a question in that hall and stands up and says nothing at all, in a public room, and works out in nine seconds what it would cost him to open his mouth.** Ends with the bench-clerk, a piece of chalk, a notice on three doors, and the question of the volume: **the oath says the stewards of the crossing shall answer for it and shall answer first — and who is the steward of this crossing?**

**Ch 46 · path time, and the fourth hour of the first of Fallowmonth · Mara.** The four hundred words that went north, addressed to no person, listing four documents and the spine that is in three of them and none by itself. Then six days: the writs still running, the town saying the chalk, six lines on the board with no rules and a clerk who has stopped wiping the lower half, and a stone that moved at two in the morning. **The crossing is a line and two hands and seven people on a deck, and four of them were told in advance and three were not, and the one who was told nothing on purpose is a copyist of twenty-six because the only person in a thing who cannot be got to change is the one who does not know what the thing is.** Tovan holds the near end, the reeve witnesses, Aren translates, and **the page comes through in oiled linen with a boat and a rope on the wax and the four conditions on the outside of the wrapper and the arrest entered against a stranger on the twenty-second day of the ninth month, and the fair copy of a fair copy with the press-mark and the name.** The fair copy is read out twice on a bridge deck in Merefen. **Mara says the name has been in a provincial file since Greenmonth, and that the ferrywright's answer to *then it should not have come* is four minutes is quick, and neither of them is a person who is the mechanism.** Sera Quen's terms on the spot: a steward at each end, a witness at each end who is not the steward, a day written down before anybody moves — **and if the witness is not here then the road does not open, and it does not fail, it does not open, and nobody is to say afterwards that it failed.** The woman who keeps that road writes the date and the hour and the name of the person who kept the road, *because a road that is not written down is a door.* Ends on the far side discovering that the argument in the north is not about it.

**Ch 47 · the first into the second of Fallowmonth, and the sixth hour after it · Aren.** The volume climax. **Two thousand feet on a market day on the arch, entered in the bench-clerk's book under a heading he invented for it, which was *on the day*.** He is on a staging under the second pier; four men are in a boat under him, one a hull-hand of the cooperative and one **a stranger nobody in the city can account for, which is not a moral defect, it is what eleven years does to a place.** The first thing that happens is not a sound: the pier comes up half an inch and does not set down. **He does the old move.** *A Bind is not a charm. A Bind is a man who knows a load path putting his intent into a material and the material taking it.* He could see the load path because he was the best man in the room and the room was a bridge. **And the force went into the pier exactly as it should — that is the sentence, and not one word in it is wrong.** One line of crack from the waterline to nine feet above the arch, the market comes off the crown, a hull-hand struck at the fourth rib, a boat over, a stranger under the arch foot. He gets the stranger out in eleven seconds. **Then he stops, in the second minute, and the only sentence in the notebook about it is: *I did not take it out because it was going to fail. I took it out because it was mine.*** He does not argue and does not apologise; he goes into the hall and says four sentences, and the fourth one gives the failure to himself in front of two hundred people. **The man with the mallet says *I have a bench to sit in an hour with a hole in the river and I would like to be able to sit in it*, and sends him out.** The work that follows is done by the older members of the bench, by **Alis Merrow** with his clerks and his oil and an entry in his own book, by the Hushwater cooperative with a woman who would not let anybody touch the crossing book, by Captain Ruen with a crew he did not have to have, and by **Bellweather's own two stewards — Sera Quen and the man with the mallet — who answer the bench-clerk's question with two names at the bottom of a sheet, neither signing the other's line.** The **narrower oath** is made up on a drying floor in six hours and nine sides of minute: it is four lines long, and it is a promise about a book and about who answers and about a door, and it releases every person in that city who has been asked for a promise about a stone. **The repair is stood in the open.** The road opens wide at the fourth hour with four sets of hands and Aren translating, which is the work he is actually for. **He comes up out of the Old Toll sill at a quarter to six into a fever, and the fourth course is open, a hand's width, with the faces in register, and it is warm.** The notebook carries the drift — *the warning was three weeks and it was out by three weeks, and the three weeks is on this side and not on mine* — and the verse's second hole, and Stage 1, and the last line: *it is better than it was, and what I have lost is not the power. What I have lost is the belief that I am the one it should be spent on.* Tovan reads him his own page in the rain with the book flat on the stone upside down, and the last thing in it is that he is the man the town asks about his brother.

**Ch 48 · the second to the ninth of Fallowmonth · Mara.** The page comes down Weir Street in a cart and the ferrywright's one sentence is *the line is holding, tell me the line is holding.* Two lines and then ten minutes at a window, which is the longest she has stood there in four years. **Three kinds of holding because one kind of holding is one kind of failure** — a custody, a lending, a fact — and a fourth place that is a salt merchant's brother's lock-up on a canal wharf **entered as a transaction and not a favour, at a farthing of salt, and the reason a lease can be ended and a roof can be lost.** Then the reading, in a room, with people in it: **a leaf of a provincial ledger with *absent* against the ninth of Greenmonth, the finding of the fourth of Greenmonth, a bench minute voted on a Saturday, and a fair copy of a fair copy.** Four documents about a bell, and not one of them written by the person the Crown blames, and **a watch captain looking at a column at the top of his own form and saying *I filled in the column*, and being asked the third question, the order, and answering *clock, Crown's cloak, chain paper*, and the fourth fact of that night is a Crown's letter that named an office and went out on a post the morning a bed of stone was full of people in another world.** The claim is entered **against nobody** on the seventh, the eighth of Fallowmonth, the eleventh hour, with the name on a separate sheet under a separate number and the reason for the separation written once: *a claim and a name may be read together by a court that wants to and by nobody else.* The villages are served **by name, in the county's spelling, figures separately, `sum not stated`, and eight ruled lines with the eighth empty and a reason under it copied in the hand of a boy of nineteen.** The brine weights are **cited in the claim and not filed**, and the second line about them goes in the day-book four lines from the first. The Crown's two instruments are entered **lawful and never mentioned again**, in four lines, and the last of them is *this court has nothing further to say about either instrument at any time.* Ends on a form going to the county council with a printed space headed *Function of the office*, filled with the founding's own words, and the space above the rule left empty.

**Ch 49 · the ninth to the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth · Aren.** The fever broke on the fifth and he is out on the ninth. **A scar is not a crack — a scar is a place a thing came through and can come through again** — and the fourth course is open and cold and there is nothing in it, and he says the real thing out loud on a bridge in daylight: *I know why it opens when it opens, and there is nobody in this kingdom I could say it to, and that is not a mystery any more, that is a trade.* The town has changed its mind about him without changing its opinion about him, and the mechanism is a bench: **a bench put a block and forty yards of hemp into a parapet at its own cost, with the guild's man's name on the specification and not the warden's, and nobody at all asked the road warden** — *that is what a legal loss is for: to be proved wrong by somebody else and to have the somebody else's handwriting on it.* Rell meets him in a lane in daylight and gives him three facts and the price of them. **The four Crown's jobs come back on the sixteenth and he refuses the schedule and takes the copying post, and the reason is the same reason as in Longlight and is not the same reason**, and the difference between the two years is written out in four lines and dated: *he is a man with a road-marked palm and a sister in a claim in a county file and no office at all, and a man with no office is a man nothing can be asked of, and there is a form in this county with a column for exactly that.* The bench is asked to do it twice and carries the second visit against three marks and about six with two marks in the chest. **He tells one person**: palm up on a table in a filing room, and she writes it down as his statement and not her observation, and asks him one question — *if it costs you more the better you get at it, then who pays it, and is the answer still you?* — and he says yes and adds that it is going to stop being him in about a year and he wants to be a tradesman at a rate of pay on a contract then. **The one System display in the batch** is four lines of chalk on a slate on a parapet at the fourth hour of the twenty-ninth, and the fourth of them is: *Answer — the far side decides. You cannot make a city answer. That is the term and it is not a defect and I would like the man in the north to stop looking at it as though it were.* Ends with the road to be opened on a named day, written at the top of a book on a north bank, and the woman who keeps that road entering that the north steward is a carpenter and not the man who cut the seat.

**Ch 50 · the last week of Fallowmonth, low water · Mara, with Aren in the room.** The board at the top of the market, ruled, with *bring nothing* under it, and *Ask the keeper. The keeper'll have it written down with the hour on it* under that without a rule, and she does not write it down anywhere. **The bell comes back on the twenty-fourth in daylight in front of three hundred people, carried by the watch captain himself, on the ground of a repair to a parapet and a scrap-roll entry of the eleventh of Mudmonth, and Rell's four lines in his own hand say that if any officer above the county thinks that wrong he may come and take it off the bridge.** The band is the town's work, out of a gate-post, forty-one rivets, and the guild's man with the levels says *it'll hold as long as the crack does, and the crack is not a crack, it's a split.* **The split is sealed from both sides at the same hour with a dull red oathlight by a Bellweather-Living steward's hands, four hundred miles off, and the two lines in the trade say it is a job and not a treaty and that the north man is not on that deck because a person who is not of the trade cannot stand on it and be safe.** Nothing in the bell breaks again in this volume. **The channel opens on a named day with a Merefen steward, a Bellweather-Living steward and one independent witness at each end**, the day written in four books in two countries, and the first thing across it is **a toll dispute about nine tons of paving stone, argued in two languages for eleven minutes and settled on line ninety-one of a crossing book** — a cart on wheels is not a man. **Edric Hunn comes to Merefen on the twenty-seventh, asks to be recorded, is recorded, and offers rank, back pay, a shield for the Archive and an inspection, and is refused in ninety seconds in a room with a clerk in it** — no to the rank, yes to the back pay as a wage on a schedule and not as back pay, *give the shield to the bench in a form with a number on it and let her enter it against nobody and let the county clerk's refusal stand beside it*, and no to the inspection, because a writ of production into a city with a bench and a debt and a grandmother's granddaughter's name on the front of a book is not a record and is a war, and **he cannot choose what that city does, and he was not in the room when the narrow oath was made and could not have stopped it.** Lysa Fenn refuses the request for names on the record, in the same room, with a clerk in it. He leaves having asked, in a doorway, for somebody else who can shut a road, and **there is, and it is a carpenter and a watch captain and a woman with a press and a reeve and a bench of nine and four people in a city four hundred miles off.** The claim is filed, four copies go out, the brine weights stay cited and not filed, and **the eighth ruled line in the served schedule is still empty and a woman notices it and writes down that she looked at it and wanted to fill it, because a keeper who has never written down that she wanted to do a thing is a keeper whose wants get into documents without her noticing.** Ends on the Sedge Reach deck at the fifth hour with a tool bag, counting load points out of habit, a note going out and an answer coming back through the rain, a warm palm he does not need, and nothing in that sound that is his.

## Volume 01 close — block-level notes, Chapters 1–50 read as a finished book

**The fifty entries above stand. None of them is rewritten. What follows is only the four places where the close found that a chapter means something other than the summary says it means, or where a summary is silent about something the next volume depends on. Cross-reference `outline/volumes/volume-01-close.md` and `outline/volume-02.md`.**

1. **Chapters 1–10 (the nineteenth of Mudmonth) — the bell is not a key and the elapse is the shame, and the summary should say so harder.** The chapter's hinge is not the crossing, it is the moment in Chapter 5 when Aren stops himself mid-sentence on a bridge because he has already begun arranging his face to hear these people's grief, **and a stranger from the north who wanted a story is the man who went to a ferry yard and promised it would take a scar and cost him a boy's hand.** The volume's method — a working fails because the promiser chose the wrong anchor and named the wrong witness — is established in Chapter 6 and never repeated in that shape again. **Summary note: the batch's first ten chapters already contain the whole thesis in miniature, and no later chapter restates it as cleanly.**

2. **Chapter 6 — the lesson is *counterweight*, not *anchor*.** The summary has this, but the load for later volumes is the method, not the moral: **Sera Quen teaches that you are the cost and not the anchor, and that the witness is whoever would have refused, and she makes him say out loud, in front of eleven people, that he would have refused it.** That is the book's whole ethics in a ferry slip and it is never stated again at that altitude. **Aren's one carryover, the hand that sets a thing true against a thing out of true, is born in this chapter, appears in Chapter 10 in a filing room over a salt merchant, and comes home a second time in Volume 02 in a kiln shed in the County of Ash — a hand re-pressing a soft tile. The summary for Chapter 6 should be read as the origin of a three-volume motif, not a one-off.**

3. **Chapter 30 — the summary records what he told her and not what she did, and what she did is the more important half.** He tells Mara what a crossing costs in the body. She enters it as his statement and not her observation, says she is not going to be relieved, **and in the same stretch she begins the practice the whole series runs on: deciding in advance what she will not ask, and entering the not asking in the day-book so that nobody can say later she took it out of a man who was ill.** She writes that on the twenty-second of Fallowmonth in Chapter 49 and again, in Chapter 92 of Volume 02, the not asking has become a wound. **The thread runs from Chapter 30 to Chapter 92 through two volumes and the reader is given both ends and no warning, and that is correct and must not be smoothed.**

4. **Chapter 47 — the notebook is the volume's second document set and no summary of it exists.** Four things are in that notebook and every later volume depends on all four: **the verse's second hole** (two notes become one and the one that is left is the higher one, the one Ilyra used to end on, and he had it in his head for nine months and did not know it was there until about the fourth hour of the second of Fallowmonth in a room above a cooper with a fever on him); **the palm** (it is a road and not a mark, he can feel which way a thing in this county wants to go, he felt it in that arch at the sixth hour with a man's rib broken under his right arm, and there is no one he can tell); **Stage 1 by qualification and not by award, and the sentence that the whole volume ends on — *it is better than it was, and what I have lost is not the power, it is the belief that I am the one it should be spent on***; and **the drift**, the warning of three weeks, the six days here against six months and twenty days there, and the sentence that a drift is not a surprise and not a warning that failed. **The drift is stated in Chapter 47 and Volume 02 states the three-week figure in writing in Chapter 51, four chapters into the next volume, and that is the earliest the close was willing to let the shape of the cost be visible.**

5. **Chapters 48–50 (the resolution) — the summary does not record that the volume's three best sentences are all about custody and none of them is Aren's.** The claim is entered against nobody and the name on a separate sheet. The bell is laid down by a watch captain who was instructed by nobody and asked by nobody, on three grounds that are all the town's, with four lines in a county file offering to be the man who takes it off the bridge again. The narrow oath is made on a drying floor by nineteen men and two stewards and releases a class of promise. **And the volume's closing argument is a marshal's, said in a doorway to nobody: a road that cannot be refused is a road that will be used, and somebody has to be able to shut it, and I have been the somebody for eleven years, and I would like somebody in this room to tell me, now, today, with a clerk writing it down, that there is another somebody, and that he is not you.** **The answer is a list, and the list is eleven, and Volume 02 is the volume in which somebody counts the four people he has.**

---

## Volume 02 index

**Volume 02 — The County of Ash (Chapters 51–100)**, outlined in `outline/volume-02.md`. Main world: the first of Embermonth, YR 312, to the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, YR 313 — two hundred and eight days. Two crossings: main-world absences of twenty-six and eighty-eight days, path intervals of twenty-three days and nine months and six days. Stage 1 into Stage 2, qualified at the climax and not by award.

| Blocks | Chapters | Content |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 51–60 | The form and the registrar: the count instead of the list, Halven's shield and the ledger, the second severance's index leaf, the refused crossing, and the going. **Written.** |
| 2 | 61–70 | Twenty-Three Days: the stock count, the roll, the law, the struck, the physician's finding, the five terms, and the first fever. |
| 3 | 71–80 | Ordinary work in Merefen: the yard, the copying post, the bench, the election, the count answered, and the crossing out. |
| 4 | 81–90 | The Nine Months: the rite, the softening, the coping, the child, and the climax that is a form. |
| 5 | 91–100 | Eighty-Eight Days, and a roll in three hands: the return, the breach, the settlement, the ledger. |

## Batch 0006 — Volume 02, Chapters 51–60, "The Form and the Registrar"

Block 1. POVs **Aren ×5 (51, 53, 57, 59, 60) / Mara ×2 (52, 58) / Sabin Dain's chapter opens on Aren, and Chapter 60 is the near end of Chapter 59's morning.** **One crossing out, on the ninth of Embermonth at the fourth hour, which is refused, and he goes anyway on his own legs — so the crossing out happens and the road does not open, and the road does not fail.** **The word *Gatehouse* is not spelled out in this batch; Chapter 51's notebook spells it once and Chapter 57's refers to it as a word already written once in that book.** No System display in the batch. The kiln clerk's slate in Chapter 59 is a county's own ledger line read back to the woman who refused to be named, and is not a system panel. **Three names of the five the card allows — Sabin Dain, Nell Tace, Cresset Vaine — plus the returned face Bram Ottery, and Ilsa Soke named on a paper from the far end in Chapter 60, and the kiln clerk addressed by trade with his name withheld on the page by design.** Weekday chain from the audited anchor: **Embermonth 1 Wed · 2 Thu · 3 Fri · 4 Sat (market) · 7 Tue · 8 Wed · 9 Thu (market).** The sixth, the fifth and the ninth carry the chapter dates; the seventh is a Tuesday and is not a market day, and the chapter says so.

**Ch 51 · the first of Embermonth, a Wednesday · Aren.** A second Form R.C. 4 comes back from the Office of Provincial Registration **and a person comes with it**: **Sabin Dain**, 33, an aid to the register, not a member of anything, who walked to four doors in Weir Street and stopped in a gateway because a filing room two flights up a stair belongs to a man who weighs salt. She reads the marginal instruction out loud before anything else, because a woman who is about to be difficult reads the difficult part first: *where no office of keeper is found in the county schedule, the person having knowledge shall be entered under the column headed Keeper, and the entry shall not be construed as the creation of an office.* **Nineteen forms in eleven months and an answer to none, and every answer a figure.** She fills the fourth column with *Aren Kest, road warden, in the roll of the Merefen bridge guild, and no other office*, and writes under the box *State the office, if any* that a man who writes *none* has conceded that there ought to be one. Her want in her own words: **that no family in this country is ever again removed from a record by a name nobody entered**, and she has asked twice in writing to have the instruction explained and been told it is standard. She has come on her own time, unsent, and a dated form is coming within the month. **The reverse also prints, in the same hand, as a standard of measurement: *a road held, three weeks*** — the Gatehouse's own figure, in an office's hand, and the office cannot say whose it is. The notebook spells the word once and puts down the shape of what is coming: a dated form can only be refused with a court, the answer to a dated form is a name in it, **and the cheapest lawful way to act on a road you cannot find the owner of is to stop asking for names.** Ends on the line he will read in a kiln shed: *a thing that cannot be refused and cannot be tried is not a document, it is a road with nobody at the far end of it.*

**Ch 52 · the second · Mara.** The four-hundred-word list that went north on the twenty-seventh of Longlight has been **answered twice, and the two answers are from two places.** Bellweather-Living's is four lines, dated the eighth of Fallowmonth, eleven days after the paper went, in the fair hand of the woman who keeps the crossing book on the north bank, and the whole of it is about a bell: the sealing held, the third stroke is now leaned on and is not called cracked any more, the toll is a farthing too high and has been left, and line ninety-one stands and the eel-man's story is not believed and has been checked twice. **The second answer is dated the first of Fallowmonth, four days after the paper went, in a hand that is not a clerk's**, and it is from **a county called Ash, on a river it calls the Kiln**, and it says that a man who fires at the kiln at Kilnholt was struck off the roll of persons eleven months ago, has not gone anywhere, is at his kiln, and **cannot be found because a man who is struck cannot be asked anything.** It ends: *tell us what a record owes a man who is in one and not in it.* **She looks up *roll of persons* in the county's own printed index and finds it means a list of a village's inhabitants carried by a constable, which is the thing they do not have.** She spends the rest of the day proving there is no county of Ash in this country — the county's index, two gazetteers, a directory, a Crown's circuit map, a rent table, a shilling on a map she did not need, and **eleven near misses that she does not cross out, because a keeper who crosses out a thing she has looked for has stopped looking.** Sarn Oyelaran makes her lift the words *this office cannot tell* off the page and enter the nine minutes instead, **because an entry of an office's own ignorance is the only fact in a book that nobody can ever check**, and both of them are entered and Sarn's name is on the amendment. The paper goes in the day-book and **not** in the press, and is sent to nobody: *a man can enter a claim against this office and be answered in a court, and a county cannot be served.* Ends on the shortest line in the book, against nobody, with the hour: **a question nobody is asked is not a question this office has refused.**

**Ch 53 · the third, a Friday · Aren.** Toban's count closed at the sixth hour in the gateway with four men in it, standing, because a man who sits down to count money is a man who finds some. **Five weeks and it is spoken for**, the factor's stone gone in three carts, the county town's wall going, a culvert at Four Elms, **and nothing after the wall.** Wages: four men, twenty-five days, seven pence the day, seven hundred pence, fourteen marks two shillings and fourpence, added twice by him and once by his wife, and the book is at eleven marks and a bit over and the bit over is three marks two shillings and fourpence and is his. **He has been eating that gap since Longlight, and the trust's four marks and six comes out of the same pocket as the wall.** He has been rounding a figure for three weeks so people would believe sooner and stopped on Saturday, and the stopping has cost him two men's good opinion. **Then the dated form comes: a count of persons, a space for a figure and no space for a name.** He refuses the temptation to refuse it — *a refusal is a thing a man can be cross about, an answer goes in a book* — and writes an answer that is a fact and not a number: **this yard builds crossings and does not inspect them, because a thing a man builds and a thing a man inspects cannot both be his**, plus the number of the lines, the hour, and a refusal to name his four men, *because the moment a yard's men are numbered they stop being men and start being a return, and I have seen a return.* The carrier says two boys on the west channel have heard the warden is abroad and the story they have made up is about a man who is sent to places. **Toban's reason for not voting is a brother who went off to another county on the first of Fallowmonth at the fourth hour of a Monday morning and has sent no word, and the notebook at the end of the chapter says the same date in his own hand.** **Ends on the notebook, and on the sentence that governs the rest of the volume: *I have sent nobody anywhere, and if the day comes that I do, it will not be because somebody asked me to, and that is the day the road stops being a road and I do not think I will be able to tell which day it was.***

**Ch 54 · the fourth, a Saturday, a market day · Mara.** Reeve Aldis Fenn calls an extraordinary sitting in the long room over the wharf, and says out loud, before anything else, that the room is not a bench. Nobody is on oath and nothing binds anybody. **Her question is the count's own question: how many persons in this county can say no to the Old Toll crossing, and be believed, and go on living in this town afterwards.** Captain Rell, who has been *picked* and has a nail in his own wall with a seal on it; the guild's man with the levels, whose no is worth exactly a quarter of an inch; **Cresset Vaine**, 33, a stocking-maker who sells to nine families and has never been asked a question in a room in her life, and whose want is a figure she can say out loud in a market. **The answer is eleven and it is not four.** Bram Ottery stands when his trade is read and asks for one thing: **that the eleven not go on the guild roll, because a roll in this town has a column headed *Keeper* on it now.** The reeve agrees and gives the reason — a bench clerk asked her in a corridor on **Thursday** whether this was going on the roll, and said *then it isn't anything, is it*, and she has thought about it for two days and **he was not being cruel, he was asking the ordinary question.** The eleven goes in Mara's day-book against no person, and she says the sentence the room came for: **a count is a list with the names taken off, and a list with the names taken off is the one document I have spent four years refusing to make.** Lysa Fenn, at the back, in fifty minutes, says that if eleven people can say no and be believed then the instrument is not a document — **it is the eleven** — and asks for it minuted. **At the fourth hour, up the stair: Nell Tace**, 36, of Wickmoor, with a second part of her fever. Eleven names with a column headed *removal* and a column headed *ground* and eleven lines under it, and **she is the twelfth, and she is still able to answer.** Her want is two things and she has no interest in the bridge and has never heard of the marshal. She says *Culling Order* once, with no member attached to it, and does not say it again. Mara tells her the truth: **it is not this office's war, and she is not going to tell her she can do anything about it, because she would find out in a month and she would rather she found out from a person.** The eleven names are entered under their own names with a date and are not sent to anybody and are not totalled against anything, and the reason is written in full: **this office has a claim in a county file two hundred miles off that has a woman's name on a separate sheet under a separate number, and that is the last thing in this business that was done to somebody, and I am not going to be the second thing in somebody else's book.**

**Ch 55 · the fifth · Mara and Halven.** The endorsement comes by the county post in a grey wrapper and is four lines printed on the back of a sheet Aren Kest read in a gateway four days earlier: **an officer shown to hold a volume entered on a press register and not on the shelf, who discloses the same of his own motion, is endorsed, and any process depending on the volume's absence is discharged, and the endorsement is not to be withdrawn, there being no procedure in this office for the withdrawal of an endorsement.** Number 4 of the season; discharges the writ of enquiry on the reeve's office. **Halven, 61, thirty-four years a registrar, comes up the stair with a locked box and tells the trade before the story**, because he wants it checked: the offence of a missing volume has no name and no penalty and a provincial court cannot try it, and he committed it in his own hand twice, in YR 302 and YR 309. The book is the *Anchors, schedules and dispositions, Alder Vale*, press-mark **press four, die eleven**, and its first leaf is a schedule for three bells on the Alder Vale crossing, of which two are *removed* with no office against either and the third is **retained** — and the retained one is on a parapet four hundred miles away. The second leaf is the whole of Volume 02: a column headed *county* running past Alder Vale to **eleven other counties, one of which is Ash**, and the disposition word against Ash is **FIRED.** She reads the counties twice, looking for a shape. He says he has had the word since Hearthmonth of the year before last and has never found Ash on a map. **She says FIRED is a kiln word, and he says he has been waiting two years for somebody to say that.** He sold it on the fourth for four shillings and the endorsement, in a locked box, under a receipt for the box and not for the contents. **Then he discloses it, unasked, in a form, and asks that the disclosure say he did it of his own motion and not on a fee**, because the record of his refusal is in the same county file as the endorsement, and in four years a clerk will open that file and find the shield taken by one man and refused by the other and will look up the number. His want is Volume 11's: *I want there to be one document in the world in which it says I did it.* The consideration is corrected from four shillings and an endorsement to **the endorsement**, and Mara enters that she asked which of the two and that he answered in about a second, which means he had it ready, which she is not going to enter and is going to think about for a year. Ends with the box on a cart at the second hour, and the two new shelf-register entries, and nine minutes of copying into a press.

**Ch 56 · the sixth · Mara and Tovan.** The endorsement comes up the stair read out loud by the reeve's own clerk, who says he is not sorry for either bar coming off. **In the same hour the third writ is answered in the spaces on the ground that the office named in it holds no process because the endorsing discharged the only one it had, and this county is going to rely on that difference for about a year.** Then a one-sided form: *does the office named below hold any record relating to the declared object, in this or any other county? Yes / No. If yes, state the county.* **She reads it twice, looks at the wall for the length of nine, and calls it the cleverest thing anybody has sent her in four years and a half, and notes that it arrived by the same post as the thing that took the bar off.** She reasons both ways out loud and then writes **yes** with the county, on six grounds read back to a second keeper: the office holds one record received on the first of Fallowmonth from a road nobody described; the office has jurisdiction in no county outside this one and asserts none; **the keeper can be compelled to answer for the form and can be sued for the form and cannot be sued for the record, because the record is not the keeper's and has never been copied**; and the reason she is answering at all is that the alternative is a lie in a document. **The second answer's holding problem is the chapter.** Four kinds of holding because one kind of holding is one kind of failure, and the paper can go in none of them: not the toll-holder's roll, because it would acquire a provenance it does not have; not the yard, which holds the fact of a paper and not the paper; not the wharf lock-up, because a transaction is a thing a man can be held to in a court. **So it goes into the day-book and into the press, and Sarn says she has just written that a record cannot be sued for and put it in a place it can be, and Mara says she did it in that order on purpose: if she had waited to build a fourth holding she should have had to lie in the gap, and a keeper who leaves a gap on purpose has stopped being a keeper.** The lease: the second floor ends at Rainmonth, the salt merchant's wife has been asked eleven times since Wolfmonth of last year and has never told him, and what she wants is **two rooms and a door on a lane, because a stair is how the Crown's man comes up on you and a door is how he has to knock.** Tovan prices three rooms, warns that one room is two rooms' work and no more, and tells her the yard has four weeks in it. **And he says the third thing: he has not asked her where **his brother** went, has wanted to every day since the **first of Embermonth**, and the not asking has been a trade and not a virtue, and there is a line in his book on the second of Fallowmonth with a rule over it that she is welcome to read and is not going to like.** She does not read it that afternoon. She pays **four pence a year** for one room over the chandler's at the north end with a door on a lane, gets a tradesman's receipt, enters it against no person, and writes under it and under the farthing of salt: **a door on a lane is the only thing this business has ever bought that a marshal of a Crown cannot serve a writ on, and it cost four pence, and she would like somebody in about eleven years to compare that figure with the four marks and six a year this county pays a bench of nine to keep a bridge.**

**Ch 57 · the seventh, a Tuesday, not a market day · Aren.** A registrar of thirty-four years is on a bench-board in the yard with a receipt for a box and an uncompleted counter-signature, **because he wants the letter he is writing about it witnessed and will not sign it in a room with one person in it**, and the yard is the only place in this county with four men in it. The letter goes into the yard's book with four signatures, one man declining because he has not got a hand for a name. **Rowan Petch, 26, three days on the road, his own money**, with four days to write a sentence: the Office has asked the provincial court to *state the purpose for which the county file in the matter of the crossing of Alder Vale is held in this court*, dated the fourth, returnable the eleventh. **Every answer he can think of is a sentence and every sentence is a claim**, and the file says its purpose on the face of the writ and the purpose is the one sentence a person could be made out of. Aren's method, twice: **the question on the sheet is a purpose, which belongs to whoever had it; the question you can answer is what is in the box, when it came, whose hand put it there, and what is on the top of it — and a return that answers nothing is a document, because the only thing anybody can do with *here is the box and here is when it came* is open the box.** And the forms reason: **a blank in a return gets ruled through and dated, and a ruled line with a date against it reads as *nothing to state*, and a question closed by a return is a question nobody has to ask again — so the office has asked the question that can be closed, and from where Petch is standing the wrong question and the right one produce the same sheet of paper.** **Halven's contribution: a press register is not a record of a shelf, it is a record of what a man said he did with a shelf, in the hand of the man who said it — the most honest book in this kingdom, honest about the wrong thing** — and he writes his two entries out from memory and says plainly that a man's memory is not a register, and that Petch must not put it in a returnable paper, because in four years somebody will read it and see a hand he recognises. Petch's want, in his own words: **nobody in eleven years has asked him what a press register is for and he could tell you in four seconds, and the fact that nobody has asked is the answer to the question.** Then Aren says **it is a bell** — before he knows he is going to — and stops, because a provincial clerk has heard it, and the word is the thing a person gets made out of. Petch refuses to write it, refuses to have it entered, and gives the reason that is the chapter: **if I keep it out of a book for you I am doing your work and not mine, and in four years you will have been right and I will have been a man who knew, and there is no office in this kingdom that a man can be told about for keeping a word out of a file.** He decides in the gateway, out loud, in front of four men, to file a return that answers nothing, to leave the purpose out **in terms**, and to end by asking the office to supply its own purpose in its own hand under its own seal. The four facts go into the yard's book with the hour. **The notebook** then does the volume's arithmetic for the first time with the figure in print on the other side of it: six days of absence against a printed warning of twenty-one is not an error, it is **minus fifteen days, and the warning was longer than the absence**; the warning did not fail, the only thing that was ever going to be measured by it is him. **The fourth warmth of the palm was the twenty-fourth of Fallowmonth — the day the bell went back and the channel opened and the road went both ways in one day — and it is the only one of the four he did not cause.** And two costs entered and not said to anybody: the Sedge Reach scour unread since the sixth of Mudmonth with the bench's marking due on the ninth, and **three Fridays of the copying post, which is nine shillings, in the county's hands, because a man who is owed three Fridays is a man who has been somewhere.**

**Ch 58 · the eighth, a Wednesday · Mara.** Second working day in a room with a press in it and a day-book in it and a person in it, which is not a room but a corridor with three heavy things in it. **The trigger is a form:** she put a county into a Crown's form on the sixth and cannot go on not thinking about it, and the only thing the office holds that can be asked a question about a county is a page with nine lines on it and no name in any of them. **The bar has been off the press since the ninth hour of the sixth** — fifty-three days, lifted by a four-line endorsement, which is the most instructive object in the county. She takes out the index leaf and not the line, and reads it and does not copy it, with a second keeper in the room. The leaf is the front of the toll houses' register of outgoing writs, four hundred leaves, press-mark **press four, die eleven, first impression of the season.** Four columns — *County, Date of tender, Returned, Answered.* **Eight writs of severance, all Alder Vale, all inside about a week, the *returned* column filled eight times out of eight and the *answered* column empty in all eight, and the first return is the twentieth — six days, which is a bench and not a river.** And the office that made the eight could not today say who said no. **The ninth line is out of order, which is the whole of it:** it sits in the middle of the Alder Vale run, the *county* cell is **empty** and empty in the way a cell is ruled and left, the tender is in **YR 305**, the *returned* cell is empty, and the *answered* cell has **a date eleven days after the tender** — *six days is a bench; eleven days is a man writing to a superior and waiting to be told he may.* She enters **one fact** and then **three refusals in full**, because a refusal that is not written down is a thing a person did once: that the ninth line is of Ash (it is not said to be, and a county cannot be served or refused or asked, and a keeper who fills an empty cell becomes the source of a county); that it was made in the same hand (a hand does not leave the office, a hand cannot be produced, a hand is a resemblance, and a man who wants a resemblance will go a long way and will not put the fare in); and that it is the paper of the first of Fallowmonth (a date can be looked for, a press-mark cannot be argued with, a hand cannot be produced, and the join would be a copy, and **four sentences on an oiled wrapper written by somebody else's clerk bind this office and this office cannot waive them**). Sarn's beat, and it is the batch's best: nine refusals in four years and a half and **the wanting is now the only part of this business that is not in a book**, and in four years a man will be able to say exactly what she knew on the eighth of Embermonth and how she came to know it. Then the other document goes on the table — the four-line body line of the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301, which has **a name in it**, because a creditor's register has a column headed *tendered by* and the line for that day says *Edric Hunn, marshal* — and the index says in nine lines and no letter anywhere **who was answered.** Same book, eleven pages apart, a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, and the register has not been asked for by anybody and will not be, because a private book in another country is not a public book and a clerk says so in eleven seconds. **And the two documents are in two different hands — one copying beautifully and fast, one copying correctly and slowly — and neither is the hand of a man who signed anything, and a third hand holds the withdrawal, which is in neither.** What ties them together is a press-mark, and a press-mark is not a man and cannot be asked a question. At the fourth hour the **eighth ruled line** again, ten days after the first time: *on the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth I wanted to fill that line because I did not know what was in it; today I wanted to fill it because I think I know; a cell a man wants to fill because he knows what goes in it is not a cell any more, it is a mouth* — and she enters that she has now been in one room with **three empty cells in one week.** And then one more entry, which decides Batch 0010: **the only kind of holding that has ever answered a question in this case is a holding that cannot be served**, this office has one, it is a room on a canal wharf at a farthing of salt, and **it is not going to be used for this, because a roll of persons in a lock-up is a thing a creditor could sell**; so if this office is right about what is wanted, somebody has to make another one, in the country where the register is, out of his own book, at his own cost, on his own paper, with his own name at the head of it, in a matter where no court in this county has jurisdiction — **and this office cannot do it, cannot order it, and will not pretend it can.** Ends four hundred yards down Weir Street, where a boy of fourteen brings a sheet that did not come by the post, in a hand that is not a clerk's, dated in a calendar this county does not use, naming two people: **we were asked a question by a stranger and we have written our answer down, and we do not know the answer, and the writing of it is the answer.**

**Ch 59 · the ninth, a Thursday, a market day, the fourth hour · Aren, the far end.** He comes up out of the road on **clay steps** and puts his right foot back to find the edge, and there is no edge: the road behind him is the length of a cart and then it is the third step. **The weather is wrong** — flat yellow light off a hill that stops at four hundred yards, and everything in it the colour of a kiln, and a river that makes almost no noise and runs the colour of milk over a working ground, and the smell of **nine days of kiln.** He is at the foot of a nine-span bridge in the middle of a market, and the bridge has four hundred yards of tiled kerb down both sides, a fired tile set into the face of every kerb stone, one to a stone, **and a road in this county is a record**: the device impressed, the kiln's mark on the back, the date, and a man can turn a stone over with a heel and read who cut it. **On the coping of the second span is a bell** — six inches by four and about the thickness of two fingers, fired clay and grey, on a short iron, a rope hanging in two loops, no yoke, no headstock, no striker and not cast — and from two hundred yards in bad light he reads a mark on it, **and the mark is a hand laid flat on a book, which he has seen four times in eleven years in three countries and can name, and he does not name it, because a name in a room is a thing that goes into a book and he has been the man who said the name in a room five times.** **A leaf of the crossing book comes through with him**, written at the third hour while both ends were awake, in the toll-man's hand: *line ninety-four, and the weather is filled in by the person who is here and it is a wet morning with a wind off the ash and I do not know what to call the wind so I have called it ash* — **a road that is refused is worth more than a road that is open, written three times in a book, because a thing written once is a saying and a thing written three times is a practice; a county that refuses a road has something to refuse it with, and we have a bell and a book and nine men on a bench and it has taken four generations to build any one of the three, and on the day it went we rang it, and I am not a clever man and the ringing is the whole of what we have and it is not nothing.** Aren's own line, in his own head, in the margin: this county has nothing to refuse with and has never been asked; the county on the other side of the river has something to refuse with and has never refused anything; **he does not know which of the two is in worse order and he has been in both.** The woman on the fourth span is doing the ordinary work of a road with her hand on a kerb tile she has looked at nine thousand times, and she asks **are you the one who sends people** — and he says no, and she says **that is what the last one said too**, and he asks who and not when, and she says a man who did not give a date and that is not a county's answer, it is hers, and that he asked in the right order, because everybody asks when. He puts his foot back to find the edge: **it comes out at the third step and it goes about the length of a cart, and both of the men it did that to were told by their own offices that they had opened a door, and one of them went home and one of them did not.** A man of sixty with a ledger and a black thumb to the second joint and a slate, writing down what a stranger says on a bridge. **And the refusal is arithmetic, not argument: a line and two hands, a date, a witness at each end who is not the person keeping the road — and the hand is not here and the witness is not here and the date means nothing to anybody in this county, and every part of the arrangement that matters is on one end.** He does not argue, because every reason he could give is a reason why he should be let in, and a man who gives reasons to be let in is asking a favour, and a refused favour is a smaller thing. He says instead: **you did not open it and you were right, I am not the man who sends anybody, and it made no difference, which is how I know you were right.** She tells him to get off her kerb, because he is a heavy man on a wet morning and the tile carries the only account of a man who has been dead thirty years. At half past four, when the market breaks, the man with the ledger asks for the woman's name for the column headed *hand*, twice, and is refused twice, and she says: **there is a column and there is nothing in it and both are the truth; you have written nine lines in this book this month and the county has had a road open across the Kiln since before I could walk and nobody has ever asked who keeps it, and the answer is that everybody keeps it and nobody is named, and that is how you have a road; put a name in that column and in four years you will have one name on a road in a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths, and when he is gone there will be a column and a name and nobody on the bridge, and there will be a clerk two hundred miles off writing that the hand of the Ninebridge is a person.** He says nothing at all, **because the last time in his life he was in a room with a refusal better than his own argument, a bench of nine had refused a Crown's inspection on a deck in the rain and had gone on holding the stone for four generations.** He goes back down the steps on the side he came up and puts his hand on the coping, which is cold.

**Ch 60 · the ninth, the fourth hour, the near end · Aren.** The day's line made up at the third hour in the gateway: a rope of four fathoms spliced at both ends, the near splice out of a cooper's bin, the far splice cut by a ferrywright on a river that is not in this county, **and it is not the anchor, and a man who confuses the two is a man who cannot say which end of what he is holding.** The return is the document the arrangement is written on, and the chapter prints it: two keepers, a date, a tide, a witness line at each end — and **both** witness lines empty when the sheet is filled in at the third hour, one of them filled before the fourth hour and one of them not, **and the one that is not is the whole of why this day is going to be what it is going to be, and Tovan could tell his brother that at eleven o'clock the night before.** And then, in a gateway, with a yard going on round them, **Tovan Kest reads out the line he has had in his book with a rule over it since the second of Fallowmonth and has never read to a person in eleven years**: the eighth of Greenmonth, the eleventh hour at night, a parapet at the near abutment, a brother with a lamp who asked for the bit he had written for himself and read it out and read it twice and gave it to him to sign and **did not ask him one thing, not one, in four hours, standing on a stone in the dark, to a man who is seven years younger and has been his brother since either of them can remember** — *he was not asking me for anything, he was telling me, and there is a difference between a man who tells you a thing and a man who asks you for one, and I have not been able to say in words what the difference is.* And the reason for the two months since he wrote the line down, given at last: **the day I ask is the day you have to answer, and a man who has to answer is a man who is going to think about the answer on the far side of a thing instead of about the work** — and Aren says that is a good reason and is not true, and Tovan says it is the reason he has. The reeve comes with a sheet and a rule, reads the last line in two seconds, and refuses the witness line herself, once, and it is the first thing she has said in nine years that anybody in the town has written down: **I am the reeve of this town, a reeve is the town's witness, a road this town does not own cannot have this town as its witness, and if it has then in four years somebody will say the town stood there, and the road will have been shut by a signature and not by an argument, and an argument is a thing that can be had again on a different day by a different bench with a different temper in it.** So he goes into the market and comes back with **Bram Ottery**, 47, unemployed since the fourth of Greenmonth, who says first that he is not going to say anything about the fee (fourpence a week out of the yard's own box, twenty-four days a year, about thirty-two weeks of standing, **a hundred and twenty-eight pence, ten shillings and eightpence**, costed in pence twice), and then gives the reason, once, and it is a bad one and the only one he has: **I would like to stand somewhere, once, while a true thing is being done. Not a road. Not a crossing. One morning, in a gateway, and there will be a thing on a piece of paper and it will be true, and I will have been part of holding it up.** Tovan says write that down; Aren writes it in the back of the notebook; **and Tovan enters the witness and the fourpence and the hour in the yard's book and nothing else, because a man's reason for standing in a gateway is a thing believed, and a yard is not an office.** The bell goes at a quarter to the fourth hour, the boy of fifteen alone because the far end is not there and has not said so, the third stroke leaned on; the sill is under his feet the whole time and nobody looks at it — **a dressed block, forty yards of tarred hemp, the fourth course open the width of a hand in register, and no glass in this county and no third seat, and the stone cold.** The line goes out and **nothing comes**, and that is the whole of what Merefen knows about it. The day's costs, entered flat: **the scour under the nose of the third pier unread and the bench's mark-day reading not taken**, because the man who has read that scour twice went out of the county at the fourth hour; **four weeks and a day in the yard**; and **three Fridays of the copying post, which is nine shillings, in the county's hands.** The story on the west channel is already changing shape by the second hour and has two people sent in it by the fourth doorway. At the sixth hour the reeve writes, and on the eleventh a line comes back in the fair hand of the woman who keeps the crossing book: **the road had not opened at this end either, and it had not failed.** He reads the eighth's paper again at half past four and copies its date exactly — *the nineteenth day of the rolling, being the sixth day after the low water, the year counted from the first firing* — and writes underneath that he cannot check one word of it, and that **a man in a filing room in Merefen is going to read a copy of that line in about seven months and will not be able to tell from the line which side of it the year was on.** Then he says it out loud, in a gateway, in daylight, to four men and a market: **she did not open it and she was right, and I am not the man who sends anybody, and it made no difference; and they have written down the names of the two people who would hold a line without being asked, and they have got the date wrong and it is their own date and not ours, and they have told us they do not know the answer, and that is the first time anybody has told me they do not know something and put it in a book and sent it to me on purpose.** So are you going. **There is no road. There is a county, over a bridge, with a market on it.** He goes over the Ninebridge at the fifth hour on his own legs, behind the woman with the roll of tools, and a market does not get out of the way of a man. **In the first street of Kilnholt the man with the ledger asks for a trade, not a name**, and gives the reason in four hundred years of county practice: **once is a clerk, twice is an officer, a clerk is asking a question and an officer is establishing a fact.** He will not put a name in a cell, and he cannot leave a trade blank, because a blank makes the county's figure short by one and **he counts the county and not himself and has never in nineteen years been short.** So Aren gives his trade — **a sett-cutter, who cuts setts and sets them in a road and reads where the load goes, not good enough to be trusted with the second thing and the only man anybody has got for it at the moment** — and the ledger man writes it under *trade* and asks for the county, and gets: **there is no county, or there is one and it is not on any map in this world, and a woman with a press in a fen county spent a shilling on a map she did not need, and I would write it down if I could and I cannot, and I have written *north*.** The paper's second name — the person the county chose to witness its end of a line — is in the notebook in the order it was given and is not used once. First entry in that column in nineteen years that is not a place anybody in that county can walk to. And Aren asks the man his name, and is refused the way the woman refused, and the man says: **then you have been here an hour.** And goes along the kerb with the black thumb going from tile to tile, counting, the way a man does who counts fires and does not count himself.

## Batch 0007 — Volume 02, Chapters 61–70, "Twenty-Three Days"

Block 2. POVs **Aren ×9 (61–69) / Mara ×1 (70)**. **No crossing, no Bind, no System display, no gate, no stage, no revelation.** **The word *Gatehouse* is not spelled out in this batch at all**, the phrases *Culling Order* and *custodian* do not appear, and the private-keeper clause is not mentioned, referenced or explained by anybody. **Four new names against the cap of six and two slots unfilled — Pernel Quist, Colm Arreld, Hesper Lound, Ostry Vaile — plus Bel, four, of Kilnholt, a child, who is named and does not count.** The kiln clerk is addressed as *the man with the ledger* throughout and **his name is withheld on the page by design**; Ilsa Soke is a returned face and is **not named in this batch** — she is *the hand of the Ninebridge* and *the hand*, and she does not correct him, and the reason is given on the page. The tile on the coping is shown twice at close range, recognised, and **not explained by anybody**, including on the last page.

**Path clock, the block's own frame, printed in Chapters 61, 62, 63, 66, 67, 68 and 69 and checkable day by day.** A day of the rolling is a day of a county's clay-digging and firing season; a firing is nine days and takes a cord of wood a day; there are two low waters a year; the year is counted from the first firing; **the low water is the thirteenth day of the rolling** (item 168, and Batch 0006's paper is consistent with it). **Ch 61 the first day = the twenty-second day of the rolling, being the ninth day after the low water** (the same day as Chapters 59 and 60, which is the point: the chapters and the arrival are one day). Then 23 / 24 / 25 (Ch 62), 26 (Ch 63), 27 (Ch 64), 28–31 (Ch 65), 32 (Ch 66), 33 (Ch 67), 37 (Ch 68), 40 (Ch 69), with days 34–36 and 41–44 carrying the two hundred and six askings and the sittings. **The twenty-third day is the last day on the far side and Chapter 69 closes on it.** No path-side chapter dates itself by a main-world day, and **no main-world weekday is used in the block** — an earlier draft had "a Thursday of the rolling" in Ch 69 and it was struck for exactly this reason. **Ash's own two weekday names are on the page, a Monday in Ch 61 and a Friday in Ch 62 and Ch 65, and they are the county's own and not a chain; a later writer may not build a weekday calendar for Ash out of them.**

**The arithmetic, and it is the batch's engine and it is printable.** **941 hearths · 2,450 people · 2,906 tiles fired on the roll to date · 2,906 − 2,450 = 456.** Chapter 61 gives the reader the whole working in a kiln shed: the count off the wall (1,680 + 1,226 = 2,906, so the column foots), the division in fours (2,906 = 726 fours and two over; 941 = 235 fours and one over), and **the man with the ledger takes the hearths' one and says which remainder he means — "Two over on the clay and one over on the fires, and the one over on the fires is the one tile in this county that has not got three brothers and has not had since the first firing"** — then the hearths, the people, and the difference said out loud four times — by the kiln clerk twice and by Aren twice. **The two remainders are on the page and they belong to different quantities; neither is the subtraction the chapter is for.** **No figure anywhere in the batch is derived from 456 by scaling it, and Chapter 66 says so out loud and refuses to give the rest of it a number.** The three Ash quantities are never added together except in Chapter 61, and **no Ash figure is set beside a Bellweather or Merefen figure anywhere**.

**Ch 61 · the first day, the twenty-second day of the rolling · Aren.** He is walked, not driven, fifty minutes to the third building in from the low end of a street in Kilnholt, and the county's introduction to him is a stock count being done in the ordinary way on the eleventh day off a rolling: the man with the ledger reads the wall off a slate, the boy puts it, the count is 2,906, and nine hundred and forty-one hearths is the county's figure about itself, and a hearth is a fire and a fire is what a person has, and **there is no column in this county for a person and there has not been one since the roll was begun.** He looks for a load path and there is nothing: no arch, no span, no wall anybody has ever doubted. He asks how many people there are, and the man with the ledger stands in the middle of his own shed and discovers that nobody in nineteen years has thought to ask and that he has had the number in his head the whole time. **He says two thousand four hundred and fifty out loud for the first time in nineteen years, and then says four hundred and fifty-six, and stops, and does not say what it is, and does not go on**, and changes the subject to two hundred and forty faces on the far wall that are for a house and not for a person. His contempt for a sermon is stated in the trade's order: *this county has always counted fires and has never counted itself, and I want that said in the order it is true and not as a sermon, because I count and I do not preach, and there is one of these in this shed and it is not me.* **Aren does it in a minute and a half, out loud, and a room of nine stops** — the man at the packing bench leaves a tile on the bench, the boy does not read the next number, five of the nine go still — and then the room goes back to work and the total is the same at the seventh hour as it was at the eleventh, **because nothing in that shed had changed except the number of people in it who knew.** **Ostry Vaile, fifty-eight, hands black to the wrist, says the number is right and that he has known since the fourth rolling, and that it is his mother, and that she has been fired twice, and that the second time was in the winter and there was no fire at all, and that nobody asked her.** Ends on the number in a room where nobody is speaking, and on the hand of the Ninebridge telling him, on the way out, that the county asked him nothing and told him everything.

**Ch 62 · the second to the fourth day, days 23–25 of the rolling · Aren.** The Roll of Persons at the assizes: forty-one shelves, a stove, a long table, and **Pernel Quist, 44, nineteen years in the job**, who does not ask him a single question and tells him which chair has a leg out of true and that she has not got to it in four years. **A tile to a person**: seal-tile, six by four, four faces and a back — a device pressed with a die, a name written in wet clay with a stick, a hearth, a trade, and **on the back, pressed by the person themselves in wet clay, their own right hand**, so that the mark is theirs and not the office's, and it is the only part of this county's records that is anybody's at all. **The law in four sentences: a person is on the roll if two other persons witness them *and* their tile agrees with the entry** — not one, both — and she has struck a name nine times in nineteen years and eight of them were dead, and the ninth is her own mistake in her second year, uncorrected on the page and corrected every morning for eighteen years. **A person who softens cannot be witnessed by anybody including themselves, and the law takes them off the roll by itself while she is writing, and she has never had to be the one, and she is proud of that and it is the best thing about her office.** A struck person **is struck from the count**; she says it nineteen times a year; he tries four ways over three days to get at the difference between the count and the roll and gets four true answers and not one of them is the difference: **the two words are the same length and they sit next to each other on the same page and a person who has to look at both of them to see which one he wants has not got a job.** The five exclusions, read off the back of a sheet because he was not given a copy: cannot witness, cannot be witnessed, cannot be married into a hearth, cannot be given a name at a kiln, cannot be buried in this county's ground — **north of the low water, in the peat, where there is no ground and no grave, and a marker driven in with the kiln mark on it and no name on it, because there is no name to put on it.** He goes the whole length of forty-one shelves with a lamp and there is no column for the people, and her reason for not looking a third time in nineteen years is the chapter's best line: **a woman who goes looking for a thing she is going to find does not find it as a searcher, she finds it as a person who wanted it, and then the thing that goes in the column is a thing a person wanted and not a thing a person counted.** And she gives him the drawer, out loud, in front of him, without showing it to him: a physician's finding of four years back, about reading, which she has read twice and thought about for four years, and her conclusion, every word of which is correct, is **that it is a finding about reading and not a finding about a roll** — a roll is not read, a roll is witnessed — and underneath it the harder half, which is that his finding would be excellent against her office, is a very poor answer to the question he is actually asking (*how do I make a man stop reading a mark*), and the answer to that is not fewer marks, it is marks he cannot get at, **and there is not one thing in that room she can do that with.** Ends on her best argument, given to a stranger before he can get it from the kiln: **the tile is the account of a person that does not need them, and that is not a claim about them and not a punishment and not a judgement, and it is the only reason there is a county at all** — and in nineteen years nobody has ever come to that room to be useful.

**Ch 63 · the fifth day, the twenty-sixth day of the rolling · Aren.** Magistrate **Colm Arreld, 54**, of the assizes, in the room with the stove, and a clerk at the end of the table who is not named.
