The fear in the room is of the spring, and it is legible before a word is said: a county almanac with the digging days in a different ink and two carts' worth of ash-wood hired by the quarter. **Four lines of county law, read off the table and not off the paper, signed by three magistrates, and the third signature is nine months old and the man has been dead since the eighth of the last rolling, and nobody in the room says one word about it, and the reason nobody says one word is that a signature is a signature.** The law: a list unexpended for a year may be burned in the market square at the sittings before witnesses; the striking is thereby dissolved; the clerk of the roll shall destroy the tiles of every person named; **and shall enter the destruction in the roll, and not the name** — which Arreld defends as mercy and is arguably the volume's smallest cruelty, because the line that saves two hundred names from a page also means that when the tiles go the roll says *two hundred tiles went* and nothing else. The offer: **the List of Persons to be Struck, two hundred and six names, eleven months old, in a locked press, burnable the day the sittings sit.** Arreld's want in his own voice: the softening to stop before the spring, because the spring is the digging and the rolling is heaviest and the last two winters were the worst. **He asks Aren what he thinks, properly, in front of a clerk, and it is the first time in this book anybody has asked him for an opinion instead of a service.** What he says is *I would want to know what happens to the tile* — and the tile of a struck person is a fired tile with their name on it and their own hand on the back, in a box in this building, which the law destroys only when the list is burned and not before, and burning the list therefore means two hundred fires in houses with nothing in the account of them, **and Arreld says that is not a worse thing than a name that cannot be read, and he is right about the fever.** The figures, in a copying book in a hand that is not a clerk's: **forty-one people off the roll with the softening in the four years before the list, and a hundred and twelve in the eleven months after, by month: nine, eleven, nine, thirteen, fifteen, seventeen, twenty-one, twenty-four, and no place in that column where it turns**, and a man who has read a column forty times is looking for the place where it turns. And the part he has said to nobody: **the list is read.** The rollkeeper reads it every morning; the physician reads it; the man with the ledger reads it because he cannot leave a column unread; he has asked all three in writing and has no power over any of them. **And Aren has nothing, and the chapter is honest about why: he has never in five months been a useless man in a room and there is no second set of skills for it, and there is nothing in that room to look at.** He refuses to be a voice — *a man who says the correct thing in a room in order to be the man in the room* — and throws out one trade observation instead: **you have got a record and a disease and you have been looking for the disease in the record instead of asking who is reading the record, and burning the list might take out the hottest line and leave the roll.** And he asks for a week, and knows before he finishes the sentence that he will not get it.

**Ch 64 · the sixth day, the twenty-seventh day of the rolling · Aren and the hand of the Ninebridge.** He goes out to the bridge with a sett-cutter's bag he did not want and brought, and calls her **the hand**, and explains why in a sentence — *it is a gerund in my trade and a noun in yours* — and she does not correct him and says why she will not: **if I give you my name you have a person; if you have got the hand you have got a job, and a job can be asked to turn up.** The cutting shed at the north end of the Ninebridge belongs to the Ninebridge kiln and holds every soft tile in that part of the county on boards under wet cloths, about four hundred at any one time. **A soft tile is a mark in clay not yet fired; it lies in a wet board for seven years; it dries and wets and dries and the face creeps and the mark walks off the edge, and re-pressing is the repair, and every person in this county re-presses their own tile on a Saturday in about a minute, and there is no law in nineteen years of this county about a soft tile at all.** The law's silence is the chapter's engine: a person is on the roll if two others witness them and the tile agrees, so **a tile that disagrees with the entry takes the person off the roll by operation of the law, with no decision by anybody**, and the county cannot count presses because a press in a house is not on a wall in a shed. He gets it wrong at once — his hand covers the half-inch mark, and the mark comes out as a flat place with a wet thumbprint, and a woman at the third hearth down is off the roll that evening because a stranger came in. **The rule: do not press the tile, press the dish; if you press on the mark you take the mark out, and if you press around the mark the mark stands up in it, and that is the entire trade and it is a thing a child does.** **Bel, four, at the low end of the rack on a stool, nine right in a row**, and he understands inside a minute that she is not better than he is: **she is smaller**, and the whole difficulty is that he has too much hand, and there is no such thing as too much hand in any trade he has been in. The man at the cutting bench — nineteen years at the bench, not named — warns that the low rows are the children's and the children's are not to be done by a man from off a road. Her terms, and her want, and the chapter's close: **a road in this county is the only thing in it that is not an argument** — the roll is an argument with a man in it, the law is an argument four lines long, the list is an argument about two hundred names, the tiles are an argument everybody won in the third hundred and eighth year and it has cost them the only thing they had, which is a thing they can argue with — **and she offers him exactly one thing: that he is useful and not a visitor.**

**Ch 65 · the seventh to the tenth day, days 28–31 of the rolling · Aren and the hand of the Ninebridge.** **The four days of learning a thing a child does, and the four days were not the difficulty; the difficulty is that every trade he has been in was taught to him by a shortage and the answer here is to be less than the thing.** He ruins four tiles: press in the middle and the mark smears and a woman at the third hearth down comes off the roll; press too much and his knuckle comes up on the back, where the kiln's mark goes, **and a tile that cannot be traced to a kiln is a tile a man who wanted to be on the roll could not be kept off it, and the only people in this county who would want to be on it are the two hundred and six.** And the same both ways: the law is not a trap, a record does not have an opinion about who you are, and **there is nothing whatever in nineteen years of this county about a soft tile.** **On the third day Bel presses her own tile wrong**, and he says one word — *again* — and she puts it right and goes back to the check round, and he has been in this county nine days and it is the only thing he has been useful for, and she says she would have got there in about a week, and a week in this county is the difference between a person on a roll and a person not. **The suspicion is not lifted and should not be:** he learns it in four days and no man learns a thing in four days, and the cutting-bench man asks the woman, in front of him, how long, and gets *four days*, and afterwards says the true shape of it — *a man who is good at a thing that takes a child a minute is a man who has been good at something for forty years and is showing you* — and she defends him in tradesman's terms: a setter is a man who has spent nineteen years making a thing true against a thing out of true at a scale where a mistake is a man in a hole. **On the fourth day he gets it, and nothing happens when he gets it, and what his hand is doing is a hand setting a thing true against a thing out of true** — the motion of Chapter 6 and Chapter 10, the fourth of four times it has come back to him without asking. **It is on the right side of the world for the first time and it belongs to a county and not to a city**, and what he has been doing for eleven years is the other thing: a thing that needs a place and somebody who has agreed the place is the place and a promise and a witness who is not the man doing it, and if any of the three is missing it does not fail, it does not happen. **A Bind does not travel and a motion does** — stated once, plainly, and he puts nothing into anything. **He does not get stronger. He gets a second use for the hand he already has, which is the whole of Stage 2, and nobody in either country has taught it to him and nobody in either country knows he has it.** Then the coping of the second span, from four feet, in daylight: the object is **not a bell**; it is a fired clay tile, six by four, with a rope on it in two loops and no yoke; the mark on it is **a hand laid flat on a book**, which he has seen four times in eleven years in three countries; the rope is about a year old and the tile is older than anybody alive who has ever touched it; **he does not touch it, does not turn it over, does not go and find out about the back of it.** And later the same fourth day she asks him what it is — *everybody who comes to this town asks me about that* — and he says **"I don't know,"** and she says "Good," and thanks him for it, **and the chapter ends on his notebook line admitting that he has lied to the only person in this county who has thanked him for anything, and that a thing a person has said out loud and been believed, and believed wrongly, is a debt and not a release.**

**Ch 66 · the eleventh day, the thirty-second day of the rolling · Aren.** About two hundred struck in the county and nine of them at the firing floor of the Kilnholt kiln at the change of shift, and the reason they are there is that a kiln-worker is a kiln-worker and a hod does not ask a man's standing. The county's first lesson to a stranger, and it lands in four seconds: **a man reads a stranger's hands before his face, and moves him eleven inches along a wall for holding a bag.** The man at the loading end wants nothing: **not money — we want to be asked**; nobody has asked any of them for eleven months, and every thing that has happened to every one of them has happened because nobody asked, and the day somebody asks is the day it stops, **and it will not stop because it is right, it will stop because it is written down.** **Ostry Vaile says he has been right since the autumn and would like to be left alone and uncopied, and the most defensible sentence anybody says in the volume is *I would like to be left alone and uncopied*, and the book does not argue with it.** Aren gives him the other half honestly — being findable is worth more than you would think, and a man nobody can check is a man somebody can do anything to — and Vaile answers with the number four: once to say the law had changed, once to say his tile was out of true, once to tell him his mother was not on the roll, in a kitchen, in front of her, and once in the winter to ask whether he would say he had not seen a thing. **And then the sentence the book is built on, in nineteen seconds, to a room of nine: a person who is struck has to be able to answer for himself, and if he answers he goes back on it, and if he cannot answer then he was never on it, and we have been keeping a list of things.** And the notebook records that he knew in about a second and a half that it is the answer **and that it is the most dangerous sentence anybody had said to him in five months, and that he did not say the second half, and will not, and the reason is that once it is in a form in his own head it will get into his own hands and he has watched his own hands write a list down in front of witnesses once already in another country.** The form, given by the man with the ledger because Vaile will not: four columns and a foot, device, name, hearth, trade, and on the back the person's own hand, **and a fifth space headed *struck* for a name, a date and a witnessing hand, about the size of his two fingers, and no sixth space and there never has been, and the sheet is the same width it was the day the roll was begun.** Nobody has met the man who ruled them. **A column is ruled before anybody knows what is going in it: somebody ruled five and stopped, ninety-four years ago, in a room in the assizes, in an afternoon, with a knife — and the fourth hundred and fifty-six, and the woman fired twice and the second time by her sister's husband with a thumb and a wet cloth in the morning before he went, because a soft tile on a shelf in a kitchen goes out of true in about three weeks and a man who fixes a board on a shelf has done nothing wrong.** The notebook's last page: the presses in houses are where the rest of the surplus is and he refuses to give it a figure, and then the entry that is the shape of the eighth ruled line four hundred miles off, which **nobody in this block may name and nobody in this block knows**: *a cell that is ruled and has never been filled in is not the same thing as a cell that is blank because there was nothing to put in it, and there is no way to tell the two from the cell, and both look exactly the same, and a man who is given a cell has to be told which one he is looking at by somebody.*

**Ch 67 · the twelfth day, the thirty-third day of the rolling · Aren and Hesper Lound.** A room over a chandler with a table and a drawer in the table, and a girl of fourteen who did not ask him anything, **because a physician who shuts a door is a physician who is about to find out something, and this county cannot afford to have anybody shut.** **Hesper Lound, 63, the county's one physician for nineteen years**, with about forty pages in the drawer and four of them read, once, by one person, in a room with the door shut. He says why he is doing it today, and it is the chapter's method: **a physician's finding in this county is a piece of paper with a man's name on the top of it, and the day it comes out of the drawer it stops being a finding and becomes a thing a magistrate may use, and he has watched a magistrate use a physician's page three times in nineteen years and in two the physician was a fool and in the third the magistrate was a decent man who took a true thing and put it in his own instrument.** He reads the four pages out loud, and they are the volume's moral argument as a medical finding and they are stated once: the softening enters by the mark, not through the eye, and a man who is read carries it into the marks of his household; it travels fastest along the marks that are read most; **the marks that are read most are the roll marks, because a clerk reads the roll every morning and does not know she is doing anything**; **therefore the cure is not fewer records**, because fewer records is what a frightened office does and a record that is not read cannot be corrected and a record that cannot be corrected is not a record, it is a claim, and this county has been living on claims for four years; **and the cure is records that do not agree with each other** — three copies, three hands, three places, none authoritative alone, restored when two agree and removed when two agree otherwise, the third being allowed to be wrong, and no single office able to do either. And his want, in his own voice: **the only thing wrong with the County of Ash is not the fever, it is that nobody has ever read a physician's finding out loud.** **Pernel Quist misunderstands it cleverly**, and the misunderstanding is the chapter: three copies means three offices and three clerks and a woman struck from the count by two of three that are a room and a press, **and she is entirely right about that and wrong about the rule, and the physician will not repeat the sentence because the second time a person hears it they hear it as an argument and not as a fact, and because the whole difference is that in her office a person says nothing and in his a person says everything, and the record that disagrees with the person is the record that is wrong, and there is no such thing in this county as a record that is right and the person who is wrong, and he has looked for one for four years and has found about two hundred men for whom it would have been very useful.** Then he asks Aren what he makes of it, and **Aren refuses**, and says why in the sentence the rest of the volume is built on: **if I tell this room not to burn that list, I am telling a county with a fever in it to keep a list, on my word; I am the man who puts lists into counties by arguing about them in front of witnesses; I have been doing it for eleven years and I am not going to do it again in a hat.** And the refusal the block is built on is the other side of the same coin: **he is the reason anybody in that room has to be careful, and he has been the reason for about a year and a half, and he has never once been in a room where he could tell.** Quist's one line back: *I told you on the fourth day that nobody comes here to be useful.*

**Ch 68 · the sixteenth day, the thirty-seventh day of the rolling · Aren, Arreld, Lound and Quist.** Four people in the room with the stove, which has not happened before and will not happen again, and the reason it had not happened is that a magistrate's room holds a magistrate and a clerk and a rollkeeper when there is a press to open and has not held a physician since a man called Ostrand was alive. **The kind misunderstanding, and it is the best room in the block**: Arreld has had the finding copied out twice, has the errors and not Lound's two corrections, and reads it as saying the same thing the magistrate has been saying for a year in two languages for four years, and wants to say the words *on the advice of the physician to this county* at the burning. **He is being kind, and what he wants is a physician's name on his instrument, and he hands Aren the chance to put a second name on it — and it is the second time he has been asked and the worst of the three and it was not asked to be polite.** And he is not wrong about the fever, and the finding says the cure is not fewer records, and both men are right, and there is nothing in the room to stop it. **Aren sees the sentence he is about to say and sees the file it will be in in four years, in about two seconds, the way he has had two seconds about it four times in eleven years, and every one of the four times he was right — and does not say it.** Then the refusal of the list, out loud, and the hat sentence, and the seven days: **the list is not burned on the day it is a year; in the seven days every one of the two hundred and six is asked once, by somebody who is not the office, in his own shed, by a person who works with him, and he is not asked what he wants because a man who is asked what he wants is a man who has to know the answer; the answers go in the asker's own hand and go nowhere anybody can produce them; the tiles are counted at the kilns by the people who made them.** And he says in the room, before they agree, that it is not enough. **Lound says the third term is wrong and gives the week anyway, and says the man has got it partly from the man who is going to tell him it is wrong, and adds that being asked is a fire and a fire does not stop being a fire because it is a good fire. Quist says no, twice — *a thing that cannot be produced is a thing that cannot be corrected* — says she will not pretend she thinks it is right, and says she has said no to the magistrate once in nine years in a corridor.** And Arreld grants it, and says the wages out loud first, and gives **three** reasons and two of them are wages: the wedging shed is a day and a half and taking it costs the last day of a rolling and forty men's day of wages; the wagons are on a schedule that is not his and the last two winters were the worst; and **a column that does not turn inside a fortnight has not turned in a year, and if he burns the list and is wrong he has burned two hundred and six tiles and the column is still going up, and a magistrate who has done that is finished — *I would rather be a man who waited and was wrong than a man who was right and could not do it again*** — and he asks the clerk to write all three down as reasons and not decisions, for the first time in nine years. He asks the rollkeeper in the room and not in a corridor and takes now as a week, and is minuted in those words. On the street the hand of the Ninebridge tells him what seven days cost the last time they were asked for in this county — a man off a road nine years ago, a list out of the clerk's roll, and **a woman two counties off who is called something that is not a thing she is called here** — and asks him the question she has not asked him in twenty days, because he has just asked the county for time and time is the only thing anybody here ever wants from her: **are you the one who sends people?**

**Ch 69 · the nineteenth to the twenty-third day, days 40–44 of the rolling · Aren and the hand of the Ninebridge.** The nineteenth day is the fourth day of the week he asked for and **his whole business for nine hours in a shed at the edge of a village is carrying boards** while two men go round the hearths asking *are you on this list / have you been on it since the autumn / have you been asked before this, by anybody, in any year* and the second man writes the answer in his own book and shuts it. It takes a minute and a half a house, nobody makes a copy, and it is the only civic work in this county nobody has had to invent a form for — and **he is the only kind of man anybody here has ever been glad to see: a man who comes into a shed and asks nobody anything for nine hours.** On the evening of that day a kiln-man brings a barrow over the Ninebridge with a fired tile under his arm, five lines pressed in a hand that is not a clerk's and not a die's, in three sizes of writing because line two is forty-one words, cut with a broken piece of a saw and fired in a rolling a week old. **She makes him read it out, and her reason is the batch's best argument for itself: a county that has never refused anything has never had a reason to withhold a name, and she has never read out a thing a room gave her, and a stranger in a room in this county is the only thing that has ever been given a thing and not been the person it was given to — and if he says it, then in nine years a clerk in another county will have a copy of what he said, in his words, in a file.** The five terms, printed: *1. The road is not to be made to carry a person. 2. No name is to be read out on the far side of the line by anybody from the north, and no name is to be written in the line's book at the north end. 3. The north is not to send anybody who can be asked to come and look. 4. A person who is struck may answer, and if a person who is struck answers, the person is restored. 5. The road is kept by a hand at each end and a witness at each end, and the witness at Ash's end is chosen by Ash.* **On a tile and in a book, because a tile cannot be copied out of and a book can, and the county has understood in four years what a woman in a fen has understood in eleven months and he does not make the comparison for her, and the tile is the one that cannot be lifted.** The mechanism, on the page, and it is not magic: to restore a struck person the roll must be re-read, at the person, with the book in the room and the tile on the table and two witnesses whose names are in the book, **so term four is a room, and a book in it, and two hundred people to go through it one at a time, and the whole of it resting on one book in one room in one building.** He gets the tile half and she gets the room half and neither of them puts the two together, and he says the sentence that has to be said: **it is not a sentence, it is a room, with a book in it, and two hundred people to go through it, and the whole of it resting on one book in one room in one building.** Then the notebook, on the low stool at the end of the rack, which is the volume's most damning page: the parts of the working set out in the trade's own vocabulary — **anchor, the roll, one book, one room, one building; witness, the person answering and two whose names are in the book, and the rule that a witness is whoever would have refused, and here there is nobody who would have refused, and she said no in one word in front of him, and a witness that is also the anchor is not a witness, it is a second reading of the same hand; counterweight, the county, and the cost of a week of wedging and forty men losing a day; bound, the promise that these two hundred are the people**, and a bound has to be the narrowest thing you can make it, the width of the split, the length of the timber, and this one is the whole of the county. **A bound is a promise, and a promise about two hundred people to one office is a shape he has had refused before him by a man in a room in another county on a paper about a number of persons in a place that could not be asked a question, and he has half of the other half of that and has decided, in writing, on the fourth day of a week in a clay county four hundred miles off, to leave it alone, and has written the decision down so that a clerk in Merefen can see it was a decision and not an oversight.** And the figure: **he does not know what a person weighs in a working, he has set a deck for forty tons on a hand's width of oak twice in his life, he is going to say two tons each, two hundred of them is four hundred tons, and four hundred tons into a bed of river gravel at Ninebridge is on the right side of four hundred and is the correct order of size for a thing he does not understand — and A DOCUMENT'S LOAD IS NOT IN TONS, I KNOW THAT, I HAVE WRITTEN IT IN TONS ANYWAY, THAT IS THE SENTENCE TO READ IN FOUR MONTHS' TIME, and in four months, or thereabouts, which is the same thing at this rate, and I have not done the sum because the sum is in two calendars and I have not got both of them, and the one I have got does not have a week in it.** And then: **I am going to build it anyway, that is written down as well, because I have written the mistake down before and read it and gone on, and it is the second time, and the only new thing in this book today is that it is the same mistake both times and noticing it has never once stopped me, and a man who has noticed a thing twice is not being careful, he is being consistent.** And the honest end: on the first morning she asked him a question and he gave her the correct answer and she told him to get off her kerb, and it cost her nothing to be right and it cost him the road, and he has not gone back to check, because a man who goes back to check is a man asking to be let in. He closes the book and does the bottom of the rack. On the twenty-second day the sittings sat and the list was a year old and **was not burned**, because the clerk of the roll was asked in the magistrate's own room and said no, and her reason was not the fever and not the four pages: **two hundred and six tiles broken and wedged back is a day and a half of a wedging shed, and in about eleven years somebody who is not her is going to have to fire a roll for a person who has been struck and put back, and she would like the county to have spent the day and a half.** On the twenty-third day, on the bridge, a second tile in the barrow: small, four by three, a hand laid flat on a book, and it has been on the coping since before she could walk and both men who might have known are one dead and one who did not know. She has taken it down and will not give it to a magistrate, because a room is where a thing like that ends up. **He does not say the name of the mark, because he has it in a notebook from a paper of the eighth of the last month and has never met the woman it belongs to, and if he uses it now the refusal she gave him on the first morning turns out to be a thing he was holding the answer to since the morning before it, and there is no worse way to lose a refusal than to arrive at it already holding the key.** He says it is a good tile and a well-pressed one and that the face is true and has not been re-pressed since it was made, and tells her to put it back on the coping, **and that it is not doing any harm where it is and there is a woman in this county who has known about it for forty-one years and has never once asked anybody to take it down, and she is right.** The coping is cold and has no warmth in it whatever, and that is a fact he writes down about a hand and a habit four months later and does not know is a fact yet.

**Ch 70 · the fifth of Wolfmonth, a Tuesday · Mara, and the return.** In the room over the chandler at the north end at the fifth hour, one room doing two rooms' work, and on the desk under the day's work **a fair copy in her own hand, with the hour on it, of the four lines that came into the outer room on the twenty-ninth of Longlight** — the man who fires at the kiln at Kilnholt, struck eleven months, has not gone anywhere, is at his kiln, and cannot be found because a man who is struck cannot be asked anything — entered on the second of Fallowmonth at the eleventh hour against no person and **sent to nobody**, because a man can enter a claim against that office and be answered in a court and a county cannot be served. He comes in by the lane door at the sixth hour with a fever and grey about the gills and gets eight feet and takes hold of the end of the table. He is given his bag's shape and the grey clay in its seam and she does not ask. **He gives her every number before she asks and in that order: twenty-six days from the fourth hour of the ninth of Embermonth to this morning; twenty-three days on the far side, three rollings and part of a fourth and two low waters; the printed three-week warning; **the absence and the warning as the pair and not the path interval, with a sentence saying he did not sum the pair he says out loud first — the sum is five days**; and that he has had it in a notebook since the seventh of the last month in front of four men who did not understand one word of it** — and the last year's was minus fifteen, and he has not moved it, and he asks for one true thing he predicted, and she enters it as a prediction and tells him a prediction entered in advance is worth about a third. He enters his own figures on the office's own account, against no person, and under it: *three figures of six are his account and are not verified by this office, which has no means of verifying them and did not ask, and the reason this office did not ask is entered elsewhere and has a date on it.* Then the four lines, and **he reads them twice and does not go white and does not make a noise, and she watches a man read four lines about a man he had stood eleven inches from in a shed and be told nothing about** — "That is the county" / "That is a copy" / "You have not told me that" / "No, and I have known since the third day and I did not know it until the sixth hour this morning, and I would like that entered in the same book with the hour on it." And he has had one question for her since Thursday and does not ask it, because he has used up his asking in this county. **She asks her one — two documents that happened twice in two countries and are written down once — and apologises in plain words for it, because a man who is asked a question wants one he can take.** **Tovan Kest comes at the fourth hour and does not knock, and shuts a door he has not shut in four years, and will not let his brother out of the chair and will not help him and says both are on purpose.** The yard: **four weeks and a day on the ninth, spoken for, is twenty-nine days; less the twenty-six he was gone is three days; a wall job taken on the seventh off the Fallow Elms road paying in wood and not money, because four men cannot be fed on three days' work, so there are eleven days** — and he did the sum in the yard at about the second hour before he came up, and put it in the book with the hour, and did not write his brother's name against it for the first time in a year, **because the yard's book holds a thing set and that is a thing set.** And he says it out loud: *that is not about you, and I have just told you the arithmetic of it and there is not one word of that arithmetic about whether I forgive you.* **The Sedge Reach:** the scour under the nose of the third pier is a washtub — **and the chapter says eight weeks, which the Volume 02 close audited and left standing: the scour is a washtub from the sixth of Mudmonth and this chapter is the fifth of Wolfmonth, so most of a year is the truth. Known defect, continuity item 264; do not re-derive it and do not treat it as a fresh error, and do not repair it in a later phase** — the repair is eleven tons of stone and a fortnight shut, nobody has read it since the ninth of Embermonth, the bench set the reading for the ninth and it did not happen, set it again for the twenty-fourth and waited until dark and it did not happen then either, **it is eleven days past that, and a bench of nine that has named a day twice and had it twice is going to name a third, and the third will be taken alone by the guild's man with the levels and Tovan will have to believe it.** And the copying post: **the county's surveyor's line, dated the third, not a letter, saying the post is not held and a man is to be put in it, not naming who, not stating a day, and not saying the three Fridays — and the three Fridays is nine shillings that is in the county's hands since the seventeenth of Fallowmonth and there is no post for it to be in.** She enters the want, and says she is not taking the thing he actually asked for and will not enter a want where she has a fact. And then the block's close, and it is in a day-book and not in a file, on the page with nothing against it and no reason on it, and the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth's line, and the eighth of Embermonth's line: **she has seen the way he stands** — weight back, hands loose at his sides and not in his pockets, a man coming up a slip when the deck is moving, and he has been doing it since the ninth of Embermonth and has not noticed, and she has watched a man do it on a slip from her window for four years, and there is nobody else in this county who would have seen it — **and she is not going to ask him, and she wants it written down that she knew for a month before she did anything about it**; **a protection is a thing that is done to somebody and goes in a file, and this is a day-book over a weigh-house in a room with a door on a lane at four pence a year, and what is in it now is that she has seen a thing about a man that he has not told her, and the reason he has not is four inches above the line** — so it is not a protection any more, it is a thing she is doing so that nothing has to be said by either of them, **and the difference between those two is about two months and she has been doing this one for about two months, and about two months is how long it took her to write it down instead of doing it** — and she is not going to ask him, and this entry is not a rescission of the promise, and in about nine years a man may read this page and say she had the fact and sat on it, **and the answer will be on this page: because I promised, and because I have noticed that I stopped doing it for him and started doing it for me, and a promise that has quietly turned into a thing I want is not a promise, and I am not going to break it, and I am not going to pretend that I have not noticed that I have stopped noticing. And that is the last line I am going to write about it.**

## Batch 0008 — Volume 02, Chapters 71–80, "Ordinary Work in Merefen"

Block 3. POVs **Tovan Kest ×2 (71, 74) / Mara Vey ×2 (72, 76) with Rowan Petch and Marda Oakes inside hers / Aren Kest ×6 (73, 75, 77, 78, 79, 80) with Lysa Fenn in 73.** **No System display, no gate, no stage, no revelation, no melody cost.** The word *Gatehouse* is not spelled out anywhere in this batch; the phrases *Culling Order* and *custodian* do not appear; the word *Bind* does not appear; **the private-keeper clause is not mentioned, referred to or explained by anybody, and the marginal instruction at the foot of the R.C. series is referred to as printed and is not read out.** **Four new names against the cap of six and two slots unfilled — Gwil Trewhella, Marda Oakes, Sennet Roil, Ivet Salter.** No named supporting character dies; no unnamed one is arranged into a death; no arch goes down and no glass breaks.

**Main world, Chapters 71–77, and the day map is now closed for the block.** Ch 71 the **ninth of Wolfmonth, a Saturday, a market day, the fourth hour** · Ch 72 the **fifteenth, a Friday, not a market day** · Ch 73 the **twenty-first, a Thursday, not a market day** · Ch 74 the **fourth of Frostmonth, a Wednesday, a market day, the bench and the second asking** · Ch 75–76 the **ninth of Frostmonth, a Monday, a market day, the election at the fourth hour and the count in the afternoon** · Ch 77 the **fourth of Hearthmonth, a Friday, a market day, the crossing out at the fourth hour.** The county's market days are Saturday and Thursday, and **the ninth and the fourth of Frostmonth and the fourth of Hearthmonth are markets because the chain gives them to be and not because a chapter wanted one; the fifteenth and the twenty-first of Wolfmonth are Fridays and Thursdays and are not markets, and no chapter in this batch wants a day.** Two dates that are facts about earlier chapters and are not free: **the copying post pays weekly in arrears and the surveyor's line is dated the third of Wolfmonth** (Ch 71, Ch 76) and **the yard job off the Fallow Elms road was taken on the seventh of Wolfmonth, a Thursday** (Ch 71, as the wall in the yard and the three carts of ash). Ch 78–80 are **path time in the County of Ash, the first twenty-two weeks**, dated only by **the rolling, the two low waters, and the year counted from the first firing** — the fourth week, the eleventh week, the twenty-second week — and **no main-world weekday is used anywhere in them.**

**The two eighth ruled lines, kept apart and never joined, and the city is still not on the served schedule.** In **Chapter 74** the guild's maintenance specification of two leaves has a second leaf of **eight standing conditions whose eighth line is a seat** — *that a seat so cut will be used and will fail, and that the number of uses is not stated here, because it is not a thing this bench may know* — and **the guild's man with the levels does not read the eighth, and gives his reason in his own words, and has it entered with his reason and not the bench's.** In **Chapter 76** the served schedule of the eighth of Fallowmonth is on Mara Vey's desk with **eight ruled lines, the eighth of them the city's and empty**, and she puts her hand flat on the weight over it and takes it away. **The two documents are in different rooms, in different chapters, in front of different people, and no character in this batch puts them together, refers to the other, or wonders about either.** The specification's eighth line is about a seat and there is no seat; the schedule's eighth line is the city's and there is no city. Both decisions are entered as decisions.

**The count, and the two numbers in it, and they are not the same count.** **Eleven on the fourth of Embermonth and eleven in the return that goes to the province on the ninth of Frostmonth; twelve in the guild hall on the ninth of Frostmonth at about a quarter past four, because Bram Ottery was put on the roll and elected and the roll went from thirty-one names to thirty-two.** The reeve reads both and enters both with the date of each and enters that **they are not the same count, because one is a count of persons and one is a count of names on a roll, and if a clerk in two hundred years adds one to the other it will be a good sum of two true things and a lie.** Nobody in the room can prove that a count which goes up by one when a man is given a vote is a count measuring the wrong thing, and neither chapter pretends otherwise.

**The money in this batch, added in pence and footed, per the standing rule in item 145.** The Old Toll visit, Ch 74: **lime, four bushels at one shilling and sixpence, 72d · sand and oak-dust for the bed, 36d · tarred hemp, forty yards at twopence, 80d · nails and cramps for the shuttering, 18d · three days of the guild's man with the levels and a boy at the bench's day rate of ninepence, 27d. Total 233 pence, which is nineteen shillings and fivepence, which is four marks three shillings and fivepence.** Against a chest of **two marks (96d)** and a year's income of **four marks and six (264d)**, with **two marks (96d) of subscriptions promised in notes from two men** — in hand four marks (192d), and **short by forty-one pence, which is three shillings and fivepence.** Ash's extra season, Ch 78: **two hundred and six tiles at one in three fit to keep is six hundred and eighteen into the kiln · about forty fit to a rolling is fifteen rollings wanted against the five the county has done every year for nineteen years, which is where "three times the tiles" comes from · fifteen rollings at nine days and a cord a day is a hundred and thirty-five cords, against forty-five cut in a season, so ninety cords more · ash at the kiln gate at two shillings and sixpence the cord, 2,700 pence, fifty-six marks and one shilling · the cutting of ninety cords, about forty men one day at five pence, 200 pence, four marks and eightpence. Total 2,900 pence, which is sixty marks one shilling eightpence.** The farthing of salt is Chapter 89's price and is not paid in this batch. **No Volume 02 figure has been derived from the Chapter 10 column by scaling it, and the eleven marks and fourpence and the fifty marks one shilling sixpence are not set beside any figure in this batch.**

**Ch 71 · the ninth of Wolfmonth, a Saturday, a market day, the fourth hour · Tovan Kest.** **The volume's labour problem stated in a yard with four men in it, and the chapter's spine is a sum being corrected on the page in front of them.** The book said **eleven days at the second hour of the fifth of Wolfmonth**; the wall off the Fallow Elms road was re-spoken for four days at the fifth hour of the seventh, and went to **fifteen**; **Pei Tarrow, 52, asked at the first hour of the eighth to be put off two days instead of three, which took one day off, and the figure is a fortnight** — **eleven, fifteen, fourteen, with the hour against each, and Tovan reads all three out and says a yard that gains four days without anybody being able to say where they came from is a yard that has stopped counting, because a yard that has stopped counting is being run on somebody's memory.** **Tarrow's asking is not a plea and he says why in his own voice, in the gateway, in front of four men: he has a barge on the western channel and a market day, he has been called a symbol for eleven years and has no book and no list and no dead, and he wants freight; and he does not want the day, he wants the day off the yard's board and to be somewhere else with it. The review repair pass put him in the room. He was reported speech before and the card asks for a recurring face and not a symbol.** Eleven cords of ash-wood in the corner by the gate, being the price of a wall, and **one man at the drawknife says ash is good, that a trade four hundred miles off would give a good price for it by the cord, and asks nobody what trade, and no one in the chapter acts on it, and nobody in the chapter knows why it matters** — it is the fuel of a thing that has not been built yet and is not in this county. **Gwil Trewhella, 39, a carrier on the western channel, comes in at half past three** with the story he has had in four doorways — *they've got a man, and he's off in the north, and nobody knows when he's back, and I am asking whether somebody is minding it* — and gets the yard's whole argument and no day, and the answer to a man is not a date but the number of lines in the book's week and the hour the last form was read, **because a road is a promise and a promise is not a trade and a yard cannot answer for one.** Then **a man comes up Weir Street on foot with a form in a leather bag: a ruled table wanting a county, then the hundred, then a figure, then four words under the figure, *how the figure was compiled*, and no column and no box for a name anywhere on the sheet, and no date on it.** Tovan reads the small print on the back because he reads the small print, and answers anyway, **because a form with no date on it does not have to be answered and an undated form that names nobody can be put through any door in the kingdom without asking the man at the door** — the number of the lines is nine, the hour is the fourth, and **against the space for a figure he enters nil, and nil is a figure, and it is the only figure in that county that cannot be asked about a person.** He tells the man not to write *very good* in a book he cannot get at. And he says out loud, in the gateway, on a market day, that he has not asked his brother where he goes and has not sent a word, which he has never said in that yard before. **He also says the answer is the same answer a woman in a filing room two hundred miles off has been making out of a press for four years, that he did not get it from her and she did not get it from him and he has never seen her face, and that if the two of them got it out of the same place then there is a third thing that knows and neither of them has met it.**

**Ch 72 · the fifteenth of Wolfmonth, a Friday, not a market day · Mara Vey, and Rowan Petch inside her room.** One room over a weigh-house doing two rooms' work, a copyist reading a shelf register aloud for pleasure, and **a twenty-six-year-old clerk of the provincial court who has been back three days from three days on the road at his own cost and files the return on the twelfth.** The return is printed in full: **four facts about a box, and then the sentence that is the whole instrument — the court cannot state the purpose for which the file is held, and the court does not decline to state it, and if the office holds a purpose then the office is the holder of it and the office is the only body in this kingdom that can state its own purpose in its own hand under its own seal, and this court requests that it do so, and will then answer it.** Sarn Oyelaran sees in eleven seconds that there is no reading of it that is comfortable, which is the whole of the trick, and that she has not seen it done in nine years. **Petch's own sentence about registers is his and not Halven's and is a worse one than Halven's, and it is *the press register is the register of what a man said he did with a shelf*; Mara tells him it is now hers, in her room, on her paper, and he does not argue.** And **the flat line — a register answers what was entered on the shelf and nothing else — is not spent here and is Ivet Salter's in Chapter 79.** He put *I have met both* in the second position on purpose, because a sentence in the first position is the point and a sentence in the second position is an address, and a man in a hurry reads an address. **His want is unchanged and in his own words: to have been careful once while somebody was watching, and being careful when nobody is watching turning out to be the cheapest thing a young man can do with his life.** Then the bomb with a slow fuse: **on the twelfth, in the outer room, a clerk reading a column out loud got to a county and said *this one, the file, has a person in it now*, went on to the next county, and four seconds later said a name — Sabin Dain, thirty-three, eleven years in the office, an aid to the register, not a member of anything, who told him so twice in a corridor — and the office has entered her into the county file in the matter of the crossing of Alder Vale as the person having knowledge, in her own hand, on the fourth of Embermonth.** A name in a county file is a person who can be produced. **Mara looks at the name, asks him for no further fact about the woman, and has her own not-asking entered with the reason: a keeper who holds five facts about a person begins assembling them, and a keeper who assembles facts about a person is a person with an interest in a person.** Then **Dain's second letter**, dated **the twelfth**, arrived on the fifteenth, three days in the bag, **signed** — a woman's writing, not a clerk's initial — **asking for a figure of persons in the county and the four words under the figure, returnable on or before the ninth of Frostmonth.** Mara's recognition is that it is a very good instrument. **The gap between the day a thing is dated and the day it arrives is the only fact in her business she has never been able to do anything about, and it was three days in Fallowmonth and it is three days in Wolfmonth and it will be three days in Greenmonth.** The day's book entry: a claim number against the office and against no person; **a figure is not a list, and a list with the names taken off is a figure, and this office has said that in a room of forty people and in this book and has now said it to an office of the Crown in writing;** no opinion entered; **the not writing the opinion entered, for the third time this year, because the not writing is the part of it that is a decision.**

**Ch 73 · the twenty-first of Wolfmonth, a Thursday, not a market day · Aren Kest and Lysa Fenn.** The moot hall, nine years, a clerk with his back to her on purpose, and **a form dated the twelfth of Greenmonth that has been on her table since the fifteenth without an answer on it and expires on the ninth of Frostmonth, eighteen days away.** She sent for him to get his account of the Crown's man in Weir Street, in the words he used in the yard. **The first time in this book a technicality of her own trade beats an instrument: a returnable paper has a party; this paper names no person and no body; it names a county; a county is not a party and cannot sue or be sued and has no officer that can take a return; therefore the paper has no party, a paper with nobody in default at the end of its own stated period has not been answered and has not been refused, and it expires — not refused, refused is an act and an act can be appealed.** And the office will have to issue another one, which will have to name a person or name an office, **and if it names an office the office has to exist, and a county court that has searched the charter, the county list and the Crown's schedule of provincial offices has found no such office and has said so in a finding of the fourth of Greenmonth with a date on it — and the fifth clause of that finding says any office thereafter acting in the matter is on notice that this court asked and was not answered, and the asking is dated.** And nobody in that building has ever turned to the second page. **She is quietly delighted and frightened in the same hour, and she says both, in that order, and says she has decided the order because a woman who is frightened first and delighted afterwards has arranged her own feelings.** The fright, written down in full: **she has just built the best answer in this county out of the fact that this court is not a person, and in about four years somebody with a good head and a clean sheet is going to make this office a person, and then a person can be a party and can be in default and can be fined and can be shut, and the answer dies; and there is no procedure in that office for withdrawing anything, so an endorsement once given stands for ever, while a magistrate can only be removed by a petition of the county, which takes two years and a majority — so the answer is four years, and a woman can be removed in an afternoon by a man in a room, and she has nine of the other kind behind her and would take four more.** Her want, in her own voice, unchanged: **that the law be useful to people who cannot afford a title or a patron, and she names the mechanism of the last year's instrument — a writ of enquiry is lawful and cheap and needs nobody's name and does not matter what is in the box, and this county has four people who read notices and three of them work for this office.** And **the eleven-year-old salvage filing**: a request in writing and signed, received on the eighteenth, asking the court to state whether it holds any record relating to a declared object in the matter of Alder Vale in YR 301 in the matter of salvage — **and she refuses, in writing, once, and puts the reason on the file, because a filing of eleven years standing in that matter is the only account a certain person has of a certain thing and this court is not going to be the office that tells a stranger it is there**, and she tells him in advance, in the room, that this is the fourth time and that if it comes a fifth time she will answer it, and she has told him now so that the fifth is not a surprise either. **He has been told three times in advance on purpose and is not surprised, and says nothing, twice, and both times correctly.** The chapter closes on a woman who won a morning on a technicality and knows exactly what it cost, and on a man who has been the best reader of a room in this case for about a year and a half — **and who watches her be delighted and frightened and understands, in about the length of a fire ticking eleven times, that this is what competent looks like when it is not him, and says none of it, because a sentence said in that room at the fourth hour of the twenty-first of Wolfmonth would be in a file in nine years in somebody else's hand, and she has the finding and the paper and the refusal.**

**Ch 74 · the fourth of Frostmonth, a Wednesday, a market day · Tovan Kest, in the room and not in it.** **The recovery anchor: ordinary work, in daylight, in a hard frost, with a cold chisel.** And the reason a bench of nine is on a bridge at the fourth hour in the first hard frost of the winter instead of in the summer is that **a chisel that is warm rings and a man cutting a course can hear whether he has found a natural bed or has made one, and in a frost the iron is dead, so the fourth course of the Old Toll can honestly be re-set once a year by nine men who are not a bridgewright's crew.** The **specification of two leaves**; the first leaf is the work; the second is the guild's standing conditions, of which there are eight, **and the eighth is a seat, and the guild's man with the levels does not read it, and gives his reason, and has it entered in his own words and not the bench's** — because a leaf that is reprinted is a leaf that has had a line taken off it, and in nine years somebody will want to know what the line said, and because there is no seat and **the number of uses of a seat that is not there is not a thing this bench may know either.** The forty minutes are on the fifth line of the first leaf, and the man at the fish weir wants the opening at the joint left open the width of a hand, **because a dressed stone in a splice is a lie about the fact that the stone moved, and there should be one place in this town where a man can stand on a bridge and look at a mark and know the mark is where the thing was** — and the guild's man with the levels holds the rule for two hours and shows him it is not a weep, it is a bed of shells gone out of the second course about eleven years ago, and both men are partly right, and the bench does the work, courses dressed and re-set and the opening left, **and the man at the fish weir asks that the foundry's man vote against it out loud so that it is in the book that somebody disagreed, and he does.** Then **the second asking**, put by the reeve and minuted as the second: **that the materials for the visit be found, four marks three shillings and fivepence against two marks in a chest, found by subscription and not by rate, because a subscription is a thing a man can refuse in person and a rate is a thing a man finds out about in a quarter** — and she says the whole of her own interest in it, which is *I want it done and I do not care one way or the other who pays and I know what that makes me.* And **Tovan Kest says the true thing for the fourth time in six months, standing, with the level case down on the table because a man who has said a thing three times sitting down deserves to say the fourth one standing: a bench that is generous decides, a bench that is asked is being told what it is for, and a bench that is asked three times and says yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription — and he has watched it happen once, in Longlight, four hundred miles off, in a city whose name he will not say in a hall that keeps a roll, where a bench of nine said yes in the first month of a winter, yes again in the third, and yes a third time in the eighth, and by the ninth it was voting on a list made somewhere else, and every vote was unanimous and recorded as unanimous, and a book in that city has four years of unanimous minutes in it and not one disagreement in the whole of it.** It is anticipatory, the asking it speaks about has not happened, **no character in that room may call it the third**, and he asks that it be minuted today that he said the thing that is going to be necessary one day and that the day has not come. The minute is the shortest thing in the chapter and the only one anybody will read in nine years, and it ends: *The sum is short by three shillings and fivepence.*

**Ch 75 · the ninth of Frostmonth, a Monday, a market day, the fourth hour · Aren Kest.** **A bench that has never voted elects its officers for the first time in eleven years, and the reason is a column: the guild's own roll has four columns and the third is headed *Keeper*, put there three months ago because a form came down from two hundred miles off with a column with the same word at the top of it, and a column headed *Keeper* wants somebody against it, and a bench that appoints in private has a form and an answer that do not match.** The reeve first: **Aldis Fenn is re-elected, eight hands, and she does not vote on her own election and gives the reason — a reeve has a casting vote when the bench is level and a casting vote is not a vote, and she has said that sentence in that hall every year for nine years in the same words, because a woman who improves her own reasons is a woman who has noticed she is being asked twice — and the minute is the shortest in the roll: *Reeve elected, eight hands. The reeve did not vote on her own election. Reason entered.*** Nothing to write in the *against* column, and the bench-clerk enters the word *none* against *against* and says it is the first time in nineteen years he has had nothing to write there. Then the roll: **Bram Ottery, forty-seven, nineteen years in the county, asks to be on it, and asks for the vote and not the office — a man who lost his post and nine months and three weeks for a true thing he brought in unasked would like to have a vote while he can still hold a pen** — and the guild clerk reads the yard's entry twice, and the fourpence a week paid out of a private box and not drawn, **and enters that the arrangement is two months old — sixty-one days, the ninth of Embermonth to the ninth of Frostmonth — and has not been improved on**, and the foundry's man objects to a man being given a vote for a speech, and four against, four for, one abstention, and the reeve's casting vote. **And the count is read out again in that hall, because the reeve reads it at every election now and nobody has ever asked her to stop, and it is eleven, and it is eleven again, and a minute later it is twelve, because a man was given a vote, and nobody in the room can prove that is the wrong thing to count and everybody in the room can see it, and the reeve enters both numbers with the date of each and enters that they are not the same count.** Then the warden. **Road warden of the Merefen bridge guild, carrying the Sedge Reach, the wharf road and the winter work; the man at the fish weir declines in nine words; the guild's man with the levels nominates himself and withdraws twice so the clerk enters it twice; and the man at the drawknife, who is not a member and brought a level case, nominates Aren Kest — not for the refusals, but because we have one man in this county who can read where the load goes and nobody has ever asked him whether he would, and I would rather be refused in front of nine men than find out in nine years that we never asked.** Four hands. Four against. Level, and the casting vote, and the reeve's face does not change. **And he says the thing that is in the roll and that the volume ends on: I accept the vote and not the office. A vote is a thing a town gives a man, in a room, with a date on it; an office is a thing a man is held to. That is not the same, and I have watched them get confused in about four places, and every one of the four was a bench that had voted something into a man's name and then, in a worse year, found out that a vote is a thing you are given and an office is a thing you answer for, and the answering is the part that is not given. I will come when I am wanted, and I will read anything put in front of me, and I will say the true thing, and I will not vote, and I will not hold the crossing, and I do not intend to be the man who holds this crossing.** And the third thing, two seconds late: **a bench seat, a wage, a fourth camp and a master's hut refused in a hall in eleven seconds, and one warden of a bridge guild by eight hands and a reeve's — same word, *accepted*; a refusal had cost him nothing, the price was on the man afterwards and the price was a bench that had learned his name was worth a vote, and he noticed it and said nothing, because a man who argues in a room that he ought to be disqualified from a vote has asked the room to do his arithmetic, and a room that does a man's arithmetic has stopped being a room and become a clerk.** The roll stands at thirty-two and the name on it that was not there in Fallowmonth is not his. And at half past four **the man at the fish weir asks the question nobody asked — the road wants a witness at this end, the witness is the man just put on the roll, a man on the roll cannot witness a thing the roll's own bench is voting on, and there is a bill coming that wants the road shut a fortnight — and the reeve says nothing and her clerk enters that the reeve said nothing, and the minute ends there.**

**Ch 76 · the ninth of Frostmonth, the afternoon · Mara Vey.** **The count reaches the province.** The return goes at the sixth hour to the county clerk and not to the office, because a thing put in the county's bag sits in the county clerk's basket for a week, **and Mara stands at the lane door for longer than a woman stands at a door and then shuts it and stands against the inside of it, which she had not done in four years.** The sheet: **county Merefen, persons eleven, how compiled stated at length — read out in a hall on the fourth of Embermonth at the fourth hour in the presence of forty persons with a date and an hour, entered in the day-book against no person; of the eleven, four appear in a file of that office and seven do not; the figure contains all four and names none of them; the figure is a figure and is not a list and was not made by taking a list and stopping, and the order of the eleven is the order in which they stood up, which is not an order** — and **the column headed *Keeper* is left empty and no reason is entered.** The day-book entry is the volume's political victory and it is in the record: a claim number against the office and against no person, no opinion, and the not writing the opinion entered for the third time this year. Then **Marda Oakes, 45, of the county town, a member of the county council, comes up two flights of a stair over a weigh-house and asks whether the office is a business and is told it is and leaves her card.** She has read the two writs of enquiry, of the ninth and the twenty-ninth of Longlight, and separately the finding, which is a different document and does not pretend to be the same one; **she wants the Archive to be a corporation and she says so in the first minute and has three reasons in the wrong order**; **she says the half — that a county council can write to a keeper and be told no, and a woman who said no can be told she said no, and the county has no remedy** — **and then refuses on purpose to finish the sentence, and says why, and the reason is the volume's whole last move: you are not an office yet, and when you are one, everybody who helped you will be a person who helped you.** The other half, in her own words, is that the finding of the fourth of Greenmonth is the most carefully made document she has ever read, it has been read by four people and three of them work for this office, **it is a notice, a magnificent notice, and a notice does not stop anybody — it only stops people who read them, and there are four of those in this county and three of them are yours.** She leaves a card with a name, a county and a room and no seal and no office, and it is the only time in four years Mara has entered a card against a person. **She does not ask one question about the file. She is the first person in about a year to come into that room and not want a name out of it, and neither Mara nor Sarn can work out whether that is a kindness or a method.** And **the three lines.** In the day-book, unopened for twenty-five days because it asked nothing of this office: dated at the place of issue **the thirteenth of Wolfmonth, a Wednesday** — four days after a carpenter wrote nil on the back of a week's wage sheet in Weir Street — in a woman's writing, signed: *Received. The office notes that a count of persons is not a list and proposes a count of persons for the county as a whole.* Set beside her own return of eleven. **Nobody in the room sees what it is, and the day-book says so: a count of persons who have had knowledge of a declared object is a different thing from a count of the persons in a county; this office has no instrument that can touch a figure for a county; the county's schedule of provincial offices has been searched and does not carry one; and this office never asked for a figure for a county — it did not occur to me, it occurred to them on the thirteenth of Wolfmonth, and I am the only person in this county who has read both documents and I do not see anything in them.** **He is twenty-five yards from that door and will not be in the county in three weeks**, because a letter in a file in the guild hall over the wharf, made at a bench on the ninth of Embermonth, binds him to a bench of bridgewrights four hundred miles off, and the only two people in the county who know it are the two who made it, and one is not a member of any bench and the other does not vote. And the served schedule on the desk under a weight, **eight ruled lines, the eighth the city's and empty**, and a hand laid flat on the weight above it for about four seconds as a way of not doing it.

**Ch 77 · the fourth of Hearthmonth, a Friday, a market day, the fourth hour · Aren Kest, and the crossing out.** **The volume's set piece, prepared properly, for the first time with a far end that has written terms.** A line of four fathoms made up at the third hour in nine minutes with the hour against each of the nine; the five terms read out at the reeve's request from a fair copy by the toll-holder's clerk, **and the second line read twice at the boy's asking, and the second line is forty-one words long and it is the reason a meeting on a market morning in a fen county is quiet, because there is a thing everybody in that yard wants to do and the county wrote down that it is not to be done and the county's word is the gate.** The bench sat at the fourth hour because of a bill that is not until the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth and wants the road shut a fortnight, and it had one item of ordinary business with a date on it: that the road be kept, as it is kept, on this day. **Eight hands and the reeve's, and the reeve voted, and the clerk entered *the reeve voted* without comment and read it back and nobody asked him to.** Which is why she is in the yard with a sheet and a rule, and why everybody looked at her when the witness line was read out, **and why she refuses the fourth line, in the same words as the ninth of Embermonth, word for word, and asks that the reason be minuted in the words she is about to use and not in better ones, because if it is minuted in better ones then in nine years a man will read that a reeve of Merefen had a reason and it will not be her reason: a road this town does not own cannot have this town as its witness, and if it has, then in four years somebody will say the town stood there, and the town will be right and the road will be shut, and the road will have been shut by a signature and not by an argument, and an argument is a thing that can be had again on a different day by a different bench with a different temper in it.** The man at the drawknife said nothing this time; hearing it twice is a different thing from hearing it once. **She will hold the sheet.** The bell at a quarter to the fourth, the boy of fifteen alone, leaned on the third stroke, the sill under his feet the whole time and cold. Then the line goes out, and **the yard stands there, because there is no gate at this end and nothing whatever happens at this end until the far end decides to open it, and the gate is five lines pressed in wet clay in a county that is not on any map in this kingdom, and a county that has never refused anything in its life is going to take its time.** It took about four minutes. And **the one sentence in the yard that was not about a road**, said by Tovan at the gate with his hand on the rope: *The yard is a fortnight. When you come back it will be a fortnight or it will not be, and if it is not then I will have had to put a man off, and it will be Tarrow, and I will not be able to pay him and he will not be able to ask me. I am telling you now so that on the day you come back and I say nothing about it, you will not think it was your brother.* Then he comes up out of it on clay steps with the flat yellow light and the smell of nine days of a kiln, **and the road opens, and the far end of the line is a woman with a roll of tile-cutters' tools at her hip and a person the county chose, and neither of them said a name, because the second line is forty-one words long and the not writing went into the yard's book as a fact and not as a want.** He asks one question, because she offers one, and it is about the tile on the coping, and she gives him the answer he already has — forty-one years, his grandfather's grandfather or nobody, the man dead since the eighth of the last rolling — **and says she has never once asked anybody to take it down, and that he has known what it is since the first morning and did not say, and that this is the whole of what has happened between them in four months and she would like it to go on being the whole of it.** **Nine months and six days.** And about four hundred people in a county that has never once refused anything in nineteen years went to work without one of them ringing a bell, **because there is no bell in this county and there never has been one, and nobody there has ever had a reason to know it.**

**Ch 78 · path time, Ash, the fourth week · Aren Kest.** **The long middle's first beat: a room, and a price, and nobody says *we are right* or *you are wrong*.** The drying floor over the cart shed at the Ninebridge, ninety-one feet, two hundred people in it, and every one of them there because of what they are paid by. **Magistrate Colm Arreld is not in the room and has left a paper in the county's own book: he will not give that room an instruction and will not be asked for one in a room of two hundred people, because the last instrument he made is on the table untried, and a magistrate who makes a second thing before the first has been tried is a magistrate who has decided the first was wrong.** The man at the cutting bench gets up, says he is not going to say *we are right* or *you are wrong* because both are free, and costs it: **two hundred and six people, each needing one tile with their own right hand on the back; one tile in three comes out of a rolling fit to keep; so six hundred and eighteen go in; about forty fit to a rolling; so fifteen rollings, and this county has done five every year for nineteen years, and fifteen is three times five, and that is where three comes from and it is not a figure anybody has chosen.** Then the hand of the Ninebridge, forty-one, who said *useful and not a visitor* at the door of a cutting shed in his first week: **the ninety cords is not the price — the price is that the cutting is done in the same weeks as the digging, and a man cannot be in the ash with a saw in the fourth week of the rolling and have his own hearth burnt down in the same week, and in this county a hearth is what a fire is, and a fire is what a person is, and there is no column in this county for a person.** And: **a county that fires three times the tiles has to cut three times the wood in the same weeks as the digging, and about forty men lose a day, and the county cannot pay forty men for a day it has not got, and a county that cannot pay a man for a day does not go and beg — it puts him on a list; and the kilns can do that sum in a morning and the magistrate can do his in a year, and they are the same sum, and one has a law on it and the other has a price, and a price is not a law but a price is the one that gets kept.** Quist **refuses to read the roll out in the room**, for the second time in nineteen years, and gives the reason: the day I read that book out in a room is the day it is a thing that can be read out; **and refuses to give the county's figure of nine hundred and forty-one hearths, because the moment that number is said out loud in a room where the struck are standing it is a count of persons, and the county has never once had a count of persons; and says she has said *I do not know the number of men in this county* four times now and every time somebody has looked at her as though she had refused, and a keeper who puts a want in a cell where she has a fact is a mouth and not a cell, and she learned that from a man in another county in about a minute and a half.** The whole column is written on a board in the ash-driver's hand and the boy with the slate copies it, **including the blanks, which is what that boy does and is the only thing anybody has ever said about him** — including the last two lines, which are *men whose day is lost if it is done in the digging, about forty* and *men out if two kilns stand idle for a rolling, about forty*, **and the man at the cutting bench has it entered that the last two lines are not the same and that he does not want them added, because a man who loses a day and a man who loses a season are not the same man and a room that adds them has been told a story it likes.** It is on a page headed with a number nobody had ever put at the top of a page before, **and it is the first page in this county about people and not about tiles.** Last of the two hundred and six, **Ostry Vaile says the thing that is not a plea, and says it because he has spent a year being a plea in about nine rooms and knows what a plea sounds like from the inside: the two lines at the bottom of your column are not the same forty and I am the one man in this room who is in both of them, and I have not counted and am not going to, because a man who counts his own name is a man who has begun to be a list; I have not lost a day because a hod does not ask a man's standing, and if the cutting stops in the fourth week I will lose every day I have got, and then I will be a man who is struck and a man who is out, and there is nothing in this county that can tell the difference between those two things or anybody else either — and it is entered in the book by somebody who is not me, and I would like it entered now, while I am able to ask.** It is the second line on that page and the first entry in nineteen years in that office against a man by name that is not a removal.

**Ch 79 · path time, Ash, the eleventh week and after · Aren Kest.** **The long middle's second beat: the kilns close, and the third place is a creditor's press, and a woman of sixty-one in a province two hundred miles off says in a letter the sentence that decides it.** The ninety cords were not cut; there is no office in this county that can put forty men in a wood for a day and pay them for it; **and a kiln does not stop the way a trade stops: a charged rolling is nine days of clay and cannot wait, it goes back into the wedging shed, and the wedging shed is the scarcest room in the county and is the room the rollkeeper kept a list out of, in a corridor, nine years ago, for a day and a half.** She says it herself, on the bank of the shed with a hod in each hand, to about twenty men standing in the doorway because the floor will not take twenty pairs of boots, **that the reason she gave in that room was the correct reason on that day and reasons do not stay correct, and that she is not resigning, and that a rollkeeper who resigns is a woman who has said the county's records are not worth a room, and this county has had four of those in nineteen years and the county did not stop.** The two kilns stop in the twelfth week. **About forty men out — the number the ash-driver wrote in the fourth week and will not go past, because he will not write a number in a week he has not counted.** The two live kilns go to half and take twenty-seven of the forty. **The other thirteen do not go anywhere, and that is the only figure in the whole of this business that everybody in this county has agreed on without an argument, and the reason it is agreed is that it is the ugliest one: a struck man, an employed man, and a man whose kiln has shut are three different people and the room knows it and does not say so. A struck man is on a list and can be put back and has a tile in a box. An employed man has a hearth, a fire, a wage drawn at the end of a week, and two witnesses whose names are in a book, and is the only one of the three who can be asked a question in a room. A man whose kiln has shut is none of those and is not on anything at all, and there is no column in this county for him and never has been, and a man who is not on a column cannot be put on one, and a man who cannot be put on one cannot be given a vote, and a man who has not got a vote is not a witness, and a man who is not a witness cannot be restored.** Six of the thirteen nobody in the county knows what happened to, and they are six lines with nothing against them and no reason on them.
