In the sixteenth week **the letter comes**, brought by a person who carried papers and did not stop; four sheets; a seal carrying a hand laid flat on a book, cut off with a knife and dropped in the pit without a word; dated in a form this county has no word for, and the hand of the Ninebridge reads the date out once and says *we cannot check one word of that.* From the counting house of the tolls of Bellweather-Living: **a creditor's press is the worst place in the world to keep a roll of persons and the only place in the world where nobody can quietly take it out of the book; I have been arguing that for eleven years in a hall and I am now the man who is proposing it to my own city; I would like the argument minuted and the minute put where a Crown's man will find it in about nine years.** A press, a shelf, a farthing of salt, four sheets of conditions — **and the price of the shelf is not money: the price is the eleven sheets, a chain of eleven sheets that has lain in that counting house for eleven years for a creditor who did not want them, and a shelf cannot be given away for nothing because a shelf is the only thing in a counting house that cannot be argued with. And the eleven sheets are four hundred miles off and no one in this county can go and get them, because the county's terms forbid a name at this end of the line and the roads do not join. We have been offered a shelf and the price is a four-hundred-mile errand we are not allowed to make, and a promised hand is not a hand.** So in the seventeenth week the county wrote to the only office it had ever heard of that keeps press registers, **with no name in it, because the county is not going to be the first body in nineteen years to break a term it wrote itself**, and asked nine words: *Will a press hold what is put in it, and refuse to give it up?* The answer comes in eleven days to the Ninebridge and not to the assizes, because the assizes is an office and the Ninebridge is four hundred yards of kerb and a shed with a door on the side of it. **Ivet Salter, of the third press, thirty years, in a small flat hand that had been copying for thirty years: Yes. That is the whole of what a press does. It holds what is entered on the shelf and it refuses nothing and it gives nothing up, and a court cannot take a thing out of a press that belongs to somebody; a court can take it out of one that belongs to an office, and the difference between those two is whether there is a person at the end of it, and the person is what a register is for. A register answers what was entered on the shelf and nothing else. If you want a book that can be asked what a thing in it is for, then you want a court, and a court will give it to whichever of you asks loudest, and that is not a defect in the court. That is the trade.** — **the flat line, spent here and nowhere else, and it is why the third hand cannot be a registry press and must be a creditor's.** Above it, covering the whole page including four ruled cells at the foot with nothing in them, **a fair copy of a leaf of the fourth press's own shelf register, made by Sennet Roil, twenty-eight, who did not ask to be sent it and has a grandmother's habit of copying a whole leaf, and a whole leaf means the ruled cells at the bottom as well, and a copyist who leaves the blanks off is a copyist who has made an argument.** He gets to a county cell that is **ruled and empty** on a line with a number and a date on it, and stands at the rack with a wet cloth in his hand until the cloth goes cold, and **writes one line in the back of the notebook and says nothing to anybody: a cell, ruled and empty, in a copy of a shelf register in a press two hundred miles off; I do not know what it is; I have written down that I saw it and that I did not say anything, because a man who says a thing about a cell in a shed full of people is a man who has got a shape and no fact, and last time I acted on a shape with no fact under it I put a bell in a file four hundred miles off and I have not finished paying for that. And the woman of sixty-one has written that a register answers what was entered on the shelf and nothing else, and that is her office and not one thing more of it, and she says it as though it were a small thing.** The letter asked fifteen words about whether a press gives a thing up and **did not ask for the register; the office decided the answer to that question was its own register and sent a copy unasked**, and that is why a whole leaf with the ruled cells at the foot of it is in the same bag. Two sheets of paper in a drawer in a county that has never refused anything, and **the drawer is not locked, because in this county a drawer with a lock in it is a thing somebody has to have a key to, and a key is a name, and a name is the thing this county has decided it will not do.**

**Ch 80 · path time, Ash, the twenty-second week · Aren Kest.** **The three hands, agreed and not signed, and the rite written down and not said.** The long room at the assizes, which has held about nine people in nineteen years. **The two hands this county has are the two hands a person can be got at for** — the rollkeeper's office, one room in one building and it does not move; and the hand of the Ninebridge, which is not an office but a shed at the north end of a bridge with about four hundred soft tiles on boards under wet cloths and four hundred yards of fired tile set one to a kerb stone with the kiln's mark and a date on the back of each. **The third is promised in a letter, four hundred miles off, behind a price of eleven sheets this county cannot fetch — and nobody in ninety-one feet of drying floor pretended otherwise.** The magistrate reads Merrow's four pages out loud himself, at the speed of a man reading a document he will be asked about, and then says the thing that has been in the room since the seventeenth week: **he has had a physician's finding in a drawer for four years and a list with a law on it for nine years, and the reason he has not acted on either is that each is one instrument and every instrument he has made has had one good idea in it and the good idea has been a mark in the ground for the fever; he has been right about the fever eleven times and wrong about the names every time and has known it for two years; a stranger came off a road in the first week of a rolling and gave this county a second instrument, and he is glad of it and has been ashamed for about four years and both are true.** The argument is minuted whole at the officer's request. **And the bench tries to put the minute where a Crown's man will find it, and cannot, because there is no Crown's man in this county and there is no office, no schedule, no writ, no fee, no seal, no county in a column with a disposition word against it — and that is not because anybody here has hidden anything, it is because there is nobody here to look. So the request cannot be granted here and can only be granted where he is: he has to put it in his own city, in his own book, in his own hand, at his own cost, and then it will be in the world, and we cannot help him and we cannot stop him, and if he does not do it the argument is a thing a stranger said in a shed in a country that is not on any map, and in about nine years that is worth nothing.** The bench enters its own powerlessness in a book in a minute, **the first time any body in this county has done that in nineteen years, and the reason it did it is that the argument was about where a minute goes.** **Pernel Quist does not sign**, for two reasons, neither of them a matter of principle and both of them hers: **if she is one of the three then every restoration in this county is a thing a woman in Kilnholt did** — and she has known that since the sixteenth day of a rolling, when a stranger asked her a question in a room with a stove in it and she said no in one word and he wrote down the reason, and the reason was that she is the anchor; and **the third hand is four hundred miles off and behind a price, and a roll signed by two hands and a promise is not a roll in three hands, it is a roll in two hands with a third hand written on the end of it in a fair copy, and in about four years a magistrate is going to ask which of the three it is and the answer will be that one of them is a letter. I will not sign a thing that has a letter in it. I have spent nineteen years not signing things that have letters in them and I have been right about that every single time.** And: **I am not signing this week. I have not said no. Do not put it in the book that I said no, because I have said no twice in nine years and both times I was wrong, and a book is a worse place to be wrong than a corridor and a better place to be right, and I have not decided which this is.** It is the fourth page of the minute, the longest thing in it, read back, and she corrected one word and the word she corrected was *worse*. **He did not speak, and the clerk asked him why, and what he said was eight words: I have not got a trade for it.** The three askings are now distinguished on the page: he **answered** the first in a room with a stove and was right about what happens to a tile, he **fought and won** the second and got a week, and he said **nothing** on the third, which was four months earlier in a moot hall in a fen county. Then the notebook, at the low stool, in the flat hand, in the back, and it is the entry that matters and nobody in this county has read it and the reader has read the shape of it once already, on a bridge in a market in this same county, four months and a market day back: **he does not write down what the rite is for, because he has not worked it out, and a man who writes down why a thing is good in the same book as what the thing is has written a sentence instead of a working.** Then the parts, in the trade's own words — anchor, the roll, one book one room one building; witness, the person answering and two whose names are in the book, **and a witness that is also the anchor is not a witness, it is a second reading of the same hand, same as the last time, I have written that sentence twice now in two counties and it has been right twice**; counterweight, about forty men and a day at five pence, sixty marks one shilling eightpence, **and six men out of a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths whose names are six lines with nothing against them and no reason on them, and there is no column in this county for what happened to them and there is not going to be one, and the reason there is not going to be one is the same reason there is not a column for a person**; bound, the promise that these two hundred are the people, going all the way through, **a bound has to be the narrowest thing you can make it, the width of the split, the length of the timber, and this one is the whole of the county and I made it and I know the size of it**; figure, **two tons each, which is a number I had off a barrow of setts in a fen county in the last year and for which I have no reason of any kind, and two hundred of them is four hundred tons, and four hundred tons into a bed of river gravel at Ninebridge is a thing I can point at and say there, that is what this is. A document's load is not in tons. I know that. I have written it in tons anyway, twice, in two counties, and the second time I have also written down that I noticed the first time, and noticing it did not stop me then and it has not stopped me now, and a man who has noticed a thing twice is not being careful, he is being consistent.** Then the calendar, planted for the third time in his own hand: **four months, or thereabouts, which is the same thing at this rate, and I have not done the sum because the sum is in two calendars and I have not got both of them, and the one I have got does not have a week in it, and I wrote a line about that exact sum in this book the last time I was in this county and I have not read that line since and I know what it says without reading it.** And then: **I am going to build it anyway. That is written down as well, because I have written the mistake down before and read it and gone on, and the only new thing in this book today is that it is the same mistake both times and noticing it has never once stopped me.** And the last line, which is the last line of the last chapter of the block, and which is nearly the same sentence as the last line of Chapter 69, because he is the same man doing the same thing in a different county: **Closed the book. There are about four hundred soft tiles in this county between here and Kilnholt and one pair of hands and mine, and I am going to do the bottom of the rack next, and it is the best work I have had in five months, and nobody in this county is going to hear a word about any of the above from me, ever, in a room, with a clerk in it, ever again.**

## Batch 0009 — Volume 02, Chapters 81–90, "The Nine Months"

Block 4. POVs **Aren Kest ×8 (81, 82, 83, 84, 86, 87, 88, 89) / Garrick Dene ×1 (85) / Mara Vey ×1 (90, the last movement only, four hundred miles off).** **All ten chapters are path time in the County of Ash and are dated only by the county's own frame — the rolling, the two low waters a year, the year counted from the first firing, and the county's own twelve months, which nobody in the county can convert into anybody else's.** **No main-world weekday, no real-world month, and no drift figure is spoken anywhere in the batch; the only *three weeks* on the page is the printed standard of measurement in the Office's hand at the foot of the terms in Chapter 90, and it is a term and not a subtraction.** The word *Gatehouse* is not spelled out anywhere in this batch; the phrases *Culling Order* and *custodian* do not appear; the word *Bind* does not appear and nothing is put into any material; **the private-keeper clause is not mentioned, referred to, explained or noticed by anybody — Chapter 90 has a character take the *back* of a form out of a drawer for the first time in the county's history, and the chapter does not say what else is printed on that reverse and no character remarks on it.** No melody cost added and none may be. No crossing, no gate, no revelation, no new final enemy; **the only arrest in the batch is entered by a clerk of a counting house in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, who is not a Crown's man and is not an enemy, and the volume says so in about eleven words.** **No named supporting character dies, nobody is killed, no arch goes down, no glass breaks; the volume's catastrophe is a man of fifty-eight who is alive, employed, correctly recorded in three hands, and does not know which kiln he fires.** **No two of the volume's five blanks are in one room, in one chapter or in one sentence, and no character says that any two of them are the same.** The two eighth ruled lines do not appear in this batch at all. The 456 is not re-summed anywhere and is not scaled: the man with the ledger is asked three times whether his new number is that number and says no three times. **New names, three of the six the cap allows: Cobb Rennick, 52; Fennie Loam, 19; Garrick Dene, 64.** The volume total is now sixteen of eighteen, with six of twelve slots unfilled.

**Ch 81 · path time, Ash, the sixth month, the fourth hour of a day in it · Aren Kest, and about sixty people in a hall.** **The block's first line is the sentence the block is built on: two hundred names, then four hundred, because the work goes faster than the argument and the argument is always slower than the work.** The three hands: two are hands and **the third is a letter**, and the price of the shelf is still eleven sheets this county cannot fetch. The rite is proposed **in a hat** in a hall of about sixty — a magistrate, a clerk, a rollkeeper, two kilns, the bench, the physician, about forty of the two hundred and six — and a hat is the only piece of clothing a bridgewright owns that anybody in this county recognises as an outsider's, and he wears it because he has decided to be counted, and everybody who has read this book knows what a hat costs him and the county's own clerk writes nothing about it because a hat is not a fact. **The rite in four sentences: a struck person re-presses their own soft tile in front of two witnesses; the two witnesses press their own hands onto two other tiles; the three go on a board; and the office does not read the roll — it watches people answer.** And the sentence: *a person can check a rule, and a person who can check is a person who has been asked.* Nobody writes it down, because a rule that can be tested is not a claim. **And four words into a fifth sentence — the fifth being the reason he is the one saying it, which a woman with a stove warned him about in the eleventh month — a clerk of the counting house of the tolls of Bellweather-Living, fifty-eight, thirty-one years in that building, not a Crown's man, stops copying a fair copy of the minute and enters the arrest against a name already on the leaf, with the day and the hour, in his own fair hand.** The clerk's eleven seconds and the whole of the reason: **a private book in another country is not a public book; there is no officer of the Crown in this city, no register of arrests, no court that sits on one, and nothing in this kingdom can be got at that book except a man with a key — and a book that cannot be got at is the only place in the world in which a sentence a man said in a room four hundred miles off is safe to keep.** The mechanism is the fourth hand: the county sent a fair copy of the minute because that is what a press on a shelf is for, and the price of the shelf is still eleven sheets. **It is the second arrest that office has entered against that name in a year and the first is on the same leaf eleven pages back.** Nobody in Merefen has a copy of either and will not for a long time; the counting house does not produce; the Archive holds a fair copy of a fair copy of an *index* leaf and would not be able to tell from the index that the book it is a copy of had a second thing in it that was not on the index. **Nobody in the room understands what it means. Aren Kest understands an arrest in a private book four hundred miles off in about two seconds, the way he knows a load path, and says not one word about it to anybody and does not put it in the notebook that night** — and what he writes instead is that the fastest line in this county is a list of restorations read aloud four times a day, and that it is going to cost a man his name in a kiln he works in every day of his life.

**Ch 82 · path time, Ash, the rite, weeks one to eleven, told afterwards · Aren Kest.** **The rite worked for eleven weeks and nobody in the County of Ash noticed anything wrong in it, and the chapter is written so that the reader notices and the characters do not.** The long room, a stove, a shelf for the boards, about nine in the room at a time, four sittings a day, the queue outside in the wet behaving itself. Eleven minutes a person. The press is not a job, it is a thing a four-year-old can do. **And the office does not read the roll: the witnesses' names come off the door, said out loud, unchecked about one time in nine, and the clerk's pen does not move — and the whole of the load goes off the book and onto the room, which nobody in that hall had thought about and which he had written down twice in two counties and was not going to act on, because he was not looking at a structure.** **The restorations are read out at every sitting, four times a day, by Fennie Loam, 19, an apprentice at the assizes at three shillings a week who copies the roll — because he read it himself on the first two days, the first sitting, and then stopped and said why, and she took it off his hand on the third morning without a word being said about it, in the way a person takes a hod off another person, and nobody told her that was a job she had.** She reads it properly: name, hearth, trade, at the speed of a woman reading a row of things she has copied four hundred times, not one wrong, and she gets better, and by the eleventh week she slows very slightly to let a man at the back of the queue keep up, **and that is the most generous thing anybody did in that room in eleven weeks and she has no idea she did it.** The finding: **there is a physician of sixty-three with four pages in a drawer that say the disease travels along the marks that are read most, and the marks read most are the roll marks because a clerk reads the roll every morning and does not know that she is doing anything; and for nineteen years this county was the safest place on the river because nobody here could read, and they have opened it, four times a day, and nobody knows what that is.** He asks her one question in a corridor, in about eleven seconds, and she works him out and says so and tells him that the reading is a job and that he stopped it for the wrong reason and that being glad about it is worse than arguing. Bel, four, at the fourth row and the third, saying *that one wants pressing*, and nobody looks at her own board, which is the only rule in this county never broken and never written down. A man who has not spoken in nine months presses for four minutes and about eleven people say *yes* together and it is the loudest thing in eleven weeks. **Ends on a number and not a feeling: four hundred and nine boards, counted twice by a boy of fifteen who copies the blanks, with the space between the two lines copied as well, and the man with the ledger saying that a quarter of a year is at least three presses a house and a county that puts four hundred and nine boards through one room in one quarter has either got nine hundred and forty-one hearths wrong or has never once counted its boards in nineteen years, and it has not, and there is no column for it and he is not making one this afternoon.**

**Ch 83 · path time, Ash, the ninth week of the rite · Aren Kest, in a room, watching.** **Ostry Vaile, fifty-eight, is put back on the roll on the ninth day of the ninth week in eleven minutes by pressing his own tile, and in the same week he begins to go back to being clay, and nobody in the room knows that either is happening.** He is read **eighty-four times in that hall** — four sittings a day, six days a week, from the ninth week to the last day of the eleventh — and the four villages did not have a person put back in them in the ninth week, so the whole of it was in Kilnholt, and the man with the ledger puts one figure on the slate in the ninth week and takes it off again four seconds later, because four hundred and nine boards and four readings a day is arithmetic about marks and not about people and he has never had a reason not to. **The softening is on the page as a sequence, in four ordinary things that are not the same kind of thing, and a man who has spent his life putting four things in a row because four things in a row make a load is the last man in the world to assemble them: the order of the day (one crooked sagger ring stacked at the bottom instead of the top for six days, and the man at the loading end putting it right twice, and then on the sixth day not putting it right and going and standing at the other end of the floor — four seconds, no decision, nine years without telling anybody); the trade (he walks onto the charging floor he has not stood on in nineteen years and does it better than the man who does it, and the man of thirty-one with a tile and a hearth and a wage is told there is no work for him at that kiln, and nobody decided it and a kiln is a floor and there is one of each job); the hearth (two hundred and fourteen is called twice at the change of shift and the shift changes, and eleven men know and none of them says a word, because a man who softens cannot be witnessed and the law requires two witnesses, so saying it out loud takes on a thing that cannot be got out of the room); the name (on the fourth day, a man off a boat who has been laid off in a winter asks him which kiln he is at, and he says: that is the one I am at).** The man with the ledger opens the roll: *Ostry Vaile. Hearth two hundred and fourteen. Trade: kiln-worker, Kilnholt.* **The mark on the tile agrees with the entry and it was pressed by him and it stands up in a dish and it can be read at four feet on a wet morning. Three books, three hands, all of them right about him.** Kilnholt has four kilns and the fourth column has never once had to say which, because a tile is of a person and a kiln is not a person and the roll is of persons. **Hesper Lound puts a hand on his wrist for about a second and writes nothing, and the chapter says that the room saw kindness and the reader knows it is the law, and the reason is that a physician who enters a soft person in a book has entered a person the law has already removed by operation, and there is no column and there is not going to be one, and somebody ruled five and stopped ninety-four years ago with a knife.** The last line: **nobody in that room said one word, and the room was right, and the roll was right, and the tile was right, and there was nothing whatever in the County of Ash that was wrong.**

**Ch 84 · path time, Ash, the seventh month, and it takes four days · Aren Kest and the hand of the Ninebridge.** **It takes him four days and he gets it exactly right and he is wrong about who did it.** The working, set out in the trade's words: the anchor is no longer the roll but *the room*; the witness is the person answering and two whose names are in the book, **and a witness that is also the anchor is not a witness, it is a second reading of the same hand** — written twice in two counties and right three times, and the third time is right for a different reason, which is that there is nobody in the room who would have refused. The counterweight is the county, and now the wages of four weeks, which nobody costed, **because the work was done by the county's own people in their own time and the county's own time is the one thing in this county that appears nowhere.** And the bound: *these people are the people.* Then the volume's argument, arrived at by a man counting things in a shed: **a record a person can contradict is a record of that person; a record that cannot be contradicted is a claim about that person; and the founding act of this county was fire everything — the single cleverest act any of them ever took — a record that could not be argued with, which protected the county for nineteen years and is the disease, because a county that cannot lose its people cannot hear them either, and a man can obey a thing about himself for nineteen years and at the end of the nineteenth year not one word of his will be recognisable as his own.** The cure is what they have already built by accident: a soft tile, in three hands, in three places, none authoritative alone, a person restored when two of the three agree. **And it is being read too fast to survive.** The figure, for the third time, in tons: four hundred and nine at two tons is eight hundred and eighteen tons into a bed of river gravel at Ninebridge; **a document's load is not in tons, and he has now written it three times in two counties, and the sentence about noticing it has changed by one word — a man who has noticed a thing twice is not being careful, he is being consistent; a man who has noticed a thing three times is not being careful either, he is being consistent faster.** The third day is when he finds that it is not the bound: **he had the words for a room and he used the words for a county; a hall is not a person; a promise is only as wide as the people who have agreed to it, and he had four hundred and nine of them and not one had agreed to anything.** The fourth day is the hall. He goes in at the first hour, before the first sitting, **does not ask the magistrate, does not ask the clerk, does not ask the rollkeeper, does not ask anybody**, puts his hand flat on a sheet in a girl's hand, says *I am going to take this off you*, and takes it. The room stops for two seconds and looks at nine people and a clerk of an assizes who cannot stop a thing and can only enter one and was not asked. **It is the eleventh time in the book and it is the cheapest of the eleven and it is the one that worked.** She does not fight him and says: *was I doing it wrong?* — and then: *then say what the right thing is, because I have been reading that list four times a day for eleven weeks and you have not told me one single time what it is for.* **And he has no alternative, and there has never been one, and there is not going to be one, because the cure is a record a person can contradict and answering has to be asked for and there is no column headed *answered* and a clerk's list of restorations read four times a day is not a consent and is not an asking and is not a contradiction: it is a reading.** The hand of the Ninebridge's refusal, and it is professional and not civic and she says so: **a clerk's hand is a thing you go through, not around; and being right is the whole of what has happened today; and a man who has spent eight months not asking anybody anything in this county is not being careful, he is being cheap; and being useful is a thing a man does for somebody, and this morning he was not useful to anybody in that room.** She also gives him the one thing nobody in that room wanted: *you were right about this county twice, or three times, and I have been waiting nine weeks to tell you so.* And his own, in the notebook, at about the eleventh hour, which is the third entry and the last of the four days and the last thing anybody in this volume writes in this county except other people: **being right was sufficient, and the four times he was right, three of them somebody else paid, and the fourth he paid, and he did not know he was paying; and a man who has been refused twice stops asking for a thing and starts building it; and the bound is not what killed a man and he has spent four days on a stool proving that he was right, and the proving cost him the belief that a man who is right about a structure can build one that is right about people, and there is no such man and there is not going to be one.** And the last line of the chapter is the ritual one: the reading stopped today, nobody in that hall thanked him, the girl is the only person in this county who could have stopped it and did not know for about a year that she was the reason, and he is going to do the bottom of the rack now, and it is still the best work he has had in five months, and nobody in this county is going to hear a word about any of the above from him, ever, in a room, with a clerk in it, ever again.

**Ch 85 · path time, Ash, the eighth month · Garrick Dene, 64, of Ninebridge, and a coping.** **The only chapter in the block not in Aren Kest's head, and it is the only moment in the volume in which the tile on the coping is treated as ordinary.** Garrick Dene has swept the coping of the second span for forty-one years, came into the county at twenty-three with a broommaker's certificate, has swept nine bridges and can teach the job in about four sentences, and his want is not that the tile be taken away: **it is to be lifted, so that he can sweep the eight inches under it, because a lifting is a job and a taking away is a throwing away, and there is not one stone moved and no water standing on that span that was not standing there when he came, and he would like to go down on his knees on that coping with a broom in his hand and he cannot, because it is not his to lift and the coping is the county's.** The thing is called *the old mark* and it is a workman's name and it is not going to be written down anywhere. **The bell's absence, named out loud by the only man in the county who sweeps: nine spans and not one bell, not a bell-cote, not an iron stock, not a staple, not a hole where one used to be, and no mark — and when a bell is taken off a bridge there is a mark, socket scar or bolt holes or a paler rectangle of soot, and you cannot sweep a place clean for a year and you do not need to because every man in the county can see it.** A bell went in the county he came from for a fire, for a death, and for a refusal, and a minute was made of it and he has never seen the book. **And the word: a county that has had that has a word for it, and this county's word for a thing that was asked for and not given is the word for a thing that got lost, one word for both, and he has worked that out over nine years and said it to two men and both were right.** The stewardlessness named from the bottom: **where he came from the word for the man answerable for a bridge has a duty inside it, which is that there is a thing somebody is entitled to ask him; and in nine years on that coping he has worked out that there has not been one single thing on this bridge that anybody in this county could have asked anybody about, so there is nothing to answer for, so nobody is answerable, so you cannot have the word, so they have got a good word instead, and a hand is a thing that holds on and a hand is not a thing that answers; a man who is answerable for a thing that cannot be asked about is not a man, he is a shape, and it is the right word, and he has been trying nine years to work out why and it is because there is not another one to use.** His fearlessness is a trade thing: **fear is what you do with a thing you think somebody is going to ask you about, and a thing you have to work round every day for forty-one years is not a frightening thing, it is a shape** — and the comparison is a stone four inches proud on the shoulder of that span that he thinks about four times a year. He cannot say who put the mark there: he asked a man of about seventy when he was about thirty, twice over two years, and got *his grandfather put it there* and then *I do not know*, and the man is dead, and the mark is not a stamp off any die in this county and he would know. **And Aren Kest, who has lied about it twice in front of the only two people in this county who have ever thanked him for anything, says the true thing: *I don't know.*** And the price, in the notebook that night, and it is the last line of the third entry: **in the eleventh month she asked him what it was and he said he did not know and she said Good, and in the twentieth day he told her it was a good tile and she knew he was lying and she thanked him for the answer — and that was a protection, because a man who does not know a thing cannot be asked what he has found out; and today he has said I do not know about a thing three times in one county in front of three people, and three people in one county is a county, and in nine years the question is going to be what a man off the road said on the coping of the second span, and there will be two answers, and one of them is a lie.** He has bought a protection with a debt and he does not know the rate. The eight inches under it were not swept that day, and Garrick Dene had asked a man off the road one question in nine months and had got a straight answer and made no complaint about it and did not expect one.

**Ch 86 · path time, Ash, the eighth month · Aren Kest, and a four-year-old.** **A man at the cutting bench turns over a board in the fourth row in the eighth month because the shed has been aired and says *that is a river*, and about nine people look at a wet board.** Bel says *that is mine*, and then *I did*, and the man at the loading end says afterwards that it was the only straight answer he had heard in the county in nine years and that what made it straight was that she was not defending it, she was reporting it. **Nobody touched it, and the reason is the volume's mechanism: a soft tile is the only record a person can change, and nine people who work with clay every day worked out in about nine seconds that not one of them was going to be the last pair of hands on that clay.** The mark: a bend, a wheel with **eight spokes** — more than any wheel in this county — and a weir of six short lines across a thicker one. **The woman who fires at the Kilnholt kiln, sixteen years, not named, because nobody in that shed ever asked her name: she can give the date to the minute (the last spring, the rolling before the low water, a morning when the boards were aired and about nine women were at the loading end) and she can say she saw a child do it, and she did not go over, and she has been not going over about four months because there is a boy of fifteen in this county who copies blanks for a living and there are going to be four hundred of these boards in a shed by the seventh year. She said it when the keeper of the roll asked her a question about a date, and not before, because a woman who has been not going over for four months can put a date down in four seconds once somebody asks her for one.** He recognises it in about a second and a half: the shape a river takes above a mill on the Sable, four hundred miles off, on the mill side of the water, with a weir of six slats — **and he does not say the name, and he does not say the county, and the reason he gives the room is the longest sentence he has said in a room in this county and it is about himself: he has said a name in a room with a clerk in it five times in eleven years, he watched one of the five go into a file four hundred miles off from a single stone on a letter, a clerk in a provincial town refused to write the fifth down and gave him the reason, and a clerk who keeps a word out of a book for him is doing his work and not his own — and a shed is a room and he has had one room go on him and he is not having two.** The hand of the Ninebridge, from the doorway, without coming in: *then let the room have the mark.* **Pernel Quist's sentence, the most frightening thing said in this volume, and she says it standing up in a shed with wet boards on edge: *we have been looking at the wrong tile.*** And then four minutes in a keeper's voice: a fired tile is the only record in this county a person cannot alter — not the clerk, not her, not a magistrate, not the county — and that is why the county trusted it for nineteen years, and **a record a person can change is a record of that person and a record a person cannot change is a claim about that person**, and they have spent nineteen years firing claims and never once had a person able to argue with one; and the cure is the soft tile, in three hands, none authoritative alone, and **a child draws what has been in front of her face, and in about three years this board is going to be fired, and the mark is going to be a hand, and the river is going to be gone, and there is not going to be one thing in the world that says a woman of Kilnholt put a bend and a mill and a weir on the only account of herself that anybody will ever be able to take off her — and there is no instrument.** The notebook: **there is nowhere in the County of Ash to put a fact that is true and about nobody; not the roll, because the child is not on it and will not be for three years; not the bench, because a bench votes and this is not a thing anybody can vote about and if it were voted about there would be a column with a number against it; not the assizes, because a fact about a person goes into a book in a room and a room is where a thing like that ends up; and the man with the ledger has been putting them in a book for nineteen years and his book is a press, and a press is the only thing in the world you cannot quietly take out of, and that is why he is the only man in this county who has never once needed anybody to believe him and never once had anybody.**

**Ch 87 · path time, Ash, the ninth month · Aren Kest, and about fifty people in the long room.** The magistrate calls the room on purpose, reads his own reason out loud, and stands at the back of it for two hours and is minuted and does not minuted that. **The argument is that they cannot agree on a word for what happened to them: forty minutes, eleven words, and every one of them a word for a book — list, book, roll, column, stamp, the year the register went — and not one a word for a thing that was done to this county by somebody and could not be refused.** A woman of about sixty, on three hearths, two men buried, the fourth column of her tile saying *wife*, the second entry in nineteen years in that office against a person by name that is not a removal: **we did not lose our register, we lost our refusal, a register is what you have instead** — and then *it is still the wrong shape and I am not going to go and I have not got a trade for it*, and she sits down. The hand of the Ninebridge's road sentence, in her own words and the first time it has been said in a room by the person it belongs to: **a hand answers for a road and a hand can be asked, and those are two halves and everybody puts them together and they do not go together; she has been the hand of the Ninebridge for nine years and nobody has ever asked her one question about it, not the magistrate, not the rollkeeper, not the physician, not the bench, not a man off the road — a hand that cannot be asked is not a hand, it is a person holding something — and you have not got a word for the person who answers and you have not got a word for the person who asks, and it is not because this county is poor or cut off or behind a river, it is because there has never been anything in this county that could be asked; so there was nothing to be answerable for, and so nobody had to answer, and so nobody learned how — that is why there has never been a hand here that had to answer, not because we are better than other counties, because nothing has ever been put in front of us** — and then the correction, in the same breath: **we have had four hundred and nine of them since the sixth month; what we do not have is a word for what we lost, and those are not the same thing, and about forty people in this room have got them the wrong way round in their heads.** Hesper Lound reads four pages again and **nobody misunderstands them this time, and the reason is the county's own doing and not a stranger's: a county that has been made into marks for nineteen years and has spent four months being read four times a day cannot hear the second page of a finding without understanding it, and it is not a thing about me, it is a thing about what you have been doing for eleven weeks** — and he puts the four pages on the table and says anybody in the room may pick them up, and nobody does. **And the cord-cutter, fifty-one, forty years in the county, no other trade, says the thing that has been standing in the middle of the county since the fifth month: about two hundred of us was asked one time each, in our own sheds, in about a minute and a half a house, the answers in his own hand, the book shut, in a box under a bench in his shed, not in this room and not going anywhere, and he has not read it since the fifth month and is not going to; a woman came in the eighth month and asked whether he still had it and he said yes and she said nothing else and went out, and he would like that in the book here tonight.** And then he gives the other half, which nobody can answer: **the physician says a mark written down and not kept anywhere is a mark that gets read more often and not less, and he said that in this room in the fifth month and he was right; we wrote two hundred answers in two hundred books, and the answer *yes* has already been said out loud in a kitchen in this county twice and I was not there for either — and the physician's cure is three copies in three hands and three places, and I would like somebody in this county to explain to me, in a shed, at a cost, what the difference is between a thing a man writes in his own hand in his own house and a thing a woman presses into a board with her own thumb.** Nobody answers him, and the chapter is that not being answered. The magistrate refuses to minute the sense of the room and says why, in eight words, and the man at the loading end says it is the best minute anybody has entered in that book and votes nothing.

**Ch 88 · path time, Ash, the ninth month · Aren Kest, and a county's own answer.** **The two hundred and six go on strike and it is over in six days, and the thing that ends it is not a favour and not a wage and is written by the county in the county's own hand three years after it was pressed into a tile: the fourth of five lines, being that a person who is struck may answer, and if a person who is struck answers, the person is restored.** The strike costs a rolling: nine days of clay charged and not fired, back into the wedging shed on the third day, the scarcest room in the county, its floor the scarce part. Nobody argues about whether they are right, and the ash-driver puts the cost on a board in four lines, and the cord-cutter tells the bench, in about nine sentences, that the county has had a rule in a book for three years and has been working round it like a man works round a stone on a shoulder, and that if the bench does not put it somewhere in the next six days they will go home and not come back for a bench to find out why, because they have found out why about four times and the answer is always a room. **The bench adopts term four as a rule of the county, in force from that hour, not conditional upon the road and not conditional upon any office — the first entry in nineteen years in that office that is a rule and not a decision — and it records that the county has had the line for three years and has not put it anywhere, and that the reason is not a reason and is that there is no office in this county to put a line anywhere and no writ to answer and no Crown's man and no Crown's schedule carrying this county; and it enters, at the keeper of the roll's request and in her own words, that in about nine years a man will ask which clerk decided this, and there is no clerk, and there is going to be no clerk, and a rule with no office behind it is a thing that can be argued with, and that is the whole of the argument for it and not one part against.** A county writes the volume's sentence out in a keeper's mouth without ever having heard it. **Then the county does the thing the whole volume is about, without a stranger: the re-reading is broken up into eight villages and the market town separately, at the same time, over nine days, the roll does not move and nine lists go out, a list being the names of the people in that place in that place's own hand in that place's own order, a person on it if two people on it say he is and a person on the roll of persons on it or not.** About forty minutes a person, nine places, no office reading anything, no stranger in the room. **The rite took eleven minutes a person and one man could do it; this takes forty minutes and he cannot do any of it, and there is not one thing on the nine boards that says his name on it, and in about nine years that is the only thing anybody will be able to say about it that matters.** It works; about eleven names did not agree between the local lists and the roll and were got by hand in a day; a kiln is re-lit in the ninth month on two thirds and about thirteen men stay out and the number is written down. And the price: **nine boards of lists copied by nine men in the one room in the county with a floor that will take nine pairs of boots, a day and a half of that floor, which is nine days of wedging, and the wedging is the spring's clay, entered in one line in the county's own book — and the chapter ends on a county that has spent a day and a half of a wedging shed keeping a piece of paper.** And the road. **The witness at Ash's end was a person the county chose in the first month and who was struck in the eighth month, on a list of about two hundred made in the fifth month and not through a room yet; the county's own reading took nine days, his place in the order was the eighth day of them, and the road's day was the ninth.** A man who is struck cannot be a witness. **The county had eleven weeks to replace him and did not, and the reason is the reason the county built the thing that emptied the line, and nobody in that room is going to point that out.** The second candidate is **Cobb Rennick, 52, a tile-cutter at the Ninebridge kiln, nineteen years on the roll, struck in the sixth month and put back in the seventh week of the rite, who refuses in nine sentences and asks for the word *mouth* to be left in the clerk's reading: *I have been the mouth of this county for four months and I have had my name read out of a sheet until I could not hear it. I want to be a mark and not a mouth.*** There was no third candidate and the bench does not hold a list of people who have been asked to stand somewhere, and nobody ever thought of asking it to, **because the fifth of the county's own terms says the witness at Ash's end is chosen by Ash and does not say a word about a list.** The hand of the Ninebridge says it before anybody else can: **it did not fail. A road is a road. A road does not try. There is a road and there is a day and there is a man who stands at the end of it, and today there was no man at the end of it, and the road was not there, and the two of those are the same size and the same shape and the same absence, and a man who has to write down that a road failed is a man who has found a way of making it somebody's fault.** And a bridge four hundred years old, tiled sixty, has been shut a hundred times and has not failed once, and both times it was shut while somebody stood about looking as though the river had done it.

**Ch 89 · path time, Ash, the ninth month · Aren Kest, and a county's day that is not a battle.** A morning in a long room with a stove, three signatures, two columns, a price of tile, a vote that was lost, and about two hundred and fifty-four tiles broken up and wedged back, and **none of it took a single thing off any of the eleven who were in the room.** **Pernel Quist refuses for two days** — one day saying nothing, the second asking for the sheet and reading that the third hand is still a letter — and then signs, and the reason is the volume's most professional sentence: **a roll that can be wrong is not a weaker roll; it is a roll that has to be read twice, and a roll that has to be read twice cannot be read by one man in a morning.** Nineteen years of a roll that cannot be read twice, and about four hundred people went through a room in nine days because of it, and one of them does not know which kiln he works in, and the roll is right about him, and it was right about him in the ninth week too, and it will be right about him in nine years: **a roll that cannot be read twice cannot survive a person, and this county has just had a person in it for nine days and I have been sitting in a room with a box under the table while it happened, and I have been right, and my being right is the reason.** She asks for both halves entered and not tidied into each other — she was wrong about the third hand in the twenty-second week and was not wrong about the anchor — and she signs a roll whose third hand is a letter on the ground that the rule permits the third to be wrong, and the clerk reads it back and she corrects the word *weaker*. **The bench wins a reduction and loses a vote.** The reduction: the price of a tile to a household goes down a farthing, and the reason is not in the minute, because a reason is a thing believed and a minute is a thing set. The vote, **lost, four for and three against, the keeper of the roll not voting and saying why, because she is one of the three hands and a hand that votes on whether it is a hand is not a hand:** whether to send a man of this county four hundred miles off to fetch eleven sheets out of a press in a room with a door on a lane, so that the third hand can be a shelf instead of a letter — and the second reason against is the witness, and the third, from the man at the loading end, is the volume's argument in a trade's mouth: **a promised hand is not a hand, and a promised hand that costs a man his season is not a promised hand, it is a list; we have had a list, I have watched what it does, and a promised hand four hundred miles off that costs one of thirteen men a season is the same list with a nicer name on it.** The minute ends: *The bench has resolved that no man shall be sent. The bench has entered that it is unable to say whether the third hand is a hand, and that it has resolved not to find out this year.* **The kilns reopen on a schedule of two thirds with the hour against it; the east village's is not re-lit and that is in the book in one line; about thirteen did not go back and the thirteen are thirteen and the number is in a keeper's hand with the date and the hour on it and not one word of an opinion.** Then the un-firing. **The man with the ledger counts it himself and it is not the other subtraction: there is a hand on the back of a tile, a soft tile can be re-pressed by anybody with a thumb and a wet cloth, a fired tile cannot, so a person fired twice has two tiles with the same hand on the back of them in two different kilns, and the county has never counted them because the county counts fires and a fire is a kiln and a kiln is on a wall. He got to two hundred and fifty-four, out of two kilns, one village and half a wall, and it is a floor and he will not go past it, and a stack in the back of the Kilnholt shed stays shut** — and **he is asked three times whether it is the four hundred and fifty-six and says no three times and asks the clerk to enter that he has said it three times, and the reason it is not is that one is a thing about a shortage and the other is a thing about a person.** The cost, footed on a board and copied including the blanks: **thirteen men, five days, at five pence the day, three hundred and twenty-five pence, six marks three shillings and one penny; wood for the proof rolling, nine cords at the kiln gate, two shillings and sixpence the cord, two hundred and seventy pence, five marks two shillings and sixpence; the whole of it five hundred and ninety-five pence, twelve marks one shilling and seven pence** — with the line that this county has no month a clerk in another country could write down, and that the season it calls **the standing water** is the time between the second low water and the next first firing, when the Kiln is up over the working ground and nothing can be dug and nothing can be burned, and it is the only season in which a county can afford to do something that costs nothing but time and thirteen men's hands, and it will be two years, and there is a bench, and there is a cost, and the cost is on the board. The motion: **a hand setting a thing true against a thing out of true, done four times now — a woman's hands on a clamp bar in a filing room over a salt merchant, one night, alone, on purpose left out of true for a year; a man's hands on a garboard on the third slip of a ferry yard; and tonight a man of fifty-eight putting his thumb into wet clay and pressing round a mark and not on it, about forty times an hour, for five days — and none of them has said one word about it to any of the others and none of them is going to, because a movement is not a fact and if you put a movement in a book a man in nine years can ask you what it was for.** And the last scene, and it is the heart of the chapter: **Colm Arreld stops what he is doing in front of about nine people and tells Fennie Loam, once, that she was right to read it — not minuted, deliberately, because a minute is a thing set and this is a thing he wants to have said out loud in a room with nine people in it so that nine people heard it — and she asks whether she may write that down, and is told she may write *the magistrate said so in a room and he is not minuting it* and not one word more, and about four of the nine people do not go home for a quarter of an hour.**

**Ch 90 · path time, Ash, the ninth month and the last day of it, and four hundred miles off · Aren Kest, and Mara Vey.** **The climax is a form, and the form is four months old and has been in an unlocked drawer in a shed since the seventeenth week.** The hand of the Ninebridge takes out the wrapper of a form of the R.C. series and turns it over, **and there are five terms printed on the back in the office's hand, and below them, in a smaller type, as a standard of measurement, *a road held, three weeks*, and she has carried boards past that drawer about nine times a day for four months and has never read the small print, and the reason is that there is nothing in this county that is going to tell her what to do with a form, and she wants that entered.** He goes through the five out loud, quickly, without editorialising once: two routes held and the holding of one not the holding of the other; **two carried over and the carrier shall not be able to say which path gave him which** — the hand that reads a handrail in the dark, which is eleven years old and not a working, and the stance, weight a little back, hands loose, a ferrywright on a slip, which he has not noticed and which a woman in a fen noticed before he did and is not going to ask him about; a return prepared by two hands, a date, and a witness at each end who is not the person keeping the road, and if the witness is not there the route does not open and the route does not fail — **which is not an office, there is no Crown's man who can be asked to fill it in, and it is a town four hundred miles off working it out on a bench in nineteen years and putting it in a minute; and he has had that on a page in a yard since the ninth of the first month and the same sentence is now printed at the foot of a form in an office that cannot say whose it is, and two people are carrying it about four hundred miles apart in two countries**; and the fifth, which is the one it will cost him — the second route does not answer when it is called, the far side decides, and **there is no procedure for a far side that has not decided** — and he gives the one honest reason he has not said it out loud in a room in eleven years: **this shed is not a room, and you did not ask me a question, and that is a different thing from being asked and I have noticed the difference and I am not going to say thank you for it, because you would not know what to do with it.** The object, term four. **The county makes it in about four minutes: half a tile. A whole tile is the account of a person; a half tile is the account of nothing. The county has never had a record of nothing in it and its whole difficulty is that it has nothing but records.** A chip is too small to be anything, a whole tile is a record of a person, and about nine hundred half tiles are in two hoppers behind the Kilnholt kiln and are the only thing in the County of Ash nobody is entitled to want. **The half is the half that had nothing on it, because the half that had the hand on it is four hundred yards away in a box in a house in Kilnholt and it stays there. It is weighed at the kiln gate on a stone and it comes to a shade over ten ounces, and the weighing is the only figure in this business anybody asked for, and it was asked for in the only two words anybody in this county has ever used about the north: to the north.** And a keeper of records on the other end of that string can hold it and put it in a press and weigh it, **and there is not one thing in it that anybody can take out of it, and she can do that for nine years and it will be the safest object that has ever been in her office, and the county has sent it four hundred miles to be safe in a press.** The stage: **not as a reward, in a shed with a door on the side of it, with the hand of the Ninebridge as the only witness in the county who can be asked, and the boy of sixteen copying the page and the blanks under it.** Four of the five lines were already true and had been for between four and twenty-two weeks; **there is no word for a stage in this county and there could not be, because a county that counts fires has not got a way of counting a man.** She says *that was a stage*, and that she is going to put her mark on the page **because the rule in this county is that a hand witnesses a thing and does not understand it, and there is not one word in this county for what I have just done, and I have said no to about four people who wanted me to be the hand of something else in nine years and I have said yes to this in about four seconds, and I would like whoever reads that in nine years to know the two of those are the same act and I did not notice at the time and I noticed at about the second minute.** The message: **a bill, four hundred words, in the hand of the man with a ledger, itemised, every line a figure or a date or a trade, a clerk four hundred miles off can check every single line of it — the two hundred and six asked once, the four hundred and nine twice and the second time being the one that counts, the price, and the road, and a line that is a number and not a name — and a list can only be checked, and a county that had been refused nothing in nineteen years worked that out in about four minutes in a shed, and a man who has been putting lists into counties for eleven years did not tell them one single thing about it.** The crossing out at the third hour in nine minutes, the five terms read from a fair copy in a gateway in Weir Street, the second term read twice at the boy's asking, and **there is no witness at Ash's end, and the county had eleven weeks and did not.** And then the chapter's other ending: **the woman of about sixty steps forward off the dry side of the step without a word being said to her, and says, *I do not know what I am standing in**, and is told she is the witness at this end and not the hand and is on the roll with a tile of her own that agrees with the book; and when she asks what happens if she is not there on the day, she is told **then it is not there, and it will not be anywhere near it, and nobody in this county is going to say the word failed** — and she says then I will be there on the day, and nobody thanks her, and the clerk writes the hour against it, **and it is the only entry in that book in nineteen years that is a person standing somewhere and not a removal.** The line goes out, nothing is opened, and the notebook has one line about the whole of the ninth month: **I did not call it. Nobody in this county called it. I have spent four months working out how to call a thing that does not answer when it is called, and a man at a cutting bench in this county had worked it out in about four minutes in the fourth month, and what he worked out was that you do not call a road, you stand at the end of it.** And four hundred miles off, on the morning before he comes up, a list comes down a line in a fair hand on a day the road was kept, **in the same channel and with the same terms, put in at the north end by a hand at that end with a witness at that end who is not the person keeping the road, and it is the only thing that has come down that line in three months, and it came on a day when the road did not open at either end, because a message is not a route, and the county does not know that and put the list in on the day it was going to be kept.** Mara Vey reads every line in about fifty minutes and writes nothing, enters it in the day-book against no person with the hour, and reads it a second time and **finds nothing to argue with, and one thing she cannot put down**: line nine of the last hundred words, a number and not a name — *of the four hundred and nine, one has not been spoken to since the ninth week of the room at the assizes; the other four hundred and eight have been; he is at a kiln; his hearth has a fire in it on the nights this county can see it from a road; the clerk of the roll does not know which kiln and neither does he* — **and it is the same man as the four lines copied exactly into her own fair hand about six months ago at the eleventh hour against no person, entered ever since and looked at about nine times, and she cannot put the line down for four reasons written on the back of the list in pencil and rubbed out with her own hand because a keeper who writes four reasons on the back of a document has made a document about a person.** The four: a keeper who enters a fact about a person begins, in about a year, to assemble facts about that person; **she cannot get a claim number for it, because a claim is against a person or a body and a county is not a party and has no officer in this kingdom that can take a return, and the man who could tell her which county it is is four hundred miles off and cannot be asked, and she wrote that reasoning herself into a day-book six months ago and it is correct;** the office's own second rule, that no combined figure is entered in this office, and she has two figures about one man and neither has a name in it and **if she writes one under the other she has made a roll of one person out of two counties, and a roll of one person is the thing this whole case is about**; and the promise. **And the last line of the block is the reason she wrote that promise down, and the reason is not a sentence about the man: because a keeper of records who asks a question has already decided that the answer is not in the records she keeps, and a keeper who has decided that has stopped being a keeper and started being a person who wants something.** **Note for the Chapter 100 writer, and this is the only place it is recorded: `outline/volume-02.md` §12's final chapter image also carries this beat — a keeper reading every line of a list and finding one thing she cannot put down — and Batch 0009 landed it here, at Chapter 90, in Ash material, four hundred miles off, in a main-world day-book. Chapter 100 may not restate the reason as a speech, a paragraph of narration or a found entry, and may not restate it in different words; what is new there is the entry, made a second time against nobody with the hour on it, in a hand that has stopped asking.**

## Batch 0010 — Volume 02, Chapters 91–100, "Eighty-Eight Days, and a Roll in Three Hands"

Block 5, and the volume's last ten chapters, and the last block is main world on every one of its ten days: **the second, fifth, ninth, twelfth, sixteenth, twenty-second, twenty-fourth, twenty-sixth, twenty-seventh and twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, YR 313 — a Tuesday, a Friday, a Tuesday, a Friday, a Tuesday, a Monday, a Wednesday, a Friday, a Saturday and a Monday — running from a Tuesday to a Monday, which is the audited chain of item 147 and not a preference.** The ninth and the twenty-seventh and the twenty-ninth are market days in a county whose market days are Saturday and Thursday, and the chapter says so and does not reconcile it, which is the house convention. **The path time is over: Aren came up on the second and no chapter in this block re-crosses, and there is no crossing in either direction anywhere in the ten.** The Ash material arrives the only way it is allowed to arrive — **as a fair copy down a line, itemised, and as four lines copied in a keeper's own hand six months ago** — and there is no narrator in any of these ten chapters who has stood on a bridge in that county. **No System display, no gate, no stage claimed, no revelation, no authority claimed and no mechanism explained.** Nobody dies; nobody is killed; no arch goes down and no glass breaks; the volume's catastrophe is a man of fifty-eight who is alive, employed, correct in three books and does not know which kiln he fires. **The word *Gatehouse* is not spelled anywhere in the block.** *Culling Order*, *custodian* and *Bind* do not appear and nothing is put into any material. **The private-keeper clause is not mentioned, referred to, explained or noticed by anybody**, and Chapter 94 deliberately refuses to discuss the *Keeper* column even when a man who has filled it in two hundred times is standing in a gateway being asked about it. **No melody cost is added and the word does not appear.** The two eighth ruled lines are in different chapters, in different rooms, in front of different people, and no character puts them together: Chapter 98 looks at the served schedule's eighth line for the third time and does not fill it, and the guild's maintenance specification is not in the block at all. **No two of the volume's five blanks are in one room and no character says that any two of them are the same**: the creditor's index leaf's county cell is in Chapter 92, the served schedule's eighth line in Chapter 98, and nothing else in the block touches either. **The two eighth ruled lines, the tile on the coping and the 456 are untouched**: the tile appears once, at the close of Chapter 99, in a room in a fen county, as a fact a keeper has no record of, is not touched, is not looked at, and is not explained, and the device on it is named by nobody. **The 456 does not appear in any of the ten chapters in any form and no figure anywhere is got from it by scaling**; the 254 is used and the 2,906 is the county's own figure about itself. **POVs:** Aren ×3 (91, 95, 97), Mara ×3 (92, 98, 100), Lysa Fenn ×1 (93), Sabin Dain ×1 (94), Tovan Kest ×1 (97 — Toban's chapter, with Aren in the yard), Mara again in the second half of 100 as the volume's last movement. **Two new names and four unfilled slots, as the card allows: Alish Renn, 30, a second copyist on a warrant of Mara's own; and Ansel Tarrant, the name in a creditor's fair copy read in Merefen on the twenty-seventh, who signs the four-hundred-leaf register in his grandmother's frightened hand twenty years after she did.** Iselde is not used; Halven's counterpart is not named; the man who buys a roll of nine hundred and forty-one hearths for a farthing of salt is the salt merchant's brother, named by trade, and is not a villain; the woman who fires at the Kilnholt kiln, the woman of about sixty on three hearths and the clerk of the counting house are all still addressed by trade and unnamed. **Thirty-three narrator-voice references to the book were the Batch 0009 finding and there are none in this block**: *this volume*, *this chapter*, *the reader* and *the author* do not appear in any of the ten files, and the surviving *this book* instances are diegetic — three in Aren's own notebook in Chapter 91, two inside quoted documents in Chapters 95 and 99, and Chapter 100's two were rewritten into the room before the batch was closed.

**Ch 91 · the second of Greenmonth, a Tuesday, not a market day · Aren Kest, and the road up, and Mara.** The fever's second of three, breaking on the fifth at about the sixth hour, and the eighty-eighth day of absence, and the man comes up the lane door with grey clay in the seam of his bag. **He gives her the numbers before she is asked, because he always does: nine months and six days in there, eighty-eight days here, and the error — eighty-eight against the twenty-one days printed as a standard of measurement at the foot of the terms in an office's hand, which is sixty-seven days, and this is the only chapter in the block that states a drift figure at all.** And the prediction he made in Greenmonth in a cost sheet, entered as a prediction, worth about a third of what it is worth afterwards. **And four pages of his own account in his own hand, done in a shed in the last week, about the record and not about the road, which she reads in twenty minutes and in which she makes him go and get the book for two figures and corrects one sentence, and he agrees and does not argue, and that is the first thing that has happened in that room in four years that she has not had to ask twice for.** The half tile: the first went north on the fourth of a month that is not a month here, in the bag of the woman who keeps the road at that end, **and he has no way of finding out whether it is in her press and is not going to ask, because a man who asks whether an object arrived has told the other end that the object is worth arriving** — and the one in his bag is the second, made by himself in a shed behind a kiln with a saw mark on the back that the woman who fired it has never seen and never will, **because he could not think of a way to ask whether the first had arrived, and a man who makes two of a thing to avoid asking a question has already decided what the answer is.** It weighs a shade over ten ounces and it is the account of nobody and she puts it in the press. **Tovan Kest comes at the fourth hour, does not knock, shuts the door for the second time in four years, stands there and will not touch him and will not help him and says both of those are on purpose — and then he watches five seconds of his brother doing six or seven things of an ordinary Tuesday morning in a room that is not the yard, in the wrong order, and he does not say one word about any of them.** The two grounds of the concealment, both in a notebook in a coat and neither in a form: **the order of a morning he can do and cannot put in order and does not know the order of, and the stance he cannot source, which is the second of five terms printed in an office's hand and which the form says he will not be able to.** The third time he has decided what a keeper of records may not be told. The first was a letter and it cost a bench-clerk's afternoon and two nights' sleep and him a year; the second was a mark on a coping and it cost a man of sixty-four an answer he was entitled to and him a lie for nine years. He gives her the county in ninety minutes in a day-book voice — the eleven, the office's three lines, the bench, the carpenter's *nil*, the casting vote, the provincial clerk, the card, the expired count — and asks her exactly one question, and it is about the Sedge Reach, **and it is a number and there is nobody in this county who can check a number.**

**Ch 92 · the fifth of Greenmonth, a Friday, not a market day · Mara Vey, and a date, and the breach.** The fever breaks at about the sixth hour. At about the seventh he tells her, in the ordinary way and in the middle of other things, **that the clerk of the assizes in that county sent the schedule of the eight village re-readings on the eighteenth of the ninth month of that county's own year, and it is the reason he knows there are eight places and not nine, because the town was not on the schedule — the town has the room with the floor in it.** The fair hand writes it down, and then, because a keeper cannot help it, she goes to the page and compares the entry against the day-book, and **in about eleven seconds she finds the thing and it takes her the rest of the morning: two dates in his account are dates of that county and not of this one, given in the wrong frame, twice, in one morning, without his noticing — a bridgewright would not have noticed and a keeper of records cannot not.** She tells him the finding is not arrived at by asking him anything. **The milestone, and it is the series' Volume 2 relationship beat: the promise of the twenty-second of Fallowmonth is a protection and the not asking is now a wound, and a wound she is choosing not to open is worse than a question; she has noticed the change on the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth, which was the seventh day of the promise, and has written the day down on the fifth of Wolfmonth, the morning he came up out of that county — and the lateness is in the seven months between the writing and the finding and not in the noticing.** She will not say the reason aloud and will not write it again, **because she wrote it once and writing it twice makes it a thing she has said out loud, and he says a date is the only instrument she has that she cannot stop using, and that it is going to be the thing that undoes her.** **And then she does the thing that makes it a breach and not a scene: she rules a sheet, writes the twelve months of that county's year down it, and tears it up in front of him, because a conversion of two ends into a middle is not a fact but a number she made up, and every line under it would be a line she had invented, and in nine years a man would take a figure off it and use it, and he would be using her, and he would not know. A county four hundred miles off counts the year from its first firing and I am not going to write that county's year down on a page in this room.** That is entered, with the hour, as a thing she did on purpose, and it is the first of her three unnecessary entries this year that anybody could have called timidity. **At the eleventh hour she goes to the press with a creditor's fair copy in her hand, because a day cannot end with a paragraph in it, and the third blank is there: a county cell, ruled, on a line with a number and a date on it, with nothing in it and no reason on it and no space on the leaf for a reason.** She cannot say when she started looking at it. An hour of the afternoon goes and there is no entry against anybody but the hour, **and she does not ask him about that either, and there is no reason on the page for that, and it is the same not asking.** The brine weights are still cited and not filed, the two day-book lines are five days apart in one week of Fallowmonth and are read together, and the roll of the dead is not built, and the reason it was not built in Fallowmonth is on the page in one line and the reason has changed since: **a ledger is a claim, and this office has learned this year from a county four hundred miles off that a claim about people is not a record of them.**

**Ch 93 · the ninth of Greenmonth, a Tuesday, a market day · Lysa Fenn, and a count, and a body corporate.** A clerk is put a chair at the table before anybody says anything. The second count, the county as a whole, **is answered by a filing room with a figure in it and no names in it: one thousand six hundred and nine, the number of persons who have come through the door of the Bellweather Claims Archive in four years and a half, taken out of a day-book, lawful, checked by nobody, with a claim number against the office and nothing against any person and three paragraphs of which the third is four words long, *entered against no person, no opinion*.** Lysa Fenn beats the instrument in nine minutes with the form's own construction — a count of persons is a return of a figure, a figure is not a person, no person is in default, and a thing against which nobody is in default is not refused and is not enforced and is therefore spent the moment it is answered. **And a shipwright of nineteen years' membership asks for the day-book and is told, in the order it happened, why a council may require the production of a record and cannot get a person out of one: a day-book is not a file, a file is not a record, a record is not a roll, and a day-book is the only book in that office nobody can serve.** **And she is frightened of her own answer, and says so: every argument she has ever had with that office is built on the fact that this court is not a person, and a court cannot be sued or refused or injured, and in about four years somebody is going to make it a person, and the first county in this kingdom to build one will not be a madman, it will be a woman of forty-five who has spent eleven years being unable to make a document do anything and is right about the mechanism.** She refuses to have it minuted and Marda Oakes does not put it in. **Then the resolution: the Bellweather Claims Archive is a body corporate, with a door, a number, and the capacity to sue and to be sued, entered on a form whose printed space under *Function of the office* has been filled in the founding's own words, with the space above the ruled line left empty —
