and the reason the resolution is not what it would otherwise be is that a form's space above the ruled line is for a thing's name and the space below is for what the thing is for, and everybody who fills one in a hurry gets it the wrong way up.** Marda Oakes says the mechanism, refuses to say the price, and asks that the price be written on the back of the form and is refused three times and gets it into the minute instead. The clerk reads back the resolution, the two writs and the finding of the fourth of Greenmonth and improves nobody's sentence. **In a yard at the sixth hour, out loud, once, to one person: a body corporate can be sued, a keeper can only be dismissed — and nobody answers it, and it is the last thing she says that day.**

**Ch 94 · the twelfth of Greenmonth, a Friday, not a market day, the county town · Sabin Dain, and the reader being ahead of the plot.** She comes down the county post and walks the last four miles, and she did not come to be answered, she came to be asked. **She asks to be recorded before anybody offers her a chair — name, office, the hour, the number of the form, and the sentence *I am not a member of anything, and if anybody ever tells you otherwise in a room you should ask them for the number of the list* — and the clerk of the county council writes it and reads it back and she corrects one word, *number* was not *figure*.** Being recorded is the most frightening thing anybody can do to an officer of that office and the only thing that makes an officer answerable, and she has been nearly asked four times in three counties in eleven years and has spent nine months wishing in each case that somebody had gone on asking. She wants that no family in this country is ever again removed from a record by a name nobody entered, and she has wanted it since she was twenty-two, and nobody improves it. **And then the sentence, standing up, at the fourth hour, to about nine people including two clerks: the office is not asking you to tell us anything; the office is asking you to confirm a figure; a figure has no name in it; in four years there will be a number in a file with no name attached to it and that number will be about this county and nobody will be able to say whose hand made it — not because the hand has been concealed, because a figure does not need one, and it is not coming because anybody thought of it in a room with a clock on the wall, it is coming because it is cheaper than names and there is not one instrument in this kingdom that can make it more expensive.** She describes the four Crown instruments in this county back to the room and asks the question she came for, **does this county intend to use the figure**, and gets *no*, and Marda Oakes gives her reason for the minute: a figure is only a number until somebody needs it, and the only thing that makes a number into a weapon is a person frightened of what is behind it. Lysa Fenn is in the room, having walked the eleven miles out of Merefen in the rain that morning and said so to nobody, and says the cheapest thing anybody has said in eleven years: **a witness is the cheapest instrument in the kingdom and the only one that cannot be withdrawn** — a return expires, a notice stops only people who read it, a finding is a thing a court says and can be got out of a file in nine years, and a woman who says a thing out loud to nine people and has a clerk write it down and lets her correct the clerk is a figure in a book that answers a question for as long as the book is dry. **In a gateway in the rain, to a man she has not met, she refuses to discuss the *Keeper* column, and when he does not press she says thank you, and goes on foot down the county road with a bag, and the post is three days either way, and she is at her desk in the provincial town on the sixteenth of that month.**

**Ch 95 · the sixteenth of Greenmonth, a Tuesday, not a market day · in Merefen, from a document, and the second severance closing in the wrong country.** Four leaves come down the line in a basket on the back of a toll-taker who does not ask, and the first is a reading: **the ninth line of the index and the first line carry press four, die eleven and two impressions of one season, the ninth's county cell is ruled and empty, and the ninth is the only one of the nine marked *answered* eleven days after the tender — and a clerk who is the grandson of the only frightened hand in four hundred leaves signs nine words: I can tell you the press and I can tell you the die and I have had both leaves in my two hands under a lamp and I am not able to tell you the hand.** The second leaf is Merrow's own entry, on his own motion and not on a fee, in his own city, produced by a creditor's office in a matter in which no court in this county has jurisdiction. The third is four lines in a hall: **a creditor's press is the worst place in the world to keep a roll of persons and the only place in the world where nobody can quietly take it out of the book, and I have argued that for eleven years and I am now the man who is doing it to my own city, and I would like the argument minuted and the minute put where a Crown's man will find it in about nine years, and I do not know how.** The fourth thing on the table is the four conditions on a wrapper, and **he has broken all four in four leaves in a fortnight on purpose and has not written them down as broken, and that is how a person breaks a condition and gets away with it: not by denying it, but by not putting it next to the thing.** The chapter's spine, in Aren Kest's mouth, and it is the volume's whole argument about records: **a press-mark does not lie about a hand the way a name does — a press-mark is a fact about an object and is the same shape every time the die is dressed, and if two leaves of one book carry the same press and the same die then they came out of the same room and there is nothing in that to argue with; a name is a fact about a person, and a person can be got at, and the getting at him can be refused, and he can be alive or dead, and he has been alive the entire eleven years** — so anybody holding both leaves can set the ninth against the first, and he cannot check the hand, and neither can anybody in this county, and it is not because the book is locked. **And at the seventh hour the two instruments made in this county in the last fortnight are on one table: an Archive that can be served and a holding in a city that cannot, exact opposites, neither wrong, and the moment somebody joins them one of the two has to be the wrong one.**

**Ch 96 · the twenty-second of Greenmonth, a Monday, not a market day · Mara Vey, and the office's paper, and a copyist who is right.** Halven's disclosure is read into a record: four paragraphs in his own hand, dated the fifth of Embermonth, YR 312, at the tenth hour, the volume that was not on its shelf and whose removal carries no name, office, authority, destination, fee or initial, a schedule for three bells of which two are removed with no office against either and the third is entered retained and is the bell four hundred yards up a road in this county, and a schedule of dispositions whose county column runs to eleven other counties with a kiln word against one of them, and **I am not going to say the word; I have said it in a room once, to two people, and I have not been able to find out since which of them wrote it down.** Beside it, produced out of a wrapper, the endorsement: **Memorandum of endorsement, the office of the marshal's emergency office, the fourth of Embermonth, YR 312, the writ of enquiry on the reeve's records discharged, number 4 of the season** — and the two together are the first document in this case in which the Crown's shield and the Crown's payment for a book are in one paragraph, which happened because two officers of one office at two desks did the ordinary thing their own desk did. **Halven came two hundred miles on his own money to be in the room and nobody asked him one question, and he counted twice, and asked for that to be entered, and was entered, and was told the reason there is a page cannot be entered.** Mara enters the disclosure as a copy with the hour, his reason, and the fact that the office has no power to compel and none to protect, and stops a quarter of an inch above the paper, **because she has reached the part where a keeper of records is going to have an opinion about a man and she is not going to have it in this book, and she has four and the first is that he is a coward and the second is that he is the only person in eleven years who has done anything about a thing, and they are not the same size, which is why neither is going in.** **Alish Renn, 30, second copyist, on a warrant of Mara's own signed in the ninth month and not entered in the press** — a permit in a drawer is a thing a man with shears and forty minutes can take a leaf out of — and the correction she is given and is right about, in her own voice, having been a copyist for two years with a bar on it: **a day-book is not a file, a file is not a record, a record is not a roll; a file is a place where a fact about a person goes and the person is supposed to be able to see it; a record is a place where a fact goes and the person is not supposed to see it and often is not told; a roll is a place where a fact about a person goes and nobody can take it out and everybody can add to it. And which of the three is this office is the question I have been trying to ask for two months, and nobody can tell me by looking, and the only way anybody in this county ever finds out is if somebody with a claim comes in.** Her want, in a room, because there is no other room: **to be a keeper with a post and my name in the day-book.** And Sarn Oyelaran's key, and the third post that has been in the day-book since the sixteenth of Greenmonth and has not been signed, and the reason. **Nobody is charged, and he keeps the post — he was in the fourth press two hundred miles off on the twenty-third of Greenmonth and will be in it on the twenty-fourth — and the endorsement stands and is not withdrawn because the office that gave it has no procedure for withdrawing an endorsement and will not build one inside a quarter.** The sixth entry of the not writing this year, and the last line is that she has written the fifth decision down nowhere and has had it since the eighth of Embermonth.

**Ch 97 · the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth, a Wednesday, not a market day · Tovan Kest, ordinary work, a yard, and a bill.** The road is shut. Eleven tons of stone in four carts, a fortnight with the Sedge Reach closed to carts and open to people on foot, and the yard at **fourteen days with Pei Tarrow still in it, the same figure as the fourth of Hearthmonth, written in the week-book on the Monday with the hour against it, and the four days from the fifth of Wolfmonth still on the page in four entries in a hand his brother would know and not tidied into one.** The bench is asked for the third time and says yes for the third time in about six minutes, and **Reeve Aldis Fenn asks that the minute carry that Tovan Kest said in the fourth of Frostmonth that a bench asked three times and saying yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription, and that the bench knows it and asked anyway, because that is a different thing from not knowing.** The Sedge Reach is read on the twenty-fourth at the seventh hour by the guild's man with the levels, alone, with Tovan Kest standing next to him because the bench minuted it: **two fingers at the nose, eleven at the tail, a washtub, and it has been a washtub since the sixth of Mudmonth, which is most of a year, and the honest repair is eleven tons and a fortnight, and the reason there is anybody else in the room is that the man who read it twice before went out of the county at the fourth hour of a market morning and was gone eighty-eight days — and there is a first time in nineteen years that a shut road is a problem instead of a wage.** Then the bill, itemised, **and it foots in pence on the page before a word of it goes anywhere: eleven markets lost or held late and thin, twenty-nine marks nineteen shillings and sixpence; tolls not taken on the shut wharf road over one hundred and fourteen days, three marks nineteen shillings; three Tuesday barges held off, four marks two shillings; cartage, stone, lime, oak and nails, two marks nine shillings; and a fifth line with no figure against it at all — the scour at the third pier, nose, uninspected from the ninth of Embermonth — for a total of fifty marks, one shilling and sixpence, which is two thousand four hundred and eighteen pence, and the empty line is not in the total because there is nothing in it to total.** Beside it, in a different book and never in the same sentence: **eleven marks and fourpence owed by a trust in abeyance, two marks and three quarters at eight pence a day on the cutting contract, and twelve marks and tenpence for sixteen weeks and two days of a copying post at three shillings a week, unclaimed, in the county's hands, for a post the county's surveyor filled in Thawmonth — and a post is a cage, and a cage is a place a man is sent for and also a place a man is sent away from, and she wrote the first half of that out in Fallowmonth in four lines and did not see the second half coming, and she is not going to pretend now that she did not see it coming.** The line with nothing in it is read on the twenty-fourth at the fourth hour, and the man who reads it comes in at the fifth with a wet sleeve and says it is the best thing anybody in this county has done this year and it is one line with nothing in it. **And at the ninth hour, in a yard with four men and a boy of fifteen at a bench-board, the sentence, said once, in his own words, spent, not to be improved on: I am not going to re-teach you your own brother. You can still do all of it — that is the part I have got to be fair about, and you would know in about four seconds if I was not — and you cannot do them in the order, and I do not know what the order was, and if I told you what I think it was you would do it my way and not yours, and in about a year you would be doing a thing in a yard that is not a morning. And I am not going to sit there at about the sixth hour on a Tuesday and say it out loud six or seven times for a year.** Nobody in that yard has the word brother in a scene. **It is not cruelty and it is not healing: on the fourth of Hearthmonth, at a gate, with his hand on a rope and the yard going on round them, he said a different sentence so that on the day he said nothing his brother would not think it was his brother, and he has carried that sentence a hundred and ten days and has decided the new one is worse, because the old one was about a thing you would lose and this one is about a thing he is not going to do, and a man can be held to a thing he is not going to do.**

**Ch 98 · the twenty-sixth of Greenmonth, a Friday, not a market day · Mara Vey, and the settlement, in a county's own book.** A fair copy of a minute of a bench in a county that is not on any map. **The list is not burned. It is superseded** — the magistrate's instrument withdrawn in a room, the physician's four-year-old finding read out, the three hands named, and the superseding entered as a fact and not a defeat, **and the whole of his testimony is four sentences: the list was the only instrument I had; I have had a physician's four pages in the next room for four years and have not acted on them; I am glad to be shown a second instrument and sorry it took four years and a stranger; and I want the county to hear the word stranger from me and not from him, because he is a man off a road and I am the magistrate of this county.** And then the other half, which is the part that will be argued about for nine years: **I am not sorry about the fever. The physician is right about the fever and he was right four years ago and I have had both of those in the same room and only one of them was true in the way I needed. I was wrong about the names and I have never once been wrong about the clay.** The three hands, with the third still a letter and the bench having entered that it cannot say whether the third is a hand and has resolved not to find out this year. **Two hundred and fifty-four tiles re-ground by hand and wedged back, twelve marks one shilling and sevenpence over two years, in the standing water; about thirteen men out with the number written down; the softening slowed and not stopped, six boards in the last nine days against about forty in the nine before, entered with the hour and no opinion; Ash's dead stay Ash's and the county's own book says so in the shortest line in it; four hundred yards of the Ninebridge's parapet re-laid in tile by the man who wanted to be a mark and not a mouth; and nobody has read a name out of a sheet in that county since the fourth day of a month this county does not use, entered as a rule and not a favour.** The physician's four pages did not travel and were not expected to; a minute that says a thing was read out is a fact about a room four hundred miles off. **She puts the four lines she copied in her own fair hand six months ago beside the fair copy, reads them both the way a keeper reads two things, squares them, and does not join them, because a keeper who joins them has written down that the man in the first is in the second, and she cannot check it, and if she writes down that she cannot check it then a reader in nine years reads it as a statement that he is in it.** Then the third look at the eighth ruled line, **in the eighth month after the first look and the seventh month after the second, not filled, and the wanting recorded as not the same wanting, and the reason not given, because a keeper who explains a difference to herself in a day-book has made a document about a person and there is no person in this and that is the point.** The brine weights, cited and not filed, two lines five days apart in one week of Fallowmonth, read together, the old reason written out in full and then the new one because the reason has changed: **a ledger is a claim, and this office has learned this year, from a county four hundred miles off, that a claim about people is not a record of them.** And last, four lines in a hand that is not a clerk's, sent north because she asked: **she does not know whether re-grinding what was fired is a good idea, and she is going to do it anyway, and she would like somebody to write down that she said so, in a book that is not hers, and she has not been told whether it can be** — and the sentence about it that the man off the road gives, in a room, to three women, is that it is the first sentence anybody in that county has produced in nine months that is not about what the county has got.

**Ch 99 · the twenty-seventh of Greenmonth, a Saturday, a market day · the holding, and a name out of a copy.** Four leaves again, and the first is a purchase: **the roll of persons of a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths, in three hands, of which the third is this house, entered in the counting house of the tolls of a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, at the fourth hour, on the steward's own paper, in his own hand, not on a fee, and taken as a transaction at a farthing of salt — because a farthing of salt is the smallest thing that office has ever bought anything with and it keeps a column for it.** The buyer is **the salt merchant's brother, entered in the house's ordinary way as a party to a transaction and in no other way and without any opinion, and he is a man who deals in salt on a coast, has wanted a canal wharf for nine years, has been told twice in writing and once in a market that a wharf costs money, and does not know what he has bought, and thinks it is a debt.** He is not a villain. **And the second leaf is the argument, minuted in four lines and not improved on, and the man who wrote it signs his own sentence: a creditor's press is the worst place in the world to keep a roll of persons and the only place where nobody can quietly take it out of the book, and I have argued that for eleven years and I am now the man doing it to my own city, and I would like the minute put where a Crown's man will find it in about nine years, and I do not know how; and no single office in that county can add a name to that roll or take one off it, there are three, and a person restored is restored when two of the three agree and the third is allowed to be wrong, and I have been a creditor for twenty-two years and this is the first claim I have ever seen that can be split three ways, and I have built it in four weeks out of my own book because nobody in that county could build it, and a creditor who refuses a thing of this kind is a creditor who thinks he is a good man.** **The third leaf is the only new name in this business that has ever been read out in this county, and nobody asked for it: Ansel Tarrant, twenty-one years in that house, the clerk who signs the four-hundred-leaf register in the hand his grandmother signed it in, and the second frightened signature in four hundred leaves, and he asked for the copy to carry the remark and said he does not know why he signed it frightened and that his grandmother did not know either, and that a book four hundred leaves long that cannot be got at by anybody is the only place either of them has ever been frightened where it was safe to be, and he would like that written down and would like nothing else.** Entered against a person, with a claim number against nothing, because a county is not a party and a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists is not a party. **No want is stated for him, and the chapter says so in about four sentences and does not sentimentalise him: there is no column for a want in a creditor's book, in a county's roll of persons, or in a day-book over a weigh-house, and the absence of a column is the point.** And the buyer turns out to be the brother of the man who owns the building the day-book is in, and she is not going to ask him, and the reason is four words: *I work next door* — a person who owns a room cannot be the person who finds out what is in it, and that is not a rule anybody has ever printed and it is the rule the office runs on. Sarn Oyelaran gets the answer to a question she has been carrying for a year, in nine seconds, about a name in a day-book against a person being the one job in that office that is not a record. **The fourth leaf is a copy of a copy of a copy of a county column of dispositions, running to eleven other counties, and eight of the eleven have a boundary oath on a stone — a long field, a boundary, a season, a stone renewed in public in a year anybody there can name, by men who are still alive and can be asked what they swore and to what and in whose name — and three have nothing in the leaf at all.** She does the arithmetic, stops, and does not write the result anywhere. **It is a pattern and it is arithmetic and it is not a revelation, it can be seen by exactly two people, a registrar who sold the book and a keeper who has a copy of a page of it, and neither of them can say what an oath has to do with a severance, and it is entered as a question and not as a finding, with the hour, against nobody, and the eight are left where they are.** And last, in the same room, a tile on a coping at the Ninebridge that the county's books do not mention, known about for ten months because a man off a road said he did not know what it was, not looked at, not touched, not explained, and: **a thing you do arithmetic on is not a thing you have looked at.**

**Ch 100 · the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, YR 313, a Monday, a market day, the fourth hour, the lowest water of the year · the last image.** The nose of the third pier is out of the water for the first time since the sixth of Mudmonth, four carts on the gravel approach, about forty men, **and a road warden of the Merefen bridge guild is on the deck with a tool bag counting load points — not there as the hand of anything: on the roll, a vote, a vote on a question that is not about him, does not hold the crossing, does not vote, said in a hall in Frostmonth that he would come when he was wanted, came on the twenty-fourth and has been on the deck every working morning since.** It is a habit and not a working and it goes in no book. **His left palm is warm at the fourth hour and he does not need it, and nothing in the sound is his.** The bell goes at the fourth hour and a half on the Old Toll bridge, **which is a bell with a band of forty-one rivets round it and a dull red seam in the metal where the band was closed, and it is rung by a boy of sixteen with two years of an apprenticeship who is still not good at it, holding one end of a line, and a carpenter who is a hand and not a keeper, because that is the arrangement, and the arrangement is not his**, and at the far end of the line, on a coping on a river this county does not have, a tile-cutter of forty-one and the woman who keeps the road strike what they have, which is not a bell and is not nothing, and the fair copy says only that there is no bell in that county and there has never been one and we have never had the word for what it is that they do in a market town in a fen on a Monday. **And the county's surveyor has paid the guild's man with the levels six pence, out of a box, for a written report on the scour at the third pier, and has told nobody, and it is the smallest thing anybody in this county has paid for in two years and the only one anybody in either country has been glad of — and the difference between a bench that asks and a man in a box that pays six pence is that one of them happened in a room with a door on a lane and the other happened because somebody was frightened, and the frightened one is the one that works.** He has two half tiles and one of them has a saw mark on the back of it that the woman who fired it has never seen. **At the fifth hour a list comes down the line on the twenty-ninth in a fair hand, four hundred and six words, itemised, against four hundred words and about four hundred and nine people the last time, and every line of it can be checked, and it is the county's answer to the fourth of its own terms.** It carries the supersession, the two hundred and fifty-four, the thirteen men, the standing water, the re-laid parapet, the rule, and the woman's four lines, and the county's own figure about itself — **two thousand nine hundred and six tiles fired on the roll against two thousand four hundred and fifty people, put in one line by the county for the first time in nineteen years with the sentence that anybody who wants to do that arithmetic is welcome to come and do it on a bridge, and the two thousand four hundred and fifty never in a sum with a Crown's four thousand or a served schedule's seven hundred and twenty.** **And in the middle of a column of prices, with no rule above it and no rule below it, in the hand of a man with a ledger: one man of this county has not been spoken to since the ninth week of a room at the assizes, he is at a kiln, the clerk of the roll does not know which kiln and neither does he, the mark on his tile agrees with the entry and both are not either, his name is in three books, and it is entered as a number and not as a name because the second of the county's own terms is forty-one words long and the county has kept it.** She reads every line in fifty minutes and writes nothing, enters it against no person with the hour and no opinion, and goes down it a second time line by line, and **the arithmetic is sound**, and she finds nothing to argue with, and she finds one thing she cannot put down, **and it is not asking her anything, and there is no instrument in this kingdom that can make it ask her anything.** Then the page with the four lines on it from the twenty-second of Fallowmonth, looked at for a minute and a half with no lamp, and the entry: the same entry, entered a second time, against nobody, with the hour, in a hand that has stopped asking — **a number, and a county, and an hour, nothing else against it** — and then the eighth time this month that the not writing is entered, and the day this office wants to stop entering that sentence is not going to be the day anything has changed, it is going to be the day nobody has asked anything for a long time, and she will not know the day it happened. **And four hundred miles off, in a room above a kiln, a man of fifty-eight who fired the tiles in that kiln last spring does not know which kiln he fires: alive, employed, a fire in his hearth on the nights the county can see it from a road, not spoken to since the ninth week of a room at the assizes, his name in three books and all three right about him, and no instrument in two countries that will do one thing about it. And the woman who reads for a living has written the whole of it down, with the hour on it, against nobody, in a day-book over a weigh-house, in a room with a door on a lane, four pence a year, and there is no name on the line and there is no opinion on the line, and the pen is down.**

## Volume 02 close-block note — Chapters 51–100 read as a finished book

**The block-level note for the batch is above. What follows is what the fifty chapters turn out to be, read together, and it is not a list of what happened.** Volume 02 is a book about a records office and it never once says so. It opens in **a form** — five ruled columns, one of them headed *Keeper*, and a marginal instruction nobody understands — and it closes in **an entry**, and the two documents are the same document fifty chapters apart: a thing a person can be contradicted in, in a book nobody can serve, in a room with a door on a lane, by a woman at four pence a year. Between them: **a count instead of a list, twice, in three trades, in two countries, and neither count is refused and both expire, and the Crown's escalation is an argument and not an instrument.** The Crown's side of the book is an office doing its job — a registrar who quotes a fee for a book that should not exist, a clerk of twenty-eight who copies a whole leaf including the blanks because her grandmother was a copyist for forty years and was struck off a parish roll once, a woman of thirty-three who fills in a column she is frightened of and asks twice in writing to have a reason for it and is told it is a standard form instruction, and a magistrate who can beat any instrument in the kingdom with its own construction and is frightened of the one construction she cannot beat, because it is built on the fact that a court is not a person. **Nobody in this book is a villain and four people in it are better than the man it follows.** Ash is a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths and two thousand four hundred and fifty people that has spent nineteen years being a claim about itself: **a fired tile is the only record in that county a person cannot alter, and a record a person cannot alter is a claim about a person, and the founding act of the place was the attempt to build one, and it protected them for nineteen years and it is the disease.** The cure was built by accident in four months by a county nobody had asked anything — four hundred and nine people through a room in eleven weeks, a struck person pressing their own soft tile before two witnesses, and a clerk's list read aloud four times a day by a girl of nineteen who did not know that was a job she had, and who is not to be blamed and is not to be absolved, and **is the fastest mark in the County of Ash and the volume does not know that and neither does she.** The book's two great professionals are a rollkeeper of forty-four who kept one roll that could not be read twice for nineteen years and signed a thing she had refused twice on the ground that a roll that can be wrong is not a weaker roll, it is a roll that has to be read twice, and a keeper of twenty-nine who built a filing room out of the principle that a thing which cannot be served is a thing that answers to the person it is about, **and who then spent nine months of the year not asking a man a question she had promised in Fallowmonth not to ask, and entered the not asking seven times in a fortnight in Greenmonth, and found the cost of the crossing with the one instrument she cannot stop using, which is a date, and did not ask.** The book's two settlements are both refusals to destroy anything: **the list is superseded, not burned, and Ash's dead stay Ash's; the roll is in three hands and the third is still a letter and the bench has entered that it cannot say whether the third is a hand and has resolved not to find out this year; and the only kind of holding that has ever answered a question in this case is a holding that cannot be served, which is why the last document of the volume is a man entering a roll of nine hundred and forty-one hearths into the book of a creditor's office in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, at a farthing of salt, and minuting in his own hand that a creditor's press is the worst place in the world to keep a roll of persons and the only place where nobody can quietly take it out of the book, and asking for the minute to be put where a Crown's man will find it in about nine years.** The book's two men who lose something and are not consoled are a bridgewright of thirty-two who can cross and has lost the order of an ordinary Tuesday morning with his brother and cannot say which path gave him the way he stands, **and has written both down in a notebook in a coat and neither in a form, and has told a keeper of records every number he has because a man who reports his own costs is the only kind there is and he is not going to stop being that one over a morning** — and a man of fifty-eight in a room above a kiln four hundred miles off who is alive, employed, correct in three books, and does not know which kiln he fires. **What the volume argues, in five trades, in two countries, and never in a sentence:** a record a person can contradict is a record of that person, and a record that cannot be contradicted is a claim about that person; a list with the names taken off is a figure and a figure cannot be refused and a figure expires for want of a party; a thing written down can be taken from you and what cannot be taken from you is being asked; a day-book is not a file and a file is not a record and a record is not a roll; a costed thing is not a true thing and a true thing is not a consented thing and a hall is not a person; **a road is a line and two hands, a date, and a witness at each end who is not the person keeping the road, and if the witness is not there on the day then the road does not open and it does not fail, and nobody is to say afterwards that it failed** — which is a sentence a woman with a roll of tile-cutters' tools said in a market in a county that had never refused anything in nineteen years, four hundred miles from the town that worked it out first, and neither of them ever heard the other say it. And the thing the volume does not do, and is not permitted to do, and which is therefore the only thing a reader is left holding: **it does not say why he can cross, and it does not say what is on the coping of the second span at the Ninebridge, and it does not say what a hand laid flat on a book is, and the maker of it is dead and it has been on that coping since before the roll was begun.**

**One line added by the Volume 02 close, and it is a pointer and not a note.** The volume has been read as a finished book and audited block by block against the outline's cards; the audit, the promise-by-promise audit, the deviations that became canon, the state ledger, the three hooks and the two findings the close had to make are in **`outline/volumes/volume-02-close.md`**, and the canon is `state/continuity.md` **items 255–269**. **None of the fifty entries above has been rewritten and no entry's meaning has changed.** Two figures were corrected in place and both are marked: the promise's age in Chapter 92, which the close found to be one hundred and ninety-three days and not the two hundred and twenty-three the files carried, and Chapter 70's "eight weeks," which stands as a known defect. **Read the close before writing anything in Volume 03.**

## Volume 03 index

**Volume 03 — The Hearth Marches (Chapters 101–150)**, outlined in `outline/volume-03.md`. Main world: the thirtieth of Greenmonth, YR 313, to the twenty-fourth of Hearthmonth, YR 313 — two hundred and thirty-four days elapsed, two hundred and thirty-five inclusive. Two crossings: main-world absences of ten and one hundred and seventeen days, path intervals of nine days and thirteen months. Stage 2 held and proved; Stage 3 not reached and not to be.

| Blocks | Chapters | Content |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 101–110 | The Figure and the Stone: a bench asked a fourth time, a column of eleven counties, a delegation from a border county, a form that printed a man's name as an example, an offer, three rules, a contract, a leaf, and a bell rung on the wrong day. **Written.** |
| 2 | 111–120 | The River Changes Course: the fair copy that is not a message, a second count answered, the six terms put to the reeve, a crew that goes seven days early on somebody else's calendar, nine days in the Marches, and a refusal that is correct. |
| 3 | 121–130 | Thirteen Months, the first third: the council, the salt, the working that is refused and dated, the struck, the vault, the league, and an oath that can be broken. |
| 4 | 131–140 | Thirteen Months, the second third: the guild's waterworks, the river in the wrong month, command refused in nine seconds, the road given away, the notice, and the arithmetic stopping. |
| 5 | 141–150 | The Span: the plan that is not his, the boundaries re-cut, the road given to a brother and a guild on the county's own form, nine hours on a failing structure, a limp, the return, the account, the last image. |

## Batch 0011 — Volume 03, Chapters 101–110, "The Figure and the Stone"

Block 1, and **main world on all ten days**, which is what the card asks for and what makes this the quietest block in the volume so far. POVs **Aren ×6 (101, 103, 105, 109, 110, and the road warden inside 104 and 106) / Mara ×2 (102, 106, with the Marches' letter in 103) / Tovan ×2 (102, 108) / Lysa Fenn ×1 (107) with Reeve Aldis Fenn inside it and in 104 and 110.** **No crossing in either direction anywhere in the ten, no path scene, no System display, no gate, no stage claimed, no revelation, no mechanism explained and no authority claimed.** The one italic line in the batch is in Chapter 110's notebook and is a quotation of the printed drift warning, not a voice. **Two new names and four slots unfilled, as the cast plan allows: Thessa Wend (104) and Oswin Rill (104).** Nobody dies; nobody is killed; no arch goes down and no glass breaks. **The tile on the coping is not touched, not looked at and not named, and its device is named by nobody. The eighth ruled line in the served schedule is not looked at. The brine weights are neither mentioned nor filed. No Ash figure is on the page in any of the ten, and the 456 is not on the page in any form.** *Culling Order*, *custodian*, *Gatehouse* and the marshal's name do not appear. The private-keeper clause is printed, quoted and cited in a file and is **not** explained in a sentence by anybody, and Sabin Dain does not explain it and does not change her want. **The breach is not repaired, no apology is made available to anybody, and the not asking is entered for the ninth time in Chapter 106.** Weekday chain from `outline/volume-03.md` §17: **Greenmonth 30 Tue (not market) · Longlight 1 Wed (not) · 4 Sat (market) · 9 Thu (market) · 13 Mon (not) · 16 Thu (market) · 19 Sun (not) · 23 Thu (market) · 27 Mon (not) · 30 Thu (market).** Every one of the ten chapter dates and every market-day call in the prose matches.

**Ch 101 · the thirtieth of Greenmonth, YR 313, a Tuesday, not a market day, the day after the low water of the year · Aren, and the bench asked a fourth time.** Nine men pointing on the third bay of the Sedge Reach and a road mended and holding; the third joint done three times for two different reasons and only one of them in the trade; a wagon four inches over on the gravel and the bench-clerk refusing to enter it, on the grounds that **a place where things are kept out is not a place where things are entered.** In the hall over the wharf the bench is asked a fourth time, for the Old Toll, and is given two marks and a book, and the whole asking takes five minutes. **Three things the reeve asks the minute to carry, and the third is the reason this page is in the roll:** a man of the guild has been paid six pence twice out of a surveyor's box, on the twenty-fourth and the thirtieth, for written reports on the Sedge Reach and the Old Toll, and has told nobody either time, and tells the bench himself in about eleven words, and the county's surveyor is in the room and is made to say that the box is his own house and has no name, and the minute carries it. **And a man with a cart asks whether a name entered twice in one book is one name or two, and the bench-clerk says one, and has not entered the second, and entered the first and has been sorry for sixteen weeks and cannot take it out, and enters his own error in the margin in a fair copy.** The reeve asks for the Old Toll on the bench-clerk's own ground: a bench asking for a thing it has no power over, and saying so in a room. The guild's man with the levels reads the Old Toll in the afternoon and his report is copied into the roll for the first time in the roll's history: **a crossing in trust in abeyance is a crossing nobody is paid for, and a crossing nobody is paid for is read by whoever is passing, and being read by whoever is passing is not the same as being read by anybody whose name is on it.** And at the eighth hour the road warden asks a gateway whether there is anything from a county it has never been to, and there is nothing, and the asking had no reason under it, and it turned out to be the reason.

**Ch 102 · the first of Longlight, a Wednesday, not a market day · Mara and Tovan, and two letters in one bag.** A room over a weigh-house and a yard on Weir Street in the same chapter for the first time in the volume, and the two of them do not discuss the ninth of Embermonth of the year before. **Ivet Salter's letter, dated the twenty-ninth of Greenmonth, three days in the post, in a small flat hand thirty-one years a copyist:** four parts, and the fourth repeats the flat line she put at the foot of a letter two years ago — *a register answers what was entered on the shelf and nothing else* — **and adds the half she did not say then: a return is a fact about a person and not a claim, a person can be asked about a fact and not about a claim, and there is nothing in this kingdom that can put anything in front of a man in that county to ask him anything.** And she sends the fair copy of the leaf unasked, having weighed for two days what asking would cost, and asks for nothing and signs nothing. **Mara copies the fourth part out entire into the press and enters the hour against no person, and the reason is one line and is not about Salter.** Then a waggon from a border county four hundred miles off brings a wrapper addressed to the yard **and**, underneath, to the keeper of records at Merefen in the north, and there is no clerk in a yard, so it goes up the two flights. **Two sheets, not copies of each other:** a letter of business — no writ, no bell, no Crown's schedule, a boundary that is the water, a stone sworn in public every year by the men who hold the ditch, four villages gone white in the ground, a request for a bridgewright and not a Crown man, a guild refused a wage three times, and the last line *if the answer is no, write the word and we will not write again this year* — and a return. **The return's five columns are printed in full. The third column is Aren's own words from a yard on the first of Embermonth, copied by a county clerk out of the office's printed worked example, and the fourth is Oswin Rill, seed-carter, of a village in that county.** The instruction is on the reverse, in the office's hand, verbatim as Chapter 51 has it, and under it the worked example, and the worked example is a real man and a real guild and has been on the foot of every form of the series for ten months. **Mara's finding, in four sentences, and it is the batch's hinge: a county clerk did exactly what the sheet told him to do; the only worked instance the office has ever printed is a real name in a real county; and every county that has ever filled in that column has been shown how, and the way shown is a man's own words saying what he is and what he is not — so it is not a case, it is a standard.** And at the end of the chapter, in a yard, **nothing that belongs to a man goes in this yard's book and only a thing set** — a contract is a thing set, his brother is not a party to it, and the yard takes the work.

**Ch 103 · the fourth of Longlight, a Saturday, a market day · Aren, and a column of eleven counties, and the arithmetic again.** A keeper of records who has not eaten, and bread and cheese eaten standing up, and the fourth leaf of the purchase in fair hand on the table. **Eleven counties and eight stones and three dashes, and the answer is the same as it was on the twenty-seventh of Greenmonth and he gets eight.** His contribution, and it is a bridgewright's sentence and not a filing room's: **a list is a thing you can refuse; a column is a thing you get put in** — a list has a person in it and the person knows and can say the whole of the line; a column has a county in it and the county does not know, and if it is a person it can be asked, and if it is a court it cannot be sued, and if it is ground with a stone on it it cannot do anything but be in the column. **And the question he asks is the one that opens the volume: what is the column headed.** Nothing — the copy is a copy of a copy of a copy, the foot of a page does not carry the head of a column, and the head is on a parent leaf in a creditor's press in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists, and a creditor's press is a thing that can be asked and not a thing that can be made to answer. **Mara enters the question with the hour and no opinion, and the entry is about arithmetic and not about a thing she has chosen not to do, and she says the difference is that a column is not anybody's business and a person is.** And at the seventh hour a carter brings word up the hill: a delegation at the upper post since first light, eleven of a council and two more and a board in a wagon, four hundred miles and eleven days, and a line said twice on purpose — *the stone is not to be sworn in the presence of anybody from this side.* **And the sentence goes round the market in a form nobody in the hall used**, and the only copy of the six lines in this county on the fourteenth of Longlight is in the head of an unemployed man in a market, and a document in one head is a record and a document in a hand is a claim. And the two of them stand at a table and say the thing the batch turns on: **the office did not have to do a single thing. It printed a form with a real name and a real guild on it, at the foot, as the way of doing the thing, and a clerk four hundred miles off did the rest on a wet day with a quill.** And down in the yard a woman of thirty-eight has been sitting on a bench with a board across her knees since first light, and the keeper of records goes down to ask her whether she wants tea, because she is curious, and enters that she was curious.

**Ch 104 · the ninth of Longlight, a Thursday, a market day · the guild hall over the wharf, and the delegation.** About forty standing and eleven sitting and a queue at the corridor press before the fourth hour. **The delegation speaks first, before anybody has asked it anything, and refuses outside command in seven sentences:** no writ, no bell, no Crown's schedule, the only body in the county that meets, four hundred years — corrected on the page to **a hundred and forty** — of other people's commands, the last one that worked so well that the ground has gone white under four villages, and *we are not a rescue.* **The six lines are read off a four-foot board of lath and lime in chalk, and they are fixed for the rest of the volume: (1) the road is not to be made to carry a person; (2) no name is to be read out on the far side of the line by anybody from the north, and no name written in the line's book at the north end; (3) the north is not to send anybody who can be asked to come and look; (4) the stone is not to be renewed in the north's presence; (5) the road is kept by a hand at each end and a witness at each end, and the witness at the Marches' end is chosen by the Marches; (6) the line is not to be opened on a day the Marches have not named.** **The road warden says yes to the fourth line in about nine seconds and asks that it be entered that he did not ask what it means, and the reason is in nine seconds and not in the room.** Then **the guild's first hand speaks before it is asked and states four terms, and the third of them — a stranger's labour taken at the guild's rate and paid in the county's coin at the crossing, and not in goods — is better than anything the north has brought**, and the fourth is that the guild does not work with a stranger's oath in its water and will not be told what that means, which the room takes as a trade superstition and which is not explained. The minute-writer of the council asks the question nobody had asked — *who pays the guild* — and the council's answer is a nine-year-old line in its own minute saying it cannot pay a wage because the men with money are the nine who renew the stone. **And then the delegation says the real reason it came four hundred miles: the fourth column of the return has a man in it who says he did not put himself there, and he is in the hall.** Oswin Rill, forty-four, seed-carter, of Nether Dray, comes half way into the room and stops. **He has wanted for two years for a man's own hand to be the only hand that puts his name anywhere, and he says the price out loud: the office that filled it in will send that county nothing at ever, the county will get worse, and his own village will blame him, and a price is worth nothing in a village and is worth something in a room.** And at the eleventh hour, **the first question a stranger has ever asked Thessa Wend, 38, of Sallow Marsh, who has read two gauges twice a day for eleven years and has eight thousand and thirty-one lines in a book in a hut at a weir:** *how deep is it at the Sallow side in the last week before the low?* — eleven at the weir post, nine at the Sallow foot, the Sallow foot is the one the ground is under, and nobody has ever asked her for one of them and she would like somebody from this side to ask and has wanted it since the first year and is not going to say why. And she asks him to tell her in about nine seconds, the way he said yes to the fourth line, and he does not answer her. **Nothing is decided. The bench voted on nothing, which is a first in the roll. And the last thing that happened in that hall is a woman putting her hand on a board that came four hundred miles in a wagon and asking: who is it that is going to read it?**

**Ch 105 · the thirteenth of Longlight, a Monday, not a market day · Sabin Dain's letter, and the office's arithmetic about a man.** A letter cut open with a knife along the left edge by somebody who wants the contents and not the shape of them, four days in the post against a standing expectation of three. **Three things in three foldings.** The first is a dated standing offer: **a road warden's rank in the eastern counties — a command and not a custody, the grade given to a man who is answerable to a person and not to a community, and which may be held by a man nobody appointed, because the office has found that a man appointed by a community may refuse — eleven marks a year, a clerk, the right to send men; and a sealed record of himself, kept by the office, produced to any body that asks the office a question about him, and never produced to him, because a record a person cannot see is a record a person cannot contradict; for both of the above, the eastern paths; made out three years ago and held open, the number of persons not stated.** And at the foot of it, in the office's hand, unremarked: **the instrument of the ninth of this month a year past, in force, not superseded, barring the assembly of any body of this county in respect of the object named in it — and the object is not named on the sheet, and the road warden has wanted to know what it is since the twelfth of Greenmonth, and he writes four candidates in a notebook and crosses out the last three words, *ask somebody who is not me*, because there is nobody in this county who is not him at anything.** His reading of the offer, which is the batch's central line: **the office is not recruiting what he can do, it is recruiting what a form did to him, and it worked that out from its own paperwork.** The second thing is a fair copy of one leaf of the return, nine days in the Archive, with an office's marginal note of nine words — *Fourth column relied upon as the office of record. Return forwarded. No further returnable date* — and **the box headed *State the office, if any* is empty and has always been empty, for the reason a woman gave in a yard**, and the third column is his own words copied by a clerk, and **the entry beside the fourth column is Rill's own hand on the back of the same leaf, two years earlier: *I do not keep this. I have not kept this. I did not put my name in the fourth column. Put it back and tell them I said so.*** And the office has had that leaf on a shelf in a third press and has never turned it over. The third thing is a sheet folded twice, not the office's, dated separately, **saying that the offer was drafted three years ago, that she found the drawer, that she recognises the man in it, that she is sending this to him and not to his bench, that she does not believe it is going to work, that she has not said so to her own office, and the whole of her reason: an office that cannot get a name will get a figure, an office that cannot get a figure will get a rank, a man who takes the rank is a man who can be asked where a road is, and when he cannot say the office will have a man bound by a record he has never read, and she has watched it work on two men.** And the notebook question he writes before he does anything else with the page — *how many of the roads the office calls the eastern paths does the office know I hold, and where did it get the phrase* — and does not send, and the three lines he does seal are *Received. I am not able to answer on the eastern paths and I am not able to refuse them, because I do not know what they are.* And **at the bottom of the fair copy, under a rule, in the smallest hand on the page, in office furniture that is on every sheet the office has ever printed: *Retained. Series R.C. Eastern. Two classes.*** And he does not write it down and he does not go up the stair, because a line like that in a notebook is a thing that has to be explained and the only person he has to explain it to is four hundred yards away. And the notebook's fourth line: *a hole somebody made on purpose is the only writing that has never lied to me, and I have never once in thirty-two years been able to work out whether that is a good thing to be or a bad thing to be.*

**Ch 106 · the sixteenth of Longlight, a Thursday, a market day · Aren and Mara, and the three rules.** A working morning, a cup of cold tea left on the sill since the ninth. **Three rules entered in the day-book against the office and against no person, in a fair copy, with the hour, and it is the shortest thing in that office and the only thing in four years and a half entered by two people on the same morning. One (his, and hers is better than his: *a cost is entered before it is incurred, in the book of the person who pays it and not the book of the person who decides it, and an entry made before is not a promise and is not a confession*). Two (hers: *a thing that nobody dissents about is entered as though somebody had dissented, with the name and the hour* — the name of the thing, never the name of a person, on the strength of a county boy of nineteen who copied a reason onto a served schedule nine months ago and asked whether it would still be there in a year, and it is). Three: *an authority is entered with the words that made it, and an authority that nobody can name the source of is entered as a habit, and a habit is a thing a county does to itself when nobody is looking* — **and neither of them says where the last clause came from, and a reader has it, because a woman in a damp coat said it in a yard on Weir Street ten months ago and this keeper of records has four lines in her own hand and has never said so.** Beneath the rules, in the same ink, is the paragraph the volume requires: **that the rules were made because of the ninth entering of the not asking on this page, that they do not end it, that a rule about what is written down is not a rule about what is done, that a procedure built around a thing that is not being said is a procedure and not a cure, and that the day this office stops entering the not asking is not the day the rules have done their work and this office will not know which of the two days it has reached.** He says *Mara* and she says *do not* — *if you say thank you I have to write down that you said thank you* — and says give me the numbers. **And he gives her the numbers and not the morning:** two roads, one of which does not answer when it is called and has not since the first of Embermonth of the year before; twenty-six days and eighty-eight days of absence against a printed twenty-one, the printed figure unmoved and the office unable to say whose it is, said twice this year and not to be said a third time in a month; five days and sixty-seven days of error, a quarter and three quarters of the absence, not a constant, and *I wrote down in my own book two volumes ago that the error is in me and scales with the work, and I have now been out long enough twice to be able to say that I do not know whether that is a fact or a thing I wanted to be true* — and she enters it and enters that she has entered it. **And then she says the thing that makes it the ninth: the thing in the inside pocket of his coat on the ninth was not a bridgewright's thing, she saw the corner of it, she has known since the fifth of Greenmonth that there is a morning, she has not asked nine times, and there is no reason on the line and there is not going to be one.** She signs a page in her own book, which is not a thing a keeper does, and writes at the foot that it is a true copy, that it is in the only book in the office that cannot be served, that it is not to be copied or read out of the room, and that anybody in this county who is interested may come up the two flights and read it here, which has been the practice since the first year. **So the third rule is now a page anybody in this county can walk up a stair and take out in their head and use at the bench, and neither of the two people who wrote it will ever be asked whether it is true.** At the ninth hour the Marches' minute-writer comes up with the board-rubbing and asks for a true copy of the six lines in a book that is a person's and not an office's, *because a copy in a book in a room is a thing a court can have and a copy in a chamber over a barn is a thing a court cannot*, and she makes it, and **her own marginal note goes against the sixth line: *the line is not to be opened on a day the Marches have not named* — a condition of this office, and the office does not know what it is a condition of** — and he tells her where he intends to be on a day and she enters it with a date and a name, and it is the first name out of that county in her office in four years and a half and no Crown's hand is near it. And at the eleventh hour he comes down the two flights and stops on the bottom step: the third rule is the only one of the three that is about him, and he had let her write it, and he has a warrant with a district and a clerk and eleven marks a year in a sealed envelope two hundred miles off that he has not told her about, and he is going to cut a dressed block for a crossing a bench cannot pay for.

**Ch 107 · the nineteenth of Longlight, a Sunday, not a market day · Lysa Fenn, and the same building twice, four days apart.** One magistrate and no clerk. The paper was read out in the guild hall over the wharf on the fifteenth, a Wednesday, and the reeve was in that building and heard it and said nothing. **And at about the ninth hour a man of about fifty stands in the doorway and does not come further in, with a boundary of thirty-one years and five and fourpence in a twist of paper: the fee for a plan is four shillings and eightpence, which is fifty-six pence, against a note that would cost a shilling, and a shilling is four days of a common labourer. She notes it in a line and a quarter of an hour, takes the shilling, and enters the shilling against the court and not against him, and the reeve's office calls it a discouragement and is right about the arithmetic and wrong about what a court is for. He goes down the four steps. And in the same room, in the same week, in the same hand: eleven people in a county four hundred miles off, of whom not one is a person under this court's practice, and a proceeding about a watercourse that forty men on each side can be made parties to and did not swear. The two facts are the same sentence doing the same work, and the sentence is that a person can be heard, refused and appealed, and a thing that cannot be refused and cannot be appealed is not a court and is a form.** Her answer is four minutes and it is correct: no person is in default, nothing is pending, the instrument is spent upon this answer and requires no further returnable date. **And it is the same construction as the count of the ninth of Greenmonth, and it is the reason she is frightened, and the reason takes about nine seconds: a body is not a person is the sentence she has won eleven arguments with, and on the ninth of Greenmonth this county made the Bellweather Claims Archive a body corporate with a door, a number and the capacity to sue and to be sued, and she cannot keep the first half and drop the second, and she has looked for the sentence that lets her for an hour and it is not there, and she writes that on the back of a paper on a Sunday where nobody will find it unless somebody goes looking, which is a thing she has now done once.** Then Reeve Aldis Fenn comes in on the way to a chest upstairs and says the one thing she will say: **I am not going to help you**, decided on the Wednesday and not acted on since, *because a paper in that room does to the people it names what it does, and I am not going to be on the same side of that as you* — and then that she voted for the office and would again and that she is the one who is frightened of it now and did not consult the bench and was not asked to, and why she voted: a thing with a door and a number can be written to and a keeper can only be written about, and it has come back in four months as her handwriting. And two sentences in return: that what she is frightened of is not that the office is a person but that she is the person in it and in about four years somebody will bring a form to that door and name her in the first line of it; and that **she has been a magistrate nine years on the sentence that a court is not a person, and that sentence is now the most expensive sentence anybody in this county has been given away for nothing, and she gave half of it away in a room on the ninth of Greenmonth and did not see what she was doing, because she was pleased.** And the reeve will not put a line about either in a minute, because a minute that carries her sister's fear is a minute that carries her sister's fear. **They concede nothing. The paper is answered in four minutes on a Sunday, filed on the Monday, correct, spent, no name in it anywhere, and no Crown's hand has written a name in this county since the twenty-seventh of Fallowmonth and there is still not one.**

**Ch 108 · the twenty-third of Longlight, a Thursday, a market day · Toban's yard, and the conversion, and the money.** A man of sixty from the Marches has been in Weir Street two weeks, over on the fourth, feeding, and on the twenty-third he forgets his oilcloth and that is why the conversion is fought. **The yard's figures, on the board, in pence, footed before the total is said aloud: the yard's rate is eight pence a day and has been held nineteen years; four men, thirty-two pence; the boy at four, being half, and he is fifteen and copies the empty space under a number as well as the number; thirty-six pence a day; ninety working days; two thousand eight hundred and eighty and three hundred and sixty is three thousand two hundred and forty pence, and that is three shillings a day for four men and a boy and a good day's work in a market town.** **Pei Tarrow, 52, in the yard, called a symbol for eleven years and never once asked anything, wants freight and says so in front of four men: ninety days is a gap and not a season, and he wants it written that he goes for the ninety days and is back before the sowing with the number and the hour, and he does not want his name in the book in the same line as the word survivor, which is not in the book, and he says that eleven people have said a thing to him as though it were a gift and not one of them has asked him a question, and *a man who has stopped waiting is a man who is dangerous to a yard, and I would rather say it here than have it said in a gateway.* And the man who takes it all off the boy's contract and reads it out in front of the four.** The conversion: **288 bushels of salt at nine pence and 216 of seed at three, and the two come to exactly the contract sum.** A bushel of salt at the Old Toll is three shillings, which is thirty-six pence, which is four times their nine, and the man from the Marches does not budge: a bushel in his county is a bushel in his county and he was argued down from eleven in his own market. The yard's man at the drawknife says the yard burns nine stone a year, that the Old Toll and the fishing town and the coopers will take some, that this is two seasons' work, **that a yard is not a merchant, and that the day we start buying salt to sell salt we are a different trade and he will have ruined this yard for four hundred miles of river and called it a contract** — and knows in a second he has won the wrong half. Tovan will not take the seed: two hundred and sixteen bushels at three pence is six hundred and forty-eight pence with no price at all in this county and he will not write one, *that is not a complaint about your wheat, it is that a thing with no price is a thing the yard has to carry and a yard has one cart and a fortnight in it and a cutting contract and eleven marks and fourpence of crew pay owed to us by a trust with no owner.* **And he takes it anyway, in front of four men, and says the true thing: I have been outargued by a man with a sheet and a yard is not a court.** And then the guild's first hand does the one thing in two weeks that makes the four men trust him: **he gives up the payable fifth — six hundred and forty-eight pence — for a reason about a promise, and says the yard should not take a yard's money on a promise from a county it cannot check, and says it in a gateway in front of a cart, and asks that it be noticed in a room, and does not say it again.** The week-book entry, four lines with figures and a fifth with none: the sum footed; the salt worth four times their figure and neither season being this one; **the seed with no price and the space left empty on purpose**; the last fifth payable on a day they name, *that is not set, and I have entered it anyway with the hour against it, because the alternative is not entering it and then not knowing that I took it*; and the fifth line, which has no number and never will: **his brother went out at the fourth hour of a market morning in the ninth month of last year and was gone eighty-eight days and the date of it is in his own account and it is not this county's date, and I have entered it with the hour against it, and he does not know I have entered it, and I am not going to tell him, and nobody in this county is ever going to ask to see this page.** And the paper itself is in a minute book in a chamber over a tithe barn four hundred miles off, and a yard's book holds the fact of a thing and not the thing, and a man in four hundred miles is at this moment sowing the seed.

**Ch 109 · the twenty-seventh of Longlight, a Monday, not a market day · Captain Rell, and then the file, and the man who never filled it in.** In the watch house at the third hour, a bench, a stove, an unlit lamp, a board with four nails and three on the bench, a commendation with a dust line across the middle of it. A form of the R.C. series asks this watch to confirm from its own knowledge the name in the fourth column of a return in a provincial file, on the ground that a person having the care of a road is a person whose movements the watch may follow. **Rell has been a captain twenty-six years and has never been able to answer a form, and sends for the one man in the county who will not sell him anything.** Four minutes: *write that this watch has no knowledge of the matter and no power to obtain any and returns the sheet unaltered* — the form answer, and the reason it is the correct answer is that a thing against which nobody is in default is not refused and is spent the moment it is answered — and the reason it is being given for nothing is that the man in that column has been asking for two years for a thing nobody will give him and Rell is the only office in the kingdom that can put a name in a hand and take one out. Rell writes it in eleven seconds and does not say thank you, and **the two of them had been in a room together three times in eleven years and this was the third and it was the last of them, and neither of them mentioned the eleventh of Mudmonth or a bell or a stair.** Then the Archive, at the sixth hour, on a Monday with no market: **a whole leaf, not a page and not a copy of the page and not a leaf with the cells at the foot left off**, because a copyist who leaves the blanks off is a copyist who has made an argument, and this woman has been copying thirty-one years and her grandmother for forty. Sent unasked on the first, arrived on the fourth, held twenty-three days, and Mara's request went the other way on the second and the two crossed in the bag. **The front: the object, the date, Aren's own words in the *Person having knowledge* column, a seed-carter in the *Keeper* column, *How often: twice a day, and the same man has not read this line before he wrote it*, and an office's marginal note of nine words. The back: *I do not keep this. I have not kept this. I did not put my name in the fourth column. I told the man it was wrong and he said it had to be full or the office would fill it. Put it back and tell them I said so. — O. Rill*, dated two years before the return.** Two hands about one man and one of them is his: the fourth column is a claim, in a hand that is not his, put there by a stranger with a printed sheet and a bag of string, and the back is a record, in his own hand, dated, signed, addressed to nobody, **and that is the only thing in the whole of this business a man has ever written about himself in a form designed to prevent it — and the sentence it proves has been arrived at four times in four years and a half by four people in two countries, and has never once been printed on a form, and the office that printed this leaf has a column headed Keeper and a line in a margin in a third hand nobody has ever read.** The entry, ordinary, with the claim number against nothing and a name on it, the first name in that book for four months; and then, separately, the reason it has not been sent to anybody, **in four words: *there is nobody to serve*.** Not to the office, which would put a name in a hand; not to the man, who is four hundred miles off on a road nobody here can keep; not to the delegation, which was here eighteen days ago and went home on the eighteenth on a calendar of its own and nobody in this kingdom can say whether it got there. **And entered, against no person, and entered because a keeper who stops at the reason stops short of the fact: the office has two instruments and no third — a press that cannot be served, which is a holding, and a letter, which is the only thing it has ever sent a person could answer — and it did not write the letter, because the letter is one sentence and the sentence would put a name in a Crown's hand in a provincial file where a figure could be kept about it, and the one protection this office can give anybody is a book nobody can serve, and it will not trade it for a sentence it would like to have said; and Rill said in a hall eighteen days ago that the price of what he wants is that his county gets worse and his own village blames him, and the same price is on the back of his own leaf in his own hand, and there is no instrument in this kingdom that will pay a price on behalf of a man four hundred miles off, and she has looked.** She writes the four words on the wrapper, puts the leaf in the press un-copied, **because the whole value of it is one man's hand on the back and no other hand anywhere**, and at the ninth hour the road warden comes up with a dressed block he carried four hundred yards, and she says she has entered something she has not sent to anybody and is going to tell him what it is and is not going to ask him anything, and if he has come to be asked something he has come on a bad morning, and he has come about a dressed block and a bench asked four times and a man of sixty who gave up six hundred and forty-eight pence in a gateway, and has brought no numbers, and she reads him the one line on a wrapper that is not a fact and will not ask him afterwards which he did with it.

**Ch 110 · the thirtieth of Longlight, a Thursday, a market day · Aren, six questions, and a thing that is not a decision.** The thirtieth of Longlight is a Thursday and the thirtieth of the month before was a Tuesday, and a man who counts things gets from one to the other in four seconds, and the printed figure at the foot of the terms has not moved in a year. The market in one street with the water on the left. **The yard's sheet was accepted on the twenty-seventh and is going out on the Saturday, and it is a contract and not an acceptance, because the other end has not said yes.** **The boy is not going** — said out loud in a yard for the first time, in the week-book, no figure and no line made for it: *he is fifteen and he is the best copier I have ever had and I have had two and I am not going to have his name under a sheet that says four men and a boy.* And a charter of the Marches' own is the paper in dispute, four hundred miles there and four hundred back. Then the notebook, at the wharf rail, and **it is a thing that is not a decision and he will not let it become one in a book, because a decision written down by a man with a notebook and a tool bag is a thing other men find and act on, and he has spent two years watching other men find things he wrote down.** **Six questions and two answers.** One: is there a man at that end who holds a length of water — there is, a guild of nineteen men and its first hand, a hand and not a keeper, who says his terms first. Two: will he hold the road — unknown, and not asked, because a man who has just lost an argument about a bushel of seed is a bad man to ask whether he will stand at the end of a line for ever. Three: who is the witness at that end — unknown and not his to name. Four: what is a day to them — **unknown, and I cannot convert a rolling of theirs into a day of this calendar and there is not one of those in the other, and I have tried it twice on paper and both times the answer came out as a number with no meaning and I threw the paper away both times.** Five: what the office calls this road and where it got the word — unknown, asked in a sealed letter, not answered. Six: whether a man from the north is permitted to stand at the end of a line at all — **the board answers, in the third line, and I have read that line eleven times in three weeks and I do not know whether I am the man it is about, and I am not going to ask anybody in this county whether I am, because a man who asks that is a man who already knows.** And the six people he has told none of them about, each with what they would have done and what it would have cost: the keeper of records, *I am asking a woman with a filing system to keep a thing out of a book, and that is the sentence and it took me three weeks to be able to write it down*; the magistrate, who is frightened of a sentence she made herself; the reeve, who voted for the office and would want it minuted; the bench, which has two marks in a chest; his brother, who has a fifth line with no figure on it about a date in the wrong calendar and does not know there is a page of his own; and the woman in the third press. **And then the reason, and it is the best reason anybody in this case has ever had, out of four lines in a minute of a bench four hundred miles the other way: *we did not lose our register, we lost our refusal, a register is what you have instead.*** A county that was cut off has no office, so a line written in it cannot be served, cannot be answered, and cannot travel, **and that is why a promise kept a hundred and forty times in a field is the only durable thing anybody in that countryside has: it is the only record in the world that does not need an office to be true, because the men who swore it are still alive and can be asked** — and he has had that answer in his own book since the first of Embermonth of the year before and it has never once told him why the second route does not answer, and he has stopped asking it, **and that is the part of the habit a rule about authority cannot reach.** And then the sentence the batch ends on, in capitals in his own book: **AN INSTRUMENT THAT DOES NOT WORK IS NEVER HEARD FROM.** *There is no failure anywhere in this. A county that cannot be served, a file that cannot be produced, a form that cannot be got at, a road that does not open on anybody's day. Every one of those is working exactly as printed. Nothing has gone wrong.
