And nothing has gone wrong is the condition a thing is in when it has been doing harm for a long time, and the only way anybody ever finds out about it is when one person stands up in a room and refuses, and a refusal has to arrive somewhere, and there is nowhere in that county for it to arrive.* And six questions, two answers, a bag of salt going four hundred miles on Saturday with four men in it and a boy who is not going, a yard with a contract and not an acceptance, a contract accepted by one end of a line not being a contract, and **the twenty-second of a month I do not have a name for, when he is going to find out whether a road opens at all.** And last, on the fourth hour and a half, **the bell on the Old Toll bridge, which should not have gone: the arrangement is a Monday, because that is the day the far end keeps, and the carpenter says so, and the boy says he knows it is wrong and says the wharf was loud and he wanted to see whether the far end heard it on the wrong day, and the carpenter says that is not a reason and holds the line anyway, because a man who has been a hand for nineteen years holds a line a boy is on.** **And at the far end, on a coping on a river this county does not have, nothing was struck, and two people stood on it and waited and went in, and neither of them has ever been asked a question about it by anybody, and it is not a bell and it is not nothing.** The arrangement is not Aren's. He did not go up the road and did not ring it and nothing in the sound was his, **and for the first time in three months nothing at the far end of it was anybody's either.**

## Batch 0012 — Volume 03, Chapters 111–120, "The River Changes Course"

Block 2, and **the first block with path time in it**: main world to the twenty-second of Rainmonth, twenty-one days, then three path-time chapters, days one to nine of the first crossing. POVs **Aren ×6 (112, 115, 116 inside, 118, 119, 120) / Mara Vey ×1 (111) / Lysa Fenn ×1 (113, with Reeve Aldis Fenn elsewhere) / Reeve Aldis Fenn ×1 (114) / Tovan Kest ×1 (116) / Pei Tarrow ×1 (117)**, with Coll Ferrow inside 116, 118, 119 and 120. **One crossing out, made on the twenty-second and not opened; no return in this batch** (the return is the second of Harvestmonth and belongs to Chapter 148's country). **No System panel and no Gatehouse line in the batch at all.** **Two new names and four slots unfilled, as the cast plan allows: Coll Ferrow (111) and Delya Munn (120).** Nobody dies; nobody is killed; no arch goes down and no glass breaks. **The tile on the coping is not touched, not looked at and not named, and its device is named by nobody — it appears only as "a coping on a river this county does not have" and through a clerk's minute clause that the coping is not described because no person present has asked what is on it. The eighth ruled line in the served schedule is not looked at. The brine weights are neither mentioned nor filed. No Ash figure is on the page in any of the ten, and the 456 is not on the page in any form.** *Culling Order*, *custodian*, *Bind* and *Gatehouse* do not appear. The private-keeper clause is not mentioned, referred to, explained or noticed by anybody, and Sabin Dain does not explain it and does not change her want; **her only appearance in this batch is the fourth enclosure of a reply she will read, and he neither accepts nor refuses.** The breach is not repaired, no apology is made available to anybody, and **no tenth not-asking is entered — the ninth stands as entered on the sixteenth of Longlight and Mara Vey says so in order to refuse to enter one.** Weekday chain from `outline/volume-03.md` §17: **Rainmonth 4 Mon (not market) · 5 Tue (not) · 9 Sat (market) · 12 Tue (not) · 13 Wed (not) · 14 Thu (market) · 15 Fri (not) · 22 Fri (not, the crossing out).** Every one of the chapter dates and every market-day call in the prose matches, and **the batch prompt's own list for the twelfth, thirteenth and fourteenth was a day out and the prose follows §17.**

**Ch 111 · the fourth of Rainmonth, YR 313, a Monday, not a market day · Mara Vey, and the returned march, and a sheet that is not a message.** A sheet comes up the hill sixteen days on the road — five days longer than the delegation took coming — from the council and the bridge guild of a county that is not on any map in this kingdom, and it is signed at the foot **Coll Ferrow**, sixty, first hand, and it says five things: the line is not a road; **a day is named for four men of a yard and the day is the day those four men will be on that county's road, and no day has been named for the line and there will not be one until the line is made**; a sheet is not a road; a stranger's labour is paid at the guild's rate in the county's coin at the crossing, and they lost that argument in a gateway in Weir Street and are not coming back to it; and the woman of thirty-eight of Sallow Marsh has not given a line of her book and a line of it in the sheet is not hers. **The delegation's own arrival is unknowable from this county and is entered against no person.** Ferrow's name at the foot of a sheet, in a good hand, is the first name in the case that anybody has put anywhere on purpose, and it sits beside the office's printed worked example at the foot of every form of the R.C. series, and she enters that in four lines. The county mail goes out on the Tuesday: **a fair copy of the column of eleven counties, with one question at the foot of it and the head of the column asked for, addressed to a counting house in a city that does not admit to the Crown that it exists** — *a message is addressed to a person and a copy is addressed to a shelf* — costed in pence (a rider three days at thirty-two a day is ninety-six, sheet and wax and the boy's copying are six more, **one hundred and two pence, the first thing this office has ever spent on a letter and it is spending it on a column**). The fair copy of the six lines of chalk goes in the same bag with the six lines written out in full under them, per the third rule, with the date and the hour and the fact that they came off a board of lath and lime standing in the guild hall over a wharf and have not been taken down. **And the marginal note she wrote against the sixth line on the sixteenth of Longlight — *a condition of this office, and the office does not know what it is a condition of* — is withdrawn in her own hand, dated, with the hour, because the sixth line is not a condition of her office: it is a condition of a calendar four hundred miles off, and a door which opens on somebody else's day is not a shut door.** She is entered as having been wrong in the same direction three times this year and having no procedure for it. He comes up at the tenth hour to give her a number and nothing else — the twenty-second, at the fourth hour, a Friday, and not a market day, checked twice because he has lost a market twice by inattention — **and tells her, in the same breath, that the sheet named a day for four men and did not name one for him; and she does not ask him the question, because he has not made it a question, and the count of not-askings stays at nine.**

**Ch 112 · the fifth of Rainmonth, a Tuesday, not a market day · Aren, the book, the boy, and the far end.** The mail goes out of the lane behind the weigh-house at the seventh hour with two things in it that are not letters, and the north end of a road is a place a man can stand in — the rail on the wharf, the tool bag at his feet, the Sedge Reach four hundred yards up the same road holding a fortnight — **and the north end of a road is also a book, and the book is the reason.** He ruled it on the eleventh of the month before in a room over a cooper on the back of a cost sheet: eleven inches by eight, four columns with no headings, a fifth under a ruled line for the hour, and **no column for a name**, because the second of the six forbids it and because a book with a column of names in it is a roll. **The bell went on the Monday, the day it goes, and at the far end of the line, on a coping on a river this county does not have, something was struck at the fourth hour and a half and a Monday is the day the far end keeps, and it kept** — two people on it, neither of whom struck it, and the same sentence went into the minute of that county's assizes in a clerk's flat hand, **including the clause that the coping itself is not described in the entry because no person present has asked what is on it, which is the only part of the sentence anybody has ever read twice.** Mara Vey is told on the fifth and enters it with the hour against no person, and writes the word *Monday* into her book because a day of the week is the only part of it that is not a fact about two counties. In the afternoon he walks four hundred yards up the wharf road to the Old Toll, and the boy of sixteen is on the north abutment with a bar and a wedge doing something to the cradle that nobody asked him to do and that is plainly right. **The boy says he rang the bell on the wrong day on purpose and has not asked the carpenter about it because the carpenter will give him a straight answer and he would rather not have one until he knows what he thinks**, and that he could have asked Aren and did not, *because you are a man in a guild and I am two years of an apprenticeship.* Nothing in that exchange put a question about the far end into anybody's mouth, and the notebook was on a hook by a door for three hours. He looks at the sill on the way back — a dressed block, forty yards of tarred hemp, a fourth course left open the width of a hand by a vote of nine men — and puts his palm on it for a second and a half. **No glass, and not going to be a third seat.** At the seventh hour he writes the nine, the second time in his life, in about nine minutes: *a line, a hand at each end, a date, the hour, a witness at each end who is not the man who keeps that road, the book at each end.* **Seven of the nine he can make at this end, and the two he cannot are the date and the book at the far end**, and the third of the six says the north is not to send anybody who can be asked to come and look, and he does not ask anybody in this county whether he is the man it is about.

**Ch 113 · the ninth of Rainmonth, a Saturday, a market day, and the morning of the twenty-second · Lysa Fenn, and a second count.** A form of the R.C. series in the Saturday post from the provincial town: **a proposed count of persons oath-bound in a border county, by the county's own schedule and by the stones where the county keeps its stones, dated the twenty-eighth of the month last, returnable on or before the twenty-second, with a ruled space for a figure and no column anywhere on the sheet that a name could be put in.** In the same bag is a second sheet, **dated the twenty-sixth, the day before it was asked for, in a woman's small upright hand, returning nine thousand two hundred and sixty for a district of eleven counties**, with three lines under it, of which the second is the one that undoes her: *one of the eleven has returned nothing in eleven years and is returned here at the average of its neighbours* — **a county that has never sent a return has been returned, at the average of two counties that do, so that no line shows a place where a place ought to be, and nobody has done anything wrong and there is no officer to blame** — and a third: *this is not an instrument, it requires no answer, it names no person, it cannot be refused, it is a figure and it will be used.* The construction works again in about **four minutes, having taken nine the first time, and she writes the four down and is frightened of it, because a thing that gets faster is a thing being learned and not argued.** On the Saturday morning the first person in four years and a half comes to that court door about a Crown form — a man of about fifty who keeps the sluice, with a brother nine miles up the western channel he has not seen in nine years — and asks whether his county is on it, and she cannot tell him, and tells him what she can: *a figure with a place on it and nothing to put a person in, and a person comes to the door and asks to be a person in it, and there is nowhere to put him.* **He does not give a name and she does not ask for one, and it goes in the door-book, which has no column in it, and there have been eleven entries in four years and a half that are *no name given*.** On the morning of the twenty-second, a Friday, not a market day, she answers: the register of the office, ruled in eleven columns, **48. 51. 39. 62. 55. 44. 71. 58. 66. 43. 60, run-totalled to five hundred and ninety-seven, and footed a second time in the other order and got the same, and both footings left on the sheet** — a figure about this court, of whom not one is in a border county and not one is oath-bound, written in the ruled space because the space is a space for a figure. **She does not write the second paragraph she had got as far as, because a second paragraph is a speech and a speech from this court on a count of persons in a county four hundred miles off is a figure with a hand on it.** No person is in default, nothing is pending, the instrument is spent. And at the fourth hour that morning a road warden went out of the county with a tool bag and a book with four columns and no column for a name, and **no name was written in any Crown's hand in this county, and there has not been one since the twenty-seventh of Fallowmonth.**

**Ch 114 · the twelfth of Rainmonth, a Tuesday, not a market day · Reeve Aldis Fenn, and a board that cannot be copied out of.** The board is against the end wall of the guild hall over the wharf, four feet by two and a half, lath and lime, the second line gone over twice, **and it is still here because the man who keeps the minute of that council said one sentence before he got back on the waggon and the sentence is in the roll of this hall: *it is no use to us, a board is no use to a court, and the copy in your book is the use.*** A board cannot be copied out of — nobody can serve a notice on lime and lath, and what it has is the thing it was in the room for — and a copy in a book is a thing a court can have, and once a thing can be produced it is a text and a text can be held up against a person. The six lines are read twice off the fair copy, the second time by a man who is careful, and the hall finds three different answers to *which one is the price*: the fourth is the price, the sixth is the door, and Aren Kest says so from the back in about nine seconds, *and adds that a man from the north standing in a field is a document, and in nine years the stranger is the only evidence anybody has about the stone*, and that he has been carrying a marginal note in a book in this town about the sixth line for three weeks and does not know why it was there. **And at the fifth hour a schoolmaster's son with a stick of whitechalk and a yard of linen gets a rubbing off the second line and the man at the drawknife says one word — *no* — because a board cannot be copied out of and the boy has just copied out of it, and that is the only correction this county has made to anything that came out of that wagon, and the bench-clerk enters it with the hour and does not enter the schoolmaster's son's name, because rule two is on the same page as the entry about a man paid six pence twice out of a box.** Then the fifth line, which is the witness line, and the reeve refuses to stand on it **word for word as she refused on the ninth of Embermonth of the year before at eleven o'clock at night with the market four streets off**, and the only change is the gesture: last time she put the rule down, and this time she put her hand flat on the copy, and she asks that it be minuted that she noticed the slot. **The bench-clerk enters it, and what the roll carries is the shortest possible way of saying that a reeve voted and declined to witness: *the reeve voted.*** Then the bench votes on the six lines as six lines — seven for, two against, the toll-holder because the sixth line puts a county's calendar in charge of a road, and the man who keeps eels because the third line is the north's protection and not his — and the vote is the first in the roll, and the line can be made for **three days of work and about two marks and twelve pence, and there is no money in the room, and the reeve asks that the minute carry that too.**

**Ch 115 · the thirteenth of Rainmonth, a Wednesday, not a market day · Aren, and the cost of being ready.** Three days of four hands and a boy at the yard's rate, footed on the page in the yard's book before the total is said: **four hands at eight pence is thirty-two a day and thirty-two for three days is ninety-six; the boy at four pence for three days is twelve; ninety-six and twelve is one hundred and eight pence, which is nine shillings, which is two marks and twelve pence**, out of a chest that had two marks in it on the thirtieth of Greenmonth and is now lighter by the whole of it, and the reeve asks that the minute carry that too. **Forty yards of tarred hemp off a cooper's bin would be another two shillings and cost nothing and the man at the drawknife will not put it in a county's minute at nothing, so the minute has it at a pot of beer, which is a bad rate and is the first beer in that roll.** The book has been ruled since the eleventh of the month before in a room over a cooper and the three days went on the line itself: the hemp along the parapet of the Old Toll, four marks, and a day of a man with a bar putting the two ends into the abutment. **The boy copies the four marks and the four lengths and the hour, and then copies the nine out of the back of the notebook, because a list of nine things is a thing set, and the man at the drawknife reads it and puts his thumb on the fourth item and the ninth item and does not copy them.** And the answer to *which of those can you make at this end* is **seven, and the two are the date and the book at that end, and the date is the sixth line and there is a sheet four hundred miles off that names a day for four men of a yard and does not name a day for him.** The witness at this end is fixed in a gateway on Weir Street: **Bram Ottery**, a watch sergeant with no office, who was the witness at this end on the ninth of Embermonth of the year before, who was paid fourpence a week out of a private box, did not draw it, and will not have it in the yard's book at all. **He says two things: he will be a man off a bridge and not a man in a guild; and the last time he did this he wrote down that he had been in daylight in a market and a clerk four hundred miles off copied the writing and not the daylight, so he wants it in the yard's book in this county's hand and before he is asked and not after.** In the afternoon Aren cuts a dressed block for the Old Toll parapet at the bench's own cost and a peer of the wharf road tells him a man in his brother's yard puts a bar through oak better than he does, and he says *he does it better than me*, and the peer writes it on a tally-board, **and there are now two pages in two books in this town about the two of them and neither has ever been shown to the other.** And in the afternoon a quarter-sheet goes into the bag for the provincial town: **a reply to Sabin Dain that neither accepts nor refuses, because both would be a promise made by a man who does not know what the eastern paths are**, and under it, *a word on what the office is buying* — a command rank and not a custody, a sealed record of a person is a record a person cannot contradict, and the office is not buying what he can do, it is buying what a form did to him. **Nobody in this county knows it was written, and it is the fourth thing in a notebook in a coat that he has told nobody about.**

**Ch 116 · the fourteenth of Rainmonth, a Thursday, a market day · Tovan Kest, and six hundred and forty-eight pence.** The yard is running the contract. **The sheet itself went out of this county in a bag on the second of this month and is in a minute book over a barn four hundred miles off; what is in the yard is four lines with figures on them, an hour, and an empty space under the line about the seed, and the boy of fifteen copied all four and copied the hour and copied the empty space as well.** He reads his own line out and says the third term — paid in the county's coin at the crossing and not in goods — was said out loud in the hall over the wharf on the ninth of the month before by a man of sixty from that county, **and that we lost it and we are going.** Then the arithmetic, in the week-book, with the hour, and it is the only page in that book in nineteen years nobody has asked to be corrected after: **four men at eight pence for ninety days is two thousand eight hundred and eighty; the boy at four pence for ninety days is three hundred and sixty, and the boy is not going; two thousand eight hundred and eighty and three hundred and sixty is three thousand two hundred and forty. The county is paying two hundred and eighty-eight bushels of salt at nine pence, which is two thousand five hundred and ninety-two, and there is no seed because he would not carry it. Two thousand eight hundred and eighty less two thousand five hundred and ninety-two is two hundred and eighty-eight. And the boy was three hundred and sixty, and two hundred and eighty-eight and three hundred and sixty is six hundred and forty-eight — which is the last fifth of the whole sum, the one the man from the Marches kept on the twenty-third of the month before and then gave up in a gateway in this street for a reason about a promise, and which nobody in this yard wrote down. The two six hundreds and forty-eights are the same number and they are not the same money.** Two hundred and eighty-eight pence is six marks and six shillings a man. **He tells the man at the drawknife, and not the four, that a word is coming in about three weeks that will not be a lie — the salt is bad, or the salt is short, or the crossing was not there when we came to it — and that the yard is not to answer it and not to settle it out of its own pocket, because a yard that pays a claim on a contract it has not been paid for has bought the contract twice; and that if it is a fourth thing, he does not know what the fourth thing is, and that is the yard's honest position and it is in the book in that order.** About eleven people come into the yard on the market day because a man from that county was in it for a fortnight and was fed, and what they bring is the second-hand version of him, which is that Merefen has a bridgewright, and the man at the drawknife says that if anybody in that market thinks so they may come and stand in the yard and be one. **Pei Tarrow, fifty-two, who has been called a symbol for eleven years and not once asked anything, says the other half of the ninety days: eleven years standing in this yard and nobody in this town has ever put a question to him, and he is going four hundred miles to be a man in a yard that has his wage in a book, and he has never in his life not asked, and that is new and he does not like it.** The last thing on the page for that Thursday is a line with no figure on it, the sixth such line in that book in a year, about a day the county named, and a page of his own about a date of his brother's that is not this county's date, **and he has not told him and is not going to, and nobody in this county is ever going to ask to see it.**

**Ch 117 · the fifteenth of Rainmonth, a Friday, not a market day · Pei Tarrow, and a day nobody in Merefen can check.** Out of the gate at the sixth hour with a cart and no load, which is the one thing in that street anybody would have noticed and nobody did, and it is going to stay that way. **The day is a day because a county four hundred miles off named it, and it named it by saying when four men of a yard will be on its road — not by a number — and a sheet came up the hill on the fourth saying that no day has been named for a line and there will not be one until the line is made.** Eleven days to the crossing, which is itself the second-hand number, and the only one of the four who was in that yard on the ninth of the month before says so and is told to take eleven and keep for twelve. The keep is told standing up: **four of them, eleven days, at eight pence a day is thirty-two a day and thirty-two for eleven days is three hundred and fifty-two pence, and the sheet pays the crossing and not the road and the figure for the road is not on the sheet and the figure is nothing, so it is three hundred and fifty-two out of the yard, which is seven marks and sixteen pence, and he is not going to put that in the same sentence as the eleven marks and fourpence the trust owes, and that is on purpose.** Pei writes the figure on the back of his hand and wipes it off a mile out and says the true thing about a market, and the man at the drawknife answers that the woman who has read that water twice a day for eleven years and has never been asked for a line of her book is in a village called Sallow Marsh, and that if they go and ask her they are the men who were sent to ask, **and the third of the six says the north is not to send anybody who can be asked to come and look, and he has read that line out loud in that hall twice and is not going to be the reason it is true.** Eleven days on the road, the first camp in stubble in the rain, the tarpaulin off the tools in four minutes and the beds got dry, and the man at the drawknife takes out the page he copied off the contract on the tying-up bench and reads the three hundred and fifty-two out loud in a field in a county he cannot name a village in; a carter's boy comes over the stubble and asks whether they are for the market at the third stile and is told they are and says the market is a day, **and the man at the drawknife writes nothing on the page, and the not writing was correct, because a day a boy told a man is not a date and a date is what that page is for.** They go under two hands and not under the master: Coll Ferrow at the far end and the man at the drawknife at this one, on the western road, with a bar he does not need until the crossing, and no hour in it and no line in it. **The witness at the Marches' end was chosen by the Marches and is not named on anything, and the four men are told that if the man at the far end of the line is not there on the day the line does not open and it does not fail and nobody is to say afterwards that it failed — which is a rule about a door, and somebody in that county has a door, and the key to it, and it is not the same door every day.** The brother's own last memory of that gate is *you will be out of this county in nine days and there is a man in your yard who puts a bar through oak and does it better than you, and you know he does*. And **nobody in Merefen was told anything by anybody on the fifteenth, or the sixteenth, or the twenty-second, and the county does not know the yard's name, and there is a road on a Tuesday in a bag in a room over a weigh-house four hundred miles off and nobody has laid a hand on it.**

**Ch 118 · path time, the Hearth Marches, day one, the fourth hour · Aren, and the crossing out, and nine minutes.** Clay steps out of the road, nine of them, the fourth gone soft, and he starts on the fourth step because it is the only dry thing within a mile. **The country is not a country of fires: in four places in four years and a half, three of them wanted a fire kept in it and this one wants the water read twice a day, and it is a river in spate in the wrong part of the year.** Eleven villages, a market town with a low roofed hall on a tithe barn, a ditch system in a grid, every ditch straight, and one of them moved. **And the thing that takes the first hour is not the country, it is that there is no count of it: no register, no roll, no return, and no office in this kingdom a return could go to — a county that is not counted is a county that can still be described, and a county that is counted has become a figure, and he had spent nine days of the last year in the second kind and four hours in the first, and that difference is why he came.** He writes the nine in nine minutes, seven of them in about four seconds each, **and the two he cannot make are the date and the book at the far end**: the line is a ditch at the third stile where the grid turns, and a ditch in this county is a boundary, and he has been brought four hundred miles to look at a boundary and has been telling himself for a fortnight he was coming about a road. The two hands are **Coll Ferrow**, nineteen men, and **one of his brother's four, whose name is not a thing that goes in a yard's book and whom he is not going to get out of a man by asking him for it** — a man can be a hand at an end and be four hundred miles short of it at the wrong hour, and that is not an argument, it is a calendar. The witness at this end is the man from the gateway in Weir Street, **asked**, not choosing him, who wanted it in this county's hand and not his own. The book at this end he has. **The book at that end he cannot enter, and there is no instrument in this kingdom that will let a man lay a sheet of paper on that bench, and the county has no office in it at all.** A woman of thirty-eight of Sallow Marsh comes up from the low ground and stands two yards off and will not come closer, and says the sixth of the six: **the Marches named a day by saying when four men of a yard will be on their road, and the four men went on a day and he came on a different one, and there is no arithmetic here and there is no arithmetic anywhere.** *You are a day late, and so is the day you came on, and both of those are the same fact. It is not a thing that is short of you. You are not a man it is waiting for. You are a man who has come on a day nobody agreed to come on, and nobody here is going to be unkind about it, because a county that was unkind about days would not have lasted a hundred and forty.* He has the line and two hands and a witness at each end and a book at his end; **a day is the only part of it that is theirs, and he has the one part he cannot make.** Then he calls the second road once, and it does not answer, and it is not a refusal because a refusal has a person in it and not a *not kept* because a *not kept* has a date and a *not kept* is a thing somebody did. **He writes the rule on the page above the nine, in his own hand: nobody in either country is to say the word about it, not afterwards, not kindly, not in a letter, not in a book, not in a minute, not in nine years — a road that does not open on a day is a day that was not opened, and the moment a person puts a word on it, the word is a thing somebody chose, and a thing somebody chose is a thing somebody can be shown.** Under it, one line: *a road with one end is a length of string.* **He came up with nothing, and the rest of those nine days happen without one.**

**Ch 119 · path time, the Hearth Marches, days two to five · Aren, and nine gauges and nine books.** Day two: he walks the low side with Coll Ferrow and a boy of fourteen with a pole until the light goes, and he can tell the year of about nine ditches by the way the bank has gone over on the downstream face, and Ferrow says nothing for two hours and then says *you are the first man from anywhere who has looked at the bank*, and makes the boy read the water and then read it again, and the third time the boy says what Ferrow said the first time, and Ferrow says that is a boy learning a gauge and the day the boy says what he said the first time is the day he has to be careful. **About forty men hold water, the wages come out of a rent roll that has been shrinking for nine years, and a man paid out of a roll that is going down stops being able to refuse anything, and by the time he notices he has stopped being able to refuse anything, and that is not a thing anybody has to be wicked to arrange.** Day three: a woman takes him into the lowest of the four white villages, where the ground is the colour of a bone and is wet under the boot and the grit is not sand. Day four and five: **the gauge column.** Nine gauges on a river of that size, nine keepers, nine books, and a woman of thirty-eight who reads the weir post and the Sallow foot twice a day since the first sowing eleven years ago and has **eight thousand and thirty-one lines** — twice a day for eleven years, and one for the day she started, which she did not know was the first one — in a small quick left-sloping hand that gets worse in the wet, with a whole season written smaller a third of the way in because she had been ill. **Nobody has ever asked her for a page and the council has never once looked at it, and the reason is not carelessness: a boundary here is a ditch, the man who holds the water and the man who holds the oath have been the same person for a hundred and forty years, and asking a ditchman for his book would be asking a man to show his own account of a thing he also judges.** In the middle of the book are **nine lines in a different hand and a different kind of entry, and she lets him find them**: a week she was not in the county for, a ditch that was or was not cut, a field under water to the third row, and *whoever reads this, there is a week in this book that is not in the book, and I have left the space for eleven years.* **A gauge read twice a day by the man whose land it is under is not a gauge, it is a thing he has agreed with.** And at the door of a low house, a woman of about sixty on three hearths, who is not on the oath — not because anybody struck her, but because the oath is renewed by the men who hold the water and she does not hold water, and there is no form in that county that says a woman may hold a ditch — and who says, unasked, *which of them asked me?* **Nobody. I have lived on this ground forty-one years and I have never been asked what I swore, because I never swore anything, and I would tell you if you asked, and nobody has.** And then the sentence the whole of the volume is built on: *you will go home and you will write it down and you will say a woman of about sixty was on three hearths and was not on the oath, and that is all that will be said, and it will be said in a room where there is nobody from here.* On the sixth morning, before light, she comes down the bank again and tells him the book is hers and that if it goes into a book four hundred miles off it stops being a thing that can be contradicted and becomes a thing that is true, and a thing that is true does not need a person standing behind it. **He writes the two things he came for and the honest third line: eleven years, twice a day, eight thousand and thirty-one lines, nobody asked, the council never looked, and a bridgewright can see the mechanism in a day and a half and the woman who has the eleven years has not been asked for them once, and if you were to ask her she would have to be asked, and being asked is the whole of it.**

**Ch 120 · path time, the Hearth Marches, days six to nine · Aren, the terms, the refusal, the stone, and the figure.** Up two flights into a room over a tithe barn: eleven people, all eleven of that council, all eleven of them in a guild hall over a wharf in a fen county twenty-five days before. He puts four terms on the table and three of them are the Marches' own six with the price and the door left in, **and the fourth is that the water is to be read: nine gauges, nine keepers, a book with eight thousand and thirty-one lines that has never been asked for, and a true copy of a month of the readings to sit in the line's book at this end, where anybody in this county may come up two flights and read.** A woman of about thirty-four with a slate, the clerk of the council and the only other person in the room who writes things down, asks the room a question nobody has asked, and it takes about **eleven** seconds to establish that nobody knows the answer: **who is paying for the stranger's rider.** A road warden says what a minute keeps and throws away unless somebody stands in a room and says the why, and the men who say the why here are nine and have a hundred and forty years of practice at saying a why in a field where anybody can be asked about it afterwards. Then the reason the fourth term breaks the second of the six, in his own head, and nobody says it for a while: **a column with a woman's name at the head of it is a name, and he had not thought about names since the ninth of the month before.** The first hand of the guild says the third term is theirs and is the best thing anybody has put in the room and wants it minuted that he said so, because in four rollings somebody will tell his nineteen men that the stranger wanted a thing off them. **And Delya Munn says no, and the reason is a hundred and forty years long and it is not hers: a boundary in this county is a ditch, the man who holds the ditch is the bound and the man who holds the water is the ditchman and they have been one word for a hundred and forty years, and the only thing this countryside has ever had that does not need an office to be true is a promise kept out of doors by men who are still alive and can be asked what they swore — and he has offered to take the water out of her hand and put it in a book in a county four hundred miles off, and a keeper is not a keeper.** Then the refusal, and it is one question, asked by a woman of thirty-eight who has never been asked anything, in about four seconds: **how many people in your county are going to read it.** And he has to say that he does not know, because there is a woman in a room over a weigh-house who does not know this county exists and he has not told her, **and that is the answer, and she did not have to argue, and the road is refused on his terms and accepted on theirs, entered as a fact and not as a defeat.** And then the thing that happens in that room: **they have no word for refusing. The word they have is the word for a thing that got lost** — and a thing that got lost can be looked for, and a thing that has been refused cannot, and the whole of their law is that you may ask for a thing back. *In nine years there will be a man with a book who says that a county refused a road, and he will be telling the truth, and nobody there will be able to look for it.* On the eighth day the stone is sworn and he is not in the field, and a woman of fifty-five comes and tells him the hour and does not ask him to come, **and that is the first time the fourth of the six has been kept in four weeks, and it was kept because a stranger was in the county.** On the ninth he is taken to it by the woman who will not have it lifted, who has been asked twice and has said no twice and put the reason in the minute, **and who has had the same two men bring a different reason each time and has found that the fourth man will bring a reason better than hers.** A trough, water in it, a stone with a cut renewed so often that the face is a palimpsest a foot across — and eleven yards off, a bank that has gone over on the same line, by the same men, in the same month, at the same stage of the water, for a hundred and forty years. **He says nothing, because of the third of the six, and because a man who has been in a county five days with a bag is exactly the man the third line is about, and because the woman with eleven years of numbers has never once been asked for them, and if he says the thing about the bank in a gateway here then the one thing this county has that the north does not have is gone inside a day, and it will be his, and it will be in a book, and it will be a thing a keeper could not contradict.** He writes it with the hour against it: *seen, not told, and not to be said in this county by me.* Then the figure. Nine days on this side, counted off the sun twice and the shadow of a weir hut once. **A road held, three weeks, printed in an office's hand, twenty-one days, and it has not moved in a year.** Ten days gone against twenty-one is **minus eleven days**, the warning is longer than the absence, it is the first crossing in this case inside the printed warning and the only one he can hold in his head — **and the error is the absence and not the interval, and the interval is nowhere in the sum.** And he writes that the ten is not known yet, and that he is writing it on faith, **because a man who waits to write the figure is a man who has had the answer first and learned nothing afterwards.** And then the sentence the volume turns on: **a good result teaches a man nothing and a bad one teaches him everything** — eighty-eight out against twenty-one was sixty-seven days and about three quarters of the absence, twenty-six out was five days and about a quarter, and **the ratio is the thing and not the figure, and it is a quarter and then three quarters and now more than one, and it is not a constant, and this one he cannot do anything about because there is nothing wrong with it.** And the half he cannot check, which is the half that matters more, and it is not about him: a rolling here is four weeks and a month in the county he came out of is thirty days, and there is not one of those in the other, so the nine on this side and the ten on that side are not two halves of one figure, and no paper will make them comparable, and he is not going to try it again, **and the not having a method is the finding.** He put the lamp out. The book in the hut had two more lines on it that day and the council would not look at it, and the road had got lost, and the stone had been sworn in a field he was not in, and a woman of about sixty on three hearths was not on the oath and had never been asked what she swore. **He had one question left and it was not the one he came for, and it was the only one of the four hundred miles that could not be done by a man with a bag.**

## Batch 0013 — Volume 03, Chapters 121–130

Block 3, first third of thirteen rollings, all path time in the Hearth Marches. POVs Aren in all ten, with the council of eleven, Coll Ferrow, Delya Munn, Thessa Wend, the slate woman, the minute-man, the boy of fourteen, the yard's four men, the woman of about sixty on three hearths, the unnamed woman of sixty-six in the seed room, Ordmar Gace (49) and Aled Rhys (27), and, in Chapters 125 and 126 only, a boy of eleven from one of the four white villages and a man of sixty-eight of the roll, neither named. No Merefen scenes, no main-world dates, no main-world weekdays, no conversions. Zero section rules and zero bold standalone lines in the block. Nobody dies, no arch goes down, no glass breaks. **16,315 words after the Batch 0013 review repair, per chapter 1614 / 1765 / 1440 / 1129 / 2118 / 2632 / 2026 / 969 / 980 / 1642; Chapters 125, 126, 127, 128 and 130 carry the repair and the other five are the first pass untouched.**

**Ch 121 · first rolling · the council refuses a second time and states the guild's terms.** Eleven over the tithe barn take a boundary dispute, then Munn refuses outside standing and the minute enters it as fact; Ferrow states the guild's terms, better than the north's, salt at nine pence the bushel; the slate woman foots a rider at twelve pence a day for sixteen days, 48 + 48 + 48 + 48 = 192 pence, and the charge is kept off the four white villages and off the sixty; the bench sets a walk to the lowest field.

**Ch 122 · first rolling · the field and the mechanism said by the gauge woman.** The bench stands in Sallow Marsh's lowest field with sixty round the headland; the ground is bone-coloured and gritty; Thessa says the salt lay a foot and a half down since the ice and rises with water, and the ditch stands eleven yards off the trough on a bank worn a hand's width a year for her eleven years walking it, and for as long as the stone has been kept; Aren says nothing because of the third of the six; Munn rules nothing can be done to the salt in a morning; the sixty are not asked.

**Ch 123 · first rolling · the hut and the brother.** Thessa keeps her 8,031 lines under a board, with nine lines in another hand for a week she missed; she tells Aren alone that her brother Perrin drowned where the gauge-reader owned the water and signed the labour; she gives the council nine readings in her own hand and a leaf on the hand's width, dated by the county's own count; he asks how to keep a promise kept a hundred and forty years that is ending its keepers, and she answers keep it and be ended, or break it and learn whether you can be asked.

**Ch 124 · second rolling · the naming and the refusal.** Munn says in front of eleven that there is a cut that would take the water out of that ditch in an afternoon, and the man off the road has done worse in a shorter time; Aren refuses, saying the reason is not a rule but that the last time one of these was cut the anchor tore and a community lost contact for ever, and the man was told afterwards the witness had been forged, and he wrote it down; nobody understands and he does not explain; Aled Rhys, 27, barge hand, uses refused without heat.

**Ch 125 · second rolling · the roll, and the two doubtful.** Aren counts the chest roll with the minute-man: about sixty households of the four white villages on the roll with no oath mark, fifty-eight certain and two doubtful; the woman of sixty on three hearths, whose doorway warning from before the crossing is referred to and not re-told, tells him the sixty are not a party and not a grievance but a kind of weather, and says again that he will write them down; he writes two lines with the hour and then understands that the roll has the sixty once and his book has them a second time, and that one of the two places can be contradicted and the other cannot; over small beer the minute-man says a question is not a fact and cannot be entered, and that this is a trouble he has not had in twenty-two years; Aren goes to the first of the two doubtful and a man of sixty-eight who has been on the ground fifty-one years tells him his mark is a thumb in a flood year, refuses to have it corrected, and says the truth about why: he has wanted nothing off this county since he was nine, and to be asked and to stay silent and have the silence written down is the only form of being asked he will accept, because a book that says a man said nothing is a book that says a man agreed; **Aren decides not to enter the four true words anywhere, and on the chamber steps tells the minute-man that one of the two said nothing at all, which is the answer, and the minute-man writes it, and it is the first entry that man has made in twenty-two years that is a silence.**

**Ch 126 · second rolling · the not asking becomes the subject, and a gateway.** The slate woman asks when the council will ask the sixty what they swore; Munn says widening the oath risks it and keeping it ends them; Rhys says he will be asked for his nothing; the yard's drawknife man says strangers were never asked either; **the minute-man then puts his pen down flat and says, before anything is entered, that he can keep a fact about a person and a fact about this council but cannot keep a question, and that a household which is asked and says nothing will be entered as a household that did not care to be asked, written in a good hand by a man who writes down what happens;** the minute enters the question raised and not decided with the hour and that no person in the room had been asked; **a boy of eleven comes up the stair from one of the four white villages with one question, asked in a gateway because the household would not say it in the room, and it is whether being asked would put them on a list; four people answer it in about a minute, all four tell the truth, and not one of them writes a line; Aren gives the boy the answer it was sent for, which is that in his own county a man is a number in a column and a person can be moved out of a count by a man with a pen, lawfully, cheaply and quickly, and the boy is frightened by the end of it; Ferrow tells him on the stair that he was right and has spent it, and that in five rollings, when a list does come, half of four villages will sign whatever is read out because the council was right; the minute-man enters the question and refuses the four answers because he did not hear them from the people who gave them; Rhys lays his want, pay on the day the water goes down, in nine short true sentences and does not vote; **Aren adds his third line and then deliberately leaves the gateway out of it, writes a fourth line about the four answers being in no book, and decides to go and see the seed keeper in the morning to find out whether forty years of keeping with no book on it is a kind of holding or a kind of hiding.**

**Ch 127 · third rolling · the vault, and three marks on the outside of it.** The unnamed woman of sixty-six shows nine sealed jars of wheat ungrown forty years and a wall with two earlier answers built up; she refuses the cooperative's thanks in advance; her want is the seed in nine jars and not in a wall; Aren touches nothing and carries no thanks, and says he wants the seed used rather than kept, and that a seed only in one room is an answer built up in a wall; the ledgers give sowing dates and yields and the falls are in the four white villages; **she then asks the question she has no right to ask a stranger, which is the year of her own second course, laid at thirty-one, which she has wanted to be told for thirty-five years; he answers that a course of stone gives the year it was quarried and the masons' season and not a man's harvest, and she says that is two thirds of her question; her second question is the floors and not the seed, and the reason is that in nine years or in a flood a man will be in the dark with a lantern and there are three feet of wall between the outside of the room and the older room, and a man who cannot find the floor will dig for the seed because the seed is the only thing anybody has heard of; he asks leave, tells her what it will cost the wall, and marks three things on the outside only, above last year's flood line: the older floor's height, the flood line, and low down the floor inside the third course, because the lowest mark is the one that saves a man, who is looking for a floor above his head and will put his lantern through it otherwise; she allows it, keeps the measure in her head because a measure in a book becomes an office, and says she has not decided whether she has been made a better keeper or a worse one and will decide in about a year; she tells him not to be sorry in a seed room, because a person who is sorry in a seed room has come to be forgiven; on the way out she says there is now a mark on the room and no keeper of it but the two of them, and that the water cannot read marks, and that the wall is the only thing in this county that does not need a book to be read, and that she has been wrong about that for forty years.**

**Ch 128 · third rolling · the league.** Ordmar Gace, 49, on twelve short acres, wants a man to say what he will not plant and keep his field; he foots nine days at eight pence, 24 + 24 + 24 = 72 pence, eight bushels of salt, and refuses the season though not the wage; with the council on money and against it on one thing, the lease; the yard's men recognise the reckoning; **Aren's closing note is now that Gace thanked Ferrow and Munn for refusing him flat and went away owing them nothing, and that a man like that comes back, and that what he brings will not be a term out of a bridgewright and will not be read out of a form.**

**Ch 129 · third rolling · the first proposal that is not his.** Gace proposes an oath that can be broken, on purpose, in public, by its swearers, on a day named in advance, with a release, a consequence, and no office behind it, a list of households to be helped; the council enters it without opinion; the woman on three hearths hears through the boards that a list to be helped differs from a list to be struck.

**Ch 130 · fourth rolling · Ash's fourth term offered wrongly, and corrected, and withdrawn from.** Aren offers that a person struck may answer and answering restores, and knows within a breath and a half it is wrong, a count removed is a wound and an oath unsworn is a promise, and a promise cannot be mended by answering, and there is no roll here for an answer to land in; the council declines without reproach and the minute enters the offer and the declining as facts with the hour; **he refuses the calendar conversion in his notebook, which is the plant, and then enters one line under it, which is the first decision he has made in this county: that he will carry nothing else up those stairs unless a person on it asks him by name;** nine days later a two-line fair copy comes down the line unasked for and with nothing sent after it to say it was coming, in the hand of the woman four hundred miles off who keeps the road at that end, **and the leaf says at its own foot that it is a true copy of four lines out of a minute of a bench in the clay county at that end of the road and gives the room and the hour so that anybody who doubts it can go and stand in the room and look, and carries two more things in the copyist's hand, that in nineteen years that office has entered two things against a person by name and this is the second, and that the woman's fourth column says wife and is not a trade and the entry is not a removal**; the line is we did not lose our register we lost our refusal and a register is what you have instead; he knows now where he read it and who wrote it, and tells nobody in the county, because the county does not know he knows; **the drawknife man asks him how long he is here, and he answers honestly that he does not know what he is doing in the spring, says nobody in that chamber has asked him for one thing, and asks to be put on the staging at seven; the drawknife man takes the sill as evidence and not as a promise, because a yard that took promises kept them in a book and this county has not got one.**

## Batch 0014 — Volume 03, Chapters 131–140

Block 4, second third of thirteen rollings, all path time in the Hearth Marches. POVs Aren in all ten, with the guild of nineteen, the council of eleven, Delya Munn, the minute-man, the slate woman, the bound, the yard's four men, the boy who reads the gauge, the digger, the woman of about sixty on three hearths, and the keeper of the seed room. Three mason's marks, nine sealed jars, a foot of water in a stone room, a four-line notice with no name at the top of it, and a column of eleven counties are on the page. Nobody dies.

**Ch 131 · fifth rolling, the guild's bench-day · the plan that was written before he came.** The guild sits on the fourth week of a rolling and the council on the second and has done for nineteen years by accident, so the bench-day falls in the week the oath-stone is sworn. Ferrow puts a book on the table written in the twelfth rolling and says he has not altered a line of it and that a man who corrects his own plan before anybody has read it wants to be agreed with. Four hundred and forty yards of ditch lies eleven yards off the trough. **The mechanism is said once here, in trade terms, by the guild and not by a stranger: a trough is dug to a level and does not walk, a ditch's head has not been at the trough for a hundred and forty years, the oath has been sworn eleven yards from the truth every year since the first one, and a ditch whose head is not at the trough does not take the trough's water, so the water stands against the low side of the bank, and a pond against a bank on a mile of boundary is a water table a mile wide, and that is the salt.** No drawing, because a drawing of water is a picture of something that has already gone. Munn asks what the field is going to be in four weeks when nine men say true words for the first time in a hundred and forty years and do not know it, and Ferrow will not tell her, because it is not a thing a first hand of a guild is entitled to do to a bench. The bound foots sixteen men at eight pence, 128 a day, three days, 384, iron and elm at 96, **480 pence, ten marks, fifty-three bushels of salt at nine pence and three pence over, and the three pence is the guild's and is written down twice.** The minute-man asks whether the council is asking the sixty for salt, and nobody answers for long enough that the room has plainly decided it is not going to, and Aren answers it: not off the four this year, and there will be nothing on paper in the year it is. The drawknife man asks in a guild room that the man who swore nothing should be entered as having sworn nothing. The drawknife man asks Aren in a lane what he swore, and gets an answer about a guild, and does not write it down.

**Ch 132 · sixth rolling, three days · the trench, the two fingers, the yard's four men, and the note in the book.** Three days of work from a first morning. Three pegs on the old bank's shoulder, because the shoulder is the only drawing the county has and you find it with a boot. The yard's four men are faster than the guild's and Ferrow watches for a quarter of an hour and goes and stands somewhere else. The boy with the pole reads two inches over the winter mark and says it twice without being asked, and Ferrow says in about nine sentences, to nobody, that the pond is filling, and that nobody in that room has ever looked down. **The new line comes down two fingers to the left, the same error the same first hand made eleven years back on the same system, and he says a leaf is not a survey and a decision made in a day on a wet bank is two fingers out and always in the direction of the man's own comfort, and writes the expectation of a finger and a half into the book with the hour against it, because a thing you expect to be true in a year is worth more in a book than a thing you found out in a day.** The bound reads the same 480 pence out of the guild's book. The council takes the plan and the minute says the council took the plan and the guild did the work, and does not say the council ordered it, because the council did not order it and could not, and if it did then in nine years there would be a book with a council in it that had ordered four hundred and forty yards of ditch. **The water then sits for nine days, and the pond against the low side of the bank does not move.** Thessa's board is half an inch up and stays there. He enters in his own book a decision that he will bring nothing else to that chamber unless a person on it asks him for it by name, and she tells him it is the right decision in the wrong county and that his column has a number and his does not. The drawknife man says the fen county's distance aloud once in a lane, and the market has a price on it within a week, and the price is too high, and nobody tries to put it right.

**Ch 133 · sixth rolling · a causeway on four days' notice, and a line of dead alders.** A hundred and sixteen feet of elm deck on four bays across the old bend, handrail on the upstream side only, built in four days because four days is what the ground gave. **The money: nineteen men at eight pence is 152 pence a day, four days is 608, with 96 of timber, which is 704 pence, fourteen marks two shillings and eightpence**, and Ferrow says out loud in front of the room that in nine years somebody will find that figure beside the 480 and call one of them a favour. The woman of sixty on three hearths comes to the guild room for the first time and says the name of the thing the deck is for, and nobody says it. **A man with a lantern in a wet week goes into the seed room behind the chapel, puts the lantern through the floor inside the third course, goes down on his hands and knees, and stands up on a floor three feet off the ground that is dry, because the middle is on gravel and the far end is not.** The keeper of sixty-six will not have it in a book and will not have it told. Ferrow asks one question in a yard and goes on shingling. The deck is finished on the third and a half day and the fourth day goes on the two ends, which is where a causeway's cost is, and the two ends are eleven feet wide with an oak sill and the deck eleven inches above it, and not one line of that is in any book. The standing water is in the trench for thirty-one days, counted as thirty-one and not thirty or thirty-two. Thessa's board goes four inches under the winter mark. **Ferrow tells Aren, and only Aren, that he built it for a day he cannot name, gives him the date and not the reason, and says that a council which expects a flood is a council with a plan.**

**Ch 134 · seventh rolling · four hours, and the Water comes into the old bed it has always had.** The water stops rising and the stillness is the last of the quiet. He writes four words and no reason in his own book — *a man who is not asked is not to ask* — because going to the council would have made him the source, and he promised a yard and not a book, and he tells only the man entitled to know, at a staging, in about six sentences, and Ferrow says he has saved the guild an hour and will lose the argument about the four words afterwards. **Then four hours of the guild, and Ferrow's account of it afterwards in nine sentences: a line of dead alders along the old bend whose youngest is older than the bound, which means the bed is there; the new bank is the weak part because it is three weeks old; and the only thing that can be done in four hours is to put a deck where the old bed is and hold it.** The first water comes over the new bank four hundred yards below the deck and looks like nothing, and the sound changes inside two minutes. The deck goes three feet sideways in the time it takes to say a word, because the bar is through the upstream rail. It stands eleven hours and one hour and forty minutes of it with eleven men, then goes the length of itself. The Water comes into the old bed above the town at about the eighth hour, and the seed room is four hundred yards below and east of the deck. **And in a room over a weigh-house four hundred miles off a keeper of records reads a line in a day-book that says a road held, three weeks, and has ninety-six days of error against a printed three weeks, and the bell is rung and nothing at the far end of it is struck, and she enters the hour against nothing and writes under it that this office is now keeping a column about a road in a county that is not on any map, and that she is the shelf.**

**Ch 135 · seventh rolling, two days · the flood, the salt in the new land, and nine minutes.** The first hour belongs to the seed room: nine sealed jars onto a chapel step in the order they stood on the shelf, counted, at the seventh hour. On the third trip in one of the four has a lantern, does not know there is a floor, goes down on his hands and knees and stands up on it with the jars over his head, and the only person he ever tells is a man with a bar on a deck about an hour later. **The Water comes into the seed room at the ninth hour and does not read the three marks on the outside of that wall and cannot.** Then Sallow Marsh: the water comes up the lanes from the ditch side and not down, because the trough filled from the top. The four lowest villages go under between a foot and three feet in eleven hours, and **nobody dies**, and the salt comes up in the new land because the flood took the top inch and a half off it. The woman of sixty on three hearths loses the barley and says one sentence in her own doorway. **The boy of fifteen goes in off the deck at the sixth hour and a half of the second day; the man at the drawknife goes in off the near bank with head up, feet down and one arm and has him out in about nine seconds and another eleven to the bank; the woman of sixty-six rings the chapel bell nine times and stops, and will not have the number in a book; the boy says four.** The number nine is in the council's minute in the county's own form with no name and the reason no name is entered, and the minute-man calls it the second silence of his career and the easier one. Thessa writes one line in 8,031 lines that is not a reading of a post, and bookmarks it, and nobody ever sees it. The crew's ninety days have run out and they stay, on the council's account, unfootable, and the minute-man enters four men and the word *none* in a money column.

**Ch 136 · eighth rolling · the marks in daylight, the three columns, and a name.** Ten days after the water went down, the two of them go and look at the wall, and the leave is the same leave with a worse condition: he is to look and not to put anything on it. The marks are exactly where they were. **The man at the drawknife asks the woman of sixty-six her name in a yard, because he is going to have to say it in a room in nine months and has found he cannot, and she says it once, and it is the only time anybody in that county says it.** The ledger is put up on the guild's board in three columns and footed in pence in front of nineteen men: **two men at eight pence is 16 a day, thirty-one days is 496, elm at 96, which is 592 pence, twelve marks, one shilling and fourpence**; the deck, 608 of men and 96 of timber, 704; and **a third column that is four men of a yard and the word *none*, because a man who is not being paid is not a debt, and it is the first line in nineteen years in either book with nothing in a money column.** The council takes all three onto its account. Ferrow says the third column is in two books in a county that has never had a man on its account from outside it, and that in nine years somebody will put the two side by side and say the county has a debt of four men it cannot name. Thessa's nine-day sheet, the boy on the wet bridge reading about nine seconds, a man at a sluice on two whites that are the same colour and are not the same.

**Ch 137 · eighth rolling, the fourth hour · the command, and the withdrawal broken in public.** Forty ditchmen on the floor of the room under the barn because a thing that happens to everybody in a county gathers everybody in one room. **He comes up the outside stair with a bar and a timetable, which is the old move, and Ferrow says the withdrawal out loud in front of eleven people, and Munn asks whether it is true, and he says it is, and he says in front of the room that nobody asked him for it by name, and gives no reason, and the minute-man enters the whole of it with the hour against no person.** The timetable is good and he reads it out. It is keyed to the hour, and the four stops in it are keyed to the hour too, and the stops came off Thessa's board and he would not have her name on them and would not have the book, and the room makes a small noise that is not surprise, because four of them said in a gateway six rollings ago that there was no list. Ferrow makes him say that he has put three claims in a room where he promised none, and the minute-man enters it exactly as said. **Then the minute-man asks the one question that decides the rolling: who is this to be, in the book.** The answer is under the council's name and no man's, and Munn stands up, which she does not do, and gives the reason, and reveals that she gave one of the four gateway answers in the second rolling and that a county which answers a child in a gateway has already said yes and cannot take it back and has it in no book, and then makes the keeper fetch a form out of Ferrow's book that has been in the county since a landlord asked for a ditch on his account nineteen years ago.

**Ch 138 · eighth rolling, the same sitting · nine seconds, and the hand-over.** The keeper reads the line to himself and then says it, and it takes nine seconds, and the eleven on the bench and forty on the floor hear the same nine words at once and nobody argues: **the council declines, and the guild will do it.** It is entered as a fact and not a defeat, and the reason is minuted in Munn's own words and not summarised, and the minute-man says that a book which records a refusal without recording that it was right is a book that invites the next man to try again. Ferrow puts the guild's own plan on the table, written in the twelfth rolling before Aren arrived, keyed to the water, with four stops and no work at all in the last three days. **The cost is footed in the room: 152 a day for nineteen and 32 a day for the four men of the yard is 184 a day, and six worked days of that is 1,104 pence, twenty-three marks exactly, and it lands clean because the plan says stop.** Aren concedes the plan is better in three places at his own request, names them, and says the load-bearing one: a sheet keyed to the hour has no last day and cannot be footed, and the stop has to be a mark on a board and a mark on a board is a reading and he had a reading and made it into an hour. **The hand-over is four minutes in the county's own form: the causeway and the waterworks are the guild's, the council does not order the work, and a stranger is of no council and holds no office in this county and may not be given one by this council, and he asked to be put under the council's hand and was not.** The fair copy goes on the board under the bench and the minute-man reads all of it aloud, and the room makes a space for the woman of sixty on three hearths, who has been outside that window twice and has never been in the room. The four men of the fen yard stay, and a man of fifty-two asks to be put in as not having sworn anything, in the guild's book and not the council's, with a date and no hour.

**Ch 139 · ninth rolling, third week · the notice, and the call.** A carter's boy and a basket of onions and a sheet of paper with no name at the top of it. The first line gives the hour, the county's own book, and the reason: the second of the county's terms, and this contains no name. The second says the line is not kept and the hand at that end is released and is not to be sought. The third says the book at that end is shut and is not to be asked for. The fourth is the second of the terms given back whole, forty-one words, and it is a refusal of him and it is correct, because the terms say no name and the county has kept them for nineteen years and the north has not, and the only name in the arrangement is in a file four hundred miles off in a column headed *Keeper* that a clerk filled off a printed example. **He reads the first three lines out and tells the room what the fourth one is and does not say it, and says in front of nine people that he is not going to say it.** The minute-man enters that he cannot enter a document with no name at the top of it, and enters that too, in the same entry, and calls it the first contradiction in twenty-two years and the one he is proudest of, and says it is the only thing this county has produced that an office four hundred miles off cannot get at, and that he has spent twenty-two years wishing this room were harder to find. Munn stands and says she gave one of the four answers in a gateway. Ferrow says the gateway is still standing and that being right about lists has not made the county harder to find. The woman of sixty on three hearths asks, once, for an entry that names nobody and is about a class of persons, and it is entered and read back. **He calls the second route once, properly, with the minute as witness, and the answer is the notice.** Above the entry, in a smaller hand with no hour against it, four words he did not write: *A road needs a living hand.*

**Ch 140 · ninth rolling · the column comes back, and the arithmetic stops.** In the same basket, under the onions, wrapped in oiled paper, addressed to a shelf, with the cost of it on the wrapper: **two men at thirty-two pence a day for three days is ninety-six, with six pence of sheet and wax and copying, which is a hundred and two pence of a bench's money.** Eleven counties, eight with a registrar's six words, three with a dash, and no head, because there has never been a head. **Under the column, the answer of a bench of nine in the county of clay, asked for by the keeper who sent the copy out and unprompted: nine stones in their own roll and not one between two fields, eight between two holdings of the same watercourse and one between two holdings of the same sowing, and a stone that fixes a share is a claim and not a boundary and a claim can be kept for a hundred and forty years.** Two hours in the cold room with the county's own roll chest open beside it. The eight stop being eight of one thing: **six fix a share of a watercourse and two fix a share of a sowing, and the column is a registrar's six words, and a shape is a category, and a category is the only reason a column has a count in it.** A dash is not a zero, and this county would be one of the three. **And the finding, in about nine sentences, and it is not a cause: an oath sworn in public by men who are still alive is the only durable consent a rural county can produce, and it is the only document in the countryside that names a body, and a body can be served, and that is why the records gather where the oaths are. Not because oaths cause anything. Because an oath is a name.** The Marches is the sixth of the eight, and a claim kept a hundred and forty years by men who are still alive does not sit still, and eleven yards of it is a mile of bank with a pond on the wrong side. He cannot get eight of eleven a fifth time in eight rollings and four rooms, and writes the reason. The minute-man enters the finding against nobody and **refuses to put it in the roll**, because a roll of this county is the only thing in it a person can be put in front of, and a second column is where a person goes to be got at, and four names went into one once and came out of it wrong. Ferrow is told the whole of it in a doorway, and says they are the sixth of the eight and that he has been the sixth for nineteen years and did not know, and that he would have asked two others and would not have asked the clay county, and then says Gace's proposal is the least dangerous thing in the county and that he has been calling it dangerous for four rollings.

## Batch 0015 — Volume 03, Chapters 141–150

**Block 5 of five and the last block of Volume 03. Ten complete scenes: path time for the tenth to the thirteenth rolling of a year of thirteen, the return, and twenty-two days in Merefen.** POVs **Aren Kest ×10.** Cast on the page, all established or addressed by trade and **no new name and no new face: Coll Ferrow, Delya Munn, Thessa Wend, the minute-man, the slate woman, the bound, Ordmar Gace, Aled Rhys, the boy who reads the gauge, the woman of about sixty on three hearths, the woman of sixty-six who keeps the seed room, the man of sixty on the roll, the man of sixty-three whose hand has been on the stone since he was nineteen, the man at the sluice, the man of fifty-two, and the four men of a fen yard; and in Merefen, Mara Vey, Alish Renn, Sarn Oyelaran, Lysa Fenn, Marda Oakes, Tovan Kest, the boy of fifteen, the boy of the Old Toll, the carpenter, the man who keeps eels, the guild's man with the levels, Reeve Aldis Fenn, the bench-clerk, Sabin Dain in a document.** **No System panel and no Gatehouse-voice line in the block.** No death. The tile on the coping is not touched and not looked at, **is named twice in the block as an object — Chapter 148 and the last line of Chapter 150 — and is never explained, and its device is named by nobody.** Chapters 141–147 carry no main-world month, no real-world month and no main-world weekday in any register, including a notebook; Chapters 148–150 are the second, twentieth and twenty-fourth of Hearthmonth, YR 313, a Thursday, a Monday and a Friday. Money foots three times on the page and once more as a fair copy's working.

**Ch 141 · the first week of the tenth rolling · the council's plan, the day named, and the cost.** Ferrow takes back the word *dangerous* in a room in front of forty men, having called Gace's proposal that for four rollings, because a frightened man who repeats a thing wrong in a passage has only been frightened in public. Gace's plan is put in the county's own words: the day named in advance, the release, the consequence, and **the four villages going on the list whole and not by household and not by fault, because a consequence that can be aimed is a list of persons and this council said in a gateway in the second rolling that there was no list.** The day is the fourth week of the twelfth rolling and is not moved. **The column foots on a board: nineteen of the guild at eight pence is 152 a day, the four of the yard at the guild's rate is 32, a day is 184, nine days is 1,656, and ninety-six of elm, and 1,752 pence, which is thirty-six marks and two shillings and the county has not got.** And the second half: the four hundred and forty yards of the fifth rolling cost 480, the difference is **1,272, twenty-six marks and two shillings, and Ferrow says in the room that the difference is what it costs to do a thing twice.** Aren sits at the end of the bench and says nothing, because his own entered withdrawal from the fifth rolling forbids carrying a second instrument up that stair uncalled for and Ferrow named it aloud in the eighth, so it can no longer be used in private without being a lie about the same thing twice. **The minute-man notices the year ending, in the county's words: a year is thirteen rollings from the first sowing, a rolling is four weeks, the year is a year and a rolling, and the record of a sowing in this county has been a stone, and a stone is a boundary and a boundary is not a return.** The man of fifty-two asks that the book record that he knows which of the two sums was the county's and which was the crew's, and gets nine words with a date and no hour.

**Ch 142 · the eleventh rolling · the list, and the sentence, and the want.** The list is made three weeks before the day so that a household can be told in a room what it gets for declaring itself loosed. **The form is Ferrow's, three columns and none of them a name: roll number, the household's own mark, what it would be helped with, and a fourth ruled line for a household that would rather say its need out loud.** It is keyed to the roll and the minute-man raises that the two books are in one room and a man with both can turn a household into a person, and proposes the answer, which is to write the knowing at the head of the list in the council's hand where a man who does not like it can find it. **The first mark is the man of sixty-eight's thumb, and it is a thumb and not the flood-year smudge on the roll, and he will not give his name and does not want it, and his four true words are still in nobody's book.** The second is the woman of about sixty on three hearths, who observes that the list is a list of people who were never asked to swear, and asks for the asking to be entered *after* the mark, not before, and refuses to say thank you for a seed question. **Twenty-nine of fifty-eight put a mark and one took a mark off, and the reason the man of fifty-two's ninth-rolling warning came true is not deception: four voices in a gateway told a child there was no list, that is still true, and a household shown a board with a thumbhole in it signs because the council was right.** The keeper of the seed room comes at the second hour of the third day, is read the knowing first, calls the form good, and then says the one sentence, once, and nobody improves on it and the minute-man's pen does not move: **a list of households to be helped you can take back, and a list of persons to be struck you cannot, because there was never anybody at the other end of it to take it back to.** In the vault she says all three parts of her want once, in her own words,
