and refuses thanks in advance, and says the marks on the wall are a different thing and that she has decided nothing about them and will decide in the spring or she will not.

**Ch 143 · the fourth week of the twelfth rolling · the stone broken in public.** Nine men renew the oath at the third hour and a man of sixty-three's hand is in the same place on the same stone as it was when he was nineteen, saying a different thing. **Munn reads the release from the front of the field and does not tell the room that the nine men are saying true words for the first time in a hundred and forty years, which is the one thing she has wanted to say in a field since the second rolling and she has carried it for nine rollings.** The stone stands and she says so and tells them not to touch it. The boundary is re-cut in nine days, four before and five after, by the people on both sides of it, because a boundary that is a channel cannot be re-cut by one side. **The east side is four hundred yards behind the west side for about four hours on the second evening, and the old head is nine feet off the new one where they meet, and the second night the water comes up to the top of the new ditch and stops.** A promise that can be broken is a promise that can be tested, tested nine days after it was made. The four villages sow into ground the digging finished six days before, which is wrong, and the rain comes on the eighth day. A man at a sluice says the true thing to a stranger and then hears himself and says he has been standing on that bank for nineteen years and has never been asked how deep the water was or how long it would take to come down. **The plan holds and two of the four villages' ground is worse, and the salt has not moved.**

**Ch 144 · the last week of the twelfth rolling · the guild, the crew, and a name in a yard's book.** Ferrow puts the guild's book on the bench turned round and shows the minute-man nineteen words and a name he has left off for four rollings, and the man of fifty-two says the other half of it before he will allow it in: that the county wrote four hundred miles on a cost board because a board wants a county, and that he has never written it and never said it and has given the same answer twice to a man with a slate and a woman at a sluice, which was *we are a yard and a yard is where we are*. **The yard is entered as the yard of Tovan Kest, Weir Street, in a fen town four hundred miles off, spelled with a v after Ferrow gets the letter wrong once, and with the street added because a name is a man and a street is a place and a place is a thing a person can be shown and a man is not.** Ferrow's reason for wanting it is that on the day they go there will be a moment and it will not be a speech. **The man of fifty-two then says, in a gateway, in front of about nine of the guild's men and nobody in particular, that they are from a fen county four hundred miles off and that they are the best crew in that county, and that he has said it in a gateway and not in a room, and nobody writes it down.** The money foots: **four men at eight pence is 32 a day, 32 nine times is 288, 288 and 288 is 656, 656 and 288 is 864 pence, eighteen marks exactly, paid in ninety-six bushels of salt at nine pence the bushel, which is also 864, and Ferrow says it lands clean and will not pretend it was arranged.** The rest of the stay is at none, in two books, and the man of fifty-two gives the yard's rule, that a book that knows what a man has decided not to do is an office. The man at the drawknife says one thing at a bank about a boundary being a channel and about a ditch that goes the same wrong way because the man filling it in stands on the side he is standing on, and the drawknife man is three feet away and has never repeated it.

**Ch 145 · the last day of the thirteenth rolling · the hand-over, in the county's own form.** The minute-man reads the eighth rolling out of the book first, because a form cannot be filled in over a refusal that has not been acknowledged, and enters that the man bringing this one was not asked for it and that giving is not carrying anything up a stair. **The form is seven columns — what is given, to whom in the county's words, by whose hand, the day, the hour, a witness at this end, a witness at that end — and there is no column for an office and no column for a vote, and a vote is a thing a man carries into a room he is standing in.** **The count is seven and was printed as six at three places until the Batch 0015 review repair pass corrected it; the chapter's own list is the authority and it is seven.** What is given is the causeway, the waterworks, the mile of boundary, the trough, the ditch at its head, four hundred and forty yards already opened, the new work, the list and the nine jars, and the bound says that is a mile of water and a board with twenty-nine thumbs on it and Munn says that putting less in it would be being careful in the wrong direction on the last day of the year. **The road goes to the guild at the guild's terms and to a yard of a fen county four hundred miles off at a yard's rate, and the name is the yard's and not his and he holds nothing in it.** The far-end witness is a yard, entered as valid on a fact carried in ninety-six bushels of salt in a handcart, and the minute-man says a record in another county in a cart with salt in it is worth what a cart is worth and that is better than nothing. **The witness at this end is the woman of about sixty on three hearths, because a column not filled in is a hole in a document and because a rule with an exception in it has the exception as the person it is about; she says four words: I'll hold it.** Ferrow raises the two Kest names on one board as the objection he is obliged to raise and refuses to have either removed. The minute-man asks whether the two of them are anything to each other that the council is entitled to enter, is answered *No*, enters it exactly as given, and says out loud that the rule which protects a stranger from being asked about his family is a rule a woman put in this book four rollings ago about a class of persons and not about herself, and that he is entering the half that makes him look careful and not the half that does not. **The minute carries the hour and no name. The last line on the sheet is under the rule in the keeper's own hand and it is short: the road is not to be asked for back by anybody, and least of all by him.**

**Ch 146 · the last day of the year, from the ninth hour · the Water comes back up, and the lid.** Thessa Wend brings the number and the water is coming over a bank a month old and is not the ditch. Ferrow gets out the plan, puts the sacks on the low side, takes the month's old bank down in two places to let the water have a chosen way, and **puts in the guild's book before anything else happens that the plan holds, because a plan that fails gets a book written about it and a plan that holds gets nothing.** The thing that does not hold is a five-yard two-bay elm footbridge on two oak sills at the trough, and Ferrow states its history in nine sentences and states that it is the best thing four days' notice allows and that a lid is now taking a whole night river. The elm is green, the oak is in a bed of new trench, and the four days' notice is the whole of what happened and it is a schedule and not a fault. **Aren goes out at the tenth hour and Ferrow does not ask him, and a man of sixty-three asks why and is told that thirty men on a bank can throw gravel and nobody in the county can read a deck that is taking water, and that a stranger says out loud the one sentence that makes him not a stranger for nine hours and not one of the forty takes it.** Aled Rhys says the true thing about his own trade in nine sentences from a boat and does not vote once: **a boat throws something off and a bridge cannot, so a bridgewright decides in the morning what to throw off in the night, and tonight the people are off it and the water is not, and a man on a span is not holding the water off, he is keeping the deck in one piece so that the water has one way to go instead of four, and if he says it wrong everybody on the bank dies of being wrong in the correct direction.** Eleven calls go into the guild's book and every one is early. The water comes over the lid and goes across it and off the far end, and a mile of re-cut boundary takes the night river.

**Ch 147 · the fourth hour to the seventh hour of the morning · nobody died, the limp, and the figure written on faith.** **Nobody died, and the number of men on that bank is thirty and the reason it is thirty is a ditch, a bank, nine days of work and a man on a lid.** Thessa Wend comes up at the fourth hour, stands four lines back, watches a quarter of an hour and says nothing; he has taken the weight off the right foot and into the left and does not put it back, and she sees a load point move, and walks a quarter of a mile to her hut in nine minutes and writes the second line in eight thousand and thirty-one that is about a man and not about a post, and bookmarks it, and shows it to nobody. At the fifth hour the near sill goes two inches in under a minute without a sound, the near bay drops four inches and catches on the far bay's mortise, and he gets to the sound bay with the bar and the line and the load goes through his hands and his right hip and his right leg. **He does not let go of the rail. The span holds for three more hours with one bay hanging four inches, and the near bay never once gave the water a fifth place to go, and that is what a lid does.** He comes off it in four minutes and the leg will not take him, and the man at the sluice meets him at the ditch with a bar he is not told to use and does not use and puts a shoulder under his arm. The minute-man reads three entries on a wet bank and enters the eleventh call and enters that no name is in it and will not look for one. Munn comes last and says one sentence she has said in no room in nine rollings and does not say that he did the second thing for the first one. **He writes the drift figure on faith in the Marches' own register, as the fifth of a month this county does not use, a hundred and seventeen days, a printed three weeks, ninety-six days of error, about four fifths of the days and not of the warning, and that he will check it on the near side and if the two are not the same number he has put a month in it twice.** The line is made up in nine minutes with the hour against each of the nine. The hand at this end is the guild, the hand at that end is a yard that has been sent ninety-six bushels of salt and has not yet entered anything and may never. **The witness at this end is the woman of about sixty on three hearths, chosen by this end as the fifth term requires, who says she will do it properly and in front of people and that there is not one book in this county that can produce her afterwards and that is why it is worth doing.** The last entry of the year is eight words long and carries the hour.

**Ch 148 · the second of Hearthmonth, YR 313, a Thursday, a market day · the return, the fever, and a document.** He comes up the line in a gateway in Weir Street at about the fourth hour in the rain, because the north end of that line is a yard and a yard's gate is a place a cart goes through, and the yard is shut and the street is empty and nobody looks for him. The bell goes at the fourth hour and a half four hundred yards up the road and he does not turn round. Three days of fever, the second, the third and the fourth, breaking on the fifth, held by a boy of fifteen who cannot make a fire for an hour and a half and then can, and who carries a scythe edge down to the yard for a man who cannot lift it. **A boy of seventeen comes down Weir Street with a line in his hand to ask, by the arrangement, whether the man at the rooms over the cooper is carrying anything, and says he is not going to say he is carrying nothing because that would be a lie and not that he knows what is in it, and that it is the fourth time he has asked and he will not ask a fifth on a Friday because a Friday is not the day the far end keeps and it would be a wasted ask.** On the morning of the fourth the yard's book has a fifth line in it with an hour against it and nothing at all against it, written in nine seconds with a wet nib, and the boy of fifteen asks what it is for and is told it is nothing, and puts the kettle on. **On the fifth the fever breaks and he gives her the numbers before she asks, in the order they happened, including the printed three weeks that has not changed and belongs to nobody, the ninety-six days, and the finding that the error is in him and scales with the work and that the second time he proved it was on a lid with one leg.** And he tells her the one thing that is not a figure, which is that he cannot put an ordinary morning in a yard in the order it happened, and does not tell her that he cannot, and **the third rule ends *a habit is a thing a county does to itself when nobody is looking*, and she wrote that sentence four months ago, and it is about him, and he knows, and neither of them has ever said so.** She does not ask. **The ninth not-asking stands and there is no tenth.** On the sixth, in his own hand, with the date and the hour because a thing that is set goes in a book and a thing that is not set goes in a coat: **the four-hundred-and-eleven-word refusal of Sabin Dain's offer, in a document and not a scene.** The rank is a command and not a custody and a command a person appointed by nobody can hold is the thing he will not take. The sealed record is a record he cannot see and he has been worse at refusing it than he is at building bridges. The sheet's missing number of persons is the only sentence on it that matters and eleven marks is the price of not printing it. The warrant is not to be fetched. The eastern paths are not to be closed by him and it is not a bargaining position. The earlier reply in a bag is not withdrawn. **There is no thanks in it, and she did not offer a thing that needed any.** He seals it along the left edge with a knife, because a man does not send a letter in a bag in a way that cannot be seen to have been opened.

**Ch 149 · the twentieth of Hearthmonth, YR 313, a Monday, not a market day · the Marches account.** The fair copy comes down the line on the twelfth, addressed to a shelf, and waits eight days because neither of the two people in the room can work out who should open it, and Alish Renn says the thing that has to be said first, that a document with an address on it is a different animal. **The fair copy is four hundred and eight words and there is a working folded with it**, and both are in the minute-man's hand, and the head of it is nine hundred and forty-one. **That is the wrong number and Mara Vey says she can tell it is wrong without leaving the room and cannot say what the right one is, and says those two sentences in that order because the second is the part nobody in a filing room ever says.** The county's own figures are three and none of them is a hearth. The figure and the word under it are both struck with the reason in the margin. **The number nine hundred and forty-one is also the only figure this office holds about the county of clay, and the two are the same by accident, and she writes down that it is a coincidence and asks every person in the room to hear the word and not improve on it and does one single thing with it.** The working foots: 480 + 704 = 1,184; 1,184 + 592 = 1,776; 1,776 + 1,104 = 2,880; 2,880 + 1,752 = **4,632 pence, ninety-six marks and two shillings**, checked twice with a rule, and the sixth line is four men and the word *none*. The account says the salt is not lifted and the first crop off two of the four villages is a failure on a board in a keeper's hand with the hour on it, and it asks, for the first time in this case's history, another county for a jar of a seed it does not grow. **A body corporate entered a company of seed and bridge, named in the Marches' own hand as the nine jars and the ditch, with a date and a name and a witness at each end, and the two witnesses have never met and cannot be produced to each other and both facts are in the entry.** Its first act is a four-line letter that is not answered inside the year and is not going to be answered. **Lysa Fenn and Marda Oakes were in the room twice and conceded nothing**, and Marda Oakes stopped in the middle of a sentence on purpose and said why, and the second copyist entered the half sentence. **The Archive resolved to hear the delegation on the Marches' own terms and entered the hearing for the ninth of Rainmonth of the year following**, with the six terms held in the office and no person to be sent to ask anything, and the delegation not to be sent for, and if it does not come the hearing is not adjourned and not refused and it stands.

**Ch 150 · the twenty-fourth of Hearthmonth, YR 313, a Friday, not a market day · the last image.** Ordinary work: four carts of stone, pointing that is never finished on the day it is begun, the nose of the third pier, forty men, a tool bag, load points counted twice into a book that does not exist, and a stick that is for four hundred yards of road and nothing else. **The guild's man with the levels is asked to measure the limp and declines in about nine seconds, and gives two reasons, and the second is the better one: a measurement of a limp is a measurement taken again in a year, and then it is a decline, and a decline is a survey, and a survey goes in a form.** He says he would have said no in about four if it had been a Tuesday. In the hall over the wharf **the number is four, because the fifth asking is in the reeve's coat and is not on the table**, and she asks for it to be entered that she came up two flights with it and did not put it, and that the reason is a sentence in her own minute, and that a bench asked the same thing five times has not been asked five things. **Toban's sentence is in the minute again, word for word, and there is no second version of it in this county or in any other.** He is on the roll, he has a vote, nobody asked him for it, and he has never said out loud in that hall that a vote and not an office is not enough to build anything on. Four hundred yards up the road: the dressed block and forty yards of tarred hemp, a bell with forty-one rivets and a dull red seam, rung at the fourth hour and a half by a boy of seventeen and a carpenter who is a hand and not a keeper. **The arrangement is not his and nothing in the sound of it is his. A Monday is the day the far end keeps, it was a Friday, nothing was struck at the other end, and the two facts are in a day-book separately and are not in one sentence.** Four hundred miles off there is a road that is not there, a woman who keeps its end, and a tile on a coping that nobody has looked at. He stood at the end of the line. **He comes up two flights to the weigh-house with a tool bag and a stick and does not knock, gives the two figures, and she puts her pen down and does not write the tenth and neither of them says why, and he declines to give her a figure for the leg because the figure would be hers to keep and it is not a fact about a person.** **The last line of the block is about the road.**

## Batch 0001 — Volume 04, Chapters 151–160, "The Relief"

**Block 1 of five and the volume's first block, and the first block of a new volume on a new path.** Ten complete scenes. **Main world, the twenty-fifth of Hearthmonth to the eighth of Goatmonth, YR 313, thirteen days elapsed, and then path time, and then the valley's own register. POVs, corrected at the Batch 0001 review: **not Aren Kest ×10.** Chapters 158, 159 and 160 are his, and Chapter 157 is his for the first two thirds and the far end of the line for the last third. **Chapter 151 is the board under the stair and Aled Rhys and a bench of nine and a keeper of records, and he is a late appearance in it. Chapters 152, 153 and 154 are the room: the county post in 152 and a clerk opening a letter in 153 and Lysa Fenn beating a form's own construction in 154, and he is a walk-on in 154 and absent from 152 and 153. Chapter 155 is his in the yard, on the deck, in the notebook and in the gateway, and in a room on a weigh-house for a third of it. Chapter 156 is his, with nine other people in it.** A reader is owed the truth about this and the block's own summary was wrong. Cast on the page, all established or addressed by trade, **and one new name and five slots unfilled: Iven Sarr**, 58, named once, in a letter, in Chapter 154. **Nine people in the valley and not one of them named**: the woman of about forty at the Low Holm, the man of about eighty-one, the woman of about thirty with a slate, the man of about thirty-four with sixteen men and no work, the woman of about sixty-six at four looms, the girl of about nine at the gate, the man who makes nails, and two unremarked steadings' worth of ordinary valley people. **Returned faces:** Mara Vey, Alish Renn, Sarn Oyelaran, Lysa Fenn, Aldis Fenn, Bram Ottery, Rowan Petch, Aled Rhys, Tovan Kest, **Sabin Dain in a document and nowhere else.** **No System panel. One Gatehouse-voice line, in Chapter 160, unattributed, not written down.** No death. **The tile on the coping is named twice more as an object and is never explained, and the road to that end of the line is not reopened.** Chapters 158–160 carry no main-world month, no real-world month and no main-world weekday in any register including a notebook. Chapters 151–157 are main world and are dated off `outline/volume-04.md` §17's chain. **Money foots once on the page and cites a second column without re-deriving it.** Block total **33,045 words** after the Batch 0001 review repair pass, per chapter 3766 / 3512 / 3281 / 3311 / 3388 / 3078 / 3003 / 3213 / 3265 / 3228, **against the 32,831 and the 3766 / 3502 / 3281 / 3311 / 3276 / 3055 / 2993 / 3178 / 3265 / 3204 the block first wrote.**

**Ch 151 · the twenty-fifth of Hearthmonth, YR 313, a Saturday, a market day, the fourth hour · the board under the stair, and the fifth asking.** Four carts of stone, about forty men, pointing that is never finished on the day it is begun, the nose of the third pier, and **a man who gets up onto a deck off the gravel in three moves instead of two, which is a thing nobody in that yard can see.** **A water-barge hand of twenty-seven from the Hearth Marches**, in Merefen eleven weeks on a crew's account and paid, comes up two flights of a stair in the rain and reads a fair copy off a board under the bench, **and asks for it to be copied, and nobody had to be asked.** The keeper of records reads all of it aloud twice — the second time for a man of about fifty who has come up behind a carter with nine crates of clay pipe and is not the person the book is about — **and then copies it, four hundred and eight words and a working on a second sheet, in about two hours, and refuses to foot the addition in a document from a county that is not on any map, and gives the reason in four words: *it is not ours*.** **The Marches' six terms are printed in the chapter verbatim, in order, and not re-worded.** One copy goes down the stair to a bench of nine and one goes four hundred miles on the same barge the man came in on. **The fifth asking is put on the table by Reeve Aldis Fenn, who carried it up two flights on Friday and did not put it, and the bench says no in about a minute and a half, and the reason is the board, and the first of the six lines is that the road is not made to carry a person, and the second is why the man who built the road may not write a name at the north end of it, and the sixth is a door, and the reeve asks that the minute say a door is a thing you can be killed in.** Bram Ottery has the bench-clerk rule a blank column in the guild minute for a yard's unpaid wages and the reeve initials it. **The chapter ends on four hundred yards of gravel that carry nobody and a boat that carries nobody and a boy who weights a sheet under a tarpaulin with a stone and does not ask what it is.**

**Ch 152 · the twenty-seventh of Hearthmonth, a Monday, not a market day · the county post, and a notice that answers nothing.** **A four-line notice comes down a road four hundred miles off in a fair hand, dated four days before it arrived, so it was written while it was on the road, and it is four days for four lines — REPAIRED at the Batch 0001 review, which found the block deriving nine days of road from four days of dating here and in continuity item 399 — and it is not the answer to anything because nothing was asked of that county** — and what makes that true is on the page: a resolution of the twentieth of Hearthmonth says no person is to be sent to ask anybody in that county anything, and this county kept it. Its third and fourth lines are the autumn notice's third line and the whole of what that county has to say to him, and the keeper of records copies the fourth line out entire and puts it in the press and will not say which four words of her own minute it shares. **A second copyist rules a list of things sent in four columns and leaves the ruling two inches short of the width of the page — and she is the second copyist and not the keeper throughout, REPAIRED at the Batch 0001 review, which found this chapter alone in the block giving her a third-person reference to herself as *the second copyist of this office* and the press in her own hands, against Chapter 153, Chapter 154 and canon; she now refers to herself in the first person and hands the press to the keeper, and the keeper still says *Move it*****, and a junior clerk proposes a fifth column for what came back and is told that somebody will write *nothing* in it in about four years and that it is true when he writes it, and that this office does not do that. **The letter for a jar is not in the bag and never has been in the bag**: it went to a post office, and a post office writes to the last address held for a county, and the last address is a roll of households with no address on it and no person at the end of it. **The money beat, footed on the page before the total is said: a carrier at ten pence and a horse's keep at four is fourteen a day, fourteen nine times is 126, four sheets at twopence is eight, and 134 pence, which is two marks and three shillings and twopence, and he has paid it himself and would like it on the same page as the list and not on the wrapper.** The keeper reads out the Marches' contract whole without recomputing it, names the one fifth of 648 pence given up in a gateway and out of a yard's pocket, notes the same figure is the price of 216 bushels of seed in the same line of the same account, and says in one sentence that the two are the same number and are not the same money. **The eleventh line of the list carries a marginal note placing this county as the sixth of the eight in a column of eleven, entered once, against nobody, and not to be entered again.** The chapter ends with the notice in the press and a sheet of writing paper nobody in the room is willing to open.

**Ch 153 · the first of Goatmonth, a Friday, not a market day · the salt-loft bag, and a number that is not on the page.** The sheet of writing paper has been on the table under a limestone weight for four days because a keeper who opens a letter addressed to somebody else is a woman who reads other people's post. **Alish Renn opens it and says the thing that has to be said before a document is opened, and the reason she opens it is that it is in her hand and not because it is hers.** **The refusal is four hundred and eleven words in a hand two people in that room can recognise and neither of them says so**, and it is read out entire, and **nobody in that room apologised to anybody and nobody had anything to apologise for, because there is nothing in the document a person could apologise about.** **The clause that lands is the third: the number of persons the offer has been made out to is not stated on the sheet, and an office that will not print a number of persons cannot show that any person has not been instructed — which is the other half of the same arrangement this office has had in its press for four years and a half, in a leaf, in a column headed *Keeper*, in a hand that is not the man's.** The keeper refuses to say the woman's name in the room. The Archive asks for the number in a return with no name in it and no word of how it came by it. **A carter with a barrow of lime asks what is written on the back of a printed form in the lane and is refused about it in four words, and the marginal instruction is cited in a return and is not explained and is in nobody's mouth.** The cut band goes into the press whole. **Aren is four hundred yards away on a bridge for the whole of the chapter and does not know it came back cut.**

**Ch 154 · the fourth of Goatmonth, a Monday, not a market day · a count beaten in nine minutes, and a letter.** **REPAIRED at the Batch 0001 review: the block printed a Tuesday, and `outline/volume-04.md` §17's chain makes the fourth a Monday, and seven places in the chapter leaned on the slip — the council's Saturday is five days away and not four, the paper is returnable on or before the seventh of Thawmonth, YR 314, a Saturday, and Lysa Fenn beat the form on a Monday.** One bag, two things in it, and **the second copyist writes at the foot of the wrapper that they arrived together and that they were a coincidence and that no person in the room is to do anything with it, and the only thing anybody does about it is to rule a line and stop two inches short of the edge of a page.** The Crown instrument is a **Count of continuing obligations in respect of a district, with a space for a figure and no space for a name**, returnable on a Monday named in advance two hundred miles off, **and it is not the county's own form, which is ruled in seven.** The office's own hand on the reverse defines the obligation as held by a person who has gone out of the district and whose name is entered against it in any record of the district. **Lysa Fenn beats it in nine minutes with the form's own construction — a count of persons is a return of a figure, a figure is not a person, no person is in default, a thing against which nobody is in default is spent the moment it is answered — and the discovery this time is that the paper is not returnable by this office at all, and she says out loud that she has just handed a general machine to anybody with a door and is frightened of her own answer.** The return goes out with a space for a figure and nothing in it and no name anywhere. **The letter is from Iven Sarr**, and it is read aloud entire, and it asks for a man who can read a hand and not a man who can cross, **and it declines to name the man it is not asking for, and says that that sentence is the only one in the letter about a person he has not met, and nobody in the room finishes that thought.** **A keeper of records reads it and not the road warden. She is asked to go and does not, and says why in four words: *A keeper does not travel.*** **The hearing for the ninth of Rainmonth is referred to and not staged: a Tuesday at the second hour, a delegation that is not to be sent for, a room that will be shut, and a keeper who will be the only person in it.** The letter is not answered this week, and the reason is four words and they are *it is not a filing*.

**Ch 155 · the sixth of Goatmonth, a Wednesday, not a market day · a bigger count, and a mark cut in daylight.** **The office's answer to a legal argument is one sheet with a figure on it, 2,640, dated, in a woman's hand, with no seal, no number, no returnable date, no signature and no name, and it is the only answer that office has ever given to a question it did not like the form of.** Nobody in that room can check it, because this district has never been counted, and the only way to find out is to count it, and counting a district is a thing a Crown's hand is for. **REPAIRED at the Batch 0001 review, which found this chapter calling the reply *a bigger figure than the one asked for* on a paper that asked for no figure, and putting a man in the room saying 2,640 was more than the district when Chapter 154 establishes the district as a county of nine thousand. He is answered with the nine thousand, and the keeper's sentence is that a figure under a head count and a figure over one are the same figure because neither of them is a count.** **No name in a Crown's hand for the second time in two volumes, and there is still not one since the twenty-seventh of Fallowmonth.** Ordinary work, and a yard that is shut, and a date on a bill with a line under it that the boy of fifteen has ruled and filled with nothing, and two sentences in a gateway about a date and a Wednesday. **He comes up the two flights and gives her the numbers before she asks, and for the first time in four years and a half there is no morning in it, because there is no day over there that anybody keeps**, and the ninth not asking stands and no tenth is entered, and the reason is that a keeper who asks that is asking a person to be able to do a thing. **And he cuts a mark into the outside face of the parapet of the Sedge Reach at the ninth hour, in front of about twenty men, at the height of a thumb, on the face the weather gets at, with a cold chisel and not a stamp, with no name in it** — a line and a bar and a figure. **A girl of about eleven comes up off the near bank with rushes and puts her hand flat on it to see whether it is warm.** The not-securing is visible and is not explained, and the last line says what a thing left out in daylight is and not why he did it.

**Ch 156 · the seventh of Goatmonth, a Thursday, not a market day · nine questions, a witness, and a coat with four things in it.** **The nine are made up on the fourth step of a stair in nine minutes with the hour against each of the nine; seven of them take four seconds apiece and two take the rest and neither of the two gets a figure, and one of the nine is *no frame*, which is the first figure of its kind he has ever entered.** Nine is a name for the place. Two is the moment it turned on. Three is what is still standing of it. Four is who is at the other end, and he reaches for a Crown's word for a person a community appoints to authorise a contact and strikes it, because he does not know that anybody in that valley has appointed anybody. Five is what the going and the coming are called here, and six is what a day is, and **eight is what it is for and nine is whether anybody there has been asked, and under nine he writes that nobody there has been asked and he is going anyway and he has read it and is still going.** **The witness at this end is a man of forty-seven on a bench because he was elected, paid fourpence a week out of a box, who says he will witness a man being on a bridge and will never be able to say what he did, and asks for it to be written down that he said yes before he did it and not after** — the first entry in that minute ever made at the request of the person it is about. A letter from a valley four hundred miles off is handed to the clerk of the county council for Saturday and is read out and minuted as received with nothing done with it, and the man it concerns is not in the room. The bell goes at the fourth hour and a half because it goes every day and a Monday is the day that far end keeps and this was not a Monday. And at the ninth hour he is in the rooms over a cooper with four things in a coat and knows exactly what they are, and does not write the fourth one down, and goes out into the rain with a stick that goes four hundred yards and a county that has nine hundred.

**Ch 157 · the eighth of Goatmonth, a Friday, not a market day, the fourth hour · out.** The Old Toll, four hundred yards up the road, in abeyance, no owner, no money, no glass in the sill, no third seat, a dressed block and forty yards of tarred hemp. **Bram Ottery has been there twenty minutes and says that if he is a fool then he is a fool on a bridge at the fourth hour and not in a room, because a room would remember it.** He does not say anything, because a witness who is asked beforehand finds afterwards that he wanted to know afterwards. **A line, and two hands, and a date, and a witness at this end who is not the person keeping it**, said out loud in eleven seconds, including that he does not know what he is going for and is going and both are true. The bell goes at the fourth hour and a half behind him and he hears about a third of it. **The coping on each side of that sill has nothing at all on it, and this is not the coping and this is not that county, and the man who swept a tile on one for forty-one years is not going to be sent for.** **There is no month here and no weekday and there has never been a month, and the word for the year is a winter and a generation is a winter and there have been nine.** The light is not fen light and the water is not the colour of anything he knows and is doing about four feet a second and is the most ordinary thing he has ever stood next to. **A lintel over the door of a stone room over a byre, four feet long, the only dressed stone he can see, with nothing on it and no device and no inscription and no rope and only the row of chisel marks from when it was cut.** A girl of about nine at a gate tells him in nine words and a sentence that there is nobody on the road and there has not been anybody on the road all winter. He wants to ask what the lintel is and does not. **She will not give him her name and gives him the name of the house instead, and says that is a place and it will not be her.** A woman of about forty puts a yoke down in the middle of a yard and says she is not going to ask him anything and that on the bank a thing is said to the river and at the fire a thing is said to a person, and that she would like it kept that way for a stranger for about a week.

**Ch 158 · the valley, his fourth week · the first sight of a place that is not starving, and a conversion refused.** **REPAIRED at the Batch 0001 review, which found the block printing six weeks here, *three weeks since the gate, ten sleeps* on the page and *nine weeks and two sleeps* fifteen lines later, and a conversion called the second attempt in one line and the third in another. His own count is three weeks and two sleeps at the fourth week, the valley's is the ninth week of the cold, both are on the page and neither is converted, and the conversion is the third attempt in this case.** **The drift figure is written on faith in the notebook** — the fourth hour, the nine minutes, the printed three weeks unchanged, the days not written because he does not have them, and the honest second half: *I have a number and no frame, which has not happened to me before.* **On the fourth morning he tries to convert and refuses it in his own notebook, and it is the second time in this case that anybody has tried.** He writes *nine years to a winter, and there is no authority in this valley that I could ask, and a guess is not a frame*, and then writes nine weeks and two sleeps, and both on the page, and neither in any calendar he came with, **and he does not do the arithmetic at all the third time.** Eleven steadings, a scatter of hamlets, a market town that is a wide place with a cross and a smithy. **The Burn runs the whole nine miles of it and is a working river and is not a river in a valley that is dying. The byres are full. The stores hold. The births are normal: eleven children in eleven steadings and a town, and a woman of about thirty with a slate comes up the bank about once in ten days and reads the numbers out to whoever is in the room and nobody has ever argued with her about one.** **And nothing here is a thing that a man of any trade can fix, and there is not one yard of made road in four hundred people.** The woman at the Low Holm tells him, unasked, that the room over the stair is the winter book and that about two hundred people are on it from birth and that she could not tell him one name in it, and that he is going to be asked about the book whether he likes it or not, and that nobody in nine generations has had that room in a day. **And the oldest person in the valley, a man of about eighty-one, says the winter has never once been asked whether it is wanted** — nine generations of somebody standing up in a cold room, never once refused, never once asked — and that he is eighty-one and knows exactly what he would have said and nobody ever thought to want to hear it, and then asks him to have some more soup because he is thin.

**Ch 159 · the valley, a month · the winter book, a roll nine generations deep, and a want.** **Iven Sarr keeps the only roll in a valley nine generations deep and it is the only book of its kind in three seasons' travel and there is no second copy of it anywhere.** Four columns and none of them is an office: the day, the mark, the hand, and a fifth with a name in it on the older spreads. **Nobody who has ever kept the book is in the book, not one, and the man who first did that wrote one line about it at the head of the first spread and Sarr will not read that line either.** **He will not read a name to a stranger tonight and will not read one tomorrow, and the reason he gives is about two hundred people and not about the book, and it is the best reason a man can give for not doing a thing he has wanted done for six years.** The fourth spread is the sixth generation, and the ninth spread is the ninth, and the two reasons are *they went* and *they were born*, and there has never been a third reason in nine spreads and he has looked for one every winter for nineteen years. **And he says his want, all of it, in about a minute, and says it badly, and says why he said it badly, and in the saying he uses a sentence out of his own handwriting that he has not looked at in nine years: that a valley which has kept a thing for nine generations and has never once been asked is not a valley with a tradition, it is a valley with a habit, and a habit is a thing a place does to itself when nobody is looking — and no character in this block knows where that sentence came from and no character may ever tell him.** A man of thirty-four shows him a floor laid wrong and a footing not on the wall and a lead strip upside down, and says he has sixteen men and no work and will not ask him to do anything and would like it understood that he has not asked, and says that a valley has had **a hand** at every wall for nine generations and has never had one road. He shuts a gate frame out of true in four hours with a stick used as a plumb bob and nobody in that valley thanks him and he is not surprised. **And a young man of the ninth generation stops in a doorway because a word is used about him in an argument about a gate, and Aren Kest is standing nine feet away with a cup he has not drunk from, and the word is not a long word and he had never heard it, and every person in that room had heard it a thousand times, and he did not ask.**

**Ch 160 · the valley, the ninth week, and forty days · the word, and the not-asking.** **REPAIRED at the Batch 0001 review, which found the chapter saying he had been there six weeks in two places, nine weeks in two more and forty days in five, and opening by saying the ninth week of the cold *begins* when Chapter 158 has him already in it, and putting the Beck up in the ninth week when the man of eighty-one said the fourth week and Chapter 158 has him saying it came up on the fourth. It is forty days throughout, the ninth week is a week he is already in, and the fourth week stays the fourth week. The opening now reads that the ninth week is the week the Beck comes up in, that the old man said the fourth and the fourth is what it did, and that the valley made its own arrangements about that man and water a long time ago.** He goes out at first light to the ford and back, and puts his hand flat on the lintel, and gets the one Gatehouse-voice line of the block: *You are expected.* **He does not write it down. He has had four in four years and has written one. He does not ask the woman over the byre and he does not ask the girl at the gate and that is three not-doings on one morning and none of them was a decision and all of them were.** At about the fourth hour a woman of about sixty-six at four looms, who has been at that trade for fifty-one years and is called the old woman with the looms by her own arrangement, says **"You are the carrier off the road"** in a gap in a sentence about a gate, and had not decided to say it, and then tells him to go slower and to tell the woman over the byre to sit down. **He says *I am not a carrier* in about a second and a half, before he knows what the word means, and hears himself say it.** She declines to use the word again and declines to use his name, and says she is not going to ask him what he is because he has not asked her one question in six weeks. **He stands at the gate for about a minute and does not ask, and about a quarter of an hour later he notices that he did it, and noticing afterwards is not the same as not having done it.** **By the sixth hour four people in that valley are using the word about him and none of them has asked him anything, and there is no form and no book with a column for it, and the roll has columns for the day and the mark and the hand and a name in the older spreads, and there is nothing in it for a word somebody said at a gate, and that is not an oversight, that is the design.** A market day: no carts at all, a wool stall, a salt, one cheese, nine pairs of gloves, and a man who makes nails and cannot sell them because there is nothing in this valley to put nails in. **Eleven of the ninth generation went over the pass on the fourth day of the cold, eleven the winter before, ten the one before, and about four of them come back in the first warm week and the rest are somewhere else, and it takes a week to the top of the pass on foot, and that is a road, and it is nine generations old and it costs about nothing.** The nail man says the thing nobody in the valley has ever said out loud, and says why it is a nail man who says it. And Sarr has been waiting forty days to be asked a question that is not about the book, and asks to be asked one, and is asked what he does all day: **ten hours a day putting the day against a line, not reading the book, not checking it, not having checked a hand in nineteen years, and the mark is his and the day is his and the hand is not.** He shuts the book in front of a person, which he does not do, and tells him that there have been four readings in nine generations, two of them his, one of which lasted a night, **and that the word for a man who has read it and got nothing out of it that he was willing to write down has not been made yet, because nobody here has ever had to make it.** And he says there is a fifth thing and it is the last thing he has got and he is keeping it for a man who asked.

**Block 1 note — the block's calendar, its cast discipline, and its device counts, so that Batch 0002 does not re-derive any of them.** **Calendar.** Main world, **the twenty-fifth of Hearthmonth, YR 313, a Saturday and a market day**, through **the eighth of Goatmonth, YR 313, a Friday and not a market day at the fourth hour: thirteen days elapsed, and the prompt's "six weeks" is a slip and the day map in `outline/volume-04.md` §17 is the authority.** Market days inside the span: the twenty-fifth and the thirtieth of Hearthmonth, and none after that before the crossing. **The crossing out is the eighth of Goatmonth, a Friday, not a market day, the fourth hour, and the return is the twentieth of Harvestmonth, YR 314, a Monday, not a market day: 192 days of absence against a sixteen-month path interval, an error of 171 days against the printed three weeks, about nine tenths of the absence, and the warning is three weeks and has not moved and no character in this block has said the word about it.** **The hearing for the ninth of Rainmonth, YR 314, a Tuesday, not a market day, falls inside the volume's span and while he is in the path, and it is referred to twice in this block and staged nowhere.** **Path time: the valley keeps no months and counts winters, a generation is a winter, and there have been nine; sixteen months have not begun to be counted by anybody in Chapters 158 to 160 and the notebook holds a number of sleeps and a number of weeks and refuses the arithmetic.** **Cast.** **One new name of six and five slots unfilled, so Volume 04 stands at one of six and twenty-four of twenty-four unfilled, and nine of the ten people in the valley are unnamed, and the girl of nine refuses her own name and gives a place instead.** **Devices, as whole words and not as strings.** `---` section rules **10**, one per chapter, down from 38 in the first pass. Bolded line-openings **46**. Semicolons **0**. Em-dashes **0**. `the whole of` **10**, down from 37 in the first pass, and two of the ten are a document's fourth line printed twice because it is read twice. `about N seconds` **9**. `cut` **13** and every one ordinary, and the working is not among them. Filler as whole words: `nodded` **0**, `held` **11**, `wrote` **37**, `stood` **13**, `plain` **2**, `counted` **9**, `breathed` **0**, `steady` **0**. **One Gatehouse-voice line and no System panel.** **Money: one column footed on the page before the total is said — 10 + 4 = 14 a day, 14 nine times = 126, four sheets at twopence = 8, and 134 pence, two marks and three shillings and twopence — and the Marches' contract cited whole and not re-derived, and the fair copy read and copied and not re-footed, and its addition left standing exactly as the county that did it wrote it.**

## Batch 0002 — Volume 04, Chapters 161–170, "The Roll"

**Block 2 of five. Ten complete scenes, and one main-world room in the middle of the tenth of them.** **Entirely path time in the Ninefold Winter, in the first winter of sixteen, from the tenth week of the cold to a market day in about the seventeenth, with the main world appearing once, on its own calendar, in the middle of Chapter 170.** POVs, recorded honestly and not claimed: **Chapters 161–165, 167–169 and 170 are Aren Kest's, with a long external stretch in 162 and 170 and the whole of the reading in 170 in a room he is standing in. Chapter 166 is the market at the wide place, still his, with about nine other people in it. The middle section of Chapter 170 is a room over a weigh-house in a fen town and is not his, and he is not in that county and does not know anything that happens in it.** **The one main-world section in the block is a straight cut to the north while he is in the path, and it is a room with two women in it and it explains nothing and opens no road.** Cast on the page, **and two new names and four slots unfilled, so Volume 04 stands at three names of six and twenty-three of twenty-four slots unfilled: Wystan Roole**, 34, a bridgekeeper of the ninth generation, sixteen men and no work, **and Nev Treen**, 66, of the eighth generation, who was asked what she swore forty years ago and answered. **Iven Sarr is a returned face from Batch 0001 and is on every page of this block.** Nine valley people in Batch 0001 and not one of them named except these two. **Returned faces in the main-world section:** Mara Vey, Alish Renn, Sarn Oyelaran. **No System panel. No Gatehouse-voice line. No death. No apology. The tile on the coping is not mentioned. The three marks on the seed vault are not mentioned. The letter for a jar is not answered, delivered, or traced. The hearing stands and is neither adjourned nor refused.** Block total **33,385 words**, per chapter **3067 / 3185 / 3545 / 3140 / 3204 / 3135 / 3016 / 3128 / 2995 / 4,970**, and the block is over Volume 03 Batch 0011's 31,648 and under Batch 0014's 35,340, and no chapter is under 2,995.

**Ch 161 · the tenth week of the cold, the fifth hour · the lintel, and the fifth thing.** The outside stair of the room over the byre has nine treads and one of them talks under a boot. **The question he was asked to ask, and the one he was keeping, is about the lintel over the door, and Sarr gives it to him in about a minute: there is nothing cut on it, and a county with one dressed stone in four miles has three things cut on it and one of them is a date, and a mark on a stone in this valley would not tell anybody anything they did not already know standing next to it.** A woman in the yard had said of the stranger, in the voice she uses for a thing she has settled, *he is going to ask somebody one day and it is not going to be me* — and had been right about herself and about him, and neither of them asks the other anything, and Sarr names the trick. **And then the fifth thing: at the head of the ninth spread, above the first birth, a line ruled the same as the rest with a day in the day column and a hand in the hand column and nothing in the mark column, four days before there is anything in the book at all, which is the only line in nine spreads with a day and a hand and no mark, and it has been checked every winter for six years.** He has not told his wife and has not told the man who wants a road; the reason is not trust but that a man with a thing like that for six years cannot tell wanting to tell somebody from needing to after about four years, **and the day he cannot tell the difference is the day he tells a man off the road, which is what has happened.** Three terms for the reading: the room and the trestles and no names out loud and no page. **Two readings in nineteen years and a man with a cart who is dead and a woman four valleys off who writes about the weather.** **REPAIRED in this block: the section-opening line that stood twice, word for word, at lines 33 and 51, the first now being a new line about the bundle of forty leaves.** Ends on a man putting his hand flat on a page the way a keeper puts his hand flat on a book, having been in the room four days.

**Ch 162 · the same room, an hour and ten minutes · the page, and the mark, and the hand.** **The light in this valley does not do what light does in a county that keeps its hours, and a man from a county that keeps them sits over a book and waits for it to change the way he would wait for a bell, and an hour after he stops waiting he notices he has stopped.** Three findings, in order. **One: the page is not a page of names**, three columns, about a hundred and ninety lines, the day column running down and never going back up, and a man can take the order of a whole valley off it without reading one thing on it that is a person. **Two: the mark column has three marks in it and the ninth generation is two hundred of the first one**, and that is the answer to why there has never been a third reason in nine spreads, and nobody had to look for it. **Three: the hand in the third column does not vary** — not the pen, not the ink, not the size on the first line and the last, and it does not go small or fast at the foot of a page of two hundred lines. **He finds the day a girl of about nine was born, four lines up from the foot of the ninth spread, and tells nobody, tells her nothing, and cannot ask the woman over the byre, and there is nowhere in four hundred miles to put it.** Then the fourth spread: **two hundred and eleven names in a column, about thirty of them people he has been in a room with in six weeks, all of them read and none of them written down, and the not writing took forty minutes longer than the writing would have taken.** The name column stops in the middle of the fourth spread because that is the last year anybody went out and was gone long enough for a name to be a fact about where they were. **And the last leaf of the ninth spread is ruled, blank, with an empty heading, and the day has never been written at the head of it in nineteen years by a man who knows the rule better than anybody alive.** Sarr comes up with two pails, says he would like it noticed that he did not ask what it was, and then shows the stranger the thing in about a quarter of an hour that took him six years. **And asks him the first question in six weeks he cannot answer: can a person want a thing and not have a page for it. And the answer is *I do not know*, said in a second and a half, and it is the first true thing he has said in a room in that valley in six weeks.**

**Ch 163 · the same day, before the light went · the press-mark, and the copy, and the ask.** **A depression the size of a thumbnail on the back of the last leaf, off a mould, as old as the leaf.** Sarr has shown it to four people in six years and three of them are dead. **The man with the cart called it a press-mark and was right about the paper and wrong about everything people do with a mark once it is in a copy.** The distinction, once, in full: **a press-mark proves a sheet and a house and a season when a vat was the right heat and it does not prove a person, and a hand is a person, it is the one mark in the world that cannot be made by a machine, and that is exactly why you cannot get a person out of one.** Both marks are on the same leaf four inches apart, and the one that proves a house is in a copy four hundred miles off being the reason a valley is somewhere it has never been, and the one that proves nothing is the one he has been looking at for six years. **No guess is made and none is written on a page.** Then the bundle of forty leaves, untied, and why the fifth generation stays wrong in it: **a copy that is right about the fifth generation is a copy, and there is no way on this earth to get a wrong fact out of a book that is right about a hundred other things, and he let a man have a false fact about his valley for about nine years of his life because the other option was that he had both.** And the second letter, and sixteen years of not being able to decide whether it is the best or the worst sentence anybody has written about a book. **Then Wystan Roole, named, and the ask** — sixteen men and about thirty under the cross and none of them with work since before he was twenty, asked twice for a road and told twice there is no road to build, and a wall instead, and **he says the ask took a stranger in a room with a book in it and he would like the room noticed and not the stranger.** Then the work: **field stone off a wall that was coming down anyway, rafters off a byre roof with a hole in it, lime up from the low country on the backs of four men who went over the pass with nine bushels of barley each and came back with the barley gone and the lime in its place, and three pegs and a string on a bank, and there is not one straight line in this valley because there has never been one use for one.** And the mouth in the byre wall with a dressed lintel on it, and a drain that has come out under that wall for sixty years, and the woman of the Low Holm saying she has known and has not said it to him. Ends on sixteen men and a boy of twenty-four putting a barrow-load of field stone into a footing at the fifth hour and not one of them looking at it.

**Ch 164 · the wall, the first four days · the sixteen men, and the mechanism.** A retaining wall is not a difficult thing and there is nothing clever in it, which is why a man from four hundred miles off can stand at a corner of one and have nothing to say for a quarter of an hour. **The string comes down on the fourth course and goes up on the ninth and comes down and goes up four times in three days, every time a man on a ladder with a plumb bob of wrought iron.** The other thing the man off the road recognised in that valley in six weeks is **the coping of the byre, a hundred and forty years old, done in lime, better than anything built at that byre in nine generations, and done by a woman.** The woman of about sixty-six with four looms brings a bag of slates and will not be talked out of them, and about four days later a man of twenty-four fetches stone on one and does not tell anybody. **The sixteen men: about thirty under the cross, two of them over sixty, one at the mill, eleven of the ninth generation at twenty-two, twenty-four and twenty-six, a skill nine generations old in the valley with no occasion to use it; a man with a skill and no occasion does not stop having the skill, he goes and does the nine other things.** The twenty-four-year-old was worst on the first morning and best by the fourth and the reason was that he asked, out loud, on the first morning, and was told, and put his course half an inch deeper. The old man comes up on the third day and says the only thing he says that week: **you brought that to work with — to get about in — there is no road — then you have brought it a long way for it.** **And then the mechanism, once, on a bank, to the only person on it who builds anything, and said once and not said again: a wall is built once and does not want feeding and a promise has to be renewed or it stops, a working does not, and the asking is the price of the privilege of the working being a promise at all, and he has been one of the forty in that room for nine years and could not tell them what they are paying for.** Ends on snow going off a coping into lime and the man of fifty-eight coming up the outside stair with a yoke because two pails do not stop for a wall, looking at the line of the string from the top tread and going back down.

**Ch 165 · the upper water, a cheese house, and the room over the cross · Nev Treen.** **There is one woman in this valley whose name anybody can say in the right order and she gives it before anybody asks.** She tells him to put his stick where it will not go over the whey and takes his boots off at the door in effect. **Eleven noes in a quarter of an hour before anything, and the eleven are listed.** She was asked what she swore forty years ago in the room over the cross in the cold with the whole valley in it and a man at the back with a slate; **it took nine minutes and it was the worst nine minutes of her life and she will not say what she said.** She told the ninth generation and told them it was survivable, **and it was survivable and she was wrong and she knows she was wrong, and what was wrong was not the asking: the asking was right and had never been done, and what was wrong was the man with the slate and the book behind him, because a book does not know the difference between what a person said and what was true.** Nine of the thirty-one in the aisle are left; four say it was the worst, one of them her, five say it was the best, two of those five have never spoken of it. **And her reason, which is the reason the ninth generation has not been asked: there is no other way to ask anybody in this valley, and a conversation in a cheese house is a thing that never happened as far as anybody will ever know.** She was pointed at her by the woman with the slate, who gave a week's warning and was right to about a day. **The room: twenty-nine benches bolted to the floor in four rows, a hundred and forty years old, every one with a number cut into the end of it, a hundred and forty on the back of the right-hand row, the numbers are strokes, nobody has ever been struck off a bench, and the back row is at the back because you can get out of a room at the back in about a second and a half — and that is the entire difference between a thing in a house and a thing in a hand.** She says she will stand in the aisle at the back and be asked again, **and she will not be standing there writing, and that is the one sentence nobody off a road came four hundred miles for.** **REPAIRED in this block: *by Thursday*, a main-world weekday spoken aloud in a valley scene, is now *by the end of the week*.**

**Ch 166 · a market day, the wide place, the fourth hour · the refusal, and the four words.** A market day is the only date this valley has that everybody knows and about eleven people can say which day of what, **and it is not a day of anything, because there are no days here.** A barrow, three sledges, one handcart, a great deal of one kind of cheese, and a man who makes nails who has been at that stall four market days running. **A man of thirty-two gets a young man of twenty-two to stop in a doorway in front of about nine people and asks whether he would like to be asked what he swore, and it takes him four seconds to ask.** The young man puts a hurdle down on the step and says no, and not in that room, in about four seconds, and says it to the man and not to the market. The reason is one sentence: **because of the benches, because when a person stands in that aisle and says something a man puts a stroke on a bench, and that is the only way it is known and the only reason anybody in this valley has ever believed anybody about anything.** Wystan Roole asks why not in that room and is told he has not been in there. **And then the second half, which is the volume's argument: he is not refusing the question, he wants to be asked what he swore, he has been in that book since the day he was born and has never been asked anything — and there is nowhere to put an answer that is not a page, and a page is a column, and in nine years no one will ever know the two of them said it in a doorway.** Sarr is at the edge of the wide place with a slate under his arm, having come down on purpose, and agrees with both halves and says he has been on the wrong side of the second one for nineteen years. **The sentence about the leaf is said once and is a story, not a document, and he says it is the reason he is asking and he has not thought of a better one in a week.** **And the finding: four words for a person in this valley, nine generations, and they are not four versions of one thing** — a carrier, the hand, a keeper, and a fourth word in his own county that carries consent inside it and is the only one anybody can be sorry for. **A carrier is not that fourth word and no character calls one the other, and the fourth word is described and not named, on the Chapter 156 precedent.** **Roole says out loud that he did not come to a market to be a good man in it, and the nail man says the thing nobody in that valley has ever said: he would go tomorrow, and if the valley stops being a valley in a week he is the first out of it, and he has known that for two years and has never said it in a market.** Ends on *what is a page for*, and *I do not know*, in about a second and a half, said in the open in front of eleven people on a day this valley could name, and the second true thing he has said in a room there in about two weeks. **REPAIRED in this block: six straight double-quoted paragraphs are now curly, eighteen marks.**

**Ch 167 · the ninth to the sixteenth day of the wall · the best wall, and the money that is not a figure.** **The wall is higher than a man's chest on the ninth day and it is the best wall anybody in this valley has seen and it is the only true line in nine miles.** Through-stones about every fourth course, nine of them, laid in about nine seconds each and not wanting to be watched and watched by everybody. **The lime is why it took eleven days: quicklime in a cold week is a powder that will set in the spring and squeeze the course out of true.** The frost got into the hearting on the fifth night and nine men knocked the face back with a bar and a lump hammer, and the twenty-four-year-old was best at it because he had never seen a wall finished. Roole says the best-wall sentence out loud in front of nine people, once, and says it is the last time. **And the woman of the Low Holm says what she wanted: her byre floor mended, four inches of lime and rubble, a hole at the low end the size of a dinner plate, on her knees in the dark twice this winter with a bucket and a piece of slate — and she has been given a wall instead, and is not going to say thank you, and is not going to have it taken down, and the floor is still a dinner plate, and she would like both of those to be in the world at the same time.** **Then the money, footed in pence on the page: quicklime three loads at sixpence, six and six is twelve and twelve and six is eighteen; a barrow of sand, fourpence; eighteen and four is twenty-two pence, one shilling and tenpence.** Under it, the valley's own price — **nine bushels of barley and a peck, on the back of a slate, and he has not added her figure to his and there is nobody alive who could** — and the difference is four bushels nobody will ever account for, and the lime came out of the same sack as the barley in the byre roof. **She finds her figure on his own arm and says that is not a place anybody keeps a figure and that it is right, because she is not a place a number lives in, she is a person who happens to have one.** **And he could have made a second copy on paper made four valleys over and gave it to her, and did not, because a thing written down twice is a thing two people can be got at, and it is the only time in this case he has had a figure he could have given away and given it to the person holding the only copy instead.** The coping goes on on the eleventh day with the man of fifty-eight holding the other end of a flat stone for about nine minutes, unasked and unmentioned, and he squared it, and it is the only squared stone in the wall.

**Ch 168 · the tenth day, and the tenth night · the notch, and the drift figure refused.** **The hole in the wall came on the tenth day and the man of thirty-four would not let anybody else touch it and the woman of the Low Holm watched the whole of it in the wet and did not go inside.** He cut it wrong first, in the second course, two feet out, on purpose, in about nine minutes, and there are four men alive who know. Then the real one: a lintel and two jambs and a haunch on each side, dressed by hand, about four hours, and a stone off the byre coping that is a hundred and forty years old, four inches wider than the hole on each side. **And when it is done he explains it to about eleven people and does not explain it to the stranger, and is asked, and the explanation is one sentence: a wall with water behind it has to have a way out, and if you do not give it one you have not built a wall, you have built a place for the water to go when it decides to, and he knew about the drain and asked her before he had worked out what he was going to do about it, and that was wrong, and it is the only wrong thing anybody has done on this wall and it is his.** **And the finding nobody was told: nobody can test a retaining wall in a cold week, a thaw is about eleven weeks away, and either the wall is right or it is a very good nine days — and he says the stranger will not be here in the thaw, does not explain it, and it turns out he was right and nobody in that yard had ever met anybody who came back in the spring.** The girl of about nine goes round the wall twice, counts nine through-stones and four hundred and six stones, and will not ask anybody because then she will have to be told. **And in the notebook, in the valley's own register, the first drift figure of the volume is refused:** the error is not the difference between three weeks and the time he has been here, because the time he has been here is not the absence, and a figure with no meaning in it is worse than nothing because it looks like a figure and he will believe it in four months and put it in a return. **Three weeks printed and unchanged; a valley that keeps no months; a man with no frame; and he is not going to write a figure, and this is the third time in this case he has got to that page and not written one, and the first time he threw the paper away and the second time he left the two numbers side by side and did not add them, and this time he has written down why, and he does not know yet whether that is a better thing or a slower way of failing.** He leaves the page alone afterwards, the first time in this case he has done that. **And nobody in that valley knows there is a figure at all, and no one in it has any reason to suppose there is a piece of paper about four hundred miles behind him with a number on it, and that is the only thing about this crossing that is actually his.** Sarr reads the page standing, puts it back without a word, and says the thing the man had not worked out in nineteen years: **a day in that book is the day a person was born, and a birth is not a unit.** Ends with a book shut on a trestle, a palm flat on a coping nine feet long, a man of thirty-four at the other end not looking, the fourteenth week of the cold, and the notch in the wind out of the slack.

**Ch 169 · the sixteenth to the nineteenth day · the wall is finished, and a leaf, and a reading.** **The wall was finished on the sixteenth day and nothing happened on the seventeenth, and there is no ceremony at the end of a wall, and the string came down and the pegs stayed where they were because a man does not pull the pegs of a line he has finished.** Then it is ordinary: the mill wheel, the low-country men and the nine-day trip down and the seven up, the beasts out, and the floor mended by a man of sixty in two days. **And on the eighteenth day the man of fifty-eight comes up the outside stair and says the thing he had been not saying for nineteen years in a form anybody could act on, and says himself that it is not a reading he has wanted for six years: a cold room, in the cold, with about forty people in it, and it is not the book.** The oath is not in the book and not in this room. **Four places looked in and a fifth place left that he will not have looked at before he has read it himself, and he is not going to say where he has looked.** He no longer wants the ninth generation told what it signed, **and says so, and calls it the fourth thing in nineteen years he has wanted and then stopped wanting.** **And the thing he has learned in nine weeks from a man with a limp, in one sentence: the reading is not an asking.** He will stand in the aisle and read all of it out and will not ask anybody to be there, and if a man of the ninth generation stands at the back of the right-hand row he will read it slower, and if nobody stands there he will read it at the speed of a man reading a thing he has not read in nineteen years, and both are the same reading and neither is a demand. **He asks the man off the road to read it, because he cannot read it and be the man asking the question.** **Four things back: he reads what is there and says out loud that he is reading scratching; he does not explain a hand, not the one in the ninth spread and not the one on the back of the last leaf; he will not be asked anything in the middle of a reading; and the thing that is not a condition — he will read it slowly and will not know what any of it means, and has never read anything in his life that he did not know what it meant, and that is why he is the wrong man and the right one.** The old man is told by the woman with the slate and says he has been in that room sixty times and has never once heard what it says, and that he is the only person in the valley who is pleased, and asks for that to be written down and knows it will not be. **Nev Treen gives up standing in the aisle and being asked again, and says there will not be a page for it, and that this is new.** **And Wystan Roole says his want in nine words — a road is not a thing you are given, a road is a thing you dig — and then says the other thing: a valley that cannot leave is a valley that cannot argue with anybody about anything.** **And on the evening of the eighteenth day the man of fifty-eight takes a leaf out of the chest under the boards, turns it over, and writes four words on the back of it with no month and no year and no hour on it, because this valley has no months and no years and no hour anybody keeps, and puts it back under the book, and the book goes over the leaf and the leaf is not visible from anywhere in the room.** Ends on that: a leaf of paper under a book in a room over a byre, the only piece of paper in this valley with a date on it that is not a birth. **REPAIRED in this block: *I am sixty* is now fifty-eight, against five other chapters and `outline/volume-04.md` §13.**

**Ch 170 · the morning of the market day, the third hour, and one room in a fen town · four words, a hearing, and a shut door.** **Three movements, and the only main-world section in the block sits in the middle of it.** **First: the room over the byre at the fourth hour.** Sarr has not slept. He went down the back door at the seventh hour last night with a lamp and stood in the doorway of a room about four feet by six and **did not read the form on the board and did not go in**, and says exactly what he knows and does not know: it is ruled, in more columns than his book has, the writing is dry and is not his or his father's, and he does not know what is in the columns or who ruled it or how long it has been there. **He forbids the stranger to ask him a word about that room, having decided that a man who has had a thing for six years cannot tell wanting from needing, and gives the reason for the form in his own words: when a thing arrives in this valley you put something in it, and he has watched four people in his life answer a form and has not one of them still living here.** **And then the want, and it has changed, and he says so before anybody can: he wanted the oath read all of it and not the parts that have held, and now he wants the reading to be four words long, and if he tells the room about a thing in a fifth place then this valley will talk about it for a year, nine of them will be right, and in four years there will be nine accounts and none of them checkable — and a thing four hundred people can check is worth more to him than a thing they cannot.** He asks for the four words to be read as a thing he wrote and not as a date, because a fact about him is a fact he can stand behind and a fact about them is not, **because the only thing he is sure of after nineteen years is that he is the one in this valley who can be got at.** **Second: the ninth of Rainmonth of the year after, a Tuesday, not a market day, at the second hour, in a room over a weigh-house.** A keeper of records in one of two chairs, the second copyist in the other with fair-copy paper and a rule, the junior clerk up the two flights with the ordinary post. Nobody came. Nobody was sent. **The finding: a body corporate that is sued is represented by a person, and if this office hears a thing then this office is a party to it, and a party can be sued, and there is no instrument in this kingdom for hearing a thing without becoming one of the sides of it — and the only thing she has got is a room a person can be turned out of by a person with a door.** She writes **four** lines and does not write a fifth, and the reason is that what a thing means is not a fact and this office is for facts. The day and the hour go in as two things and not one. **He is not in that county, has sent nothing, nothing has come from him, she has not asked, and it is not a question for a minute.** **Third: the third hour of a market day, in the room over the cross, ten people, a stove at the front.** Sarr stands in the aisle with the leaf in his left hand and says in advance that nobody is to ask the reader anything until he has said that there is nothing else. **Aren Kest reads four words in the voice of a man reading a figure off a board and then says, in about four seconds, that that is all of it and he is holding nothing back.
