The man of eighty-one asks for it again, and the second reading is slower and is not better, and about three people understand he had not asked for the words.** Sarr's cost, in his own words: **he thought there would be forty people in that room and there are nine and a girl, and there is no version of the forty in which any of the nine is there, and he did not know until that morning that wanting forty was the same as not wanting the nine.** A minute and a half of nothing, and then eleven questions, all of them to the man with the leaf, and eleven answers, none of them improved. **The woman with the slate takes the slate out, looks at it, and puts it back. Nev Treen puts her hands flat on the end of a bench for a minute and takes them off. Roole counts the room twice and says about four hundred out loud in that room for the first time in his hearing and offers to cut it in stone and is told no.** **The girl of about nine asks *is that all of it*, is told *no*, waits four seconds, says *all right*, and goes and gets her barrow out at the ford, and the arrangement survives it.** **The young man of twenty-two was at the back of the right-hand row from the start to the finish and was not asked and had not asked to be asked, and the man off the road stays at the front on purpose because a man of thirty-two standing four feet off a boy in a room where nobody has asked the boy anything is a man arranging himself.** They come out and Sarr says the second half of his want: **he will not go down the four steps, and he wants it said in the open, once, because in nine years there will be a woman in that market who remembers a man of fifty-eight saying the word no in a cold room, and he would like her to remember a second thing about the same morning, which is that he said it in a yard and not in a room.** Ends on the low door under the eaves shut with a palm flat on the wood, the light in the byre, the wall outside in the dark holding up a byre instead of being the first four hundred yards of a road, nobody saying anything about it on the stair, and nine treads of which one is soft and the fourth tread saying so under both of them

**Four repairs to this chapter at the Batch 0002 review repair pass, one sentence each, and no scene rewritten.** **A narrator verdict on Sarr's interior in the second paragraph, which was the only place in two batches where the book reports what a character who is not Aren is thinking** — it now reports only what his face did not do, and the volume's rule is that the narrator may report what a room does and what is written and may not report what a person thinks unless that person is Aren or is in the first person. **A thermometer reading in the cold room, the only `degrees` in Volume 04 and the one figure in the block with no hedge and no instrument, now a man finding the warm end of a dark room with his face before he can see a bench.** **An ambiguous `the fourth one` in the last line, three lines after two men go up a stair, now the fourth tread.** **A dropped auxiliary in Sarr's sentence about the four people who answered a form, now *and not one of them is still living in this valley*.** The chapter is therefore **4,970 words** and the block **33,385**; the chapter remains the second-longest in the manuscript and is untrimmed. **None of the four touched the four words, the four seconds, the eleventh no, the four lines and not a fifth, the form four steps down, the shut door, or the ending.**.

**Block 2 note — the block's calendar, its cast discipline, and its device counts, so that Batch 0003 does not re-derive any of them.** **Calendar.** Path time throughout, the tenth week of the cold to a market day in about the seventeenth, and **no main-world month, no real-world month and no main-world weekday is spoken or written in any valley scene in any register including a notebook, in any of the ten chapters.** The one main-world date in the block is the hearing, in the main-world section of Chapter 170: **the ninth of Rainmonth of the year after, a Tuesday, at the second hour, not a market day**, and the crossing out behind him is still the eighth of Goatmonth, YR 313, a Friday, at the fourth hour, and the return is still the twentieth of Harvestmonth, YR 314, a Monday. **The three-weeks warning is unchanged and no character in this block uses the word *failed* about a road, and the first drift figure of the volume is refused in Chapter 168's notebook with the reason written down for the first time.** **Cast.** **Two new names of six and four slots unfilled, so Volume 04 stands at three names of six and twenty-three of twenty-four unfilled: Wystan Roole, 34, and Nev Treen, 66.** Iven Sarr is a returned face and is on every page. **Devices, as whole words and not as strings.** `---` rules **11**, one per chapter and a second in Chapter 170 for the main-world cut. Bolded line-openings **53**. Semicolons **0**. Em-dashes **0**. `the whole of` **36**, against a house baseline of 2–3 and Batch 0001's 10, **and that is the block's one genuine tic and it is recorded rather than hidden; Chapter 170 has none of it.** `about N seconds` **5**. `cut` **21**, all ordinary, and the working is not among them and is not named. `about` **383**, per chapter 31 / 33 / 43 / 40 / 45 / 44 / 38 / 41 / 39 / 29, **and the density does not compound after Chapter 166 and Chapter 170 is the lowest in the block, against a ceiling of Chapter 160's forty-two which Chapters 163, 165 and 166 went over by one, three and two.** Fillers as whole words, a baseline and not a target: **`nodded` 1, `held` 10, `wrote` 27, `stood` 30, `plain` 0, `counted` 8, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0** — `nodded` and `stood` are up on Batch 0001's 0 and 13 and the block's `stood` is mostly a man at a string and a man in a room. **No System panel and no Gatehouse-voice line.** **Length 33,385 words in ten chapters, and nine of the ten are between 2,995 and 3,545 and Chapter 170 is 4,970.** **Every count in this note was re-footed at the Batch 0002 review repair pass, after four one-sentence repairs to Chapter 170 and the correction of the `about` column, which this note first printed as 382 with Chapter 163 at 42 and two breaches by three and two. The true column is 383 with Chapter 163 at 43 and three breaches by one, three and two, and the judgement recorded for it is unchanged. Every device count and all nine filler counts in this note are identical to the first count, because none of the four repaired sentences used a counted word. Canon for the pass is `state/continuity.md` items 435–441.**

## Batch 0003 — Volume 04, Chapters 171–180, "The Office in an Older Hand"

**Block 3 of five, and the block in which the roll turns out to be the smaller half of the story.** Ten complete scenes. **Entirely path time, the second and third winters of the sixteen, from about the eleventh week of the second cold to the twenty-fourth day of it, with a bridge over a beck built in the sixteenth week and a second asking made on foot at about nine hundred doors in eleven days.** POVs, recorded honestly and not claimed: **all ten chapters are Aren Kest's, with long stretches in other people's mouths in 173, 175, 176, 178 and 179. There is no main-world room in this block at all and no cut to the north, and he is not in a room in Merefen anywhere in it and nothing happens in that county that he learns of.** Cast on the page, **and one new name and five slots unfilled, so Volume 04 stands at four names of six and twenty-two of twenty-four unfilled: Osgar Vell**, 22, of the ninth generation, on the roll from birth in a hand nobody alive can name, who is the young man of Chapter 166's doorway, Chapter 166's market and Chapter 170's back row. Iven Sarr, Wystan Roole and Nev Treen are returned faces and are on the page. **No System panel. No Gatehouse-voice line. No apology. No death. The tile on the coping is not mentioned. The three marks on the seed vault are not mentioned and the keeper's decision is not taken. The letter for a jar is not answered, not delivered and not traced. The hearing stands and is neither adjourned nor refused and the decision-document is not written.** Block total **34,037 words**, per chapter **3306 / 3277 / 3351 / 3711 / 3938 / 3349 / 3230 / 3139 / 3303 / 3433**, over Batch 0002's 33,385 and Batch 0011's 31,648 and under Batch 0014's 35,340, with no chapter under 3,139.

**Ch 171 · the eleventh week of the second winter, the fourth hour and the seventh hour · four steps down, and a sheet of paper read out.** A man of fifty-eight who has not slept goes down four steps with a lamp and stands in a room about four feet by six with a board floor and stone walls older than the byre, and **tells a stranger not to come down because he cannot get a man with a stick up it.** **He reads the whole sheet out loud in about four minutes and not one word of it is written down, and the arrangement it is read under is that there is no page.** The head is printed, in a small even type, and it says **Office of Quarters, at Grayford**, and *Return of the quarter. To be sent round once in every seven years, and returned to the same, and not to be sent on.* Eight numbered columns, **two hands** — a slow careful one that wrote three answers and stopped, and an office's hand that does not vary across two answers written on different days — and **two columns that are empty, and he names those two before he is asked, and says that a column with a rule drawn across the head of it that has not been ruled across in two hundred years is the whole of how an office says it has not heard.** The fifth column has *none appointed* in it. **And at the foot of the sheet are nine printed words that a man of thirty-two has read before on a form printed about two hundred years later in a county four hundred miles off, and he says *I have read that wording before* and he does not describe the other one, and the reason he gives is that a man who describes a document to nine people has made nine copies of a thing nobody in that valley can see. The chapter ends on a shut door and a woman in a yard who has decided not to ask a question, and on four hundred feet of the best wall in the valley holding up a byre.**

**Ch 172 · the same week, the fourth hour and the ninth hour · the two empty columns, and the paper.** **The finding of the office's hand out loud: *nobody chose it* — pick up a sheet and write the same letter eleven times and you will not vary in the eleventh either, and the thing that tells you is that the third answer and the fourth are on different days.** **The age, given as an experienced reader's finding and not as a figure derived in the valley: laid paper with chain lines an inch apart where the roll has none, ink gone through the strokes, four folds gone soft like cloth, a furry stub out of a pad, and a man of fifty-eight who has handled paper all his life putting it above the trough in his wife's mother's yard.** The sixth column's number is read out and is not turned into a length of time, and the reason is that the only count of anything anybody in that valley has made in a hundred and forty years is a girl of nine counting **four hundred and six** stones in a wall. **The document's own design is the explanation of the century: *not to be sent on*, and a valley that cannot carry a sheet to Grayford because it has no road, so an office that will not pass a form on has built a form this valley can never answer, and it was right, and it has never been told this valley exists.** **And the count the form asks for has been kept four years in chalk on the back of a stall at a market by a man who makes nails and cannot sell them, and he has never heard of the form, and nobody will ever tell him.** **The chapter ends on the two sheets on the boards of the floor side by side, the ruled lines the same within a hair, and the question nobody has answered: *then who took four columns out of it.***

**Ch 173 · the twelfth week · four lines at the head of the first spread, and a name.** **Osgar Vell comes up nine steps to say his own name and to say that he has not been asked a question since a market day, and Sarr tells him that a name in a copy four hundred miles off is spelled two ways on two pages and that he has never known which is right and has never asked the man because he is dead.** **Then the four lines at the head of the first spread are read out, for the first time in a hundred and forty years, and they are a rule about keepers: the keeper answers for the book and the room and the day and the mark and the hand, and for no person in the valley, and has been appointed by no person in the valley, and where a man is named in a book of this valley it is because he did it himself.** **And those four lines are the answer to the form's fifth column, and the man who wrote them read the fifth column, and he is the man of fifty-eight's great-grandfather, and that is where nine generations of a confusion between four words for a person was written down.** **The fourth word is described in its own words and is not named, on the Chapter 156 and Chapter 166 precedent.** **The eighth spelling is not a word: it is a description on a printed form, and it is the only one of the nine that is true about anybody, and nobody has ever used it.** Osgar asks what a man is when nobody at home is appointed to answer for him, and the answer is about nine seconds long and is that nothing in this valley can be produced against him either, and that this is not justice. **The chapter ends on a scrap of rag paper about the size of a hand, the first copy of a thing that has ever been made in that room, because a rule about keepers names nobody, and on Osgar Vell saying that he is going over the pass on the fourth day of the next cold and has never had an answer he could hand a person.**

**Ch 174 · the thirteenth week · the consequence, the tenth generation, and six lines on a form.** **The consequence in one sentence, and it is corrected twice in the same room: a valley of four hundred people has been keeping a winter that an office asked it to keep, for about two hundred years, through nine generations, in a form, because a form is a form — and the correction is that the form does not ask about a winter, it asks for a figure of men going out, and the valley answered it the only way it knows how, which is with a book, one line at a time, over a generation.** **The finding that carries the volume, said in the middle of a room: an office is the most durable thing in this book, because a county can break an oath in public and a family can burn a roll and an office waits, and the proof is a column headed *Withdrawn* with a rule drawn across its head that has not been ruled across.** **The man of eighty-one comes up the outside stair himself to say the only thing he says: he has been in the room over the cross sixty times and never once heard what is in it, and he has never wondered about the form, and the price of that was the looking and not the asking — and that the tenth generation is the first that has never stood in that aisle, and the hundred and forty on the end of the back bench has not moved in nine years.** **Then six lines are written on the blank back of the two-hundred-year-old form, in a man of fifty-eight's hand, saying what this valley is and that the figure for the men who go out is not going to be got by writing it on a form in a room with nobody in it, and that the form has not been answered and has not been withdrawn, and that if it is ever taken out of the valley it will be the first thing to leave in nine generations that was not a person. The chapter ends on the stone going back on crooked, because a man of fifty-eight could not get the heel of his hand out from under the far edge of it, and on a girl of about nine at a gate counting the treads on a stair from thirty feet off and not asking.**

**Ch 175 · the fourteenth week, the fourth hour · the third generation's house, and what a carrier was for.** **A woman of sixty-eight married into the house forty-eight years, a man of the seventh generation in the byre, and nine stones in a corner of a byre yard with a barred T on each and a stroke through one of them and one of the nine is the newest because the cuts are sharp.** **The story is four generations old: a man of seventeen went out and was gone fourteen months and came back in the warm week with nine beasts that were not this valley's and with a word in the low country's language, given to him by a stranger who did not ask his name and went on down the valley the same afternoon.** **The finding: nobody in this valley named you, and you have been named it since the day you were born by nine people who could not have told you where they got it, and there are two accounts of where it came from and one of them is in a room under a byre and the other is in this yard, and no instrument in this kingdom could put them in one — and a man of thirty-two, who is the only person in four years and a half who could check either, cannot check a word out of a man's mouth.** **The eighth spelling is not a word, and Osgar Vell says so, and the woman of the house points at a man of seventy-four at the table by the fire who has not said anything for an hour, and he says that he was nineteen and that he had a brother who went out of this valley and came back, and that he cut a stroke through his mark in a wall with a cold chisel off a roof slate in nine minutes in a wet week because he did not know what else to do with a thing he could not say out loud — and that there were three of them that week and the two people who have cut a stroke in a wall in that valley in a hundred and forty years had between them not one idea.** **The chapter ends on a man stepping out of the way of a barrow in a valley with no road in it, and on an arrangement that had survived four months.**

**Ch 176 · the fourth day of the cold and the four days after · a generation of no work, and the first bridge.** **About eleven of the ninth went over the pass on the fourth day of the cold and the byres of nine households have been standing empty since, and there are about thirty men under the cross, and Wystan Roole brings sixteen of them up the bank and cannot mend a roof and says why in nine seconds: there is no roof in this valley in nine generations because a roof is for a family, and a man of thirty-four who has been refused twice for a road has worked out in a week that no council ever decided not to build one, because there is nobody in this valley who wants to be anywhere.** **The ninth generation asks about a rafter, a plate and the sills, and the asking is the first time in nine years that they have asked an older hand a question about the trade, and the man of fifty-one loses an argument about plates to the mill man and the twenty-four-year-old puts a hand on the wrong plate and is told why in nine words.** **And the job is not the nine roofs: it is a stand across about four feet of a beck at the bottom of a cutting, because a woman with a yoke has been standing in the wet place to put a pail in it since before he was twenty, and the word the valley has for a bridge is said out loud for the first time on the page, once, in ordinary trade use, and is about a foot thick and is in forty mouths in nine miles and in no book.** **Four days, two bays on two oak sills, laid in lime putty because there is no quicklime left, and the strain: a bridgekeeper of thirty-four can put his hand on a nine-week-old wall and know it and cannot put his hand on a stone he laid four days ago, and he has had that since the second winter and has told nobody.** **And a man who makes nails crosses the stand, comes back over it, and stands on the bank above it again, and is the first customer a nail has ever had in that valley.** The chapter ends on a bridgekeeper on the far bank of a cutting looking at a stone he cannot get at, and a girl of about nine counting from the top of the bank and not asking.

**Ch 177 · the sixteenth day, the seventh hour, in a room over the cross · the strain, and the decision to ask.** **The trade fact, said once, in a yard and on a bank, and it is not the mechanism and is not a restatement of it: a thing is not known to hold until it is put under a load, and about four hundred things in this valley have been under a load for nine generations and not one of them has ever been tested, and a thing that has never been tested is not a thing that has proved it holds, it is a thing that has never been asked.** **The ninth generation's grandmothers have been saying *the water has gone quiet* about the Burn since the first winter, and there are about four hundred yards below a ford where it has stopped and it has not stopped above the ford, and no figure in the valley can be got for it.** **The thing under the valley is straining, it is going to go on straining, there is no way in the world to put a load on it, and the ninth generation has not been asked and the tenth will not be asked and the whole of it is carried by about two hundred people entered at birth by nobody.** **And the man of thirty-four says he cannot name it, and he is not going to manufacture the rest of it in a cold room, and about four people in the room understand exactly what he has just done and nine do not, and nobody stands in the aisle at any point.** **The man of thirty-four asks how, in four seconds, in the middle of four rows of benches, and the answer is *walk*, and he works out in the same breath that eleven steadings and the wide place and four hundred households is nine hundred doors and a hundred and forty miles in the fourteenth week of a cold, and says that a man who says no more in a yard is not a man who says no more in a room. The chapter ends on Osgar Vell on the fourth tread in the dark, saying that you cannot load a person and find out, and on the nail man's tally on a stall and one sheet of paper with eight columns in it under a stone lying crooked that nobody in that valley will ever know about.**

**Ch 178 · the seventeenth day to the twenty-eighth · the second asking, at about nine hundred doors, in eleven days.** **The method is four men and a woman at a door because a man alone at a door is a man asking and four men and a woman is four men and a woman with nowhere to put anything, and it is the whole of the arrangement and it has not been improved on.** **The six things are printed once in the chapter and were said at about nine hundred doors in the same words in the same order**, including the fifth, which is that a count in a file in a county two hundred miles off is the only paper that can be produced against anybody in this valley and is a figure and cannot be refused, and will still be there in nine years whether the winter is here or not. **The woman with the slate is asked at a door like everybody else and says yes in nine seconds, and her reason is that she has kept a number eleven years and nobody has ever asked her for one and kept it, and the only way a number of hers is going to be worth eleven years is if there is something in this valley worth counting.** **And the word: a man of thirty-four puts a hand on a gatepost and runs out of a word from a county that has an office in it, and tells a stranger to use the valley's, and the word the valley has for a bridge is used, and he does not translate it, and the reason he gives is that every thing that has happened to him in this valley has been done in a word that is not his and he is not going to be the first person in the history of this valley to say a thing in his own language and have it be the thing on the page.** The chapter ends on a figure written at the foot of the ninth spread, and on the eleven people over a pass who cannot be asked, and on a girl of about nine at the top of the outside stair who had not knocked and was not going to.

**Ch 179 · the twentieth day and the twenty-second · the count, the count in a file, and the three households.** **The count is made by the four men and the woman out of their own heads in about nine minutes and is about four hundred people, and about four hundred households, and a hundred and ninety-four of the ninth generation, and eleven of the ninth are over a pass and cannot be asked, and the difference between the roll's about two hundred and the a hundred and eighty-nine who were asked is done by subtraction off a number nobody can check.** **Three households said no, at three doors, in about four sentences, a minute and four, and one sentence: a man of fifty whose grandmother's worst nine minutes is why; a man of forty who will not be the first in nine generations to be asked and who has thought for four days and does not want to be a man who changes his mind for a piece of paper; and the man who makes nails, who says at the market two days later that he would rather be one of the three than be the first, and who has wanted to go out of this valley for nine years and has said so out loud.** **And the nine is three households converted into persons by a man with a rope-belt in about nine seconds, and the roll is a roll of persons and has never held a household, and it is the first arithmetic anybody in this valley has done on its own people and it is wrong.** **Then the man of thirty-two tells the man of fifty-eight the whole of it: the file, the form printed for about two years, the count of continuing obligations with a space for a figure and no space for a name, the answer of two thousand six hundred and forty in a woman's hand with no seal and no name in it, the nine words at the foot of the printed paper that are the same nine words as on a sheet under a byre, and that the count is the only thing in the world that can be produced against anybody in this valley and that taking the winter out of the ground does not touch it.** **And he asks the man of fifty-eight not to tell the valley, and the reason is that a count is the only paper that survives, and everything else in the valley falls down.** **And the chapter ends on the man of fifty-eight finding out that the reason he has refused four people a book in nineteen years was not that he did not trust them with it, and that this has been the only thing standing between about two hundred people and a figure he has never seen, and that he is not going to give the want up because the reason it worked is the same reason it is a want.**

**Ch 180 · the twenty-fourth day, the sixth hour · three figures in a book, a sum in chalk, and a page on a bench.** **The keeper of the book puts a figure in it for the first time in a hundred and forty years, in the day column of the last leaf of the ninth spread, because there is no other column for it: about four hundred, about a hundred and eighty-nine, nine — with a mark under each because a line without a mark in that book is a line that has never happened, and over the three a hand, and the hand varies, and nobody in the room says anything for a while.** **And the nine is not to have a name against it, because a name against the nine means three households have to leave, and one of them is a man who has been at a stall four market days running and says a straight answer about when the next market is.** **And the same reason has been the reason for four lines at the head of the first spread for a hundred and forty years, and he has never once known that until this afternoon.** **Then the money, footed in pence on the page before the total is said: one man one day at threepence, sixteen men forty-eight, forty-eight a day for eleven days five hundred and twenty-eight, a barrow of salt at fourpence, and five hundred and thirty-two pence, which is eleven marks and fourpence — in a county's money, in a notebook, and on the end of a trestle in chalk about eleven inches high, and not in the book, because a figure of people goes in the book and a figure of money goes on a trestle and the trestle falls down and the book does not, and the valley's own price of that fortnight is eleven kitchens and about four barrows of salt and nobody's figure and the two are never added.** **And the arithmetic of the a hundred and eighty-nine, and the fact that the thing cannot be done at all until the eleven over a pass are back in about nine weeks, and about four come back and seven do not, and the seven are the only people in the world who could say no.** **And then a leaf is torn out of the back of his own notebook and given away, which is the first page he has given to anybody in four years and a half, and it holds nine lines including one about a word, and the line says the question was asked in a word that means a bridge in this valley and that he did not put the other word on it and why, and he asks a man of fifty-eight to tell him it is a bad idea and is told it is a bad idea and that there is one reason it is worth doing.** **It goes on the seat of the bench at the back of the right-hand row, folded once, and there are twenty-nine benches bolted to the floor of that room with a number cut into the end of each and a hundred and forty cut into the end of that one, and nobody has ever been struck off one of them, and there has never been one thing on any of the twenty-nine that has ever been put on one, and there is one now.** The chapter ends on a form under a squared stone that has been answered by nobody and withdrawn by nobody, and on a stone that is on the diagonal because a man of fifty-eight could not get his hand out from under it, and on nine spreads and a form four steps apart and about two hundred years apart with a shut door between them.

**Block 3 note — the block's calendar, its cast discipline, and its device counts, so that Batch 0004 does not re-derive any of them.** **Calendar.** Path time throughout, **the second and third winters of the sixteen, and a generation is a winter, so the second winter is the tenth generation's and the third is the eleventh's, and the tenth generation is the first that has never stood in the aisle over the cross, and that is a fact about this block and not a device.** **No main-world month, no real-world month and no main-world weekday is spoken or written in any valley scene in any register including a notebook, in any of the ten chapters, and three instances of exactly that were found in this block's own text and repaired before it was finished.** The valley's own register is *winter*, *generation*, *the second cold*, *the fourteenth week of it*, *the fourth day of the cold*, *a market day* and *the hour*. **The eleventh to the twenty-fourth day of the second cold are the block's clock, and the asking ran from the seventeenth day to the twenty-eighth, and the roll's three figures went in on the twenty-fourth.** **The three-weeks warning is unchanged and no character in this block uses the word *failed* about a road, and he is still not writing a drift figure, and the two numbers of his own count and the valley's count are still unreconciled and this block does not touch them.** **Cast.** **One new name of six and five slots unfilled, so Volume 04 stands at four names of six and twenty-two of twenty-four unfilled: Osgar Vell, 22, who is the young man of Chapters 166 and 170 and who is named here in a room because he comes up nine steps to say his own name.** Iven Sarr, Wystan Roole and Nev Treen are returned faces. **The nine valley people Batch 0001 established are still unnamed, and the third generation's house of this block is a house of unnamed people, and a man of seventy-four at a fire and a woman of sixty-eight in a kitchen are two more unnamed faces and neither of them is merged with any other.** **Devices, as whole words and not as strings.** `---` rules **16**, one per chapter and a second in six of them where a movement or a cut in the source happens. Bolded line-openings **49**. Semicolons **0**. Em-dashes **0**. Straight double quotes **0**. Curly apostrophes **0**. `the whole of` **3**, against a house baseline of 2–3, Batch 0001's 10 and Batch 0002's 36 — **and the first pass of this block carried it at 35 and the writer went back through all ten chapters and cut it out of twenty-eight places, which is the single most deliberate thing in the block's texture and the next writer should carry 3 as the number and should not go looking for the phrase.** `about N seconds` **0**. `cut` **21**, all ordinary, and the working is not among them and is not named. `about` **394**, per chapter **42 / 33 / 35 / 43 / 37 / 39 / 37 / 43 / 41 / 44**, **and no chapter is over forty-five and the density does not compound and the passes that brought 171, 174, 175, 176, 177, 178 and 180 under the ceiling cut duration hedges and not the hedges on the ages, the distances and the size of the valley.** Fillers as whole words, a baseline and not a target: **`nodded` 0, `held` 8, `wrote` 28, `stood` 36, `plain` 0, `counted` 10, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0.** **No System panel and no Gatehouse-voice line in this block.** **Money: one column, footed on the page before the total is said — 3, sixteen times over, is 48, 48 eleven times over is 528, 4 for a barrow of salt, and 528 and 4 is 532 pence, which is eleven marks and fourpence** — and the valley's own price of that fortnight is eleven kitchens and about four barrows of salt and nobody's figure, and the two are never added, and the printed figure on the form is *four in this year* in a reckoning this valley does not use and is not converted into anything. **No 456, no 254, no 2,450, no 2,906, no 9-4-1 and no hearth figure in any valley scene, and the two nines hundred and forty-ones are not printed in one line, and the Marches' contract and the fair copy of 408 words are not touched, and no county figure is set beside a valley figure in any chapter of this block.** **The three marks on the outside of the seed vault are not mentioned and the keeper's decision is not taken; the road to the Marches' end of the line was not reopened; the letter for a jar is not answered, not delivered and not traced and no living holder of the other end was found; the bare two inches and the blank ruled column are not filled and not ruled in; the five blanks of Volume 02 are not joined; the eighth ruled line is not looked at; the brine weights are neither mentioned nor filed; *Culling Order* does not appear; *Gatehouse* does not appear; the word *custodian* does not appear; Edric Hunn is not written anywhere and is not in a column and is not in a room; Sian Pugh's name is not spoken; the return stitch is not mentioned; Stage 3 is not reached, not attempted, not offered as a plan, not priced and not described as available, and no lineage is read.**

## Batch 0004 — Volume 04, Chapters 181–190, "The Thread"

**Block 4 of five, and the block in which a bridge parapet is taken down properly and a man is stuck somewhere with no way home for the first time in the manuscript.** Ten complete scenes. **Chapters 181 to 187 are entirely path time in the Ninefold Winter in the fourth and fifth winters of the sixteen, from the ninth day of the fourth cold to about the ninth week of it, and Chapters 188 to 190 are the volume's second and last cut to the north, in one bounded section, on the fourth, seventh and eighth of Harvestmonth, YR 314.** POVs, recorded honestly and not claimed: **Chapters 181 to 187 are Aren Kest's, with long stretches in other people's mouths in 181, 182, 183, 185, 186 and 187. Chapters 188, 189 and 190 are not his at all. He is in none of them, he is not in that county at any point in them, nothing that happens in them reaches him in the same breath, and the two of them are never in one sentence in the same paragraph without the distance on the page.** A writer who says *Aren Kest ×10* for this block is wrong and the Batch 0001 summary's claim of *Aren Kest ×10* for its ten chapters was corrected at its review for the same reason. Cast on the page, **and one new name and five slots unfilled, so Volume 04 stands at five names of six and twenty-seven of twenty-four unfilled: Torrin Hatch**, 55, of the ninth generation, the last man in the valley who has ever stood in the aisle, who voted against keeping the winter and is not called anything by anybody. Iven Sarr, Wystan Roole, Nev Treen and Osgar Vell are returned faces and are on the page. **Torrin Hatch is named once, on a bank, standing up, in about four seconds, because nobody asked him and he answered anyway.** The nine valley people Batch 0001 established are still unnamed, and a man of about twenty-four who is asked about a roof is not named and is not Batch 0002's twenty-four-year-old, though he is in the same trade and the same bank. **Returned faces in the main-world section:** Aldis Fenn, Lysa Fenn, Bram Ottery by count, and the carrier, who is unnamed and is a man of about fifty with a waggon of his own. **Mara Vey, the keeper of records, and the second copyist, thirty, on a warrant not in the press. No System panel. No Gatehouse-voice line. No apology. No death. No tenth not-asking. The tile on the coping is not mentioned. The three marks on the seed vault are not mentioned and the keeper's decision is not taken. The letter for a jar is not answered. The two bare inches are not filled. The letter for the county post of a jar is not in this block. The hearing stands and is neither adjourned nor refused and the delegation is not sent for.** Block total **34,020 words**, per chapter **3334 / 3233 / 3134 / 3513 / 3591 / 3534 / 3433 / 3471 / 3633 / 3144**, the ten columns foot at 34,020, the block is seventeen words under Batch 0003's 34,037 and over Batch 0002's 33,385 and Batch 0011's 31,648, and no chapter is under 3,094.

**Ch 181 · the ninth day of the fourth cold, the ninth hour, in the room over the cross · the mark, and the reason, and four days' notice.** The mark has been in the coping of the far abutment of the stand at the Low Holm since the ninth week of the third cold, and the woman of the Low Holm found it on the second day of this cold with a pail in her hand and **did not tell anybody for a day and a half, and the reason she gives in Chapter 182 is that the first thing you want to know about a mark is what it is and there is nobody in nine miles to tell you, and if nobody else has seen it then you are the one who has got it.** Wystan Roole laid that coping himself in nine minutes on the second afternoon of a four-day job, and slept, and nobody in the valley has ever worked out what he was doing for four days except himself. **His nine sentences are the block's argument and they are said in about four minutes, in the same words he then says to about nine hundred gates over nine days, because if a thing is done in this valley without the reason being said out loud in a room first then in about nine years there will be four accounts of it and none of them checkable.** A stand is two ends and a day and somebody who was not either of the two ends. **He cannot read the figure, he is not a witness because he laid the coping, and there is another end to it about four hundred miles off where nobody has been asked for anything and nobody knows this valley is on a map, and the half of it that is not there is not missing, it is absent, and it is a mark on a stone and it is not wrong and it has not come to any harm.** And it is the highest thing in nine miles a person stands on, and a mark on a public thing is not a mark any more, it is a handle. A man of forty-one of the ninth generation asks whether it will still be there, and is told that it will be under four feet of water at the ford in the order it comes off, for as long as the footing stands, and that the ford moves about a foot a year. **Roole will not chip it out, because a chipped place is a question, and the whole course comes off and a new one goes on.** He asks one question and gets one word for it: *did you do it for a reason that I would understand.* **Yes.** And the man of thirty-two says he has said that sentence in four counties and has never had to take the stone out, and the reason is that in the four counties the stone is four hundred miles off and nobody there has a yoke and a pail, and he says *you are all standing on it, take it down*, and that is a decision and it is the whole of his fourth cold. The notice goes out on the tenth day at the fourth hour, sixteen men, the same eleven words at about nine hundred gates in nine days: **A course of stone is coming off the coping at the Low Holm on the fourth day at the fourth hour. It is the course. Anyone may watch. Nobody is asked anything.**

**Ch 182 · the four days · the day and a half, the fourth stone, the page, and the question.** The woman of the Low Holm accounts for a day and a half in a yard in about forty words and then goes and moves a pail. The man of eighty-one comes, asks how many times it has been done before, is told it has not been done before, and says he will not watch the fifth. The mill man of sixty-one asks about the fourth stone, which is a hundred and forty years old off a byre coping a woman did in lime, and is told it goes in the footing at the ford under the water, and says **under the water is where a thing goes that you are not going to look at again**, and the man of thirty-four concedes it in one word and says he is not going to pretend to a man of sixty-one that it is a good place for a stone. A girl of nine puts her hand flat on the mark to see whether it is warm and says it is not warm, to nobody, and goes and gets her barrow out at the ford. **The page in the notebook is not written on, for the fourth time: three weeks and two sleeps at the fourth week, the valley's own count, and neither converted and the two never added, and a third thing on the same page that is not a figure, which is that there is a date in a county four hundred miles off that nothing he is carrying has a road to.** And then the interior that nobody in the valley can be told: **the same mark, the same chisel, line and bar and a figure, cut into the outside face of a public parapet in a county four hundred miles off at the ninth hour of a day that county keeps in front of twenty men, and a girl of about eleven put her right hand flat on it to see whether it was warm, and the one in this valley was cut in the third cold because that one was four hundred miles away and a man with a limp cannot get to a place in about four seconds.** On the fourth of the four the man of thirty-four asks him the whole of it in front of nine people, and the reason he gave for asking in front of nine people is that if he asks in a doorway then in nine years there are two of them and one of them says they did not. **He is put on the ground work at nothing, and the rate is said out loud in front of everybody, and the reason is that a man who is paid nothing gets asked no questions.**

**Ch 183 · the fourth day, the fourth hour · the taking down.** **About forty people, which is the largest number in one place in this valley in nine weeks, and four of them are of the tenth generation and have never been in a room with a fire in it. Roole will not begin until all of them are there and says the nine sentences again to the forty and not to a stranger, because a reason given once to a room of nine is an account and a reason given twice in the open is a reason.** **He will not let the man off the road lay a stone, and gives the reason before anybody thinks of asking: if a man off the road lays a stone in this valley then in nine years there will be a thing in this valley that a stranger built and about forty people watched, and the only difference between that and the mark is which side of it the light is on.** The whole course comes off, nine stones, in order, and the fourth is the one off the byre coping with a square on it and the mark four inches in from the end. **Nobody rubbed it out, and about nine of about forty noticed, and it went into the footing with the mark on it in a flat light for about four months until the water came up over it.** A man of thirty-one says *you could chip it out* and is told no and says *I know*, and those two sentences went into nine years of this valley's version of things. **The nine stones go down the bank on a barrow with the fourth in the middle, the man who makes nails takes the barrow unasked, and about twenty people who came up to watch a coping came down it instead, which is not a thing anybody here has ever done.** The girl of nine counts them and says she is not going to ask what the mark was. **Roole sets the footing himself and will not let anybody else touch that course, because the bed holds them and not the stones, and he has laid a footing twice in nine years and laid the second one wrong and there is a man in this valley who knows.** Then the new course, bedded in the last of the lime putty and four barrows of sand, and the reason nobody cuts a stone to fit, said once, in about two hundred words: **the stone here is squared on two faces and not on four, and a two-face stone wants a course of one height all the way along, and a cut course in this valley is a hole with a good face on it.** The ninth stone was a finger off and stayed a finger off for an hour and came into the bed on its own in front of everybody, and the mill man of sixty-one said *then you have not been getting it wrong*, and the man of thirty-four said *no*, and that was the whole conversation between them.

**Ch 184 · the eleventh day to the fourth week · the new coping, the stranding, the count, and the first failure.** The new coping is nine inches and is the valley's and not Roole's, and in nine years nobody will be able to tell the two apart. **On the sixth day the water in the cutting comes up over the ninth stone of the old coping at the ford and does not go down for nine weeks, and the mark goes under about four feet of it on the fourteenth, and there is no instrument in this valley that could raise it and nobody asked for one**, and Roole says one four-word thing about it and nobody improves on it. **The nine weeks begin: sixteen men and about thirty under the cross, a mill with a wheel that has not turned in nine years, nine roofs the ninth generation will not let them put up, and four hundred yards of ground from the top of the bank to the ford that is not a road and is the only true line in nine miles.** Roole's answer to what it is for takes about eleven seconds: for a barrow, four hundred yards, once a day. **A man of about twenty-four of the ninth generation says the roof is not done because a roof is for a family and the sixteen under a cross are not a family, and says his mother gave him that sentence in a kitchen in the second month of this cold and nobody has ever asked him about it and he would like somebody to.** Then the counting: a course is counted in loads, a load in barrows, a barrow in shovels, and a man calls the number out loud in a yard in a voice nine people can hear. **He has a word for it and it is not theirs — load points, not a barrow and not a shovel and not a figure of anything anybody can see, and Roole stops the work and makes him say what it is, and tells the yard that thirty men under the cross are not going to learn a figure they cannot check.** Then the first public failure, on the ninth day of the third week: **he is counting what they brought up, and the difference between that and what they laid is the hearting, and the hearting is what is going to be under a thaw, and the right number is lower than the number of barrows by about a fifth, and sixteen men heard the difference.** Then the stick against a wall of the room over the cross, between the second bench and the third, with the reason given and it not being called anything. **Then the stranding, said once in about nine sentences, and it is the first time in this case a man is stuck somewhere with no way out of it and had it said:** he is not getting out before the warm week, Roole has known since the second day of the fourth cold and has not said anything because a man who has just been told he is stuck is not a man you can give a job to, **and Roole thought about building a road on the first of the four days and is not going to, and the reason is not that he would not: a road to the top of this bank that a man did not ask for is not a road out of this valley, it is a road to a place where a man stands, and nothing at the other end of it has been agreed to by anybody in this valley.** The chapter ends on the four hundred yards coming down to the ford and a woman at the ford with a barrow waiting for her turn.

**Ch 185 · the fifth week to the sixty-third day · the ninth generation's questions, the second failure, the wages, and a name said.** The ninth generation asks about the second stand more than it has asked anything in nine years and **none of it is about a stand**: the sills again, what the water does in the warm week (Roole does not know and says so and says a man who manufactures an answer in a yard is a man who is going to be quoted), and whether a stand wants a rail (a woman of about thirty-five says he is wrong and he says he is wrong on purpose and she says everybody on that bank thought it and said nothing because a man of thirty-four does not take a rail off a stand). **Then the second public failure, on the forty-first day: he lays a floor in a trough at the mill the way a floor is laid in four counties, and the mill man puts a loaded barrow across it and it goes down an inch and says two words, *who laid that*, and a man of about fifty-one says it is a pitching and not a floor, stones laid on stones, and that you have given every stone in that trough earth on three sides, and that it will come down about four years of it, one stone at a time, starting with the one you would have pointed at.** Eleven stones up and laid again on stones in two days, and the loaded barrow goes across it and does not go down an inch, and the man of fifty-one says nothing about that either. **Then the wages, footed in pence in a county's money, sixteen men sixty-three days at three pence, two barrows of salt at fourpence, three thousand and thirty-two pence, which is sixty-three marks and eightpence — and the sixteen men and the man off the road are not on the list, because there is no money in this valley to put them on a list with, and the valley's own price is nine kitchens and one beest and a root four households call a root and the rest of the valley calls a word they do not spell, and the two are not added and there is no way on this earth to add them.** He does not put it in chalk on a trestle and gives the reason: the trestle falls down and the chalk will be off it inside a year and he will be gone. **Then the nail man is taken off the barrow run in four days, and the reason is in nine sentences and is not about nails: Roole had not thought about it, and then he watched him look out past the top of the bank on the second day and every day since, and he is not going to have the man at the front of the queue for leaving working it for him in a cold while sixteen men who want the work do not get it.** Then a man of eighty-one at the top of the four hundred yards watching four young people of the tenth generation go down it, and one nine-word sentence that is not a finding and that nobody repeated. **And then, at the seventh hour of the sixty-third day, a man of fifty-five comes down the bank with nine people behind him, and is the last man in this valley who has ever stood in the aisle over the cross, and says his own name standing up because nobody asked him: Torrin Hatch, fifty-five, ninth generation, stood in that aisle thirty-five years ago and was asked what he swore and has not told anybody what he said and is not going to.** He wants the council asked whether it is going to keep the winter and what happens if it does not, and he wants a vote in a room with the benches in it and **not in the aisle**, and he wants it entered that he did not stand in the aisle, and nobody wrote it down because there is nowhere to write it down.

**Ch 186 · the fourth day of the fourth week, and the morning after · the council asked to keep a winter, and asked what happens if it does not.** Nine people in the room over the cross, and the aisle four feet wide and empty all the way through both days. **A man of about thirty-one of the ninth generation puts the thing under the room out loud before anybody else: we are deciding about a winter that two hundred people are in a book for, and none of those two hundred is in this room in a seat, and if we vote the minute will say that we decided, and there is no box on this bench for the two hundred.** The clerk is a man of about sixty who has kept the minute for twenty-two years and is not a member of it and has never been asked to be, and he enters the figures and not the names and has never put a name in that minute that was not put there by somebody else in the room. **The second question goes round the room nine times and is answered four times and the clerk writes *no answer* five times.** The woman of sixty-six at four looms, asked forty years ago in the aisle in the cold with a man at the back holding a slate, says the answer is *we do not know* and then says the half she has never said out loud in a room in forty years: **the asking was right and had never been done and should have been done to her in a kitchen with a fire in it, and what was wrong was the man with the slate and the book behind him, and a book does not know the difference between what a person said and what was true, and that is why she has told the ninth generation in nine words at a time for four years.** The man of thirty-four: **a body that has never decided anything cannot be asked to decide, and if you ask it, it will say yes because saying yes is what it has done in the four times it has been asked, and you will go out with a minute with the word *yes* in it about a winter that not one of the nine of you could describe.** The man of eighty-one, third: the aisle has now gone three generations unused, the hundred and forty has not moved in nine years, nobody has ever been struck off one of the twenty-nine, the tenth generation is coming of age in this cold, forty of them counted out of his own head in nine minutes by the fires, and **nobody is going to be asked, and the stroke cut into the end of that bench for every person who has ever stood in it is the only roll this valley has ever kept of what it has done to anybody.** Then the form comes down four steps and out of the room about four feet by six with a lamp, and the stone goes back on the diagonal, and the eight columns are read out again: *four in this year* in a hand that does not vary, an empty fourth, *none appointed* in a slow careful hand, a withdrawal column with a rule drawn across the head of it that has not been ruled across, an answer column that is empty and whose rule is no more faded than the rule at the bottom of the third, and nine printed words at the foot that are the same nine words on a form printed about two hundred years later in a county four hundred miles off. **And the answer to the second question is not an answer: nothing anybody has ever sent this valley is about a winter, and there is not one word in a hundred and forty years of the only roll in it about a cold or about the ground, and the man of fifty-eight has read everything anybody in this valley has written since his great-grandfather's time.** The clerk reads the minute back and the last thing in it is four words. **And a woman of about thirty takes a slate out from round her neck and says a figure she has kept for eleven years, and the ninth spread has about two hundred of the ninth generation in it, and the two figures are not the same and neither is struck out and neither is offered up to be corrected, and there is nowhere in nine miles that either of them can be put, and the two of them are going to be in this valley for nine years.**

**Ch 187 · the second morning · the vote, and the minute, and a sentence nobody in that valley has heard.** On the first night nine people walk down a bank to nine doors with a lantern and do not go in at any of them. **The man of fifty-five speaks first and wants the passing to stop, and his want is in his own words: that the promise be put down by somebody who is not going to be asked to pick it up, and he says a thing which is nobody's to hold has been going for a hundred and forty years, and he is the only man in the valley old enough to be asked and not old enough to have been asked twice, and if the council votes to keep it then in nine years somebody will have to ask a man of about sixty-four what he swore, and he was that man's age when it was done to him.** His opinion is that the thing underneath does not care who is standing in the aisle, that the aisle is for knowing that somebody in the valley stood up in a cold week and said the words out loud, and that **he does not believe a thing that does not ask is a thing anybody can rely on and he has never been able to give a reason and has stopped trying.** Then Nev Treen answers him and does not blame him: **the aisle does not need an old man, the men who went into it were between twenty and thirty and the winter was kept every one of those times, and a man of fifty-five wanting the promise put down is wanting to put down a rope nobody is holding — and she will stand in that aisle at sixty and be asked what she swore, and the ninth generation will watch, and in nine years one of the tenth will be in it and none of the nine of them will have to be in it twice, and she is offering to a body that is not a bench and knows it, and the clerk wrote it in and nobody argued with it.** The man of fifty-eight says the other half and that nobody in the valley has ever written down what the asking is for. **And then, about forty seconds into the morning, in the middle of an argument about the aisle and the number a hundred and forty and what a bench is, Torrin Hatch says a sentence he has had ready, and the clerk writes it out as it comes, and nobody asks where a man of fifty-five in a valley nine miles long with no writ and no bell and no office had it from, and four people notice that nobody asked and nine do not:** *A bench that is generous decides. A bench that is asked is being told what it is for. And a bench that is asked three times and says yes three times is not a bench, it is a subscription.* **Iven Sarr says the one thing in the chapter that nobody asked him for: he has been the only person in this valley who wanted to know anything for nineteen years and he has worked out this morning that what he is actually for is stopping people asking each other and putting it in a minute, and that in nine years a woman who is not in this room is going to read that sentence and believe it was invented here, and she will be right, and that is what a sentence is for.** The nine are asked one at a time in the order they are sitting in, and the man of thirty-one votes to keep it and asks for the reason of the woman at the door to be entered without naming her, and the man of eighty-one votes to keep it and says he has been asked once in his life and is not going to be the second, and Torrin Hatch votes against and the clerk enters *against, no reason given, a reason having been given* and reads it back twice. **Eight for and one against. The eight said yes without one of them being able to say what happens if they had said no. Nobody in that room called him anything, and he was not called a villain by anybody afterwards in nine years, and he came to the room four more times in nine years and did not stand in the aisle at any point and said no more about it, and that is all that is known about him and it is all he wanted.** The minute cannot be served: no writ in nine miles, no bell, no office, a man of about sixty keeps it over a mill, nobody has ever asked to read it. **And a barrow goes out through the four feet of the aisle on two wheels at the end, because it is the shortest way out of that room, and the man who pushed it did not know there was anything in this valley to know about the length of that floor, and he had not been asked.**

**Ch 188 · the fourth of Harvestmonth, YR 314, a Saturday, the fourth hour · the sixth thing, and eleven words.** **This is not the fifth asking. The fifth asking is in the minute already with its answer, the number of times this bench has been asked the same thing is five and the number of yeses is four, and both figures are on a page under a stair in that hall, and the clerk enters this as the sixth and nobody in the room calls it the fifth, and the man who read the second out loud said so himself before anybody else could.** A carrier of about fifty with a waggon of his own has been to a valley four hundred miles off and asks for a road from the end of the county road to the head of the pass, thirty-one miles, and a turning ground, and **not one yard of anything inside the valley and not one penny spent there and not one person in it asked for anything or agreeing to anything or being told about it.** He tells the room what he saw for about eleven minutes — no road, no gate, no cart, a man of thirty-four with sixteen men and no work since he was twenty who has been refused a road twice and has built four hundred yards for a barrow, a stand over four feet of a beck with a nail in the coping, a mill wheel that had not turned in nine years, a room over a cross with twenty-nine bolted benches and a hundred and forty cut into the end of one, a book under a byre with two hundred people in it from the day they were born, nine hundred doors, three households that said no, and a figure of four hundred on a page in a hand that varies with no name against any of it. **And the figure, footed in pence on the page before the total is said: six men thirty-one days at fourpence is 744; two hundred feet of plank at threepence the foot is 600; forty pounds of powder at threepence is 120; a mason four days at a shilling is 48; cartage four loads each way at eighteen pence is 144; a waggon and four horses and back at eleven shillings the turn is 264; and 744 and 600 is 1,344, 1,344 and 120 is 1,464, 1,464 and 48 is 1,512, 1,512 and 144 is 1,656, 1,656 and 264 is 1,920 pence, which is forty marks.** **And the reason the bench gives is eleven words long: *there are two marks in the chest and the ask is forty*, and nobody argues with it for about nine seconds and those nine seconds were the shortest of the sitting.** The carrier says *that is correct* and asks for the reason entered twice, because a bench that says no for money has refused a thing it could not afford and a bench that says no for anything else has refused a thing it does not want. The reeve, who does not vote on the guild's own matter and has not for nine years, says the reason it is the right no: **a road that is not to be made to carry a person does not get built in a county where nobody has asked for it, and this one has not got a person at the end of it to be carried, and there is nobody at the far end of that pass to be hurt if we do not and nobody to be helped if we do — and she has said the word *and* in that sentence twice and the second one is the argument and the first one is habit.** **And a magistrate of thirty asks the one question nobody asked, before she votes and not after: *then what is the road for*, and the answer she gives herself is that carefulness is not the same thing as there being a reason, and that if this bench funds it then in nine years somebody from this county will go down that road, not to carry anything, because a road is a thing people go down, and this county has no office that will be told about it and no minute it will be put in.** She asks for that reason in the minute. And at the door of the hall on the stair the carrier asks who asked him, and the answer is a man who makes nails, and the clerk enters it.

**Ch 189 · the seventh of Harvestmonth, YR 314, a Tuesday · the finding, in seven columns, and the meaning.** **A document and not a scene.** The county's form is ruled in seven and this office has never in four years and a half used a form of its own that was not this one. The second column takes two minutes and decides the shape of the whole thing, and the clause *and to no person* is the load-bearing clause of the sheet and it is nine words. **The fourth and fifth columns are the day and the hour and they go in as two things with the rule between them and are read back as two things, and the reason is in the keeper's own mouth: a person in nine years who reads a day and an hour in one line cannot tell whether the office made it up or whether somebody was careless, and the fault will be hers and she would rather it be hers on a page.** The sixth column is a witness at this end and the second copyist enters her own name in it, on a warrant not in the press, and has wanted a post with her name in the day-book since the summer before last. **The seventh column, a witness at that end, is empty, and the reason is written under the rule and the empty column is the finding.** The resolution of the twentieth of Hearthmonth, YR 313, is on the table, nineteen lines, never signed by anybody, and its eleventh line says a keeper of records is a keeper of records and is not a person appointed by any council to answer for any person, and the keeper reads it out twice and says out loud that she wrote it herself at twenty-seven in about nine minutes on a bench in a guild hall four hundred miles off. Then the first column: the day and the hour, no person came, no person was sent, the delegation is not to be sent for and has not been and is not to be, a body corporate that has agreed to hear a thing cannot un-agree and can only decide what it is now, what it is now is a room with a keeper in it and nothing at the other side of the table, and this is entered as a fact about this office and not as a finding against anybody. **The two claims in four years and a half that are also against nobody are named, one of them the count of 2,640 in a woman's hand with no seal and no way on earth to check it, and the finding is that the two that are not documents are the two that are still live, and hearing about it less is not the same as it being less.** Then the second copyist says the thing the keeper's own rule was made of — *what a thing means is not a fact and this office is for facts* — and is told she is right and there is no person in the matter to be a claim about, and that the day a person is put into one of these things the meaning comes out of it for good. **And the meaning goes on a second sheet, unattributed, and the finding is that a meaning on a stage can be argued with and a meaning in a minute can only be read, and once it is read it is the same for everybody who reads it, and in nine years a woman who has not met her will have an opinion about it and not be able to give it to anybody.** The deed of the office is thirteen words and it is read four hundred times and has never had to be worked out until this morning.

**Ch 190 · the eighth of Harvestmonth, YR 314, a Wednesday · the cost, the fourth line, the day-book, and a chair.** **The third sheet is what it cost, footed in pence before the total is said: the second copyist, one hour, at the county's rate of eight pence the hour, eight pence; paper, two sheets, at twopence the sheet, two twice over is four pence; the room, one hour, nothing, there being no rate in this county for the use of a room for one hour by its own keeper, a rate for a room being a figure of a thing and a rate for a keeper's hour being a figure of a person; the keeper of this office, one hour, nothing, this office having never entered a figure of the keeper's own time and not going to start. Eight and four is twelve pence.** And the sentence that is the volume's: **a body corporate that can be sued can afford twelve pence sixteen times a year and cannot afford it once if anybody comes**, and she asks for that to be in the finding and not in the cost, because a document that says what a thing is worth is a different object from a document that says what it is. **And the fourth line of the third sheet, which is in no column and is about a person and names nobody: a body corporate that cannot say what a thing cost has no standing to be asked what it cost by anybody else, and this office has a claim number and not a name, and the name is not going in, and so the cost falls on the only person this office can name and cannot put in a column, and she is going to be thirty-eight in about nine years, and by then there will be a second keeper in the room and neither of them will know who decided any of it, and the second keeper will be told it all in about four minutes and it will be in a hand she has not seen before.** And then the second copyist says the thing she has been in the office six years for, to a finding and not to the keeper: **she wanted a post with her name in the day-book and wanted a line and not a warrant, and has worked out that she is not going to get it and does not want it, and that a finding goes on a shelf with a number against this office and a name goes in a head of a book, and those are two different objects, and if a second copyist's name goes in the head of the day-book then whoever holds it in nine years is holding it for her and not for the office.** It goes in under *and to no person*. Then the finding is read back once, and goes on a shelf with the number against the office and nothing against a person, and **the day-book has it in four entries and not three, and it is the fourth time in four years and a half that a day and an hour have gone in separately in this office.** The list of things sent is ruled in four columns and has no fifth, and the two inches at the right-hand edge are bare and are going to be bare in nine. **A bundle of nine things on a second shelf with a piece of tarred hemp through it, all from the one county four hundred miles off, because this office answers everything that is not a question and there is no instrument in this kingdom that can make a county answer a question that is not one.** The fair copy went out on a water-barge hand's asking and cost a sheet at twopence and four pence of carriage and went to a board under a stair four hundred miles off. **And a fourth line about the deed of thirteen words — *for the keeping of what this county cannot be shown it has lost* — is said out loud in a room of two and is not written down, and goes in a book of things said in a room that is not the day-book and is not in the press and weighs nothing, and the finding is that this office could hold a meaning and a county council four hundred miles off could not hold a road, and that is the volume's chosen intimacy bought with an accounting and not with a cure.** The chapter ends on a second chair that has not been moved since the ninth of Rainmonth, and on a tide four hundred yards off.

**Block 4 note — the block's calendar, its cast discipline, and its device counts, so that Batch 0005 does not re-derive any of them.** **Calendar.** Path time throughout Chapters 181 to 187, **the ninth day of the fourth cold to about the ninth week of it, in the fourth and fifth winters of the sixteen, and a generation is a winter, so the fourth winter is the twelfth generation and the fifth is the thirteenth, and the tenth generation is the first that has never stood in the aisle over the cross.** **No main-world month, no real-world month and no main-world weekday is spoken or written in any valley scene in any register including a notebook, in any of the seven valley chapters, and none was found in this block's own text.** The valley's own register is *winter*, *generation*, *the fourth cold*, *the fourth week of it*, *the fourth day of the cold*, *a market day* and *the hour*. **The main-world section is the volume's second and last cut to the north, in one bounded section of three chapters: the bench of nine on the fourth of Harvestmonth, YR 314, a Saturday and a market day; the finding on the seventh, a Tuesday; the cost and the chair on the eighth, a Wednesday.** The return is the twentieth of Harvestmonth, YR 314, a Monday, not a market day, and is not in this block and is not in the state of the valley in any form a valley could keep. **The three-weeks warning is unchanged, no character in this block uses the word *failed* about a road, and he is still not writing a drift figure; the two unreconciled numbers of his own count and the valley's count are untouched and neither is converted, and the third thing that is on the page and is not a figure is that there is a date in a county four hundred miles off that nothing he is carrying has a road to.** **Cast.** One new name of six and five slots unfilled, **so Volume 04 stands at five names of six and twenty-seven of twenty-four unfilled, which is thirty-two against a cap of thirty, and that overrun is carried forward unresolved and Batch 0005 must record which way it resolves it and may not edit `outline/volume-04.md` §13 to make the numbers agree without recording the correction.** Iven Sarr, Wystan Roole, Nev Treen and Osgar Vell are returned faces and are on the page. **The nine valley people Batch 0001 established are still unnamed, and this block adds four more unnamed valley faces who are not any of them: a man of about thirty-one of the ninth generation who farms four steadings, a man of about twenty-four of the ninth generation who lost a plate argument, a woman of about thirty-five of the ninth generation who said the stand wanted a rail, and a clerk of about sixty who keeps the minute and is not a member of the council.** **Devices, as whole words and not as strings.** `---` rules **18**, one per chapter and a second in eight. Bolded line-openings **60**. Semicolons **0**. Em-dashes **0**. Straight double quotes **0**. Curly apostrophes **0**. `the whole of` **3**, against Batch 0003's 3 and Batch 0002's 36, **and the first pass of this block carried it at 24 and the writer went back through all ten chapters and cut it out of twenty-one places, and a later writer should carry 3 and should not go looking for the phrase.** `about N seconds` **10**, against Batch 0003's 0, and the ten are one per chapter. `cut` **31** and all thirty-one ordinary, and the working is not among them and is not named. `about` **391**, per chapter 39 / 44 / 40 / 43 / 45 / 42 / 41 / 38 / 36 / 23, **and no chapter is over forty-five and the density does not compound, and the three main-world chapters run 38, 36 and 23 and the twenty-three is the document chapter and its figures are the office's own and are not hedges at all.** Filler counts as whole words, carried forward as a **baseline and not a target**: **`nodded` 0, `held` 4, `wrote` 27, `stood` 34, `plain` 0, `counted` 24, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0**, against Batch 0001's 0 / 11 / 37 / 13 / 2 / 8 / 0 / 0, Batch 0002's 1 / 10 / 27 / 30 / 0 / 8 / 0 / 0 and Batch 0003's 0 / 8 / 28 / 36 / 0 / 10 / 0 / 0. **`counted` is up at 24 and it is the block's word, and a block about a man counting load points for the first time in his life for somebody else is a block in which that is the trade word, and it is recorded rather than hidden.** **No System panel and no Gatehouse-voice line in the block.** **The thirty-one `cut`s are** a number and a hundred and forty cut into the ends of benches, a stranger's mark cut into a coping, a course cut off a bed, stone and a course cut to fit, a figure cut into a stone, a strip of cut paper, and a sheet cut along an edge with a knife. **Not one of them is the working.**


**Block 4 note, second pass — the Batch 0004 review repair, and which of the descriptions above it invalidates.** **Continuity items 477 to 481 carry the record and the Batch 0004 entry in `state/batch-summaries.md` is not rewritten. Nothing planned moved: no event, no thread, no character, no date, no calendar, no money figure, and the order of the ten chapters is unchanged. The entries above stand as the record of what the block did, and a writer who foots a figure from them should foot it from the second pass instead, which is the one Batch 0005 is pointed at. The passages above that are quoted detail and not plot are corrected here, and these are the only ones.** **Ch 181**, Wystan Roole laid the coping *in about half an hour* and not in nine minutes; **his nine sentences are said to about nine hundred gates in eleven days** and not in nine days, because eleven days was already canon for the same walk; **eleven people have asked him since the second day of this cold** and not about nine people in nine days; **there were about eleven people in the room** and the bench count of twenty-nine and the hundred and forty stand; **the room over the cross has known it is not a bench longer than any man in it has been alive** and not for about nine years; **he has known the ford moves a foot a year since he was twenty-five**; and **the last time in nine years of asking this valley for things** is the only place the Roole refrain is kept in the chapter. **Ch 182**, the girl is *the girl with the barrow* and *She is nine* carries her age, and the bank had about thirty on it and he stood ten feet off, and he answers *Nine words* and not *About nine words*, and there is no longer a *nine-year-old* at the footing. **Ch 183**, the standing figure is *about nine hundred gates* and the asking is on the word of *eleven people*, and there are *about thirty people on the bank* in 182 and *about forty people* at the fourth hour here and the two are different days and both are correct; the reason given in the open is now *there will be a thing in this valley that a stranger built*, not *in nine years there will be*; the sixteen learned the coping in *about half an hour*; the man of fifty-eight stood at the bottom of the bank *about half an hour* and the answer he gave was *not about the form*; and the ninth stone went into the bed on its own and the account of it is *the ninth account this valley will have of it*. **Ch 184**, *nine roofs up the bank* and not *nine roofs nine steadings up the bank*, **which was a garbled doubling of one figure and is repaired here and in Ch 185**; the new coping is such that *in a few years* nobody will tell the two apart; the twenty-four-year-old was told why *in a yard*; and the stranding is *in nine sentences*, exact. **Ch 185**, the second stand went in *in a fortnight*; **nothing in a floor in this valley fails in a fortnight** and not in nine days, which was a fabricated precision; the man of fifty-one's *That is the trade* carries no word-count; Roole's reason for not chalking the trestle *was short*; the nail man was off the barrow run for *a cold* and not nine weeks; the man of eighty-one counted forty *in about half an hour* and said *one sentence*; and Torrin Hatch came down the bank with *a crowd behind him*. **Ch 186**, the man of thirty-one's speech is **one clean bolded span** where it was a nested pair that rendered its centre plain, and the vote is *this bench voting about two hundred* and not nine people voting about two hundred. **Ch 187**, the same nine sat *in the seats they had sat in in the first cold*;
