the man of fifty-five has *thirty years* of saying it in yards; *as many again did not* notice the clerk had not asked; and *there is no writ at all*. **Ch 188**, the opening no longer gives away the verdict and the eleven-word reason, the magistrate came up *ten minutes* before the carrier, the clerk has entered *three times* as much as the standing orders require, and **Aren Kest's absence is now carried in the room's terms, *nothing said in that hall has his name in it*, and no line in Chapters 181 to 190 refers to a chapter, a book or a reader.** **Ch 189**, the `**---**` rule is a bare `---`, so the block's rules are **19** and not 18, and the keeper wrote the eleventh line *in about ten minutes*. **Ch 190**, the opening no longer prints the total before the arithmetic that foots it, and **the keeper of records is twenty-nine and the fourth line of the third sheet now says she is going to be thirty-eight in about nine years, and twenty-nine plus nine is thirty-eight and the figure was wrong in this summary and in ten other state files and all eleven are corrected.** **Devices after the repair, as whole words and not as strings.** `---` rules **19**, one per chapter and a second in nine. Bolded line-openings **59**. `the whole of` **3**, unchanged. `about N seconds` **1**, against 10 as first written. `about` **337**, per chapter 30 / 43 / 34 / 38 / 34 / 39 / 36 / 32 / 32 / 19. `cut` **31**, all ordinary, unchanged. **`nine`, which this ledger did not carry and now must: 184 in 34,011 words, 5.40 per 1,000, per chapter 15 / 16 / 19 / 18 / 25 / 28 / 24 / 23 / 5 / 11, against 371 and 11.11 as first written and against a volume trend of 2.87 / 4.58 / 7.31 / 11.11 / 5.40, and within it `about nine + noun` is 8 against 79, `in nine years` 18 against 52 and `nine words` 2 against 11. The motif is canon and none of it was cut.** Filler after the repair: **`nodded` 0, `held` 5, `wrote` 27, `stood` 35, `plain` 0, `counted` 24, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0.** **Words after the repair: 34,011, per chapter 3393 / 3213 / 3105 / 3505 / 3589 / 3559 / 3433 / 3445 / 3631 / 3138. Volume 04 is 134,478 in forty chapters and the manuscript is 736,120 in one hundred and ninety.** **A chapter's bolded ledger opening stays and may no longer deliver the chapter: it may state the situation, the clock, the place and the pressure, and it may not state the answer, the verdict, the total, the vote or the thing a character is going to say.** A second delivery inside a block is legitimate and the nine sentences are said twice; **the prohibition is that the opening paragraph may not be the first of three.**

## Batch 0005 — Volume 04, Chapters 191–200, "The Seam"

**Block 5 of five, and the block in which a valley nine generations old stops holding, a man is let into a hole in a bank because there is no instrument in nine miles that can say whether a bank is square, and a woman of twenty-nine makes him say three things out loud in a room of two and does not walk up a road with him.** Ten complete scenes, at **34,157 words** as repaired by both review passes, per chapter **3306 / 3142 / 3405 / 3287 / 3550 / 3586 / 3249 / 3503 / 3964 / 3165**. **Chapters 191 to 197 are entirely path time in the Ninefold Winter, and the block's own register puts the four days in the ninth week of the fourth cold and the eleven weeks of the thaw after the warm week that follows it, so the block covers the fourth, the fifth and the sixth winters of the sixteen rather than the fifth and sixth alone, and a generation is a winter, so the fourth is the twelfth, the fifth is the thirteenth and the sixth is the fourteenth. It runs from the ninth day of the fourth week of the fourth cold to the ninth day of the twelfth, and Chapters 198 to 200 are the volume's second and last cut to the north, in one bounded section, on the fourteenth of Harvestmonth, YR 314, a Tuesday; the twentieth, a Monday; and the twenty-fifth of Fallowmonth, YR 314, a Monday, the volume's last day, none of them a market day.** POVs, recorded honestly and not claimed: **Chapters 191 to 197 are Aren Kest's, with long stretches in other people's mouths in 191, 192, 194, 195, 196 and 197. Chapter 198 is not his at all — he is four hundred miles off in a valley, nobody in that chapter says where he is, and nothing that happens in it reaches him. Chapter 199 is his and is mostly not: the return is his, and there is a whole day of the seven days of the fever in a room over a weigh-house that he is not in, and Chapter 200 is his.** A writer who says *Aren Kest ×10* for this block is wrong twice over and the reason is not the same reason twice. Cast on the page, **and one new name and four slots unfilled, so Volume 04 closes at six names of six and twenty-three of twenty-four unfilled, twenty-nine of thirty slots, one under the cap: Nesta Garrow**, 41, who keeps the top of the pass, who is the granddaughter of the woman of about forty-one who keeps the road at the County of Ash end and is not the same person, who has not been asked about the road in about two years and has not asked either, and who says her want to a bridgekeeper and not to a stranger. Iven Sarr, Wystan Roole, Nev Treen, Osgar Vell and Torrin Hatch are returned faces and are on the page; Torrin Hatch is not asked anything. **The nine valley people Batch 0001 established are still unnamed, the four unnamed faces Batch 0004 added are still unnamed, and the nine people of the three households are eight names in eight hands in a second column of the ninth spread and are not in prose anywhere in this block and are not to be.** Returned faces in the main-world section: Mara Vey, the second copyist, the junior clerk by the post, the carter of thirty, **the man of forty-seven, who is Bram Ottery and is on the bench of nine because he was elected and not because he is on a trade, paid fourpence a week out of a private box, and is the witness at this end, which is the job Chapters 152, 156 and 157 gave him — as repaired; the first pass of Chapters 198 and 199 called him a road warden on the roll with a vote and not an office, which is Aren Kest's own description, and put the sentence about a decline and a survey and a form into his mouth, which is the guild's man with the levels' —**, the guild's man with the levels, who says it in the open to a yard of forty men in Chapter 198 in the wording of Chapter 150, a mason of fifty and a half, a boy of fifteen at a bench-board, the carrier of about fifty, the reeve, Lysa Fenn, the bench-clerk of about sixty, the apprentice of seventeen and a carpenter, and a man of twenty-five with a barrow. **No System panel but one in the block, and it is two plain lines at the end of Chapter 194 with no number in it.**

**Ch 191 · the ninth day of the fourth week of the fourth cold, and the second morning of the four days · the notice, the eleven, the eight names, and the first face.** A man of fifty-eight brings a piece of chalk out of a box on a shelf behind a fire in the room over the cross that has been opened four times in a hundred and forty years, because a notice in a valley with no writ and no bell and no office has to be on a public thing or it is a rumour by the second morning, and nobody improved on that. **The man of thirty-four says the reason once in a room of about eleven and says beforehand that he will say it again at the ford in front of forty, and a reason given once in a closed room is an account and a reason given twice in the open is a reason, and he learned that from a man of fifty-eight in a cold room in the second month of this valley and did not understand it at the time.** The reason: a thing under this valley nine generations old, holding, not a promise, nobody is going to get it out of either of them; it is straining, a man of thirty-four can feel it in a road and cannot name it, and there is no instrument in the world for putting a load on it; and a man can take it out in four days with about forty people. **And the asking is not going to be done again: nine hundred doors, eleven days, the second month of the second cold, five men, the same six things, nothing written down at any of them, and the asking was right and it is the only thing anybody in this valley has ever done that was right.** Then the eleven: eleven of the ninth went over the pass on the fourth day of this cold, about four come back in the first warm week, he has been here nine years and has never once known which four, and **the seven that went with the asking are the only people in the world who could say no to the four days and they cannot be asked and have not been asked, and this is not going to be made unanimous by their absence, and nobody is going to go and find out where they are.** The clerk of about sixty writes it in a minute in a room over a mill that nobody has ever been served with. The ninth spread comes down four steps: three figures in the day column, **about four hundred, about a hundred and eighty-nine, nine**, and to the right of it a second column ruled in a man of fifty-eight's ink, about the width of two fingers, the first new column on that page in a hundred and forty years. **Eight names in eight hands, and the ninth line empty, and the reason is the fourth line at the head of the first spread, read out in a room of nine: a name in a book of this valley goes in because the person did it themselves.** The man of thirty-four looked at the margin for a minute and a half and has not said a word about it since. **Then the ford at the fourth hour: about forty people, a face in the low bank, eleven feet of good bank above the frozen foot, a rope-belt's worth of work, and a man of fifty-eight standing at the bottom of a bank in a cold doing nothing in Chapter 183's echo — here, at the top, writing nothing on a rag paper.** About nine bars and a half of it, the ninth generation asking about the barrow run all morning, a woman of about thirty-five saying in front of forty people that the man of nails has not looked out past the top of the bank once this morning, and a girl of nine on the low end of it. **And the day ends on a decision, not a refusal: a man of thirty-four stops it at the seventh hour because in a cold in this light you cannot see the bottom of a four-foot face and there are forty people on that bank.**

**Ch 192 · the second of the four days · the face that moved overnight, the water, the ford that moves, the rope, and the step.** The face went over in the night, about the width of a hand, and the man of thirty-four had been at the top of it since the middle of the night, and the answer is that a frozen foot lets go when you take four feet out from under it in a cold, and that he had worked the step out at the second hour with a lamp and was telling them at the fourth hour so they could all be cross about it in front of each other. **The ninth generation asks where the water under the ford comes from and gets the same answer three times and a different one once, and the different one is *I do not know*, in eleven words, and nine of them wrote it down because about nine of them had brought something to write on.** The barrow run goes over the top of a face that is going over into itself, and about forty people come off a face in four seconds, and a man of twenty-two of the ninth generation is the one who is on it, and it is the most frightening thing that has happened in this valley in nine generations and nobody was hurt. Then an hour with bars on the bank and the run moved round the low end at a cost of eleven yards a load. **A man of about twenty-six asks what happens if one of the eleven comes back in the warm week and says no, and is told the four days are the four days, because a face cannot be made in a warm week, and that the moment he asks a man off a road to come and fetch a thing then a thing in this valley has been asked to go and fetch a man, and that he spent nine days taking a stranger's mark out of a public stone for exactly that.** The girl of nine is put on the low end of the run and stays on it for the rest of the four days, and a man of thirty-four says in front of forty people that a child on the top of a face that is going over is a child on the top of a thing that has not found its angle yet, and that he came off one of those nine weeks ago on his back four feet in front of a woman with a yoke. **And then the thing the whole block turns on: the man of thirty-four says out loud, in the open, for the first time in nine years, that the ford moves about a foot a year and that a man who has known a thing like that in this valley is a man who is asked to be right about it and there is nobody in nine miles who can tell him — and that nine good stones are in the footing of the second stand under four feet of water in the order they came off, and the footing is in the bed of that ford, and the bed goes down a foot a year, and he has laid a footing twice and laid the second one wrong, and he will not work it out in four days, because a man who works a thing out in nine years and writes it down has made a survey, and he is not going to make a survey in a valley with no writ and no bell and no office and a hundred and forty cut into a bench.** The rope is on the bank at the end of it and nobody asked what it was for. **The day ends on a step going into the face and a man of fifty-one saying the ground underneath wants looking at tomorrow and not tonight, and he was there tomorrow.**

**Ch 193 · the third of the four days · the bar, the ground that came back, the stopping, and the noise of the water.** At the fourth hour a man of fifty-one puts a bar to the bottom of the face and it goes in about a finger and stops and comes back with a white line on the point of it, and nobody says anything for a minute and a half, and the man of thirty-four says two words from the top of the bank: *that is the thing*. **He does not come down and he does not say anything else about it, and four people within thirty feet heard the two words and nine did not, and the man of fifty-one has never repeated them to anybody, and the man of thirty-four had thought about the wording of them for nine years and came out with two.** Four questions from the ninth generation and four answers, one of them given by a man of thirty-two who has been asked nothing and answered four questions in a morning. A woman of about thirty-five puts the bank with a beck coming out under it for sixty years and a byre with a dinner-plate floor, and is told that the best wall anybody in this valley has seen is holding that byre up, and is told twice more across a year and a half that he has not got anything, and that she is going to keep asking. At the sixth hour the loose in the face goes into the bottom in the time it takes to say a sentence, without a noise, and a man of thirty-one of the ninth generation says *off the face* before the man of thirty-four does, and forty people are off it faster than on the second morning, and nobody is hurt. **A man of fifty-eight comes up the bank with a rag paper and does not write a figure on it, and gives the reason out loud on a bank: a figure of people goes in that book, a figure of days does not, and he has not got a second book and is not going to take a leaf out of this one, because the day he takes a leaf out of that is the day that book is two books.** Then the afternoon: sixteen men on the bottom of the face in a course of a height with nothing cut to it, and a rope between a man of thirty-four and a man of thirty-two with a limp, and nobody asks, and four accounts in four kitchens agree on the fact that nobody asked without anybody having to check. **And the chapter ends on the river: at the ninth hour a man of thirty-four says four words and about forty people put their barrows down and stand in the ninth hour of a morning in a valley that does not get dark and listen to four hundred yards of the Burn make the noise it has made every day for nine generations, and then he says that is not ours, and that nobody is to go and look at it and nobody is to say they heard it, because a woman at a table with four people at it in nine years will say the man who dug the hole made the river come back and there is no way in nine miles to go back to the ninth hour of the third day and find out whose it was — and a man of fifty-eight said in a room in the warm week that the second version is not better, it is later, and that a sentence is not a figure and does not go in.**

**Ch 194 · the fourth of the four days, and the fifth morning · the course, the girl with the barrow, the hole, the wall in the parapet, and the one panel in the volume.** The fourth day was written as four and became three: a man of thirty-four and a man of fifty-eight change the chalk on the end of the back bench in a room where it is not a bench, and put a bar through the four and leave the three legible, and neither of them says why. **The fourth day is a course, sixteen men putting one across the top of a four-foot face at three feet in a day and a half with nothing cut to it, and about two hundred people came up that bank in four days and about nine hundred did not come at all.** What the man of thirty-four did for four days is said once in a yard in the warm week and goes into four kitchens: he carried, he was faster at the low end of the run than a man of twenty-six because he has got a limp and has learned to use it, and he stood at my shoulder for four days with his hand on a rope and nobody asked what the rope was for and I did not explain it. The ninth generation asks four questions about a lid. A mill man of sixty-one puts nine barrows into the course and then says one thing to a man of fifty-one about the eleven yards of made ground at the low end going before the run does, and the man of fifty-one repeats it to nine people in the warm week and will not repeat it again. **And the girl with the barrow counts the barrows going up and the barrows coming down and the two numbers are not the same, and gives both figures to a man of thirty-four, and he writes nothing down, and a man of fifty-one explains the difference to nobody and says he is not going to tell her.** Then the eighth hour: the man of thirty-four tells forty people what the four days were for and what the hole is, and **it is a hole in a bank with a barrow run over the top of it and it is not a grave and it is not a mark and in nine years there will be a woman in this valley who will not be able to tell anybody what it was.** A man of about forty-one asks whether it will still be there and is told while that course is, and that the ground under the ford has moved about a foot a year since the man of thirty-four was twenty-five and that he has not known why in the whole of the time. **The only System panel in the block, two plain lines, no number in it, and he does not write it down, as he has not written down the other four.** The fifth morning: the room over the cross, the second column, the ninth line not ruled across, the figures in the ninth spread and why a figure of weeks does not go in, the minute's entry of the eleven with no figure for the other seven, and a woman of about thirty-four with a barrow and a dog who is at a door and not at a book.

**Ch 195 · the fourth morning of the warm week · the repair, the reason he is let in, the road opening across the country, the barrow run, the count that stops, and the wages.** **A man of thirty-four comes up the bank with a bar and says three sentences in about eleven seconds: *you can tell whether a bank is square, I cannot, I have got four men here who cannot and one of them is fifty-one and the other three are under thirty and have all been wrong in front of me this month.* And then: you are not laying a stone, you are the man who says whether it is square, you will say it about four times a day, I will move whatever you tell me to move, and I am not going to ask you whether you are sure, because if you are not sure I want to know that and not have it kept.** The course comes up in about two hours, four times, and the fourth time he says nothing at all and the man of thirty-four waits, and he says *it is square and it will not stay square* and that the second half of that is his. The barrow run goes over the top of the hole the whole time and a woman of the Low Holm goes over the works four times a day and does not say anything. **And then the man who made the run counts out loud in a county's way, gets four words into the fourth barrow, stops in the middle of a yard with his hand on the barrow, and is made to say it or not begin it. He says it. It is about a fifth lower than the figure of barrows and it is right, and a man of fifty-one said the same figure in a yard in the third week of the fourth cold without knowing the other man had it — and then he says he has a figure and the yard has a figure and no way of putting the two side by side in this valley, and that he is not going to say it again, and that a man of thirty-four told him once that about thirty men under a cross are not going to learn a figure they cannot check, and he was right and it took the man of thirty-four four minutes to say and the man of thirty-two two years to find out.** Then the road speech on the bank, in nine sentences: about two hundred people are going to want to be somewhere, he was wrong about that for nine years and has said so, the day it stopped being a habit was the day a man of thirty-two stood at the bottom of a hole with a rope in his hand, **and there is going to be a road out of this valley and I am not going to put a figure on the end of a trestle in chalk because the trestle falls down and the chalk is off it inside a year and I will be here.** The wages, footed in pence on the page: 3 sixteen times over is 48, 48 thirty-one times over is 1,488, 4 twice over is 8, **1,488 and eight is 1,496 pence, which is thirty-one marks and eightpence**, and the valley's own price is not a figure and the two are never added, and a man of fifty-eight enters it in the ninth spread and says why in nine sentences: a figure of people goes in that book and a figure of money does not. And the man who makes nails is back on the barrow run and the reason is the same reason as the fifty-nine days, given in nine sentences, and the man of thirty-four says he would rather he went third than first and is not going to say it to him.

**Ch 196 · the twelve weeks, the asking in the ninth, and the seed in the aisle · the ground comes down.** **The warm week was nine days and after it there was no cold in that valley for the first time in nine generations, and a week of that valley is a thing a person standing up in a cold room says a week is, and the man of fifty-eight entered no figure of weeks and gave the reason in four words and nine sentences: the day there is an authority that could be asked is the day somebody can be made to answer for the eleven weeks and he would like to know what he is going to say then before he puts a number against it.** The costs, in order: in the third week the four hundred yards of made ground went to a slope in nine days and the ninth stone of the new coping at the Low Holm came off on its own on the fourth day and is on the bank and the barrow run goes round it, and a man of thirty-four says in a kitchen before the end of the month *it is not the hole*; in the fifth week six byres came down; in the sixth week four of the nine open roofs came in and a fifth is a roof on a house that is not there; in the seventh the mill went on the low side and the man of sixty-one who could not leave it was asked by four people in one day whether he was going and said he had not decided and decided on the ninth day and gave the reason in nine words about the race; in the eighth week the ford moved twice, a foot each time, and has not moved since, and a man of thirty-four says in a yard and not in a room because a room has a clerk in it, and says he is not going to say it stopped because of a hole in a bank and that he has four trades' worth of reasons and not the one that is true. **And the seed, about nine bushels and a peck and a peck, carried up a bank in two days and put in the aisle of the room over the cross, which is four feet wide and has had no person in it for three generations, and entered in the minute by a clerk of about sixty on the man of fifty-eight's instruction as the first entry he has made in twenty-two years that was not said out loud in a room in front of him.** **And in the ninth week the ninth generation is asked one more time, by a man of thirty-four who has had the question ready for nine weeks, and the asking is not refused: *we are not asking you to keep it, there is nothing left to keep, I am asking you one thing and it is the only thing I have got, I am asking you what it was.*** A man of thirty-one says ask it as a question and not as a thing with a person in it. The answer is **We said yes once.** Four words, entered in the middle of the page and not at the end and not in the day column, because there is no column in that book for a thing that is a fact and not a count, read back twice. A man of fifty-five says it is the only true sentence anybody has said in that room in two years and that he is not going to say another word about it and that he has not stood in the aisle and is not going to. And the ninth is asked about, and a man of thirty-four says in four sentences that he made it in about a minute on a step with a rope-belt on, and that the three households hold eight people and that there is a ninth he cannot find. **The clerk writes the four words in the middle of the page and reads them back twice and shuts the minute, and the fire goes out at the ninth hour, and a girl of nine comes up the outside stair during all of it, does not knock, stands on the fourth tread, counts the benches through a doorway, gets twenty-nine, and does not go in. And the chapter closes on the tenth week, about two hundred people begin to find out where the pass is, from a man of about fifty with a waggon of his own at a market, and nobody in the valley told them, and a woman of about thirty says a figure out loud from a slate and a man of fifty-eight says another, and the two are not the same and neither is struck out and there is nowhere in nine miles that either of them can be put.**

**Ch 197 · the ninth day of the twelfth, and the fourth day of the next cold · the road, the woman at the top of the pass, the sentence about a man, the promise nobody was to pick up, and the first three families out.** **A barrow road of nine hundred yards with a crown and a ditch and a turning place forty feet across that has no name, put up by sixteen men and about thirty under the cross, and nobody in that valley can say who made it, and the reason is that the man of thirty-four refused attribution three times in three yards in front of nine people each time and a woman of about thirty-five called him a fool in front of eleven people and he said she was not wrong, because a road a man made is a road a man can be asked about, and the day there is a man in a valley whose name goes on a road it is a road that belongs to him.** **Nesta Garrow, 41, comes down off the top of the pass on the eleventh day with a barrow and a trough-hook, and has kept the top of that pass for about two years and is the granddaughter of the woman of about forty-one who keeps the road at another end of another country four hundred miles off and is not the same person and the two have never met, and the road at that other end has been shut in four lines of writing since before the girl of the barrow was born and nothing in these ten chapters opens it.** She has not been asked about the road in about two years and has not asked, and she worked out in the last four weeks that those two things are the same arrangement and that it took nine generations to make this one and about four hundred years to make the other one, and both of them are in that valley at the same time on the same bank. **She says the one thing she came down for to a bridgekeeper and not to the stranger, and does not look at him: *I want the road made and I want it made so that in a generation there is not one person in this valley who can tell you who made it, and a road that a man made is a road a man can be asked about, and I have watched two of those in my life and both of them were men who were good with their hands.*** The man of thirty-four says that is the second half of a thing he has been saying for two winters and did not know anybody else in the world was saying it, and she says you have been saying it about a mark in a stone and a road is nine hundred yards long and is going to be used, and she would rather be wrong on the right thing than right on the wrong one. **And then the memory, and it is not a fond one, and it is said out loud in the open at the fourth hour of the ninth day in front of about nine people by a man of about twenty-eight of the ninth generation to a child of nine: nine roofs open, nine roofs open before he came, nine roofs open now, four of them came in, the mill went, the ground went to a slope, my roof is on because of the sixteen, and my daughter is nine and she will be told that a man came and the winter went and she will not be told what it cost, and in a generation a woman will say that the man came and the winter went and nothing else and she will not be wrong, because the other half is not hers and it is not anybody's.** A man of fifty-five comes up the new road with eleven people behind him and says one thing at the top of the turning place in about nine seconds: the promise was nobody's to hold, there is nothing to hold, he has been relieved of it by a man he has spoken to about four times, he has not been asked to pick anything up and would like that in the minute, and there is no column for it. **And the first three families out are the three that said no, on the fourth, the sixth and the ninth of the next cold, with about four barrows of nails, a dog and no family, and a mother and a brother of twenty-six and a sister of nineteen — and nobody in that valley arranged it and the man of thirty-four would have given anything in the wages of the whole time not to have, and the figure of nine is one higher than the number of people in them and the ninth line is empty and is going to be empty.** It ends on a barrow going up nine hundred yards and coming back down, and a man of thirty-four at the low end of a cutting with his hands behind his back, and no cheer, and eleven people in nine miles saying out loud that it is the first time anybody has gone out of that valley and come back, and one of the eleven said the wrong half of it and nobody corrected her.

**Ch 198 · the fourteenth of Harvestmonth, YR 314, a Tuesday, and not a market day, the fourth hour · the Sedge Reach, forty men, and a line in a stone course that nobody can account for.** **Not Aren Kest. He is four hundred miles off in a valley and nothing in this chapter says where he is and nothing in it reaches him.** The Sedge Reach, four timber bays, the third down in the spring and the fourth in the summer, eleven tons in stone, four carts a day out of a quarry eleven miles up the channel, a crane and a gin and four men on a rope, a boy of fifteen at a bench-board with a lime tub and a rule and a bill copying the empty space under a number as well as the number, a man of nineteen carrying a hod badly, and the lane over the first bay in use all day with salt on it. **A carter of thirty puts a barrow down in the middle of the fourth bay and stands looking at the third course from the low end, and there is a line in it about nine inches long, in the stone and not across the joint and not in it, not got at with a nail or a cold chisel, and it does not move when two men push the two ends of it with their thumbs.** He tells four men and not the yard. **A man of forty-seven on the roll of the bridge guild with a vote and not an office, who does not hold this reach or the Old Toll or the crossing and has not voted since the day he was elected, puts his hand flat on the stone on either side of the line, the way anybody puts a hand flat on a thing to see whether it is warm, and will not have it measured. The guild's man with the levels, about sixty, who has kept his own book for two years and been paid sixpence twice out of a box, comes across and will not put the rule down, and the sentence is the finding of the chapter: a thing that has been measured once gets measured again in a year, and the second one is a decline, and a decline is a survey, and a survey goes in a form, and he has told that to one man in his life and the man was not in that yard and was not going to be told by him twice.** The four men who were on the stone are asked separately and their four accounts do not agree, and a man of forty-seven says in a yard of forty men that he was on the low bank on the ninth of Harvestmonth from the second hour to the fourth and that four people are owed a thing they have not been asked for, and the mason and he agree that a course is not coming up on a boy's word and a man's memory, and nobody minuted it because there is no clerk on a reach. **The mark in the parapet of the first bay is named once as an object, a line and a bar and a figure at the height of a thumb, there since the sixth of Goatmonth, unreadable at thirty feet where a mason's mark is readable at thirty, and a girl put her hand flat on it in the summer and it was not warm.** The seam was not measured, not pointed, not cut out and not filled, and the work went on over the top of it, and the pointing was not finished on the day it was begun and the joint is half a foot short and nobody in that yard has measured it either.

**Ch 199 · the twentieth of Harvestmonth, YR 314, a Monday, and not a market day, the fourth hour, and the seven days after it · the return, the price, the offer, the refusal, and the three things.** **He comes up out of the joint between the third and the fourth course, the way a man comes up out of a river, with his hand on the stone before the rest of him is out of it, and the witness at that end is the man of forty-seven and he is not the person keeping the reach, and the seam is under his left hand and he looks at it once and does not put a rule on it.** The mark in the first bay's coping is four yards behind him and he puts his hand flat on it and it is cold and he says nothing to anybody. **The fever takes him that night and the six days after it and breaks on the seventh, and the price is on the page in three parts: a man of twenty-five who owns the crews and the yard on Weir Street held his tools for seven days and wrote nothing down and said *your barrow is out of true and I have not looked at it because it is not mine to look at*; the same man ran nine errands, four of them for other people, and told a woman of about seventy in Weir Street where his brother was on the second day and did not say anything else, and she asked him on the fourth day what the place was called and he said it was called a valley and that he did not know the name of it and had not asked; and a carrier of about fifty came down two flights of a stair on the fourth of the seven and told the bench, unasked, that the man he had described to them was in the town, and gave as the reason that nobody in that hall had asked him and it was not anybody's fault and it was also the reason the sixth asking was not the fifth.** The word is not used between the two of them and the spent sentence is not re-said. **And on the second of the seven there is a whole day in a room over a weigh-house that he is not in: the second copyist refuses to open the tenth letter from a county four hundred miles off because the day she opens the eleventh is the day the office is a body corporate that goes through a county's post, and the keeper of records opens it, and the terms are a hand, a room, a house, four shillings the week and a clerk; the eastern paths closed by that man and by nobody else; a county of nine hundred and forty-one hearths entered by number and not by name and the number not given to the man; and no question asked of him about the object of the appointment by anybody at any time. No part of it describes what the thing is and the volume does not describe it.** On the fourth of the seven he writes the refusal in four sheets, in his own hand, in his own room, with her copying it, slower than the writing and not better: it refuses the appointment, refuses the closing, says the number of persons the appointment was made out to is not on the sheet and has been asked for in a return and is not going to be supplied, and says there is no thanks in it. **The day and the hour go in as two things with the rule between them, and he did not ask her to break the second rule on the wall and she did not offer to keep it for him.** And then she makes him say three things in about nine minutes — a leg, a seam, and about two hundred people who are going to be somewhere else in a year — and none of it is an apology and none was available, and the third rule's final clause is about him and neither of them says so. She says then it is not a cure and not a settlement and not the end of anything, and that she will be in the room over the weigh-house at the eighth hour and on the two mornings after and that he will be on the reach because that is where his hands are, and **that she is not going to walk up that road with him, and the reason is that the opinion of a town of four thousand people would be about her and she has four years and a half of a body corporate that answers to nobody.** The refusal goes out in a bag in the ordinary post and a copy goes onto a shelf with a number against the office and nothing against a person.

**Ch 200 · the twenty-fifth of Fallowmonth, YR 314, a Monday, and not a market day, the fourth hour · the volume's last chapter, and it is a road.** Thirty-five days. **The seam is under about half an inch of lime in the third course of the fourth bay, it was pointed over at the joint and not at the stone, it has not moved and has not grown and has not closed, and it is the only thing on that reach nobody in that county can account for, and it is the second, because there is a line and a bar and a figure in the coping of the first bay at the height of a thumb that has been there since the sixth of Goatmonth, YR 313, and the figure in it cannot be read from thirty feet by anybody in that county and never has been.** He looked at the seam once, on the Monday he came up, and did not measure it, and a mason of fifty and a half offered him a rule on the Thursday and said that a rule on that stone is a figure and a figure is a survey and he had not got a survey. The same mason, who laid a floor in a trough at a mill in a valley in a winter in its ninth generation and told him to take it up and lay it again on stones, looked at him once after the fever broke and said *you are slower now* and went back to his stone. **A man of forty-seven has carried a hod for four hours and a half and done it well, and the bench of nine does not know he was there and has not been told and is not going to be, and he has not used his vote about that either.** The bench reads a minute in which five askings, four yeses and the entry that the fifth was not put are all in a clerk's hand; the sixth asking, forty marks footed at every line, refused in eleven words with the reason entered twice; a magistrate of thirty's reason for the no; and a document road of paper out of a county four hundred miles off that carried nobody. **A carrier of about fifty says four things and none of them is about money: he was at the bottom of the pass in the second week of the twelfth and did not go up it, there is nine hundred yards of barrow road and a turning place about forty feet across at the top of it, the woman who keeps the top of the pass came down on a Tuesday with a barrow and told him a wall is going to be wanted at the low end of a barrow run, and he did not go up it because a man of fifty with a waggon of his own goes up it to carry things and to be paid for carrying them, and the day he goes up it to ask is the day he has a reason to be listened to that has nothing to do with the asking, and he would like it minuted, and it took him nine days to work out that it was the reason and not the courtesy.** The bell goes at the fourth hour and a half with forty-one rivets in it, rung by a boy of seventeen and a carpenter, and he is twenty-five yards off and does not ring it. **A Monday is the day the far end keeps, and the two facts are in two books in two counties and neither county has ever been sent the other's book, and a fire in a room over a mill four hundred miles off has a minute in it that is not the day-book of a records office and cannot be served and is not going to be sent here.** The tile is still on a coping four hundred miles off, a hand's width, with a rope on it in two loops and no yoke, and a man of sixty-four there wanted it lifted because a lifting is a job and a taking away is a throwing away, and it is the oldest of the three things in this volume nobody in this county can account for. Then the bag, the file, the strip of cut paper that weighs a shade over ten ounces, the bundle of nine with a piece of tarred hemp through it and the tenth opened on a Tuesday in the third week of Harvestmonth, nine bushels of barley in an aisle nobody has stood in for three generations, **and the last line of the volume, which is a road up from the water, four hundred yards long, and a joint on a parapet a foot and a half short, and salt coming over a lane, and a tide at a wharf.**

**Block 5 note — the block's calendar, its cast discipline, and its device counts, so that the Volume 04 close does not re-derive any of them.** **Calendar.** Path time throughout Chapters 191 to 197, **in the fifth and sixth winters of the sixteen, from the ninth day of the fourth week of the fourth cold to the ninth day of the twelfth, and a generation is a winter, so the fifth winter is the thirteenth generation and the sixth is the fourteenth, and the tenth generation is the first one that has never stood in the aisle over the cross.** The block's own clock is *the fourth cold*, *its fourth week*, *its ninth week*, *the warm week*, *the eleven weeks*, *the twelfth week*, *the fourth day of the next cold*. **No main-world month, no real-world month and no main-world weekday is spoken or written in any valley scene in any register including a notebook, in any of the seven valley chapters, and two were found in the first pass of Chapters 191 and 192 and both were repaired: a man of fifty-eight's *I ruled it on Thursday*, which is now *I ruled it on the tenth day*, and a man of thirty-four's byre that *came down on a Tuesday*, which is now *came down in a wet week*.** The three figures of the ninth spread are about four hundred, about a hundred and eighty-nine and nine, and the fourth entry in the day column is 1,496 pence, which is thirty-one marks and eightpence, and there is no figure of weeks and no figure of days anywhere in the valley. **The main-world section is the volume's second and last cut to the north, in one bounded section of three chapters: the fourteenth of Harvestmonth, YR 314, a Tuesday, not a market day; the twentieth, a Monday, not a market day, the fourth hour, and the seven days after it; and the twenty-fifth of Fallowmonth, YR 314, a Monday, not a market day, the volume's last day, the fourth hour.** The three-weeks warning is unchanged, no character in this block uses the word *failed* about a road, and he is still not writing a drift figure; **the two unreconciled numbers of his own count and the valley's count are untouched and neither is converted, and he got to that page a fifth time in this block and did not write a figure on it.** **Cast.** One new name of six and four slots unfilled, so Volume 04 closes at **six names of six and twenty-three of twenty-four unfilled, twenty-nine of thirty slots, one under the cap, and the five-names-and-twenty-seven-unfilled figure that came down the state files did not foot and is not reconciled.** **Devices and fillers, counted on the page, and every figure here is a whole-word count so that `stood` at 42 is not `understood` at a higher number.** **34,157 words, per chapter 3306 / 3142 / 3405 / 3287 / 3550 / 3586 / 3249 / 3503 / 3964 / 3165, and the ten columns foot at 34,157. And the five Volume 04 columns counted on the page are 33,045 / 33,385 / 34,216 / 34,011 / 34,157, footing at 168,814, and the manuscript is 270,562 + 184,519 + 146,561 + 168,814 = 770,456 in two hundred chapters. The 33,777 / 168,434 / 770,076 that stood here before the first repair pass and the 34,133 / 168,790 / 770,432 that stood here between the two were each true of the page at the moment they were written and both are left where they were as the facts they were; the close must count and not carry.** `---` rules **37**, one per chapter and a second in six and a third in three; bolded line-openings **95**; semicolons **0**; em-dashes **0**; straight double quotes **0**; curly apostrophes **0**; **`the whole of` 1** against Batch 0004's 3, **and the first pass of this block carried it at 26 and the writer went back through all ten chapters twice and cut it out of twenty-four places, and the one that is left is Chapter 195's *the whole of that week's work*, which is a span of a week and not a span of years. **The first repair pass recorded this device at 2 and gave a reason built on two survivors, one of which was a span of years, and both the count and the reason were asserted and not counted and the page reads one; that is continuity item 510 and it is the same defect in the pass whose stated thesis is that asserted figures are this project's signature failure.** `about N seconds` **1**, which is the echo of the refusal at a door in Chapter 179 and is a canon figure and not a hedge — the first pass of the block carried eight, and seven were cut, and **a ninth instance stood in Chapter 197 as *in nine seconds* and carried no *about* and so never entered this count at all, and it is repaired at the second pass; a device count is only as good as the pattern it matches and a phrase without its hedge is invisible to it**; `cut` **29** and every one of the twenty-nine ordinary and the working not among them and not named; **one System panel in the block, two plain lines, no number in it, and it is not a list**; no Gatehouse-voice line elsewhere. Filler as whole words, a baseline and not a target: **`nodded` 0, `held` 7, `wrote` 25, `stood` 42, `plain` 0, `counted` 16, `breathed` 0, `steady` 0**, against Batch 0004's 0 / 5 / 27 / 35 / 0 / 24 / 0 / 0, and `stood` is up on it and is recorded rather than hidden. **`about` 404, per chapter 42 / 43 / 41 / 41 / 37 / 43 / 41 / 44 / 38 / 34, and no chapter is over forty-four and the density does not compound. **The first repair pass recorded 414 with a column of 45 / 44 / 41 / 42 / 38 / 45 / 42 / 45 / 38 / 34 and said that three chapters sat on the ceiling exactly; the page bears out no position of that column, the total was 403 before this pass took one word out of Chapter 197 and put four in, and the highest chapter is 44 and it is Chapter 198. The repair pass's own new prose had put Chapter 198 to 49 before seven quantity hedges were taken out of 191, 196 and 198.** **`nine` is 248 in 34,157 words, 7.26 per 1,000, per chapter 27 / 27 / 18 / 27 / 25 / 36 / 28 / 15 / 22 / 23, and the volume's six-block rate now reads 2.87 / 4.58 / 7.31 / 11.11 / 5.40 / 7.26. That is above Batch 0004 as repaired and above Batch 0003, and the reversal did not hold, and the finding is that the residue in this block was not `about nine` and was `nine people` and `nine of them` as a hedge for *a few people heard it*, and that thirty-three of the first pass's instances were cut and the ones that are left are the ones where a figure of people is the point. The two ceilings the Batch 0004 review set are held and held with room: **`about nine + noun` is 1 / 0 / 0 / 2 / 0 / 2 / 0 / 2 / 0 / 2, nine in the block and a maximum of two against a ceiling of four, and `in nine years` is 0 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 0 / 1 / 0 / 2 / 0, maximum two against a ceiling of three. Eight of the block's `nine seconds` were the motif number used as a hedge for a moment — a step worked out on a step in about a minute — and seven of the eight were cut, and the eighth is Chapter 199's echo of the guild's man with the levels in Chapter 150 and is a canon figure. **All three statements of that one moment on the step in the cold now read *in about a minute* and a later phase must not find a fourth.****  **The motif is canon and none of it was cut: nine stones in a course, nine hundred doors, nine generations and the ninth spread, a hundred and forty, the ninth week, the ninth day, the ninth hour, the bench of nine, the nine of the ninth generation, the nine that is three households and is eight people, nine printed words at the foot of a two-hundred-year-old form, and a girl of nine who counts things.** **Money, footed in pence on the page before any total is said, once, in this block: 3 sixteen times over is 48, 48 thirty-one times over is 1,488, 4 twice over is 8, and 1,488 and eight is 1,496 pence, which is thirty-one marks and eightpence**, and the valley's own price of that month is eleven kitchens and the standing of a mill that has not ground and a root four households call a root and the rest of the valley calls a word they do not spell, and the two are never added. The nine weeks' 3,032 pence, the carrier's road's 1,920 pence and the finding's twelve pence are Batch 0004's and are not restated. **Nine of the ten chapters end on something other than a refusal and no chapter ends on a line that is very nearly an aphorism; the first pass claimed ten and Chapter 195 ended on a maxim, and the clause was cut and the chapter now ends on a barrow run going over a filled hole in the open with two men on it and neither of them saying one word about the day the man of nails is going to leave.** 191 ends on a decision to stop digging in the light. 192 ends on a step going into a face and a man saying the ground under it wants looking at tomorrow. 193 ends on about forty people standing at a bank in a cold listening to a river they had not heard in a generation. 194 ends on a rag paper in a coat and a hole in a bank at the ford. 195 ends on a barrow run, a filled hole and two men not saying one word. 196 ends on two figures of about two hundred people that are not the same and nowhere in nine miles that either of them can be put, and the fire going out at the ninth hour with a child on a fourth tread is where the ninth week closes, which is where it stood before the first repair pass moved the tenth week above it. 197 ends on a barrow going up nine hundred yards and coming back down, and one of the eleven said the wrong half of it. 198 ends on a joint half a foot short that nobody in that yard has measured. 199 ends on a bag on the ordinary post and a copy on a shelf with nothing against a person. 200 ends on a road up from the water. **And 198 and 199 are the two chapters that are not all his, and 200 is a road and not a man, which is the model and the whole of the instruction.**

---

**Block 5 note, second pass — the review of the Batch 0005 repair, and which of the descriptions above it invalidates.** **Continuity items 507 to 516 carry the record and the Batch 0005 entry in `state/batch-summaries.md` is not rewritten. Nothing planned moved: no event, no thread, no character, no date, no calendar, no money figure, no cast name, and the order of the ten chapters is unchanged. The entries above stand as the record of what the writing phase did and of what the first repair pass did, and a phase that foots a figure from them should foot it from the second pass, which is the one the Volume 04 close is pointed at. The passages above that are quoted detail and not plot are corrected here, and these are the only ones.** **Ch 196**, the entry above narrated the weeks as third, fifth, sixth, seventh, eighth, **tenth, ninth**, which was the order on the page before the first repair pass moved the tenth week and left the ninth week's closing paragraph underneath it; the ninth week is now closed where it was written and the tenth week is the chapter's last, and the entry above has been put in that order. **Ch 198**, the man of forty-seven is **on the bench of nine because he was elected and not because he is on a trade, and is paid fourpence a week out of a private box**; he is not a road warden, there is a road warden of that guild with a vote and not an office and it is a different man and he is not in that yard, and **the sentence about a decline and a survey is the guild's man with the levels' and is Chapter 150's and is said here a second time in a yard of forty men and not to one man in a hall**; the four men who were on the stone are asked separately and their accounts do not agree, and he says in a yard of forty men that he was on the low bank on the ninth of Harvestmonth from the second hour to the fourth. **Ch 199**, the witness at that end is the man of forty-seven and **he is on that bench and is paid out of a private box and is not the person keeping anything**, and what he is told in a hall over a wharf four years ago in nine seconds on a Friday is a man with a level's sentence and is Chapter 150's. **Ch 200**, the yard's inventory at the foot of the chapter **ends on a man of forty-seven on the bench of nine and not on a road warden**, and the garbled *who has worked eleven days in a year in which he has not voted once* is cut; the chapter's other two mentions were already right. **Ch 193**, the line about forty people coming off a bank now reads *before he had finished saying it* and the euphemism the first repair pass put there, whose antecedent was a two-word shout, is gone. **And the block's device counts above are corrected on the face of the Block 5 note** rather than in a second column, because three of them were wrong on the page and one of the three carried a justification built on an instance that does not exist: `the whole of` is **1** and not 2, `about` is **404** and not 414, and `about nine + noun` is **1 / 0 / 0 / 2 / 0 / 2 / 0 / 2 / 0 / 2** and nine in the block and not 1 / 1 / 0 / 2 / 0 / 3 / 0 / 2 / 0 / 2. **A second-pass note that corrects the block's own figures in place is the better record, and the reason is that a later writer reads the note and not the history of the note.** **And one formatting fault the block recorded three times as repaired was still on the page, in Chapter 200's yard paragraph, which opens the volume's last section: a stray closing `**` with nothing to close, the residue of a cut in that same paragraph. A whole-manuscript parity sweep of every line returns zero now and returned one before. No word of the paragraph changes and the block's figures are untouched.**
