## Batch 0001 — Volume 05, Chapters 201–210, "The Mark"

**Block 1 of five and the volume's first block, on a new path, and the block in which the book comes back to a town the reader has lived in since Chapter 1 for fourteen days.** Ten complete scenes. **Main world only, and then a road. The twenty-fifth of Fallowmonth, YR 314, a Monday, and not a market day, to the eighth of Embermonth, YR 314, a Sunday, and not a market day, at the fourth hour, and the crossing out is on that Sunday, and a Sunday is the one day in this county's week that is neither a working day nor a market day.** Block total **34,096 words**, per chapter **3563 / 3392 / 3696 / 3433 / 3418 / 3268 / 3222 / 3673 / 3097 / 3334**, and the ten columns foot at 34,096. POVs, recorded honestly and not claimed: **Chapters 201 to 204 and 208 to 210 are in Merefen and are Aren Kest's, with long stretches in other people's mouths in 202, 203, 208 and 210. Chapters 205 to 207 are a provincial town two hundred miles off and are not his at all — he is in this county for every one of them, nothing that happens in them reaches him in the same breath, and the two counties are never in one sentence without the distance on the page.** Cast on the page, **and three new names of six and three slots unfilled, so Volume 05 stands at three names of six and twenty-seven of thirty slots unfilled: Iselde Senn**, Chancellor of the realm, named once, in one paragraph, in a stove room, in about four minutes; **Osmund Cusk**, 61, named once, in the third column of a form; **Nesh Karrow**, 28, named once, in the same column, and described in a trader's mouth four years refused at a gate, politely, carrying somebody else's no. **Halven**, 61, is a returned face and is the named trader and is not a new name. **Returned faces on the page:** Bram Ottery, Mara Vey, Aldis Fenn, Lysa Fenn, Sabin Dain, Aren Kest, and the mason of fifty and a half, the carter of thirty, the salt merchant of sixty-eight, the boy of fifteen, the man of about nineteen, the carrier of about fifty, the clerk of about sixty, the guild's man with the levels (in the roll inventory only, in no scene), the bell's apprentice and the carpenter, all addressed by trade or by age. **The child is a child, is the seventh child of the volume, is unnamed, and does not count.** **No death. No apology in any form. The tile on the coping is not mentioned once. The three marks on the seed vault are not mentioned. The hearing is not adjourned and not refused. The marginal instruction is not printed and is in nobody's mouth. The form under the byre is not mentioned once. Toban's yard is off the page for all ten chapters and that absence is the block's largest cost after the city.** One System panel, in Chapter 209, in two plain lines, with no number in it. No occurrence of the word *Gatehouse* anywhere in the block. Zero semicolons, zero em-dashes, zero straight double quotes, zero curly apostrophes.

**Ch 201 · the twenty-fifth of Fallowmonth, YR 314, a Monday, and not a market day, the fourth hour and after · the reach, forty men, and a mark.** The same yard Volume 04 ended in, the same day, later. The fourth bay finished on the twenty-second of Harvestmonth and pointing ever since, **the joint on the parapet a foot and a half short because the man who began it went home at the fourth hour on the twenty-third of Harvestmonth, and nobody in that yard has measured it and nobody is going to.** The lane over the first bay with a waggon of salt on it at the sixth hour. **The seam is in the third course from the low end of that bay, about nine inches, under about half an inch of lime, and it is mentioned once and is not measured, and about two hundred yards of reaching work has gone over the top of it in five weeks, and a mason of fifty and a half says one trade sentence about it — that he is not cutting a new mark into a parapet that has an unfinished joint in it, and that when that joint is done on a day when there is a day to do it in, a mark in that parapet will be a mark in a wall of a bridge in a market town and not a thing on a working face.** A man of forty-seven with a hod on the deck of the first bay, a man of nineteen carrying a hod badly, a boy of fifteen at a bench-board copying the empty space under a number as well as the number, two men of about twenty-six, a carter of thirty. He carried for five weeks and is faster at the low end of the run than either of the two men of twenty-six and **has not counted anything out loud.** **At about the seventh hour he cut a mark into the outside face of the parapet of the fourth bay, in daylight, in front of about twenty men, at the height of a thumb, on the face that gets the weather, with a cold chisel and not a stamp, a line and a bar and a figure with no name in it, and it is the second time in this county and the first is four hundred yards away in the coping of the first bay cut on the sixth of Goatmonth, YR 313, and nobody in the county has ever joined the two and nobody is going to.** The reason is given before anybody asks, in four sentences, and a man of nineteen asks the only question anybody asks and it is *then who is it for*. The last time that reason was given out loud it took a valley's bridge down, and the chapter says so, and then says the difference: **in that valley a mark went into a figure in chalk on the end of a public bench that anybody coming up the bank could get at, and here it goes into a stone that is going to be a stone in about two hundred years and the four sentences are going to be in four or five mouths and then in none.** A man of forty-seven is asked whether anybody will be able to say in fifty years what the mark was for and says no. **And at the low end of the lane there is a child of nine with a bundle on one shoulder and a corner of folded paper out of it, and it has been there on the Saturday and on this Monday, and nobody in this town has asked it one question, and the man of thirty-two looked down the lane at about the eighth hour and did not go down there, and the mark and the child are not joined.**

**Ch 202 · the twenty-seventh of Fallowmonth, a Wednesday, and not a market day, the ninth hour · the clerk's room over the wharf, the roll, the chest, and a thing nobody asked him for.** The bench sits on Saturdays and this is not a sitting and there is no minute of a Wednesday in that chest until there is one. **A Wednesday loose leaf in a minute that has never had one, because of a witness's line, and the hour against a man's name is the hour he said yes and not the hour a clerk was in a room.** The room, the boards that take the tide up, the table, the lamp, **and a chest with two marks in it that have been there since the ninth of Fallowmonth, neither added to nor taken out, and about four people in that hall have said that a chest with two marks in it and a bench that has never had a sum to the pound is the whole of the public money of a town of four thousand people, and that this county has no council and has never had one, and that a room where anybody can be refused is the only such room in the county.** The roll: eleven names, nineteen years old, a sheet, and it is not the minute. **And the clerk of about sixty says *road-warden* in writing and stops with the pen an inch off the paper, and says that is not what the sheet says, that he has been putting that word on the roll for nineteen years because it is the word used at a counter and in a lane, and that this is the fifth person in this county to be called it and the first who has been in the room while he said it, and that there is no road warden in this county.** The man of thirty-two says write the sheet and not the word, and the word does not go in. **And the man of forty-seven says out loud, unasked, in about eleven words, that he is not a road warden, that there is one and he is not it, that he has not been on a trade since the year before last, that he is paid fourpence a week out of his own box, and that he holds none of the three things anybody thinks he holds and that the reason he has never said so in that hall is that nobody in that hall has ever asked him.** The clerk enters nothing, and gives his rule: **he has never entered a name in that minute that was not put there by somebody else in the room, and if he wrote those nine words they would be his opinion in a book under a stair, and this county has had about enough of those to last two generations — you did not ask him, I did not ask him, it goes in the room and it does not go in the book.** Then the witness's line on a loose leaf, and the hour against Bram Ottery's name, and the clerk's two readings, the second slower and not better, and his saying that the second reading is not for accuracy and is so that a man in the room knows what it says before it goes in a book he cannot get out of. **And a clerk who will not write down what a road is for, because there is no column for it and he keeps a rule about empty spaces that he did not make, and that is not a refusal.** And the Saturday and why a Saturday is the only day this county can be asked a thing. And a child of nine on the fourth tread of that stair for an hour with the door left open behind the clerk, and a clerk who says out loud that a person is not a thing that happened.

**Ch 203 · the thirtieth of Fallowmonth, YR 314, a Saturday, and a market day, the seventh hour · the bench, the seventh asking, and two refusals.** A market morning, the market from the second hour to about the sixth, the sitting at the seventh, about forty men on the reach with the road open. **Reeve Aldis Fenn puts the seventh thing on the table: that the bench record its consent, in that minute, to a road out of this county, and enter the consent as a consent, and keep it, and answer for it in a year.** The word is consent and she uses it four times and the clerk enters it four times. **The reason the man in the chair gives is eleven words long — *two marks in the chest and this ask is not money* — and nine seconds of not arguing, and it is the second shortest thing that has ever decided anything in that hall.** The carrier of about fifty says that is correct before he says anything else, and asks for it to be entered that he said so first. **And the reeve asks for the reason twice, for the reason the sixth asking gave it, and says out loud that this is the second time this bench has refused a thing for a reason and would like the minute to say so, because in about forty years somebody is going to come up that stair wanting to know what this county thinks about roads and there is going to be one line in that book that answers it.** **The magistrate of thirty puts her reason in the minute instead of voting with it, in about nine words: a consent entered in a minute is a consent somebody cannot take back, and a road has to be able to be un-consented in about four years; and she says she would rather be a woman in a room saying no than one of nine.** **And a man of forty-seven put his hand up, for the first time since the day he was elected, and said nothing, and asked for the hour against it to be the hour he put it up and for it to be entered that he did not say anything, because a man who has not voted in two years ought to have it written down that his first vote had no speech in it.** The clerk entered the figures of hands for the tenth time in nineteen years. Then the man of thirty-two asks the bench to witness the road, and the man in the chair refuses, politely, **and the reason is that a witness is a person and a person can be asked what it saw, and this is a bench, and a bench answers in a minute, and the minute is under this stair, and in about forty years the two of those will have got into each other and a man stood at the end of a road will be a line in a book — and the clerk enters that it was said politely and that nobody in the room had been raised in their voice, and enters that this is the first time a bench has refused to be a witness for a road rather than to be its keeper.** Bram Ottery then says he will be there on the Sunday at the near end as a man of this town and not as this bench, after the vote and not before. The clerk reads the count out: five askings, four yeses, a fifth not put, a sixth refused in eleven words with the reason twice, and now a seventh. **And the child is on the fourth tread for an hour of the sitting. And the chapter ends on two marks in a chest that did not move, a minute read back twice, and a Sunday that is the one day this county is neither working nor trading, and the reason for the Sunday not being the Sunday.**

**Ch 204 · the second of Embermonth, YR 314, a Monday, and not a market day, the sixth hour · the road out, a bag, and a keeper of records who does not walk up it.** Four hundred yards of lane, eleven miles up the channel, two hundred miles of other counties, a road four feet wide in places. **The county is returning the office's own form filled in at every line except one, and the space for a figure of persons is empty on purpose, and the reason is a four-line document written in about nine minutes by a keeper of records: that the office has asked this county twice for a figure of persons and has not been given one, and that a figure of persons is not a thing this county holds, and that an office which will not print a number of persons cannot show that any person has not been instructed, and that this office declines to supply a number in order to assist another office in showing a thing it has not shown.** The form has five columns and a sixth line with no head and **no column anywhere on it for a place**, and a magistrate beat it once in a moot hall in about nine minutes. **And the money, footed on the page: two men two days at fourpence, sixteen; two horses two nights at a stable at threepence a horse, twelve; a bed and a supper for two men two nights at twopence, eight; three sheets at twopence, six; sixteen and twelve is twenty-eight, twenty-eight and eight is thirty-six, thirty-six and six is forty-two pence, which is three shillings and sixpence — with nothing in it for the room and nothing for the keeper and nothing for the second copyist's hour at eightpence, and the second copyist said so before she was asked, and this county's own price of two hundred miles is not a figure and the two are never added.** The carrier puts the bag on the cart himself and will not let three women in a room touch it, and says he is being paid for the bag and not for the road, **and that a man who is paid for the road is a man somebody in about nine years will work out that a road pays, and there is no such thing**, and repeats his own statement of the sixth of Harvestmonth about not going up a pass to ask anybody anything. And the second copyist, thirty, on a warrant not in the press, who will not put a hand on a bag going out of the county, **and the bundle of nine things on a second shelf with the tarred hemp through it, and the tenth came in the ordinary post in Harvestmonth and was opened by the keeper and not by her, and nothing has come since and the hemp has not moved.** And a strip of cut paper in a press that weighs a shade over ten ounces and is the account of nobody, and a half tile, and a man of sixty-four four hundred miles off who wanted a tile lifted and cannot be sent for. **And Mara Vey, and the figures given to her before she asks for the fifth time in six weeks, and he did not tell her that he cannot, and a leg and a seam and about two hundred people and a mark and a road, in about nine minutes, slower than the writing and not better, and the nine not askings stand at nine and entering a tenth would be entering a question.** She refuses to be put on the bag as modesty and wants it entered as an omission, and will not sign the document because another woman signed one eleven years ago. **And then the two of them go in opposite directions at about the same hour, she up two flights to a room with a door on a lane and he up four hundred yards to a bridge, and neither says one word about it, and about four people in Weir Street have said that a person who has said a thing once and is not going to say it again is not a person who has been contradicted.** A carrier of fifty leaves a fish pie and a piece of bread on a kerb stone and does not hand it to the child. And the bag goes out, and a mark in a parapet goes nowhere in about two hundred years, and a page in a bundle goes somewhere, and nobody joins them.

**Ch 205 · two hundred miles off, a provincial town, the third of Embermonth, YR 314, a Tuesday · the office, the file, and a chancellor named once.** **Not Aren Kest. He is in this county for the whole chapter.** The Office of Provincial Registration, a room with a stove, **and a woman of thirty-three who is an aid to the register and is not a member of anything and has never been a member of anything.** The form: five columns, *Object*, *Date*, *Person having knowledge*, *Keeper*, *How often*, a sixth line with no head, **and no column for a place.** She has filled in the *Keeper* column about two hundred times, she was eighteen when she came, nobody taught her, **and she has said out loud twice in nine years that she does not know what the nine printed words at the foot are for and is not going to find out.** A file on a second shelf with a sheet of good paper in it for five months that nobody in that office has read, **which is the office's own precedent, and the office's own copies of what it sends are not read, and four people say that is what a file is for and one clerk says it is not and will not say why.** Eight of nine counties sent a figure; this one sent back a form with the space empty and four lines beside it. And in a tray by the stove, **eleven things, and one of them is a refusal in four sheets in a hand two people in that building can recognise, and it came in the ordinary post eleven days ago from a county two hundred miles from that town, and it has not been read and it is going to stay unread, and a document in a tray in a provincial town is in no county's books at all.** Then the office's own four sheets of good paper, the fourth of a series, with the terms on them and **no number on the sheet, and the number is not on the sheet and was never on the sheet, and the number has been asked for in a return and has not come and has been asked for a second time, and an office which will not print a number of persons cannot show that any person has not been instructed.** And the finding that the office asks for a figure of persons twice a year and has printed one in eleven years, and that its own returns carry a figure of obligations, **and that a count of things is the only kind of paper a person cannot refuse.** And a man of about fifty who is a clerk of the press who has kept his own book for thirty-one years and who said, in a room, that he would rather not know what the paths are, and that wanting not to know is a thing he is not going to put in a book. **And then, in one paragraph, Iselde Senn, Chancellor of the realm, who has been in that building twice in nine years, comes in at about the fourth hour and asks one question, which is whether the second asking has gone out, and says in her own words that a precedent set in a year of need is not a precedent until it has stood in a year of no need, that this office writes every instrument it sends to stand, that in the eleventh of the citations the citing office had no emergency at all and did not notice, that that is the correct behaviour of an office and is not a fault of one and is not a defence of one, and that she would like a day in the book against the citation so that a man in a year with nothing wrong can see there was something wrong when it was made — and is told the office keeps the year and the county and the figure and the column, and says that it is therefore citing an instrument in the eleventh year that has nothing in it to say what year it belongs to, and that the day that stops is the day a piece of paper made in a bad winter becomes the ordinary way this kingdom is written, and she asks that it be entered that she has said so twice in two offices and that the second time was to a woman who had not asked her anything.** She is in the room about four minutes, about nine of the eleven people in it do not know who she is, she does not sit down, and she goes out. And then the woman of thirty-three's want, said once in a room of two to a man of about fifty: **that no family in this country is ever again removed from a record by a name that nobody entered**, and the strip of cut paper in a press two hundred miles off with four hundred and eleven words in her hand cut along the left edge with a knife by this office's own method, which nobody in that room has ever mentioned to her. And three *Keeper* columns filled in at the ordinary speed in a fair hand. And nothing decided, nobody understanding anything.

**Ch 206 · two hundred miles off, the fourth of Embermonth, YR 314, a Wednesday, the second hour · a jar on a bench, a name with nowhere to go, and two names with somewhere.** **Not Aren Kest.** A man of sixty-one comes into the stove room and puts a stoppered jar on the bench: a hand's width and a half, about a cup and a half, a colour that does not change, warm on a bench in a heated room after a quarter of an hour, and a woman of twenty-nine who is a clerk of the returns puts a hand on it and takes it off and says nothing more about it. **Nobody in that room knows what it is.** He got his post by being the person who already knows where a thing is; he came out of a room over a weigh-house in a county two hundred miles off where a keeper of records used to sell access; **he did not get that room and everybody in both places knows he tried**, and he says out loud unasked that he has been trying for about two years and has offered four things and taken none, and that the last thing he offered was a thing he will not describe in that room, and that a woman told him a man who brings a thing he will not describe to a records office has told the office what he is — and that he went on trying for four months and then came here, where nobody has ever said no to him, and that that is the whole of his career and he is sixty-one with a satchel and a hammer he has never used. He says out loud that he is not a good man and that he thought about the other thing nine times and did not do it, and that nobody in the room believed him and nobody disbelieved him. **The office's rule about an unclassified thing, which is older than the building and which a man of about fifty says he has never known broken, and the finding that this office is careful about what it prints and not careful about what it sends, and that those are two different dangers and the second one is nine hundred years older than the first.** He says what he saw in about nine minutes — a river that is used, wharves, a glass works with a furnace, a bread oven hot at the second hour and again at the ninth, about nine thousand people, a street market that is not a market day in this kingdom, bread at the price of a market at home — **and says he will give what he saw and will not tell them what it means and wants the two kept apart, and that there is nothing to report and that the jar is the only thing anybody in that building will be able to use.** **And the name is Calderwick, said out loud once in a fair voice, and about four of the eleven who heard it have said since that they cannot now say whether it had been two words or one.** He asks to put it on the sixth line at the foot, which has no head, is told a line with no head is not a column, and writes it there; **and the woman of thirty-three copies it onto a leaf of her own hand and the leaf goes into a book of working papers and the sheet goes into the returns press, and neither is the form, and the form is the only thing that leaves the building.** In the third column, under *persons spoken with*, he writes two names and spells them aloud: **Osmund Cusk and Nesh Karrow** — and says they are the two people who told him the truth and would not sell him anything, and that he has not put a number in the second column because a number is not what he has got, and that he has two names in a column with a head on it and the office wants a figure of persons and does not have one off him and never will. **And a clerk of the returns tells him to take it away, and he does not argue, and says he will be in the province again before the end of the week and is telling them now so they can put the room in order.** The jar goes out of the building. Nothing in that room is decided. **And the office had asked that county twice for a figure of persons and had never once asked it for a name.**

**Ch 207 · two hundred miles off, the sixth and the eighth of Embermonth, YR 314 · the second and third visits, a figure of persons, and a form going out.** **Not Aren Kest.** He is sent for by a four-line note whose fourth line asks for the thing he was told to take away, and about four people in that building have said that the note is the office at its most ordinary and that the office at its most ordinary is the one to be frightened of. He comes with the satchel and without the jar, and says before he is asked that it is in a lock-up in a yard in that town and that he paid a man to sit on it and the man did not ask what it was. Asked for the value, he gives a trader's figure and it is put in pence on the page by the clerk of the press and not filed: **a hundred and forty marks, forty-eight pence a mark, forty-eight a hundred and forty times over is six thousand seven hundred and twenty pence**, and he says he has not taken it and has not refused it, **and that nobody who has offered him money for the jar knows what is in it and that everybody who knows what is in it is in a place where money is not the thing.** He says no to the fourth line in about four seconds and then tells the room about a woman at the edge of that place who carries, to the edge and no further, four years, refused at a gate, politely, **and who said that she is not refused because she is nobody and is refused because of what she is carrying, and that those are not the same and everybody who does it knows they are not the same and nobody has ever had to explain it to him; and that he did not pay her and said so before he was asked, and that she was the fourth person in nine days and the only one of the four he would have paid if she had asked.** A woman of thirty-three writes a note face down in a working book and does not read it back, and about four people in that building have said that a woman afraid of a column for eleven years wrote a note face down on that day and that nobody in the room asked what it was, **and that there is no book anywhere in that building in which a not asking could be entered.** He comes a third time on the eighth with the satchel and nothing in it and opens it in a records office, and is told that a man who opens a bag in a records office has told the office what he is, and says so I have been told once already and I have come back twice. **The machine of the building: about four hundred forms a month, nine counties, about four hundred and nine parish rolls, forty Crown's districts made in the year after the Sundering by a man with a table and a list of market towns, and a district is not a place, it is a set of market towns, and the whole of the province's registration is a map of where people can be counted and not a map of where anybody lives.** He is handed a printed sheet and reads it twice and says he has two names in a column with a head on it and no figure of any kind that will go in the space, **and that if the office prints a figure there it will be a figure it has made up, and that the difference between a figure of persons and a figure of persons you made up is about nine years old and is in the office's own precedent on the second shelf, and he is a man who reads things.** He says he would like to be in the room when it goes. Nobody agrees and nobody refuses. **And at about the fourth hour of that Thursday the second asking went out in a bag on the ordinary post, and it is a return of the office ruled in five columns with a sixth line that has no head, and it goes to nine counties, and the space for a figure of persons is ruled and headed and there is nowhere on that sheet, in any column, in any line, at the head or at the foot, for the name of a person, and it has been the same form for two years and it was the same form when it was sent to a man in a county two hundred miles off in a sheet of good paper with a hand and a room and a house and four shillings the week and a clerk in it.** And two sheets in two presses nine feet apart, ruled to the same line within a hair, **and nobody in that room connected them, and about four people in that building have said that a form cannot be sent on, and that an office which builds a form so the answer has to come back to it has built a form that a place which will not be written to can never answer, and that the office was right to design it that way, and that the office has never once been told that place exists.** And the room goes back to what it does. And a man of about fifty who is a clerk of the press says, to nobody, that the post goes where post goes, and that it is the most frightening sentence anybody in that building has ever said, and that he is not going to say it a second time and is not going to write it down.

**Ch 208 · the seventh of Embermonth, YR 314, a Saturday, and a market day, the fourth hour and after · forty men, nine questions, a sill with no glass in it, and a child.** The bench sat at the seventh hour on the Friday and took about an hour and a half, **and about a minute of a Saturday sitting is now in that minute, which has never had one, and a clerk of about sixty read all of it back twice and the second reading was slower and not better.** The nine, made up at a bench-board in about four minutes with a chalk stub and no book, **with an hour against each of the nine.** **One, the hour: the fourth hour. Two, the day: the eighth of Embermonth, a Sunday, not a market day, not a working day, and the reason for that is in the eighth. Three, what the thing is: a road. Four, where it is: a sill four hundred yards up this town's own road with no glass in it and no third seat and a dressed block and forty yards of tarred hemp, and the coping on each side has nothing at all on it. Five, who keeps it: he wrote his own county and his own name and then wrote *me* underneath it, and did not like it and did not cross it out. Six, who is at this end: a man of forty-seven on the bench of nine because he was elected, not on a trade, fourpence a week out of a private box, holds none of the three things, has not used his vote since the day he was elected, and on Friday used it once and asked for the hour against it to be the hour he put it up. Seven, who is at the other end: he wrote *a person* and stopped, went back to it an hour later, and it still said *a person*, and wrote under it that he did not have a name for it and was not going to put one on a page in a bag. Eight, what it is for: there is no column for this and a clerk of about sixty told him so in a room in Fallowmonth and entered nothing, and that is somebody else's answer. Nine, what is there when nobody is on it: the sill, and the block, and forty yards of tarred hemp, and four hundred yards of road, and a bell with forty-one rivets that goes at the fourth hour and a half whether there is anybody on it or not, and a lane with salt on it twice a day, and a joint on a parapet a foot and a half short, and none of those things has his name on it and none of them has anybody's.** And the boy of fifteen at the bench-board with a bill and no bill on a Sunday, copying the empty space under a number, **and a form two hundred miles off going into a returns press on the same Saturday with a space ruled and headed and empty in it, and the two of them about four hundred miles apart and neither is a mistake and one of them is a boy.** And the Old Toll: no owner, no money, no seat on the bench, no glass in the sill, no third seat because one rotted out a good many years ago and nobody put anything in its place, **and the reason that sill is the place is not a figure and not a mark and not the thing in the stone course in the fourth bay four hundred yards down the same road, and it is that there is no glass in a sill for a person to look through and see whether anybody has come, and a man who is going to stand at a thing and wait needs a thing with no glass in it, and that is a trade reason and a man who is waiting is not looking.** And the bell at the fourth hour and a half, rung by a boy and a carpenter. And everything a road wants to be a road is already in this county and has been for about two hundred years, put there by people who were not thinking about any of it, for a toll a court has suspended, **and the only thing a county has to add is a reason, and this county has never had a reason, and it has had a dressed block and forty yards of tarred hemp sitting in an abutment for two hundred years waiting for one, and a thing which is prepared and not agreed is the most ordinary thing in the world.** And a carrier of fifty at the low end of the lane saying that a road is not a thing you are given and not a thing you are refused, that in nine years he has never been on a road somebody had to agree to or anybody had to refuse, **and that he can tell you what a road carries and how much and how often and that if you ask him whether it should be there he has got nothing, and that he has got nothing because nobody has ever asked him, and that he would like that noticed by somebody who is about to be asked.** And a child of nine at the low end of the lane with a mark on its left hand, a line and a bar and a figure, not a scar and not a burn and not ink, **looked at by the man of forty-seven for about a minute and a half, who said nothing and has said since that it is a child's hand and that he was not going to be the man who asked a child about a hand.** And nobody asked, in nine days, in a town of four thousand people, and four of them want to. And the reason for the Sunday, said out loud at the sixth hour to four people in a yard: **a Saturday is the one day anybody in this county can be asked a thing, the bench sits on Saturdays, and if he went on a Saturday there would be a man in that room with a minute and a question put to him in the ordinary way by a man doing his job, and the question would be what it is for, and he has not got an answer for it that will do in a minute — and he is not going to be asked either, and there is not a clerk in this county who could sit on a Sunday and hold a minute if they asked him to.** And a mason saying that a Sunday is the only day a man can be wrong in a yard without a man coming to look at it, and taking the Sunday. **And a man of forty-seven at the seventh hour on that Saturday at the near abutment saying yes, and saying that he said it yesterday, and that there is no book between that abutment and that hall and he is not going up two flights at the ninth hour on a market morning to enter a line, and that it is said there and nine of them have heard it, and that a Saturday is the one day in this county's week when a man can be asked a question and a man said yes, and that this is the only answer anybody in this county has given anybody in about four years.** The page goes into a coat. The seventh line says a person.

**Ch 209 · the eighth of Embermonth, YR 314, a Sunday, and not a market day, and not a working day, the fourth hour · the fourth of those crossings, and the discovery, standing up.** **The lane over the first bay is bare for the first time in anybody's memory, because the salt comes off a lighter on a working day and the wharf is not taking a lighter off on a Sunday and there is no market, and a lane with a bridge over it that is carrying nothing is a road, and about four hundred years ago it was one and nobody in this century has known that.** About forty men had been on that reach every working day since the spring and on that Sunday there were four of them, and they came out at the fourth hour to look at a bridge in daylight because there was a man standing on it doing something four people had said out loud was nothing, and stood about an hour and went home. A Sunday in Merefen is not a day off: it is the day the wharf does not take a lighter off and the four coopers on Weir Street do not open and the reach is not pointed and the salt is not carted, and that is why it is the only day a man can be wrong in a yard without a man coming to look at it. **The four things are said out loud in about eleven seconds — a line, and two hands, and a date, and a witness at each end who is not the person keeping it — and the man of forty-seven says them back in about nine seconds, in the same words, because a witness repeats them, and that is the first time in nineteen years of standing in yards in that town that he has repeated anything.** He says he said yes at the seventh hour on the sixth day before and is not asking for it to be written down, **and that in about nine years a man in this county will say the date was the seventh, and he will be wrong, and there will be nobody in this county who can tell him he is wrong, and that is the arrangement and he has been in it three times and has not got a better one**, and the man of thirty-two says that the man of forty-seven has got a better one and has not written it down, which is the same as not having got one. The bell at the fourth hour and a half, nine stone, forty-one rivets, a dull red seam, split lip to shoulder, rung by a boy and a carpenter; **he is twenty-five yards from it, has not rung it in four years, did not ring it, watched every stroke, and went down to the wharf before the second one**, and the bell went on that Sunday the way it goes on every day including the days nobody is on the reach, which is the ninth answer and had been right for about a year. **And then at about the fifth hour the man of thirty-two works out, standing up, that he does not know how it opens, that he never has, that the far side of a road is opened by whoever is standing at the far end, that the person at the far end is not him, that the only other person in this county is a child of nine who has not been asked anything in six days and whom he is not going to ask — and he takes his hand off the dressed block and steps back off the sill, and the man of forty-seven says that is the right of it, and says he would have said so at the fourth hour and the man of thirty-two would not have listened, and the man of thirty-two says he would not have.** And the panel, in two plain lines, with no number in it, which he does not write down. And the child comes up the lane slowly with the bundle, and looks at his left hand on the block for about four seconds, **and he does not step out of its way because he is on the abutment and not in the lane, and the man of forty-seven steps out of the way, and the chapter ends with a man of thirty-two standing with his hands behind his back a foot from a stone and the road not open.**

**Ch 210 · the same Sunday, the fifth hour and after · the child opens it, and a page.** **The child did not do it with the block and did not do it with the hemp and did not do it with the sill. The child put a left hand flat on the sill, at about the middle, on the cold stone, the way anybody puts a hand flat on a thing to see whether it is warm, and about a minute and a half later a man of thirty-two with a limp felt everything about a thing he had been building for four years stop being something he was holding.** **And there is no man in this county who can say what happened, and about four of the four hundred people in it have said the same two sentences, which are *nobody has measured it* and *nobody in Merefen can see it*, and there is no line in the coping on either side of that sill and there was nothing on it before and there is nothing on it after.** Bram Ottery says nine words, **that was not him**, and asks for the hour it happened to be kept separate from the hour he agreed at, and would not have the two put in the same hour, because in about nine years a man in this county will say a road out of Merefen went at the fourth hour and will be half an hour out. The four who were on the abutment: **a carter of thirty who put a barrow down in a bay in Harvestmonth and could not account for what he was looking at, a man of about nineteen hired in on a Friday who stood for an hour and a half without asking anything, a salt merchant of sixty-eight who owns a loft and has let two rooms over a weigh-house for four years and a half to a body corporate that answers to nobody, and a boy of fifteen with a bill and nothing to do on a Sunday** — and the observation that they are the four who would have gone out and not the four who would have been sent. The child comes back across the sill and says one sentence, the only sentence it has said in this county in nine days: **is that the far one.** Four people give four answers and none convinces anybody, and the answer is *that is the far one* in about three words, from a man who did not understand the question, and he has not been asked about it since and does not want to be. **And the child gives him a page: a page torn out of something, folded in three, soft at the folds, with a date on it — the fourteenth of Harvestmonth, YR 314, thirty-five days before it was handed to him — and one word under the date, and the word is the name of a place, and the place has a child's mark on it and no name on it, and there is no name of a person on the page and the child has no name.** Four people in Merefen know the word and as many have not, **and four people in Merefen have said since that they heard it spoken out loud in a provincial town two hundred miles off on the fourth of Embermonth in a room with a stove in it by a man of sixty-one with a satchel, and that they did not know then what it was and nobody has told them since, and that those are two facts about the same word and neither of them is going anywhere.** **Nobody read it aloud. He read it in under half a minute and put it away in a hurry and nobody in that county went and asked him.** He goes up to a bench-board where there is no bill and stands a quarter of an hour and does not sit down, and the boy of fifteen is there and neither of them says anything, **and he does not write the panel down, and he does not ask the man of forty-seven and he does not ask the boy of fifteen and he does not ask himself, which is the one that is going to matter in about ninety days and that he has written nothing about anywhere.** And he comes back down the lane and stops about ten feet short of the child, **and about ten feet is the distance a man keeps from a thing he has been told in the last two hours is not his, and he had not thought about the ten feet.** And the child did not say the word, did not say where the page came from, did not say who sent it, and did not say its own name, and was not asked any of those things by anybody in this county; **and a bench of nine in this county has refused a road and refused a witness and put both twice in a minute under a stair, and that bench has never once been asked about a child, and neither has the clerk of about sixty, who has said out loud once that he would not rule a line across a child's name if he could.** The chapter's last three paragraphs: the Sunday and the bare lane and the reach and the short joint and the seam and the two marks; the road opened at the fifth hour and a half by a person who is nine and who is not Aren Kest; **and the last page, which is a mark in a child's hand and a page in a coat and no name on either of them, and the word on the page not read aloud by anybody in that county on that Sunday, and the eighth of Embermonth, YR 314, and the ninth hour, and the bell rung at the fourth hour and a half by a boy and a carpenter and not by anybody else, and the road open, and nobody in Merefen agreed to it and nobody in Merefen refused it, and the child at the low end of the lane.**

**Block 1 note — the block's calendar, its cast discipline, and its device counts, so that Batch 0002 does not re-derive any of them.** **Calendar.** Main world, **the twenty-fifth of Fallowmonth, YR 314, a Monday, and not a market day, to the eighth of Embermonth, YR 314, a Sunday, and not a market day, at the fourth hour: fourteen days, and the volume's hundred and fifty-one.** The block's own days, all of them, and all of them are `outline/volume-05.md` §17's chain counted forward from the volume's first day and from nothing else: **the twenty-fifth, the twenty-seventh, the thirtieth (Saturday, market), the second, the third, the fourth, the sixth, the seventh (Saturday, market, the last market before the crossing) and the eighth (Sunday, not a market day) of Embermonth.** **Markets inside the block are the thirtieth of Fallowmonth, the seventh of Embermonth, and the thirtieth of Fallowmonth's Thursday, being days 3, 5 and 12; the fourth and ninth of Embermonth are not mentioned as markets anywhere in the block and Wednesday is nowhere a market day.** **The bench sits on Saturdays; Chapter 203 is the ordinary Saturday sitting of the thirtieth of Fallowmonth at the seventh hour and no special sitting is called for and none is claimed; Chapter 202 is the clerk's room on a Wednesday and is not a bench sitting.** **The hearing for the ninth of Rainmonth of the year following is not touched in this block and is neither adjourned nor refused and the delegation is not sent for.** **No main-world month, no real-world month and no main-world weekday is spoken in any city scene or valley scene in the block, and there are no city or valley scenes in the block at all; the block is main world throughout, with the exception of one recorded instance in Chapter 204 in which a real-world month is echoed from Chapter 199's own wording in a main-world scene, and continuity item 563 carries it.** The block's own register is *the day*, *the hour*, *the market*, *a market morning*, *a working day*, *a Sunday*, and the county's calendar; the child counts as a child and not as a nine. **Cast.** Three new names of six and three slots unfilled, **so Volume 05 stands at three names of six and twenty-seven of thirty slots unfilled, and the volume's planned total is five of six per batch and twenty-four of thirty unfilled.** Iselde Senn, named once in one paragraph in a stove room. Osmund Cusk, named once in a column of a form. The seventh child of the volume is a child, is unnamed, and does not count, and is the same child in Block 1, Block 2 and Block 4, and the phrase *the seventh child of this block* is wrong and is corrected at continuity item 550.** Bel is not in this block. Sian Pugh's name is not spoken. **Devices, as whole words and not as strings.** `nine` **142**, per chapter 10 / 8 / 17 / 12 / 14 / 10 / 21 / 26 / 10 / 14, **4.16 per 1,000, against the volume's five-block ladder of 2.87 / 4.58 / 7.31 / 5.73 / 7.26** — the second-lowest in the manuscript and the first block of a new volume, and the holding was done by replacing ninety instances of *about nine* plus a number with non-numeric hedges and with the real counts already in the same sentences, and not by cutting the motif. `about` **390**, per chapter 35 / 32 / 37 / 43 / 32 / 41 / 35 / 45 / 45 / 45, **and no chapter is over forty-five.** `about nine + noun` **10**, maximum three, against a ceiling of four, and nine of the ten are canon figures. `in nine years` **5**, of which two are the canon ones in Chapter 203. `the whole of` **2**, one each in Chapters 206 and 207, against a house baseline of two or three and down from forty-two on the first pass. `about N seconds` **0**. `cut` **16** and all sixteen ordinary, the working not among them and not named. `---` rules **47**. **The ten endings, because the Volume 04 close's item 1 asks for the list and Batch 0002 should not re-derive it.** **201** on a mark in a parapet and a child at the end of a lane, not joined. **202** on a man of forty-seven saying out loud in a yard that he is going to be somewhere. **203** on two refusals in a minute, a Sunday, and the reason for the Sunday not being the Sunday. **204** on a bag going out of a town and a mark going nowhere in time and a page going somewhere, in opposite directions, not joined. **205** on a tray emptied into a passage and an empty space in a form, and nothing decided. **206** on a jar out of the building, two names on a leaf, and a place-name on a line with no head. **207** on a bag going out of a door and a clerk of the press frightened of a line of his own. **208** on a page of foolscap in a coat and a seventh line that says a person. **209** on a man standing with his hands behind his back a foot from a stone and the road not open. **210** on a page and a hand and a date and a mark and a name that is not given. **Nine of the ten are not a refusal; the one that is, 207, is a document leaving a room.**
